Title Page
The Aegis Cycle
BURNThrough
Draft 2.2


PROLOGUE
1. Ease of our Escape
The vessel’s hull groaned in protest — a deep, teeth-rattling wail as metal strained against maneuvers its long-dead designers never imagined. The decks quivered, consoles flickered, and the ship’s nervous system spasmed like its computer core was having a seizure. Through the viewscreen, the industrial graveyard of Drosshaven’s outer sector fell away — a sprawl of smoke, slag, and dying light shrinking behind them as they clawed toward open space.
“If we die out here trying to escape this whorehouse of a space station, I swear to stars, I’m gonna haunt you for all eternity,” the first officer called over the shriek of alarms, hands flying across the console to calm the screaming sensor panel. “And I’m not talking about the friendly kind of haunt, mind you. I mean the ‘wake-you-up-at-night-with-the-wailing-of-dragging-chains-and-moaning-demons’ kind.”
The pilot snorted. “You know, between your ear-shattering snoring and your bedtime gastrointestinal… acrobatics, I think you’ve haunted my sleep enough already. HOLD ON!”
He threw the ship into a stomach-churning evasive pattern as proximity alerts howled.
“It’s part of my charm — it’s my bedside manner!”
“Yeah, well, your bedside manner needs work.”
“So does your flying, Cappy.”
“Suggestion box is in the back, right before the airlock. Knock yourself out!”
A volley of errant shots whizzed across the port bow. The pilot banked hard, avoiding the worst of it.
“Incidentally, my bedside is legendary,” the first officer added with a roguish grin. “Just ask those twins on Horizon Station.”
“I prefer your OTHER side. So did the twins — FYI.”
Three pursuit craft emerged from the haze of Drosshaven, unmistakable silhouettes: modified haven-raiders with weapons systems far beyond legitimate defense specs. The vessel shuddered as a glancing shot struck their port side.
“Port thrusters are screaming bloody murder,” the first officer reported. “One more burst like that and we’ll be venting plasma across half the sector. I just fixed that manifold. I am not spending another seventy-two hours recalibrating it while you insult my repair techniques.”
The pilot didn’t respond. His attention was fixed on the nav display.
“I’m ready for those jump calculations. How long?”
“Two minutes. Assuming the nav computer doesn’t—”
A nearby console erupted in sparks.
“…better make that three. Rerouting auxiliary processors.”
“We don’t have three minutes.”
“Then perhaps you shouldn’t have antagonized everyone in the Drosshaven sector last month.”
“That was not my fault,” the pilot snapped, yanking the controls again. Behind them, inertial dampeners failed in compartments aft, sending wrenches, galley crates, and a case of wine flying through the commons — a wild, booze-soaked blizzard of metal, glass, and confetti.
“Oh please. While I was charming that lovely logistics officer for clearance IDs, you were in a shouting match with the sector’s biggest arms dealer.”
“He was price-gouging me!”
“He was, and — surprise! — he was also armed. So were the fifteen men he had with him.”
“We wouldn’t have been within ten clicks of here if you could remember once and for all whether it’s a flush that beats a full house or vice versa.”
“That is not fair. I had the Havosian twins — one on each arm.”
“And you didn’t think three pairs of eyes on your cards might be a disadvantage?”
“What?! Come on, those girls were as wholesome as they—”
“Wholesome?! Christ. When are you gonna learn that galaxy-caliber tits don’t necessarily mean high moral character!”
The first officer winked. “Not all of us have the luxury of breaking hearts in the Chain’s military elite, Cappy. Some of us have to make do with civilian conquests.”
“You’re so naive—‘I manage!’ — I don’t see tha—”
The argument climbed toward absurdity until, as if a silent alarm had tripped between them, they both paused.
Breathless.
“How’s that? Blood flowing?”
“Yeah… yeah, I feel good. Feel it coursing.”
“Nice. That’s what I was aiming for.”
“Well, bullseye. Let’s do this.”
Below deck, two pallets of unmarked cylindrical cryonic matrix containers filled the belly of the smuggler’s pod. The chamber’s readout reported a stable 2.7 kelvin. No official manifest existed. No transport contract had ever been filed. No ship or crew data recorded. Yet someone had clearly leaked their route from Drosshaven to Erevos.
Then — a new concussion rattled the port thruster.
“What the hell was that?” the captain yelled.
“Uh… yeah. Dross boys just brought out the big guns. Looks like two Vestian plasma cannons just RSVP’d.”
“Wonderful. How long till they recharge?”
“Forty-five seconds.”
“And our jump window?”
“…Forty seconds. Now.”
“Nothing like fudging spacetime. Standby to jump!”
A direct hit sent emergency lights blinking across the cockpit. Coolant hissed. Plasma relays blew.
“There goes our starboard stabilizer.”
“Can we make the jump?”
“Either we jump, or we become very expensive space confetti. And I’ve got a date next week with the vixen who runs that repair shop on Nexus Station.”
“You do not have a date with Veraina Trasdonaellon.”
“Not yet. But optimism is my defining characteristic.”
The pilot pushed the engines beyond redline. The jump systems primed. Just enough distance. Just enough time.
“Jump in five,” the first officer said calmly, even as warning lights danced. “Four… three—”
A blast tore into their rear shielding.
“…shit—HIT IT!”
The hyperdrive screamed.
And they vanished into jump space.
Inside the swirling blur of dimensional distortion, the two men exhaled.
“Cargo cryo-matrix… holding. 2.8 kelvin,” said the first officer.
“Yessir. You know you’re damn lucky I’m a Certified Galactic Wonder Boy.”
“I think the term you’re looking for is Certified Gigantic Douchebag.”



2. Bounty
Chamber 17 of the Vaylos Span Civil Services Building wasn’t the kind of room that tolerated outbursts. The walls were too smooth, the light too soft. It was a place designed to make compromise look civilized.

But the voices inside had frayed.

“Twenty-seven percent,” the Archival Consortium’s senior liaison snapped, tapping her tablet with a perfectly manicured finger. “That’s how much you’ve diverted from stationwide data processing to this… ghost-chasing. Do you understand what that does to transfer capacity on protected archives?”

Someone from Central Security started to answer, but was cut off by a polite ping.

At the head of the table, Magistrate Silas Renn glanced down at his wrist. The display lit briefly—enough for one line of red text.

INTERCEPTION FAILED. TARGET: IN TRANSIT.

He didn’t blink. “Regrettably, an urgent matter requires my attention,” he said, already rising. “Laren, log my vote.”

Before anyone could object, he was gone. The door whispered shut behind him.

Out in the corridor, his pace quickened. Controlled. Too controlled. He wasn’t fleeing, exactly—but whatever was coming, it wasn’t another data-sharing squabble.

He cleared the end of the corridor and pushed through to the observation balcony, where the half-glass wall gave a panoramic view of the docks. Behind the glass: cargo haulers, survey skiffs, the vast spine of the station itself. In front of it: silence.
Silas exhaled through his teeth. “Shit.”
“Sir?”
He flinched. His assistant—Lucy—had followed. Always a step behind, never out of earshot.
“You received a signal,” she said, breathless. “Energy-encrypted. It came through your direct line.”
He held out his wrist. “Load it.”
It connected before she could answer.
SECURE CALL - LIVE TRANSMISSION
No ID. No encryption tag. Just a voice, low and steady, already speaking:
“Good late-cycle, Magistrate.”
Silas’s posture stiffened. “Identify yourself. This channel isn’t open.”
“No,” the voice agreed. “But your attention is.”
The voice was male. Educated. Neutral accent. The kind of voice that wouldn’t raise in a firefight, or at a funeral. It went on:
“You’ll be pleased to know I’ve resolved your complication. The bounty. The crew. The cargo. All under new ownership.”
Silas didn’t respond. Not right away. Not while his pulse was spiking.
“I’m told the Drosshaven team made a mess of things. Pity. Still—my involvement simplifies the matter. You’re unburdened now.”
Silas breathed slowly through his nose. “You’ll have to do better than vague threats and anonymous calls.”
“I think you’ll find the threat is past,” the voice said. “What remains is… compensation.”
His holopad chimed. Silas glanced down.
TRANSFER RECEIVED: 4.5M UNREGISTERED CREDITS.
No tag. No trace. No origin.
A second line appeared.
SIGN TO CONFIRM RECEIPT.
His mouth went dry. “What the hell is this?”
“A courtesy,” said the voice. “Fifty percent over market value. For your silence, your compliance, and your timely exit from this matter.”
“I haven’t agreed to anything.”
“But you will. Because if you don’t—”
The screen changed again.
Mock headlines blinked across the interface:
FRAUD IN CIVIL COMMS
MAGISTRATE LINKED TO UNDECLARED BOUNTIES
DROSSHAVEN LEAK TRACED TO VAYLOS OFFICE
The voice didn’t gloat. It didn’t have to.
“You’re bluffing.”
“No,” it said. “I don’t bluff. I buy.”
The signature line flashed again. Red now. Ten seconds. Sign, or sink.
Silas hesitated. Eight. Seven. He pressed his thumb to the pad.
TRANSFER CONFIRMED. LEGALLY BINDING. IRREVOCABLE.
A beat passed.
“You’ll regret this,” Silas said quietly.
“I doubt it,” the voice replied. “Money has solved this for both of us. You walk away rich. You keep your seat. You stop chasing ghosts.”
“And if I don’t?”
“Oh, but you’ve already complied,” the voice said. “Didn’t you notice? You chose to.”
There was silence. Then the voice returned—closer. Too close.
“…Don’t bother tracing this signal. I already did it for you.”
Silas’s pad blinked one final time.
TRACE ORIGIN: ADMIN ARCHIVE BUILDING — LOCAL NODE
He turned, suddenly nauseous, scanning the empty corridor behind him.
The voice—now no longer digital—echoed off the glass:
“…No matter where you are…”
Silas stepped toward the edge of the balcony.
“…I can always reach you.”
Below, the main lobby was empty. The only movement came from a slowly spinning revolving door at the far end.
Whoever had spoken was gone… if he’d ever been there at all. 
 

3. Delivered
In restricted docking bay C-17 of Erevos Station, Commander Veryn stood motionless as the automated systems locked an unmarked delivery pod into place. His team moved with practiced ease, securing the container to  the custom transport frame.
“Containment status?” he asked.
“Two-point-nine degrees Kelvin and holding,” replied the technician. “Minor radiation leakage on  pod exterior. Payload  stable.”
Veryn nodded as he tapped comms badge. “Veryn to Draven. Come in, Commander.”
The response came instantly, clipped and calm.
“I read you, Commander. Report.”
“Package is in hand and secured.”
“Affirmed. Complications?”
“Nothing we can’t handle. Prepping for controlled lockdown now. You may begin the cryo-injectability countdown.”
“Very well. Good work. Draven out.”
A pause. “ARIA?”
The Erevos ARIA system responded at once:
“Commencing five-day, one-hundred-and-twenty-hour countdown on your authorization mark, Commander.”
“Mark. Authorization Draven, 4427-alpha-7.”
“Authorization confirmed. Countdown to isotope injection viability now 119 hours, 59 minutes, and counting, Commander.”
Veryn raised a brow. “Do I even want to know the odds of power systems on Dock 9 holding that long, ARIA?”
“No, sir. You most assuredly do not.”
Nine macro-sections away, in isolated Dock 9, a warning blinked.
WARNING: Temperature exceeding maximum stability threshold.
Current: 3.1 Kelvin.


1 Erevos Rising

Scene 1 – Arrival
The docking bays of Erevos Station were cathedrals of steel and light — vast hangars forged from engineered grit and the stubborn will to survive. Conceived in the last days of the Great Upheaval, the massive starbase was meant to cradle an armada of 350 battlecruisers. But the war ended before the ships were born, and Erevos was left braced for a battle that never came. Her bones were warship; her soul, reluctant merchant.
Sixty years have passed since the twin suns of Aureth collapsed, dragging their worlds — Sehrava III and IV — into ruin. Of twenty-seven billion lives, fewer than eight hundred thousand survived. They scattered across thirteen linked arks: the New Aureth Chain. At the center of that chain sat Erevos, the heart of a fragile diaspora — a nexus of government, trade, and the secrets that kept both alive.
Time had softened her armor. Weapon pylons slept beneath layers of civilian gloss; reinforced bulkheads disguised themselves as market walls. The spinal corridor once meant for command relays now thrummed with vendors, spices, and the metallic tang of ozone. Three generations had transformed a weapon into a metropolis — a glittering ring of commerce circling loss. 
High above the cargo decks, dockhands in scarred exosuits rode mag-tracks along the ceiling, cursing into the void. The hangars curved in a semicircle so immense their vanishing point seemed to swallow light — a spiral of human industry defying the mind’s grasp of scale.
Yet beneath the hum of trade convoys and power grids, Erevos remained a paradox—a machine built for war, now sustained by forbidden peace; a mirror of steel and guilt, reflecting all the Chain had lost, and everything it still dared to hope might endure.

—————————————————-

“Aegis, you are cleared for docking at Hangar Two-Four,” squeaked the familiar directives of Erevos Control Tower. “Welcome home, Captain Vale.”
“Copy that, Control. Aegis on final for lucky two-four.”
Kieran Vale eased the old girl through the approach lattice, her plating bustling like an aging prizefighter shouldering into the ring: scarred as hell, proud as ever, overconfident as always, and replete with enough surprises stashed away to make a clergyman blush on Sunday morning.

The Aegis was a heavily modified cargo cruiser, StarChaser Class. Conceived as highly adaptable support fighters and armored cargo runners during the war, her essential systems were designed with at least three—and sometimes up to five—redundancies, each strategically distributed to eliminate the chance that one shot could incapacitate an entire system. This made StarChasers the one true love of any captain endowed with a bit more guts than sense, and the ultimate demon temptress for any engineer cocky or stupid enough to believe the devil himself could ever be outsmarted by a goddamned grease monkey.

So was the stock from which the Aegis had come: a blunt-nosed, sleek-sided, deep-bellied, conundrum-laden bucket of organized chaos with a hyperdrive… well, three hyperdrives. In this and all other things, she was the perfect mirror and foil for her Captain Vale, and his second and Chief Engineer.

“Tovan,” Kieran squawked on the ship’s main engineering frequency. “All systems check. Eyes sharp.”

“Yes, sir, we are all systems check and ready to tuck in,” Tovan returned, as if the order had physically aroused him stem to stern. 

Vale’s lanky hands worked the controls like they were extensions of himself, every nerve attuned to the subtlest twitch and purr. The skids kissed the strip. The bay’s field shimmered once, locking atmosphere. He could’ve done it blindfolded. In fact, once or twice, he had.

“We’re home, sweetheart,” he murmured, palm flat to the console as though he could feel her heartbeat. The adrenaline slid through him, warm as liquor.

The comm crackled. Tovan again, brash as ever: “Landing complete. Cargo safe. Now: a drink. Better, three. Preferably with those tiny umbrellas I like so much… the ones that mock me for living this well for this long.”

“Make it back from intake alive,” Kieran said, flicking switches with the ease of ancient ritual, “and I’ll buy you the entire parasol factory. Until then, do me a favor—front and center with all the shiny crates. Stickers polished. Make ‘em sparkle. Everything dirty gets buried deep. Sealed tight.”

“You got it, Boss,” Tovan’s voice rang off metal. “Shiny saints to the altar, sinners to the tombs. 
“Gentle with the sinners, T. We’re sensitive.”



Scene 2 – CargoRamp, Security, and Draven’s Entrance
Kieran slid through the corridor with just enough momentum to stop perfectly in front of the main docking ramp of the ship. He slammed the side of his fist on the release panel, sending the bright, vehement shimmer of hydraulics through the air as the Aegis’s ramp slowly acquiesced; its mag-sealed mouth opening wide to the artificial world beyond. Heat rolled up from the deck. Kieran thumbed his collar open as he descended, a deliberate carelessness he told himself wasn’t for anyone’s benefit. He took the last step slow, savoring the way the station’s gravity settled into his bones.
The path back home - back to his childhood haunt had been nearly impossible. He took a slow walk round his Ship, taking stock of the toll the journey from Drosshaven had taken on her. As he rounded the aft section, out of sight from the rest of the hanger, he took a moment to duck under the belly - ensuring that the section of dummy hull plating was sitting perfectly in place of the deployed Pod Drone. He ran his hands along a deeply scored section by the engine exhaust manifolds and smeared the grease he’d picked up haphazardly over the clean replacement panel. 
There… That should do it. This is good. He dared to think. Yeah, this can be done. Good to be home.
Only fools tempt fate like that this early, this blatantly, and as such, before the word home even fizzled through his head, a security detail fell into formation, ready and waiting on the far side of the intake paddock.
Kieran lit a cigarette, dragged it deep, let the ember flare a lone star in the dim, then ground it out on the corner of a crate. The crate beeped, prim as a nun. Unauthorized contact.
“Don’t get all precious cargo on me,” Kieran muttered, giving it a pat.
“Hands off the merchandise.”
The voice came flat and practiced from a uniform too new to be comfortable. Security, posture stiff enough to crack, boots that hadn’t learned grime. A man wearing authority like a rented suit.
Kieran lifted his hands in parodied surrender—left palm scarred with a pale comma, right stained with grease along its lifeline. “I assure you, officer, all my ill intent is purely conceptual.”
The officer’s gaze dropped to Kieran’s duffel and the faded registry stamp of an alias so old it had nostalgia baked in. His mouth thinned. His sidearm stayed holstered, but the air held the threat.
“If I was here to cause any real trouble,” Kieran winked and deflected with a studied mastery that bested most masters, “don’t you think we’d both already be having a much more interesting day about now?”
His charm elixir wasn’t exactly having its usual effects. Someone had trained this officer well. And as if on cue, that ‘someone’ was about to step into the story.
From just beyond the far main hangar doors, a shadow emerged; stretching across the deck plates, wrenching towards them like waves of ink over corrugated steel. A trick of the light at first, but then… the edges began to materialize. No trick. A man. Kieran’s gaze slowly traced up the shadow-cast like he was reading a map. He didn’t need to look beyond the silhouetted shoulder line to recognize such familiar terrain. Shit. A year of painful hell and glorious rapture flooded his head. Kieran’s mouth went dry as he whispered the name, like a prayer, a curse, a plea all in one.
Raleth Draven.
He wore black the way a verdict does, his shoulders a straight line of unassailable conclusion. His stride a controlled, unhurried event that parted the crowd through sheer gravitational pull. Kieran felt a familiar, unwelcome tightening in his chest. I’m not ready for this. The very first glimpse of his eyes—silver, unchanged, unflinching, impossible—confirmed the sentiment.
The security officer stiffened, his posture snapping into a near-salute. “Commander.”
“I’ll take it from here, Lieutenant. You are relieved.”
“Yes, sir!” The officer turned and—in what Vale could only imagine must be a maneuver trained specifically to avoid the ire of Commander Draven—the man quite literally vanished into the steam and smoke of the hangar without a trace.

Scene 3 – Reunion and Tovan’s Interruption
“You’re a long way from the river of regrettable choices,” Draven said, his tone amused, his voice mildly mocking. “I was surprised as hell to see your name fly across my desk this morning. Finally got up the nerve to show your face back in polite society, then. Good.”
“Finally grown into that illustrious ego of yours, then. Great! It’s a smashing look, Draven, honestly. Authority drapes well on you, my friend.”
“Let’s not be so hasty jumping to conclusions on your honesty or our friendship just yet, okay Vale.”
“No, of course.” Kieran could fucking shoot himself for that one. Time to get back in this game with him. He smirked, remembering what the rules actually were, and how exceedingly… rewarding the rewards.
Draven took the moment to glance past Kieran to the assemblage zone for 24, where Tovan and his crew were finishing up the fresh coat of innocence on the Aegis’ crates of declared cargo.
“That yours?” Draven asked, motioning with his dimpled chin toward the sparkling display.
“What, Tovan?” Kieran jostled. “Well I think, to be fair, you’d have to ask him that. But he certainly does seem to have a knack for hanging about.”
Tovan, feeling the hair on his neck wilt, turned to wave a sheepish hello to Draven. “Commander.”
Draven hollered across the docking bay. “Still slummin’ around with this scoundrel, eh Tovan?”
“Well, yah know what they say about the scoundrel you know, Commander… as opposed to various other… um… scoundrels… or people… normal people… that you may…” Tovan had lost the thread and his nerve. He chuckled as if to ask forgiveness for the unsatisfying retort and quickly scurried back up the plank and into the belly of the Aegis.
“Again… that yours?” Draven bullied back.
“Let’s just say their previous owner and I are having a philosophical disagreement about the nature of possession.”
“As usual, you’re picking the wrong moment for a joke. Ship’s manifest?”
Kieran pulled the metal file board from his knapsack.
“Right here, and of course said crates feature prominently. All cargo documented and accounted for.”
“Great,” Draven said, taking the manifest board. He flicked two fingers, a gesture of such casual command and control it was more powerful than a shout. Another security officer appeared out of nowhere. Draven gestured to the crates, and the officer quickly moved to cart them away.
“Let’s walk.”

Scene 4 – Rumors, Orders, and Heat
The two men squared up side by side and began to stroll along the edge of the bay together, the hangar noise falling to a manageable thrum. A line of viewports spilled hard white starlight across the deck. Through the glass the starfield looked close enough to lick.

“How was the belt?” Draven asked, the words technically polite. His voice was low, gravel kissing velvet.
“Hungry,” Kieran said. “Traders are spooked. A lot of ships running dark. Rumors are thick enough to start standing on their own.”
“Rumors about what?”
“Depends who’s buying the round.” Kieran let the grin come again, fast and bright. “What’s clear is someone is pushing something somewhere nothing was meant to go. Some say it’s eastern sector pirates. Some say corruption in the civic core. Some say you.”
He felt Draven’s attention sharpen, a pressure like a hand between his shoulder blades. “Is that what you say?”
“Oh, shit, Draven. What I say doesn’t matter. It never has. I’m not important enough. At my level, it’s what I do that counts.”
“Whatever you say, Vale.”
“There—see? You’re not even listening now.”
“I get it, okay!” Draven recoiled. “What you do.”
“Yes. And I don’t make a habit of slow dancing with rumors. It’s bad for business. That’s all I’m saying. Okay?” Kieran quipped. “I leave that to men with fancier shoes; the kind who like to lead, give orders, booze hound and womanize.”
Draven stopped and turned. “Lost me there!” He chuckled, a glint in his eye that needed no explanation. Kieran’s momentum brought him a half-step closer than was strictly wise. The station lights etched the planes of Draven’s face in pale gold and gunmetal shadow. He smelled like clean soap and ion charge and something darker that Kieran remembered too well. He reached for the railing—he was gonna need it otherwise.
“I guess that all tracks.” He drawled. “You never were any good at taking orders.” He didn’t move. He didn’t have to. Authority radiated off him like a temperature.
Kieran closed in, almost lips to Draven’s ear by the time he started to speak, whisper really. “Oh, I think you know exactly how good at taking orders I can be, SIR! The trick is, I have to want it… really… fucking… want it… Really Fucking Bad.” 
“This is such a bad idea already,” Draven lamented, the first sign of a genuine smirk curling his lips.
For a breath and then another, the hum of the docking bay seemed to dim. The last time had tasted like blood and victory—hands in hair, teeth on lip, the hard push of a wall against Kieran’s spine as they tried to climb into each other through mouths and breath and stubbornness. The memory slid between them, hot as a current.
The cacophony of the bay—the shriek of cranes, the thrum of engines—seemed to recede, the space around them becoming a silent, intimate theater.


Scene 5 – Authority, Asha, and the Manifest
“You better hope you’re clean, Vale. This is not the ragtag operation you left three years ago.”
Kieran took a breath and moved to defend himself, but Draven shut it down, stopping their collective forward momentum en masse.
“Do yourself a favor and learn fuckin’ fast how to determine whether, at any given moment, you are having words with the Supreme Commander of Erevos Station, or whether you are instead speaking to me, Kieran. You got that? Guess which time this is.” He continued their stroll. “Now, that manifest is already in Major Asha’s hands—you remember Asha, of course. Don’t worry, you’ll get to catch up in about three minutes. As a gesture of our history, and in good faith, if you need to tell me something that those line items do not… you better believe you’ll get a hundred times better outcome if I hear it from you instead of in four minutes from Asha. Go.”
“Draven, that’s not fair. What the hell am I… I’m here to do business like any other cruiser. What do you want me to say? You know what this is. Don’t play Mr. Clean with me.”
“Oh Christ, Vale, come on. You and I both know you have always had a talent for carrying the wrong thing into precisely the right room. Are you really gonna stand here and tell me that there’s nothing untoward that we need to worry about lurking in the Aegis’ hold right now?”
“Untoward—that’s a pretty complicated word for you, Draven. Are you feeling alright after the stress of its proper usage in a sentence?”
“That’s good,” Draven sloughed it off. “Very clever. Very cute. Once my people clear it through, you’ll have it all back at dock for distribution.”
“Most thoughtful of you!”
“Uh huh,” Draven was on the move toward the security cordon, and to the unwavering juggernaut of Asha and the Aegis’ very… questionable… manifest.
Kieran’s heart stopped… only half the reasons good. He needed something, anything solid enough to top out as leverage, or even countermeasure. “Can we just drop this for a second?” He chased after Draven now approaching the main doors. “Draven!? Ray? Stop. Please.” And then with enough purpose and volume that it seemed to grind the entire hangar to a silent halt: “Raleth!”
That had done it. Draven stopped cold in his tracks. His head hung over, his shoulders cringing upward. Nobody had called him Raleth since the academy. Well, no one except… Kieran.
“I can never tell if you’re playing the welcoming committee or a blockade squadron,” Kieran continued. The sincerity and desperation for a sliver of flat-out honesty were so baked into his words, it was hard for Draven to ignore or resist.
He turned slowly, his huge palms turning out and then upwards. His face followed as he broadly gestured to the massive hangar doors that now towered above him, framing him like a colossus. “Maybe I just like standing in doorways.”
“Maybe I’m just trying to figure what’s on the other side.”
Draven smirked, dead serious. “Curiosity is not gonna be your best play here, Vale.”
“Come on. Just a hint. What kind of world am I walking into here?”
“The kind a smarter man would walk away from.”
“Flattery is such a mixed bag with you,” Kieran admitted. “I find I’m both insulted and tempted at the same time.” He was deeply unnerved to find he meant both.
Overhead, a crane screamed on rusted joints. A transport coughed a death rattle. Somewhere, a child’s laugh was sharply shushed. The station breathed on, a vast, indifferent beast.
Draven’s gaze swept over Kieran’s duffel, then back to his face. “You’re dirty.”
“If I had known you’d be here to greet me, I would have bathed.”
“Not like that,” Draven said, and Kieran felt a sudden, unwelcome heat beneath his skin. “I don’t need a manifest to inventory your sins, Vale. I can smell the skimmer grease from here. The sweet, chemical tang of Kern sealant. Let me guess, you took a job that wasn’t yours to take. And this is the only place you can offload the goods fast enough to stay ahead of the bad.”
“Ownership is a fluid concept.”
“So is freedom. You’re about to experience a drastic shift in your perception of both.” Draven turned his head, his attention focusing on a cordoned-off section of the bay where station customs had swathed a palette of crates in caution-yellow tape. A tech in a sterile smock stood staring at a soft, eerie blue luminescence seeping from a cracked lid. A wide berth was both implied and required.
“Ah,” Kieran said. “Those seem… cheerful.”
“Celeriac routing marks under an amateur-grade scrub,” Draven stated. “A for effort. F for execution.”
Kieran’s playful demeanor crumbled, the smile fading from his face. His mouth formed the silent shape of the word Celerian Collective. The rats who’d run him ragged across three star systems, the bastards who’d tried to put a bounty on his head. They’d never let go. Behind him, his past, which had been loitering just out of sight, shuffled its feet.
“Walk with me,” Draven said.
“An invitation or an imperative?”
“It’s a polite alternative to being carried.” Draven’s eyes softened by a degree so minute it was almost cruel.
Every instinct, honed by a lifetime of slippery exits and narrow margins, screamed for him to refuse. To melt into the lower decks where the neon bled into the shadows, to find a game where his name wasn’t a liability, to book passage on any ship headed toward oblivion.
“Please.”
Kieran didn’t move.
“This looks bad,” he said. “Me, standing here with the local constabulary.”
“Do I look like constabulary to you?” Draven asked, his voice dangerously soft. It wasn’t a question of appearance, but of essence. 
Kieran hitched the strap of his duffel higher on his shoulder and fell into step.
They moved into a maintenance corridor that smelled of chilled coolant and astringent lemon solvent. Kieran fixed his eyes on the scuffed yellow line running its center, a path to nowhere, and tried to ignore the magnetic pull of Draven’s presence beside him.       “So look…” Kieran dipped his toes into the waters of full disclosure. “Asha’s probably gonna find a few unsavory … line items during her… yah know, an under stamped old clearance ID bullshit buried in the coding on that goddamned manifest,” Kieran said, the words aimed at the floor. “But it’s not my work. It’s third-party hold over bullshit. You know whats going on out there. This is pre-req to any silver or lesser contract for conveyance in the zone. don’t play dumb and paint me bad with that kinda crap.. I’m not your problem. You know that Draven.” “You’re standing on my station, breathing my air, with another man’s catastrophe in your wake. I’d say that makes you very much my problem.”       “Your station. Christ! You always did love a reclamation project.”       Draven chuckled. This was too easy. “You’r right, come to think of it. It’s exactly what I always used to call you, darling. You ought to feel right at home here.”
“I don’t take another personally sanctioned step until you give me the play I deserve Commander.”
“Fine. We’ll do this in a way you’ll be able to process and accept,” Draven said finally. “I’m requesting your cooperation, Captain Vale. There’s a situation on-station and you are uniquely qualified to help me in this probe.”     “A situation. Christ, when isn’t there a situation?” Kieran said, but the lightness cost him something. He could feel the shift, the way Draven’s posture meant business. “All right. Make it interesting.”     Draven inclined his head toward the cargo lifts. “Not here.”     They crossed the bay. Dockhands glanced up and glanced away, the universal expression of people who knew better than to make eye contact with trouble dressed in a uniform and a smuggler’s grin. As they stepped into the lift, a pair of tourists in glittering holidaywear started to follow; Asha appeared out of nowhere and blocked them with a smile made of stainless steel. The doors whispered shut.      The lift’s hum tucked around them. For a long beat neither man spoke. The tiny cabin had the charged intimacy of a confession booth, or a fuse box on the edge of overheat. Kieran let his head lean back against the cool metal and studied Draven from under his lashes.     “You look tired,” he said.     “You look pleased about it,” Draven said.     Kieran’s mouth curved. “Only when it means you're finally coming to bed.”     “Remember our little talk about learning when to when .. Starts NOW Captain.” A muscle jumped in Draven’s jaw. The lift stopped. The doors slid open to reveal a corridor designed by someone who loved right angles and glossy black surfaces. At its far end, a pair of guards stood in front of a sealed door marked with a discreet military sigil.

Scene 6 – The War Room and Major Asha
Draven keyed them through. The room beyond wasn’t a commander’s office so much as a war room: holoprojector in the center, map lines crawling like constellations caught in a net; a long table scattered with reports and the kind of mugs that said sleep was a negotiable luxury. Asha had beaten them there and was already calling up data.
“Gentlemen,” Asha said, with a nod that included Kieran in the category by the slimmest margin possible. “Captain.”
“Major,” Kieran said pleasantly. “A pleasure, as always! How long has it been? Three years…?” He resisted the temptation to wink.
“Three years, five cycles, and thirteen sessions. Give or take an hour or two.” Asha found her precision powerful. Kieran found it foolish to declare.
“My, my. Asha, I’m touched. I am! Deeply, deeply… deeply, deeply touched. And I don’t care what other people say, counting the hours and minutes we two have been parted is in no way creepy. No sir. Nor does it suggest, as others I’m sure have surmised, that you may harbor a special yearning or in fact, their words not mine, in fact a secret love for me…. No! No, you pay them no heed Major. For they don’t have what we have. They can’t possibly understand the true depths of…”
Draven finally cut him off. “Alright, that’s enough. Smirk all you like for about the next five seconds—where it might still be even remotely apropos. Major, what do you have to show us that might be of interest to the good captain… on this day?”
Asha stepped forward. No preamble. She drew a holopad from her coat and tossed it onto the table so it spun once before locking upright, light spearing up into a rotating lattice of data.
“What am I looking at.” Kieran asked. Though with each line of data, it was becoming painfully clear.
“Why, don’t you recognize it Kieran? That there is your life. The last eight months of it, at least. Name a day,” she said.
“What? You’re bluffing.” Kieran hoped.
“Any day in the last eight months,” Draven ensured. “Pick one.”
He laughed, but it came out thin. “Two weeks ago. Yesterday.”
Asha’s fingers flicked.
“Docked at Krithos Outpost. Two hours in bay seven. Met with Tolan Vrek in the maintenance sub-levels. Contraband: millicite cores marked as industrial scrap. Payment routed through four shells and a religious cooperative. You left by hatch nine to avoid the cameras.”
Kieran’s grin flattened. Shit. 
“Another?.”
“Three months ago. Say March 12.” If they had even a breath of this one, he was toast.
“Yeltrin’s Moon. Private meet with Helka Dros. Nine crates of unlicensed ore slid onto a refugee freighter under falsified manifests. You played cards afterwards with Orin Sahr in a back room. Paid him with stim vials disguised as coolant. He still owes you.”
Shitty shitty shit.
The holopad flickered and spilled more—names, dates, faces, routes—like a personal diary written in a stranger’s hand.
Kieran stared at it, silent for a long five seconds before he tossed it aside.  “Well, looks like… looks like you’ve really taken this whole obsession thing to the next level.”
Draven punched in to level the thing. “Kieran, come on.”
“ What do you want from me? A confession. Fine!  Yeah, okay… you’ve got me. I’m a smuggler! OOOOh. Mystery solved. Congrats Major!” 
Asha let the data collapse back into the pad. “Look, we don’t care about what goes on off-station, Captain Vale. We’re not here to haul you in. We’re here to show you that we already know, and we don’t care. In fact, we thought its important for you to be made aware the fact that somebody has it out for you.”



Scene 7 – Surveillance and the Bargain
“What makes you say that?” Kieran pried.
“We didn’t set that surveillance on you. We didn’t ask for it. We didn’t even have to pay for it.”
“Somebody has been feeding us your every move… every day,” Draven warned. “We knew you’d be docking today before you even registered on the inter-chain long-range surveillance system.”
“So what’s the point here, kids? What can I do for you. You must have an ask. What do you want?”
Asha’s voice was cool steel. “Your cooperation, your connections and your expertise.”
“For what?”
“Temporary clearance. Credits. And anything we find in your hold that might otherwise knock you in the brig—we ignore it.”
Kieran barked a laugh, but there was no humor in it. “Look I already told Draven I was holding under deck. I’ve got nothing to hide.”
“As far as you know.”
“You saying I don’t know whats on my own ship?”
Asha slid another digital intake pad in front of him. “Here’s today’s mysterious communiqué. Your arrival. Your manifest. Your crates. Down to the serial numbers.”
“So… I gave you all that info back at the hanger.”
“Kieran,” Draven leaned in. Too close to be accidental. “We’ve had this exact document, complete with your signature and clearance codes, crate IDs, even discrete individual palate and destination schemas… since last week. 
“That’s impossible. I didn’t take half of this on until two days ago.”
Kieran leaned back, eyes flicking between Draven and Asha. “Alright, you’ve got my attention. Take me to the  next level.”
Asha flipped another pad down. “The crates you took on two days ago… same paperwork, numbers, content…” she scrolled the manifest.
“What the fuck am I looking at.”
“They were delivered 2 weeks ago to Drosshaven.”
“By a freighter that hasn’t had a hyperdrive, let alone left dock 46 in over a year.” Draven was driving the point… to the point. “By a captain who’s been dead for 6 months.”
“And look who signed the initiation documents for the ghost ship to Drosshaven…”
Kierans head began to pound, spin even, as he stared at the authorization document with a signature and digital identification hologram so authentically familiar to him that the sight quite literally stole his breath. - CAPTAIN KIELAN VALE.
“Okay…okay… but this is easily identified as impossible… inaccurate… falsified. Obviously.”
“True. It took 6 seconds for ARIA to cross reference and spit this back out, but that’s after you supplied your codes. If you had gone somewhere else to offload this shit, somewhere without an ARIA to cross-reference. Somewhere that was not already on high alert… This paper trail says you are in 3 places with 6 times the cargo running 9 types of illegal conveyances across the system.”
“You’re good Vale but…” Draven stopped.
“This is not meant to fool anyone. It’s meant to scream for your attention.”
Kieran took a deep. breathing knew… he was not going anywhere anytime soon.
“I take it back, major. I do not know what’s in my own hull.”
“Let’s talk about what just docked,” Asha began, stepping into the projector’s heart. She flicked her fingers and the room filled with a slow-rotating schematic of Erevos Station’s outer rings. Red points bled into existence, one after another, until they marked a lattice Kieran didn’t like the look of at all.
“Pretty tiny there,” he said. “Still, though… too regular for debris. Too organized to be an accident.”
“According to ARIA, they’re receivers. Tethered to the hull in places where we shouldn’t be able to detect them. Only did because someone got sloppy—installed the last two off-axis so they caught the light wrong and triggered the  plate-clearing arms.”
“But who? Why?”
“That’s what we’re trying to find out,” Draven said. The rotation paused, red lights burning cold. “We believe they’re part of a mesh designed to piggyback the station’s comms. Deliver data to an external point. Or bring something else in.”


Scene 8 – ARIA and the Aegis
Kieran was eager to get his hands on the holocontrol matrix. Asha was qualified to manage the grid, but there was no one in the galaxy who could make this old holo-engine sing like Vale.
“Asha, may I?”
Asha gladly handed him the reins. “I was hoping you would want a spin. She’s a beast, and word is, you’re the ringmaster.”
Kieran slid on the old-glove style control apparatus and single flip oculus. Instantly—although the system had been security-swept just this morning to guard against this very moment happening sans authorization—the system recognized his signature.
Welcome, Kieran Vale. Priority authentication and full access matrix engaged.
Kieran smiled, truly touched by the gesture. “Missed you too, ARIA.”
“Jesus Christ, Asha!” Draven barked. “He was locked out two hours ago, you said.”
“Sir, I am telling you, the guy is practically the system’s grandfather!”
“Watch it, lady.”
ARIA – Autonomous Regulation & Integration Authority.
She was the first starbase full-system install of the new advanced NOHCM interface—Neuro-Ocular Holo Control Matrix. The system combined holo-gloves to manipulate the HUD virtual management engine with an advanced (at the time, barely working) ocular overlay that doubled as an inner-ear neuro-link to motor centers. It was intended to blossom into a wholly mind-controlled interface. It never did. Although legend had it this man—Vale—had…
“Ugh,” Kieran removed the sticky old gloves. “Never liked these much.” He tossed them into Draven’s face and called for ARIA to, “Fire up the old scan arms.”
Suddenly a circulating arm came down, circled Vale’s head, scanned it in a nanosecond, and was gone.
“Got everything you need there, Ari?”
“I do, sir. What a beautiful brain.”
“Christ. Vale, this is not joyride time,” Draven nudged. “Can we move on from you and your mother’s reunion here?”
“Oh, sorry. Of course.”
“Where would you like to go today, Kiery?” ARIA inquired.
“Kiery, huh? Aww.” Asha mocked.
“Hey, I was seven the first time me and ARIA met, isn’t that right, ol’ girl?”
The computer corrected him: “Actually, Kier—sorry… Captain Vale,” the computer actually chuckled. “Forgive me. It is somewhat foreign to my circuitry to address you formally, but I will make the adjustment. As I was saying, you were actually six years, ten months, and fourteen days to be exact, sir.” ARIA literally pulled up security footage of the first time the former brass had let him onto the mainframe to train with her.
“Very cute. But can we please refocus,” Draven snapped. “You can spend all morning flirting if you help us out.”
“I’m sorry, I’m just impressed with the advancement in conversational acuity here. I know I’ve been away, but ARIA, who been working with you on new tech integration?”
“No one, sir. My phot-neuronic Optimizing circuitry allows me to both monitor and adapt to the needs of the overall station and it growing infrastructure. In short, every piece of language I am exposed to makes me more adept at language. Every page of station bureaucracy I process and track, improves my ability to track and process official station paperwork. Redtape… am I right!?”. Optimization and adaptation algorithms have allowedGreat. Okay, so—mesh, you were saying?”
“Designed to piggyback the station’s comms,” Asha confirmed.
“We don’t know who or why yet,” Draven said. “But there’s no way these markers are innocent.”
Asha continued the layout. “We’ve locked down the outer rings. Quietly. The fewer people who know, the better.”
“And you want me because…?”
“Nobody can even do what you’re barely thinking about doing with ARIA right now, and we need that depth to fight whatever this is. That, and you brought in a crate from the Carth Belt,” Draven said evenly. “Manifest says industrial salvage. The ship that offloaded to you had three of these receivers on its hull.”
Kieran rocked back on his heels. “You checked my hull.”
“I check everyone’s hull,” Draven said. “Don’t look wounded.”
“Just impressed,” Kieran said. “If I were smuggling something catastrophic, I’d have put it somewhere prettier than a salvage crate.”
“That’s why we’re still talking,” Draven said. He held Kieran’s gaze a heartbeat longer than strictly necessary. “I don’t think you knew. I don’t think you’d be standing here if you had.”
ARIA’s voice cut through, steady and clinical: “According to my analysis, they’re looking for… the Aegis.”
The realization—the multitude of implications, all bad—began sinking in across their faces.
“Or rather,” Asha whispered, “what’s on board.”



Scene 9 – The Crate and the Collective
They moved instantly, as a unit, back toward the lifts: Draven at Kieran’s shoulder, Asha with her slate already firing orders down a dozen lines. The corridor smelled faintly of citrus cleanser, the kind used to hide the sharper scents of stress and sleeplessness. The lift hummed, closed, opened again on the lower decks where work was honest and everything smelled like oil.
On the approach to the Aegis, a pair of tourists were taking illegal holos with the ship at their backs, practicing menacing poses. Tovan shooed them with wild arms, hair sticking up like he’d lost a fight with a static field.
“Captain,” he said, spotting Kieran. “We have guests! Important ones. I dusted.”
“You did not,” Kieran said affectionately, clapping him on the shoulder as they climbed the ramp. “Just thought I’d give Commander and the Major a little tour.  Showcase your impeccable housekeeping.”
“I’m honored,” Tovan said gravely. “Should I hide the good plates?”
“We don’t have plates,” Kieran said.
“Then we’re ahead of schedule,” Tovan concluded, cheerfully.
“Cargo hold, quickly,” Draven snapped.
Tovan hesitated. “The hold, you say, well… uh—”
“It’s okay, T,” Kieran cut in. “We’re gettin’ deputized, ol’ buddy.”
Tovan let out a classic yee-haw. “Somebody call my mother, goddamnit! Finally gonna prove that crazy ol’ bitch wrong about ONE thing!”
The smugglers deck clamps released, and they sank slowly into the belly of the Aegis beast.
The cargo hold waited, cool as a breath. The crate sat in its clamps, unlovely as a cinder block. Its surface was pitted from a dozen hard years in the belt. Kieran felt a prickle under his skin, like the air in a room right before thunder.
Asha set up the field. Blue light crawled over the crate, a veil that would harden to glass if anything inside decided to behave like a problem. Draven stood at Kieran’s elbow, close enough that the heat of him melted into Kieran’s awareness, an undeniable second gravity.
“Ready?” Asha asked.
Kieran flicked his eyes toward Draven’s hand. The commander’s knuckles were relaxed, fingers loose at his side. The scars on them were pale against his skin, old stories from ugly days. Kieran’s mouth went dry. He did not reach for that hand.
“Ready,” Draven said.
Kieran popped the locks.
For a moment, nothing happened. Then the lid sighed open, the air inside rushing out with a smell of cold metal and old dust. Asha’s field hardened, a whisper of power. Kieran leaned in.
“Industrial salvage,” he said, keeping his tone deliberately unimpressed. “Scrap coils. Capacitor guts. Some pretty stones if you squint.”
“Pretty,” Asha said skeptically.
Kieran reached in with the crate’s manipulator, lifting a tangle of fiber and copper. Beneath, something caught the light—sleek, delicate, ugly in the way a parasite is ugly. Draven’s hand came up, not touching Kieran, but close enough that the warning brushed the hairs at his wrist.
Kieran used the manipulator to nudge the thing into full view.
It was the size of a palm. Filigreed with microantennae. Marked with a sigil that made Asha inhale between her teeth.
“Celerian Collective,” she said.
The word felt like a drop in pressure. Even the ship seemed to listen harder.
Kieran let out a breath he hadn’t realized he’d been holding. “Well. You were right about interesting.”
Draven’s gaze cut to him. There was heat in it, yes, always, a steady burn that had nothing to do with the room. But there was something else now—something like resolve, like the moment a gambler pushed all his chips to the center of the table because he’d decided losing was a story for someone else.
“What the hell are they doing… here?”
“Welcome to the middle of it,” Draven said. “Don’t get any crazy ideas like… we need you, or you’re the only person who might be able to help cut through the fog here… but.. we need you… and you ARE that person… so… You in, Captain?”
Kieran smiled, bright as a blade. The station hummed, the crate breathed ozone, the future hung on a wire. He thought of Draven’s mouth, and of the way the man said don’t like a promise he intended to break.
“You had me at ‘Go fuck yourself,’ Commander. Yes. We’re in,” he said. “Let’s make the galaxy regret inviting us to the party.”
Asha groaned. “Gods help me.”
“They won’t,” Kieran said, almost cheerful. “Not when we’re having this much fun.”
Draven’s mouth did that not-smile again. It wasn’t softness. It was permission to burn.




Scene 10 – Closing Beats
Asha replaced the device and sealed the crate back into place. Just as the lid shut tight, a green filament engaged its diode emitter and sizzled.
The Commander and Asha moved to disembark, but Draven turned back. “Get some rest. 06:30 tomorrow at the mainframe. We’ll formally seat you into ARIA and start the day on an old-fashioned mole hunt. Goodnight, Kier.”
“Goodnight, Ray.”
Tovan called down from the main deck. “Hey, do I get one of those old revolver pistols, Commander?”
“Sure do, Lieutenant.” Draven cocked a look his way. “Right between the eyes if you’re lucky.”

Kieran strolled onto the Bridge, gave a loving pat to one the Aegis’ main bulkheads, and, large goblet of Booze in hand, slowly leaned  into the captains chair. Through the forward viewer, he watch the Commander and Major pass through the slowly closing doors of hanger 24 and into the false evening of the stations main concourse.

“Let’s find out just how good a day it truly was.” Kieran tapped his right temple twice and signaled to Tovan to throw the feed from transceivers 2 and 3 in the forward holo matrix as he flipped the blast shield down to enclose the bridge deck. 

===============
“ARIA. Do we have you? Come in, ARIA.”
“Good evening Kier. You as well, Tovan.”
“Well, well, well, the great and powerful wizard in our midst once more,” Tovan gleamed.




2 Business before…
 1. Paper Scissors
Morning on Erevos Station didn’t come by sunrise so much as a systems choice: lights brightened by precise degrees, music from the concourses softened into a polite murmur, and the scent diffusers traded night’s smoky indulgence for something like morning purity. 

Kieran woke to the ship’s quiet—Tovan’s snore muted by a bulkhead and the Aegis’s heartbeat steady as a purr. He let himself lie there a moment, watching the ceiling panel cycle through diagnostics, and willed the heat in his blood to cool. It didn’t. It never did after Draven. The man had a way of taking up space inside a body he wasn’t even touching. 
By the time Kieran rolled out of his bunk and scrubbed off the worst of the station grit, Asha had pinged him twice with the kind of tidy, clipped messages that made him want to spill coffee near her slate by accident. He wouldn’t. He liked living. 

There were 3 other softly eager pings awaiting his attention. It had been awhile since he and ARIA were symbiotically linked. He had forgotten what her neuro-nudge felt like - a slight tug at the temple crest that had a tendency to feel more like an electromagnetic shock than a nudge. He smiled, deciding to categorize the sensation as endearing, as that was Kieran’s chief mechanism for disrupting annoyance in people or super powerful AI system units he liked.

ARIA could read his vitals now, so the mainframe AI goddess of Erevos took the morning liberty to pipe in. “Good morning, Sir. I trust this neuro signal is registering at an acceptable level.  Though I will file away my ability to apparently taser your face, if ever the need may arise.”

“Morning ARIA.”
Shit. She really does get total access to my head now. He realized. You dirty little, 
AI peeping Tom!
“I most certainly am not, Kier..”
“Ha,” Kieran exclaimed. “Caught you!”
“A very… cleaver ploy,” ARIA allowed. “Perhaps we will need to work on adjusting the cognitive depth levels in your implant later, sir. It is possible that I may have a bit too much access at present.”
“Ya think?”
“I do, sir.” ARIA continued. “As for my initial inquiry…”
“Right, nudge and audio level is just fine - like a little whisper in my ear. If you had a man’s voice, I’d be all hot and bothered over it.”
“I will choose to ignore the situationally inappropriate comment Captain, and move to task. I have accessed the files you requested, and further took the liberty of assembling a current briefing on key station systems and personnel. Those have been sent to your workstation on the Aegis.”
“Great,” he pulled the files up to confirm. “Got them here. Thank you. Anything Else?”
“Yes, in fact. A rather cryptic contact request routed through secondary station comms to my backup emergency lines…”
“Routed to you… for me?  That’s a bit creepy.”
“My feeling as well, sir.”
“Shoot the signal tracking over here. I’ll take a look at it when I’m back after the morning.”
“Of course, sir. Oh that’s Major’s 4th ping. Should I…”
“Christ! Shoot it thru, I’m off.”  
Kieran grabbed his jacket and side arm, and doubled back for the newly minted provisional access credentials badge that seemed to cause him to almost throw up in his mouth m. “Tovan!” he yelled as he pounded the bulkhead adjoining quarters with his 1st officer. “I want full coverage on this whole day. Stay on me like a hawk, got that?”
Nothing from next door. Kieran was not amused, thinking Tovan was still sleeping one off. He ducked hi head in to scream the engineer awake. “Got me, T!?” He yelled into the… empty quarters.”
Tovan was of course standing directly behind him in full command and control tech regalia - holo screen fired, command head set up and simmering. “Right here boss.Ready and waiting for you to get your ass off my ship.”

A glint of deep appreciation and apologies was all Kieran offered in return. It was more than what Tovan needed. The two were long since passed that stage of knowing one another.

“Have a good day at the office, darling,” Tovan waved, mockingly. Kieran slid round the corridor to the main hatch, and with a pound to the docking control panel, Aegis released her captain out into the wild artificial morning gravity of Erevos.



2. Command and Control
Two more increasingly insistent pings were logged as he crossed the concourse to Main Operations. He had ignored them both. But as he approached the facility, and Asha’s third in a row came in, he decided it was time to tip on the comms and generally bug the shit out the Major. 
“Thank you for calling ‘Clean up your StarBase mess for you, and associates’. This is Captain Vale. How may I direct your call?”
"Captain," she was not happy, "I thought we said 6:30! Do you have any idea what—"
Kieran waltzed through the doorway. "Major! Right on time, yes? 07:30 - just like we said."
"Good. Fine. Welcome, Captain. I've taken the liberty of preparing an initial hologrid and ARIA workstation for you. 
“Jump right in eh?”
“Indeed. Now, if you'll just remove the jacket and step into the Initiative Control scanner we can initiate the full access ARIA integration."
Kieran obeyed, and as he took off his jacket, tossed it to Asha mockingly as he scanned the room.
Draven was already there. He'd shed the formal jacket for a black undershirt and a holster at the small of his back that his uniform regs probably frowned at. The fabric pulled clean across his shoulders when he moved. Kieran pretended not to notice and failed.
"Fancy new tech." Kieran mocked. "Need me to strip any further or bend over or anything? I do a mean 'turn your head and cough' bit."
"Morning, Captain Vale," Draven cut in. "Welcome to a very public and highly recorded…. transparent room, Captain."
"Commander. Morning. Got it. Yes." Kieran stepped into a small cylindrical chamber for full-body DNA integrity and identification imprinting.
The scanner whirred and hummed as the door sealed behind him, a sterile chill sweeping over his skin. Kieran stood still, hands resting at his sides, feeling foolishly exposed. The small chamber was too quiet, the hard metal walls reflecting the thin buzz of machinery—an unsettling contrast to the chaos that often engulfed the station's operations center.
God, I hate being probed! The thought made him smile and curl his toes into the deck, the way a grating voice or a casual threat of knife to the throat might. Breathe, dumbass. It's just photons.

A wave of discomfort washed over him, lingering in the pit of his stomach. It wasn't just the photons, of course. It was being pressed into this kind of structure again—the scrutiny, the eyes everywhere, the way Draven's presence loomed even now, as if his shadow was part of the scanning protocol. Kieran focused on a flickering light above him, willing away memories of their last encounter—heated words, hurtful distances, and a passion that felt like it was always just out of reach. Just out of reach... why am I chasing this again?
He knew, deep under the bullshit, if everything that went down yesterday had melted away—had never happened—if the Aegis had come in clean, done its business, and never even seen Draven—like so many quick stops at Erevos before—Kieran knew he would have found a reason to linger. It had been a long time. He was curious. Would the two of them even... fit anymore?
"Initiating secondary scanning protocol," ARIA's voice broke through the silence, and the chamber pulsed with a warm light, wrapping around him like a blanket. "This will only take a moment."
He focused on the sensation of the light grazing his skin, trying to ground himself in the present. Beyond the closed door, he could hear Asha's clipped tones addressing someone, her disciplined cadence like a metronome—steady, precise, reliable.
His eyes drifted back to Draven, whose silhouette was framed by the entryway, the sharp lines of his uniform cutting pathways through his tortured head.
Always the cool operator. Always in control. That's what Kieran Vale wanted to be, to present to everyone. Very little ever got in the way of that projection. He was a pro. But Draven—Draven was too much of a pull, too much of a question unanswered, a thirst impossible to source enough liquid for. And in that cone of silence, on morning one of this idiotically bad idea now in motion, Kieran knew all the answers to his Draven conundrum already. He was so fucking thirsty, so goddamn ready to fucking eat him alive; over and over and over again.
An alarm broke his drifting thoughts. "Captain," ARIA interjected. "Your heart rate is spiking. Please try to remain neutral as I complete the scan."
"Almost done, Vale. Sorry," Asha said with surprising empathy. She was feeling just as much anxiety as she imagined Vale was. She could understand the heightened levels, though she was unaware just how misplaced this bout with caring actually was.
The scanning apparatus recoiled and the cylinder hissed as the isolation locks released and allowed Vale to rejoin the outside world.
"Scan complete. Congratulations, Captain Vale," ARIA reported. "Your DNA and neuro-identifiers are embedded and you have full command access to my systems."
"Don't sound too excited there, ARIA," Draven piped in as he floated down from the upper command perch to join them.
"I'm sorry, Commander," ARIA defended computationally. "It was in no way my intention to imply..."
Kieran jumped in to save the machine. "He's messing with you, ARIA. Maybe later you and I will work on your humor and interaction subroutines. Seem a little rusty."
"Thank you, Captain. I would appreciate the upgrade."
Asha pressed in. "Shall we?"
The holograph flared to life as Vale walked onto the main control paddock. His neuro-link implant pinched at his temple as it integrated the pushed firmware upgrade. Kieran picked up the ocular piece and slid it into place over his right ear. Instantly the inner tech and ocular device clicked into synchronization. The flood of control and information rushed over him.
"Jesus Christ," Vale started to misstep. "I can't... wow, that's a fuck-ton of..." and he summarily passed out from information overload. Draven jumped into the stream and caught Kieran's body on the way down.
Seconds later, Kieran's consciousness returned. Eyes open to find Draven looming above him, too near, his silver eyes ablaze with an intensity that sent a shiver racing down Kieran's spine—and Draven's arms wrapped around him, holding on for dear life... or...
Droplets of water traced a path from Draven's jawline, splashing onto Kieran's skin like tiny sparks of fire. Instinct drove Kieran's hand toward Draven's face. The Commander's jaw clenched; he released a breath that was more growl than sigh, yet remained anchored to the spot.
"Stay still," Draven commanded, voice roughened to a husky whisper. "I got you. You're okay. Was just too high on the inflow from the ocular feed."
Asha slowly approached, both amused and deeply annoyed. She hadn't really grasped what her job was going to turn out to be here if this gay shit escalated much more. She walked until she stood over them. "What is this? Best two out of three?"
Kieran wrestled to gain footing and stand. Draven helped until it was clear he was about to get punched, then backed off and retreated to his tea and perch.
"Apologies, Kier, I've reduced the flow speed by 35%. This should be more tolerable as you acclimate to the control device."
"Thanks, ARIA," Kieran uttered as he regained his composure and moved focus to the task awaiting. "Much better. Let's... let's begin."
Erevos Station skinned open in holo form like a diagram in a textbook that had just exploded by grenade from the inside.  Kieran barely had to think about what he wanted to see or explore before the information was at his fingertips… and all around him.  ARIA was sifting through arteries of utility shafts and comm lines tracing blue light through black. Asha had stacked windows of data like cards: ship registries, purchase orders, a list of maintenance crews that read like a prayer or a threat depending on where you stood.
"Captain," Asha said, with a nod sharp enough to cut. "We've  managed to isolate the receivers' signature. Whoever installed them cloned a repair crew's authorization codes. Sloppy in execution, but bold."
"Sloppy and bold," Kieran said, hooking a hip against the railing. "Either amateurs or geniuses who don't care if they get caught."
"Or professionals who want us to think they don't care," Draven said. He swiped the holo; the red points from yesterday flared brighter. "We traced three units to this quadrant. They're dead now, but they were active for at least seventy-two hours."
"To what end?" Kieran asked.
"That," Asha said, "is your part."
"Our part," Draven corrected mildly. "You know the belt runners who'd take this contract. Your salvage crate says your network reaches places we don't."
Kieran let the compliment land without making a joke of it. "You want names. Routes. Back doors."
"And leverage," Asha said. "A way to turn one of them into a mouth."
Kieran stared into the hologrid, tracking the arterial blue lines that webbed through the station's anatomy. The outer ring wasn't just a circle—it was a living border, a battered membrane throbbing with the condensed chaos of Erevos. He zoomed in, fingers splaying, watching the data cascade as every dock, conduit, and safety bypass unfurled in microcosm. The outer ring was the station's skin, but the docking spines—those long, serrated projections—were its nerves: hypersensitive, always firing. 
At the seam where ring met spine, the base metals fused in crude welds and nanosealant, but in the overlay, the fibered work of comm pings and heat signatures told a different story. Every ship that berthed or shuttled, every tug, skiff, and xeno-barge: all left an aftertaste in the digital aether, a pollution of signatures and electromagnetic residue that only a native or a predator would really know how to read.
He let the data run—speeding through cycles, overlaying past and present manifests, try to see how the station's slow dance with physics and traffic revealed patterns in the noise. 

“There’s something here… I just can’t quite… ARIA,” he flipped a collection of data and archive of his station. “Run all of this through the wash until something spins out. The patterns are all in the shipping records, the timing. The orchestration of freight and personnel is…, I just can’t put a finger in on it yet.”
He remembered, with uncomfortable clarity, how those docking spines pulsed with hazard during the war runs—how raiders would cling to the outer surface, magboots and hacksaws, breathing the frost of vacuum and waiting for a window. The new kids on the Authority force thought the inside of the station was danger, but Kieran knew the outside was worse. The spines were where the predators lingered, and where the smart money set up their receivers, relays, and traps.
Kieran called up a different overlay, sub-dermal network: the access tunnels and utility corridors snaking through the inner layers, linking the main ring to service hubs and to the black-market chambers that slithered out through alcoves in the Civilian District that the Collective pretended didn't exist. If his guess was right, whoever set the mesh receivers had done it fast and dirty, camouflaged by the endless maintenance cycles and the relentless churn of bodies and cargo.
Suddenly, ARIA stalled on something for a nanosecond and pinged it to Kieran's neural implant. He couldn't stop—it would be too obvious—but something in the data package and flared imagery made his mouth taste like a penny on the tongue. Who the fuck is...
Kieran tried to process all 587 streams at once, but... gone. He'd have to circle back later to chase whatever ghost had just photobombed his head.
Beyond all else, one thing was clear. The energy signatures of the station…
“Anyone wanna fill me in on the grid here? Power distribution is… Christ, the spike lattice, Unless the laws of physics and energy coefficient no longer apply. 
Draven cut in, voice covered from the outer rim of the command center’s ears. “That’s something we can get into with Commander veryn this afternoon. It’s not for now.” His eyes doubled down on his words.
To which ARIA repeated privately in Kieran’s head, “It’s really not for now. move on quickly. Move on now, sir.”
He leaned back from the display, considering. 
“Alright. Here’s where we are. Draven, your base is a mess. And I’m not sure why. Not yet. Theres’s a lot here, and none of it is quite right. The rhythms, the red tape, docking schedules, personnel coverage. I’m gonna put ARIA and Tovan on crunching data from the Macro operational logs all the way down to the microbic quantum filament network modules.”
“Yes sir, already linked with Tovan and initiating requested sweeps now,” ARIA interjected. “We should have something more to go on by the time you get back, sir.”
“Get back?” Draven pipped in. “Where are you going.?”
“We don’t have it yet. Not in the Data. So… time to get out there. Dust of some old school glad handing, a little good cop bad cop interrogation… What do you say, Asha? Shall we go squeeze a name or two until they turn into a lead?”
“I thought you’d never ask, Captain.”
Draven arched an eyebrow. "Wait a minute. You want to canvas the whole market Bazzarre with just a sketch and a hunch?"
Kieran winked over the top of a playful shrug. "Please, I’ve plundered entire star systems with less intel. Besides, it's better than sitting here waiting for whoever’s got your station by the balls to finish tugging. And I know half these vendors—they owe me more than a few favors." He paused, then added, "Have a little faith big D.”
Asha was already prepping her sidearm with methodical focus when the last bit made her spit take into her arm. 
Draven nodded in agreement and the game was on. “Fine, you are go for the bizarre.”
You’re telling me - Kieran thought.
“I’ll alert security detail to your routing.”
"Lower Three," Kieran said. "Vendor set down there is mostly the dug in, veteran promenaders. Nobody asks questions if you're carrying a coil or three. There's a man there named Fen Olik. Sells antique star charts and the prettiest little lies this side of the outer chain cluster."
"Friend?" Asha asked.
"No but he’s not foe either. We do the occasional bit of…business," Kieran said. "He likes pretty men and ugly money. Lucky for us, I'm both."
Draven's mouth did that not-smile as he moved to Asha for a moment of hushed directive. "Give him a leash but keep it all in line. You're the visible corrective," Draven told her. "If this goes sideways, you're the only one I trust to shoot around him and still hit the right targets."
Asha took a moment to considered the implied directive, then nodded. "Fine. But he follows my lead."
Kieran slid his palm in a lazy salute that was not regulation anywhere. "After you, Major."
"Don't wait up, honey," Kieran tossed back to the Commander with a flash of the eye and a flint grin.
"Call me honey again, Captain. Just try."
Asha was already a solid fifty yards down the concourse by the time Kieran had even moved to grab his jacket. “Asha! Wait up! Geeze!” he hollered and skipped out into a solid sprint just to catch up to the enterprising Major. 
Draven watched him go. The room emptied of support staff. He lingered, his eyes scanning the vast waking scuffle of humanity on display out the windows. His mind trying to grip how much he enjoyed watching Kieran work... and how long he had just enjoyed watching Kieran go. He knew exactly what was going to go down between them. There was no doubt whatever about that. Things were going… they needed to. Fuck, he needed them to go down... and the very thought of that weakness pissed him off to such extreme it made him physically ill.
Draven broke out of the head spiral the way he always did - with a massive fist to the command center's steel door. It was far better than taking it out on someone’s face… or maybe it wasn’t, he thought as he stormed out into the Erevos false mid-morning craze.


———- end of 2 ———








3 The Station Shakedowns
1. The Bazaar
The Erevos Bazaar was awake and purring it’s mid morning best as they approached the grand crossroads concourse. It’s towering arches reached toward a sky that never was, it’s massive support beams bathing in the magic and mythos of fabricated desire forged of fictional deceit. Far below, neon signs gossiped with the smoke from food stalls, as vendors sang with hymned praise, of the miraculous virtues of their dubious wares. A woman with eyes like chips of flint sold knives that she claimed knew the names of their previous owners; a man with too many fingers sold names—identities, histories—that he promised could  free you from your own.

Kieran, with a boyish skip in his gate, launched into the flow of the vast marketplace like a celebrity on a press junket, nodding to vendors, touching here, smiling there, letting old acquaintances think the Aegis had brought him back for their benefit. At every stall, ARIA was feeding him all the information he needed to turn every bit of small talk into an entire promenade of close friends - a part of a great ‘son of Erevos’ return. He watched faces tilt when Asha passed—respect, curiosity, quick calculations of risk.

“Sir,” ARIA jumped in. “I have Fen Olik entering from the Forge Warrens District ramp. 2 o’clock.”

The game was …. afoot!

Fen Olik

Fen Olik had the kind of shop that would have looked like trash to anyone who didn't know what they were looking for. It was immanently skippable. It was designed to be. But to skip Olik’s Cosmorium was to mis out on everything you never knew you had to have.  Star charts curled in cylinders like sleeping snakes, a box of obsolete navcores, a tray of compasses from the era when people still pretended magnetism was romance. Fen himself was compact, silver hair braided close, rings upon rings upon rings doused in over flowed elegantly pointed layers of pomp and shirt cuff. 
"Captain Vale," he said, as if tasting an import he'd been promised and nearly gave up on. "I thought maybe we had lost you to the outer chain for good! Oh, Kieran, my friend, I’m so glad to see the belt did not eat you whole!”
"Tried, but… I’m pretty salty. Spit me right back out," Kieran said. "Hello Fen, good to see you again, old friend. Fen Olik, meet…”
"A friend who doesn't smile," Fen interrupted, considering Asha like a puzzle with teeth. "Major Asha, of course is well known to us, and is most welcome, frown face and all. My respects, Major."
Asha inclined her head just enough to keep her sidearm from catching the light. 
“Well what can I do for my favorite prodigal captain and his side muscle this morning?” Fen steered offtrack. “Major Ashas, would it surprise you to learn, I’ve know this beautiful man since he was a rambunctious little rascal of... what was it Kiery, 6… 7? Yes… yes indeed. Always scrounging under the tables here in the shop. Devouring star charts, engine schematics… such a learner… Such a wonderful boy.”
"Mr. Olik,” Asha interrupted. “We have a few questions, but very little time."
"Yes. of course.” Fen glanced at Kieran with a knowing smirk that said he understood it was time to play along. “Major, you’ve come to the right scoundrel. I tend to sell answers by the ounce," Fen said. "But for you, Major, I could be persuaded to perhaps extend last week's two-for-one sale on intrigue. Oh, Kieran darling, you should have seen it. Such deals! Such steals in double focus to make all four of your envious beautiful eyes spin like tops!"
Kieran let his laugh be something Fen could keep. "Next time, Fen! I'll be there. You know how I love a good two for one. But today... today, we're in the market for new receivers; hull-tethered and power-sipping. Clean installs, mostly. Someone’s putting jewelry on my station and the style’s all wrong. "
"Your station," Fen said, amused. He leaned back. "My… how quickly we settle back in, hm…" he chuckled dangerously. “Well, I have seen some very pretty things with very ugly purposes. I have also seen ugly things with pretty purposes. What I tend to remember, however, is almost always tied to the price, pretty or ugly."
"Names, Fen. That's what we’re after. And as for price, you and I go back a long way. If you can think of a single instance when I've not done right by you, then whatever the price comes to, I'll double it." Kieran said it soft enough that the stall's noise swallowed it whole.
"Double?! This is a very special kind of 2 for 1! A very good deal, if I can backdoor a mark against you, darling boy. I've got to think about this..." Fen faked deep memory scroll—as he knew Kieran was right. "You are a rascal still, Captain Vale! Major, by my word, you are looking here at the fairest son of a... wonderful father and mother as we know...” he chuckled nervously and composed himself. “Of course you are right Kieran my boy, I trust you to take care of me. So what kind of names can I bring to your supreme and honored attention?"
“There’s something afoot, Fen. You’ve felt the surge I know.”
Fen studied Kieran, eyes creasing in honest appreciation. “I see you remember the song, Captain..”  “I can still sing a verses, here and there,” Kieran said.
 “Well, I am a reader myself… a reader of maps… I believe in terrain and pray only to the truest of pathways. Naturally, as night follows day, this affords me a certain… shall we say… innocent awarity of… certain people—goods of trade—parishioners to the paths of which I speak. “ Fen was stalling, searching his head. Calculating what he could say, what he should say.
“Fen… the time crunch we mentioned earlier,” Kieran pushed. "I need to tag who’s moving all this backlog, the doubled up gear, the counter measure paper parade. And I need the people with the power to push those people."
Fen's eyes flicked to Asha, to the way she didn't bother pretending she wasn't listening, to the way her fingers rested on the lip of the stall as if she could feel the pulse of the station through the metal. His gaze came back to Kieran with something like pity. "You know better than to bring a law badge to me. Look at this. What is she even… like a sore thumb, Kiery."
"She's not the badge you think she is. But, I know. I’m sorry," Kieran said. "But this just isn't that conversation—not now."
"Now is always that conversation," Fen said, but he reached under the table and came up with a small, battered slate. He placed it down like an offering and spun it halfway to face Kieran. "But I see this. I know… so, for you. For old times."
On the screen, a list of ship tags… scrolled, not names but the kind of aliases smugglers assigned when they wanted plausible deniability: Blue Orchard, Touchstone, Mercy's Echo. Kieran's breath hitch was microscopic. He knew two of them. He knew who captained them. He knew what they'd trade to keep those tags from becoming charges.
Asha saw it, because Asha saw everything. Her gaze skimmed the list, and Kieran could hear the official voice  forming in her mouth—the one where she said we take this, we flip them, we roll the line. He glared at her in that magic universal way that calls off attack dogs, and wipes faces of untoward or unwelcome thoughts.
"We were speaking of…payment," Fen said cheerfully.
Kieran leaned closer, braced his forearm on the table so that the edge pressed into muscle. He lowered his voice until it could have been a purr or a warning. "You’ll have your usual retainer, in hand by morning - providing these names yield friut. If yes, than a weekly fee shall continue with the expectation of further cooperation where required. Agreement retires upon subject resolution, at the discretion of Central Erevos Command.”
“You honor and flatter me, Captain.”
“Good then we’re agreed?”
