“Retainer minimum period of 6 weeks, with a balloon of one triple week’s payout should information I supply be deemed instrumental in said resolution. Are we agreed?”
“We are agreed.” Kieran said leaning in as he added, “And I'll throw in a navcore from the Acheron graveyard if you loop me in on anything that starts turning sour down here.”
Fen's rings clicked as his fingers tapped the slate. "You do love me, don't you, Captain."
“So… tell me a story, Uncle Olik. Tell me who’s at play today.”
“Of course, as you know,” Fen began to put on his public voice for the cameras. “I am always ready to help in the name of greater good, the order of things. Whenever called upon… I answer. This, I have always done. Yes? You know this. But in this case, I’m quite sure you also know… I cannot possibly give you what you seek.” Fen said to the floor. “Even if I had access to such knowledge—which of course I do not—the names you seek… these are not people a smart man tells stories about.”
“Then hum us a tune,” Asha said. “Fen… this is serious. Or we would not ask. We need more than these cargo corps. You are a keeper of maps. You study the currents. If not names, give us landmarks. You have my word and that of Commander Draven’s —we protect our friends.”
“I fear your protection may prove inadequate, Major.”
Then, as if to mirror the mood, station power manifolds began to whine and purr down, fluxing light levels and resetting mid operational systems all over the promenade. 
ARIA clocked the pattern in Kieran’s ear. “Sir that’s the 5th localized power flux since first cycle this morning.”
Asha stepped away to answer a ping from Command. 
A moment and then the lights stabilized.
“Fen…” Kieran grabbed his gaze and held it like a man grasping at cliff edges. In his eyes, a thousand days of childhood and yesterdays passed between them, a glinted breath of ancient honor, unbroken by the blinking of time.
Fen’s gaze slid slowly to the floor between then as his hand came up to meet Kieran’s chest. “My dear boy. You reach farther than you should…  I see this, my child… I see you… I cannot fail to honor this reach.” 
He leaned in to Kieran and gently kissed him on the cheek as if to say, yes, I know… me too.  Then with a deep sigh, “There’s a night market this evening,” he said. “Everyone who believes themselves necessary will be there, all pushing their unassailable usefulness. The most delicate of cuisines, the most elegant liqueurs, the most unspeakable of incantatious splendor shall this evening flood the blackened causeways of Erevos. The persons you seek… may not show their faces even still… may not, in fact, even have faces. But their presence… you can rest assured, their presence shall all encompass. They will send emissaries dressed as delicacies. Scoundrels masked as saints. Look past the pageantry, seek out the mechanics of trade. Find the shadow makers—the runners who, though donned in grand attire, will still be running. They may in fact be partial to the wondrous cuisines of Hagrass Row, I shouldn’t wonder. But I would make a point of arriving early on. They very much dislike being kept waiting. And Hagrass is not the kind of dish one wants to eat cold..”  With as much feeling as she was apt to muster, Asha said, “This is greatly appreciated, and duly noted, Fen. You honor us with your guidance.”
“I have my moments,” Fen said. “And as always when I can be of use to you, the marvelous keepers of our beautiful Erevos, this is for me— indeed for all—a double blessing.”
On the way out, Fen called, “Kieran… your friend is right, you know. About the current. It’s shifting. I can see. Holy lanes have lost the shimmer of piety. Blasphemous, ill wind sits in their wake. This I fear. Be most cautious, my boy.”
Kieran didn’t look back. “Blasphemy’s just honesty with better lighting. Good fortune to you, Fen Olik.”
“And to you, Kieran Vale. Good fortune.”
——
They were two corridors away before Asha spoke. The feeling of begrudged admiration was unexpected—it required a moment of deep breath-induced adjustment before... “How the hell did you do that?”
“Do what,” Kieran was certainly going to make her say it.
“I’ve never known Fen Olik to tip a single card, let alone toss an entire hand—not for anyone, but you…" Asha remarked, with more reverence than she intended.
“It’s a long, old story. Let just say that my story would be a painfully short one, if not for that man.  And he would say the same of me.”
Asha understood, and for this, she simply nodded to say, “I honor your truth, for with it, you honor me.”
“Okay… Alright. It’s way too early in the afternoon for this much truth and honesty.”
“Agreed. Well, if we are jumping back in, then let me be… clearly spoken with you…” She pulled the pad Fen had given them from her pack. “You should know that I know that you know at least two of the tags Olik supplied."
"Do I?" Kieran said.
"Don't be cute," Asha said, but there wasn't heat in it. There was calculation, and under that, a respect she hadn't yet decided was wise. “Don’t worry. Oh sure, on one of your usual petty smuggler’s runs, this might seem incriminating. But you are on our side now. And here, we call this sort of thing infiltration. We can use this, Captain."
"I can use it," Kieran corrected gently. "You can watch and learn."
"Touchstone and Blue Orchard," Asha said, not asking. "They don't work together. Ever. Blue Orchard runs independent, small loads, high value. They are fresh goods land operation.  Touchstone moves bulk through the quarantine routes. And has never touched anything lower than high tech goods. What in the hell would make them want to coordinate?"
Kieran glanced at her. She'd done her homework. "Whatever the motivation, there’s more than profit at work here.”
"Either somebody is making it worth their while," Asha said. "Or there is a great deal of dirty laundry as leverage being brought to bear.”
They moved through corridors that opened like throats, swallowing them into the stations operational under-layers. The further they plunged, the more the air seemed to smell of recycled disappointment and molted coffee. The kind of place where ambition went to rethink itself. They were still in the dark even with Fen’s roadmap. So institutional light was the next logical ingredient.
"Where on station are you taking us, Captain?" Asha asked. “I can feel the brains being sucked out of me.”
"Oh, come now, Major. We are about to enter paper-pushers paradise. A little oasis I like to frequent when I'm feeling low. Welcome… to The Venerable Office of the Registrars of Civic Commerce." He grinned back at her with as much silliness as he had in him.
Her mouth threatened something like a smile but didn't pay out. "Rolls right off the tongue, that one.”
“Doesn’t it? Oh and Major, we are smack dab in the middle of bureaucrat city so, you’re up.”
“Goodness Captain. Giving me the reins?”
“I figure you lend this good ol’ boy a little bit of leash, and he’ll hang himself at high noon.”
“I do so love a good hanging.” Asha pushed in front of him, rolled her shoulder, and pushed through the main doors of the suite.

2. Registrar Phael
Registrar Phael occupied a narrow office with tired plants and a desk that tried too hard to be respectable. He was fleshy in the face and delicate in the hands, his tie a little too tidy for a man who claimed to be overwhelmed. He lit up at the sight of Asha's insignia, then doused himself.
"What a pleasure," Phael said, voice damp. "What can Civil Services do for Major Asha today? 
Asha slid a slate onto his desk as if she were knifing it into the wood.
“Ahh. Manifest reconciliations. Of course." He turned to his terminal with the theatrical air of a saint presenting relics. “How is the Supreme Commander of late? We’ve not seen Commander Draven down here in some time.”
“He’s been a bit under the weather.” Kieran cooed. 
“Oh dear. Well you will send my best wishes for a speedy recovery. Won’t you?”
“Indeed.”
 "If we could…” Asha encouraged. The two men concurred. “We're looking at ten incorporations from the last quarter that all seem to demand… more scrutiny than your office has sent their way. Shell companies with devotional names and lifespans measured in hours. They are all routed through your sign-offs."
Kieran hung back, let Asha own the room. This was her territory—systems, paper trails, institutional rot. He watched Phael's tells instead: the way his thumb kept rubbing at the edge of a drawer, over and over. The man was leaking fear in a language Asha spoke fluently—just with a very different dialect. 
Phael swallowed visibly. "Yes… well these are all temporary suppliers it seems. Charitable storefronts arise when there is need," he said, hands fluttering. "We try to err on the side of mercy. The system processes them—if the paperwork is in order, I am here to facilitate the successful clearance through the system.  These all came to me clean and ready for my stamp without delay. What would you have me do?"
"Notice when all the signatures smear the same," Kieran said, mildly tempered with snark. He let his gaze drift around the office—framed commendation, chipped mug, data plaque with a tiny crack.
Phael blinked fast. "I hardly think—"
"You don't need to think," Asha said. "You need to show us the back ends of these blatant fronts. Date stamps. Origin IPs. Anyone who held your password longer than three minutes."
Phael started to bluster. Asha didn't raise her voice. She said his name once, quietly, as though reminding him how to spell it. Phael's mouth closed like a file being saved. He keyed access. The terminal vomited columns.

Kieran looked and didn't touch. That was Asha's ground here and she worked it with clean, quick hands. But ARIA was a different story. Within fifteen seconds of the logs being opened, she had grabbed every hidden pattern and abject discrepancy and whispered the next move into Kieran's head. He was immediate overwhelmed by the shapes in the numbers—the way two incorporations shared an IP block from a decommissioned habitat, the way three approvals came from the same chair at different hours with the same jitter in the timestamp. He made a small, amused sound.

Asha's fingers paused on the slate. She looked at the screen, then at Kieran, then back at the screen. "Decommissioned," she said, flat. "Habitat Seven's been cold for two years."

"Charitable ghosts," Kieran murmured. "Very generous, I’m told."

Phael rallied. "Habitat Seven, as I recall was used here as a temporary staging ground. Yes. In times of crisis," he said, "people form associations. They improvise - do their best. Aren't we all just trying to do our best, Major?"

"Your best shouldn't include the facilitation of blatant and systemic forgeries," Asha pressed. "I’ve seen enough here.” She fixed an ARIA control module to the core data drive. This device will mirror your offices activities to the ARIA mainframe for observation and further analysis. It is my sincere hope, as it should be yours, administrator, that nothing more blatantly incendiary is uncovered here.”
“Yes… No. of course. As you say, Major.” It was nonsense words of accommodation masking survival tactic , as bullets of sweat dripped from his forehead.”
“We appreciate your assistance, Registrar. But I'm afraid I'm going to have to ask you to remain on station until further notice. And if you find yourself in the market for updated security credentials, from now on, that request routes directly through my office. Clear?"

"Oh absolutely clear, Major." Phael was all head down and docile toned. He was a pro at being a nobody's somebody. "I'm honored to be afforded such a personal touch in these matters."

“Well, let’s hope I don’t find reason to put hands on any more of your sphere of bureaucracy, Registrar. Good Fortune to you.” And with that the Major quickly turned and, with locomotive force, exited the suite of offices. Kieran made a scramble so as not to be left in her wake.

“And to you both, Good Fortune.” Phael hollered down the hallway. They had already vanished from sight by the time he got to the office door to see them off. Then closing the door, he went straight to his comms link and made connection to a man who would not be pleased to learn of the registers encounter with command.


Back in the corridor, Kieran expelled a breath like he'd been drowning in there, lungs burning with the effort of restraint. "He's not the brains," he said. "But Christ—what a perfect specimen of a greased approval drone. Smiles that would lick boots clean, and a stamp ready to authorize his own execution if someone with the right clearance asked nicely."

"He's tip of the iceberg," Asha said. "Guarantee there's fifty more rubber stamp facilitator drones just like him planted in every corner of the supply chain." She looked at her slate, scrolling through the data they'd pulled. "But this—the devotional names, the coordination, the IP from a dead habitat—this is architecture. Someone designed this." Then she saw the comms ping and traced the encoded destination identifier. “Damn! it’s a dummy comms re-rout. Whoever he just called does not want to be known.”

In Kieran’s head, a soft nudge from ARIA with a simple darkening data note. “Sir, the connection reroute Major just tracked… it matches the link you received through my systems this morning.”

Fuck… Kieran could feel it now. Coursing stabbing through his veins… The past, the dark… who was this ghost. What was the connector.. He thought of so many tethers to so many gigs. So many dark corners and darker deals. 

Asha mused slowly, unaware of ARIA’s notice. "Religious cover plays well here. Devotional names get waved through because nobody wants to be the asshole questioning charity."

"And dead habitats don't raise flags," Kieran added. "They're already written off. No one's monitoring for ghost IPs."

They looked at each other, and Kieran saw the same churn he felt reflected in her face. This wasn't an opportunistic little band of smugglers. This was an entire orchestra. Measures and notes, melodies and rhythms. Time signatures and timbre, crescendos and key change. Someone playing the station like a well tunes instrument. And the two of them didn’t even know the song yet.

The Night Market wouldn’t open for another 4 hours and they both needed some time to process.

“I’ll update Draven on the mornings haul, and see you at the gates to Hegra’s Row at 06:30. Excellent work Captain. Thank you.”

“And you Major. Oh and remember, Night market attire is ‘5 to the eyes with stars! Don’t you dare show up under dressed.” 

Asha’s stomach dropped. A dress was in order. Oh GOD’s, how she loathed female wear. Kieran chuckled as he could read that look like a large print book. He backed into the flow of the concourse, pushing his way through mid afternoon bustle. He thought of how long it had been since he had cause to spoil himself - to adorn his body with a dash of fine fabric and artistry. 


———

He smiled as he linked to ARIA.
“Yes, sir. what can I do for you.”
“Please ensure that Commander Draven has a required appearance request for Complete  tie and tails this evening for the Night Market Gala. East pedimental entrance. 6:30 sharp. Tell him Asha and I will be there to meet him.”
“Oh, the commander will be so pleased.” ARIA quipped. “I see you have me doing your dirty work for you Captain.”
“Well ARIA, you don’t have any physical form for the commander to endanger upon receipt of this invitation.”
“It’s a fair point Kieran. And one I shall endeavor to use to advantage on your behalf.”
“Thank you ARIA.” and he severed the comm. It was here, mid promenade, imagining the sight of Draven in full tails and finery, and  mulling over a stroll through the haberdasheries of Bastion Row District to source his own statemented outfit, that a voice cut through the din.  A voice that carried such familiar warmth that it tingled up Kieran’s spine like a gilded memory bathed in ancient starlight and youths eternal glow field.
“Kieran Vale? Gods above, is that you?”
He turned. And the years dissolved into scattered dust. Jalen Myrr. His grin held the same endearing power blaze as Kieran remembered it had. The whips of childhood abandon now framed on Jalens face with a distinguished inlay of wisdom laden silver at the temples. He moved through the crowd with ease; a grace so familiar to Kieran that his body began the sort of chemical firework reaction that usually accompanied a sudden jolting from a vivid dream. As the gap between them closed, Kieran rallied his anatomy, demanding calm and decorum.

“Jalen?” Kieran’s face cracked into a smile of genuine, stunned delight. “What in the 13 cradles of starburst are you doing here!”
Their hands clasped, a firm, heartfelt grip that pulled them into a brief, back-thumping hug. For a fraction of a second, Kieran was fifteen again, hidden in the sweat-slicked, humming dark of a derelict freighter’s berth, sharing stolen beer and the terrifying, glorious discovery of first love’s kiss.
“I could ask you the same. My stars it’s good to see your face, Kier. Look at you!” Jalen laughed, stepping back to take in the swarthy worn wonder that was Captain Vale. “Last I heard, you were hustling supplies on the Cygnus fringe. But that was years ago. Still captaining that old rust-bucket… what was she…The Star-Prancer?” Jalen teased.
“Stargazer,” Kieran corrected, holding Jalen’s gaze.
Jalen’s face fell, instantly remorseful, then softened, the corners of his eyes crinkling. “Of course! The Stargazer. A classic. Always loved those galaxy-streamers.” His gaze held Kieran’s a moment too long, and in it, Kieran saw the same ghost—the memory of two boys who learned the geography of each other before they knew the map of the galaxy. “Tell me you at least upgraded the nav system.”
“Would you believe it’s mostly the same old girl? We’ve just… learned each other’s quirks over the years.” Kieran shook his head, the joy of the moment a sudden, warm sunbeam in the station’s chill. “But you my old friend. What are you doing here, anyway? I thought you had settled out on Gharros Station.”
Jalen glanced around the bustling dock, then back at Kieran, his gaze fond and tinged with a familiar wistfulness. “I’m just doing my part. Turns out my piloting skills are still of value, at least in these desperate days. I’ve done some work on and off for the Civilian Corp. Standard stuff. But you… you look like you’re still living the story we used to dream about.”
The words hung between them, charged with the entire unwritten volume of their past. Our story.
“Yeah… still dreaming. Someone’s got to,” Kieran said, his voice dropping a register. He settled for a shrug that felt like the most honest thing he’d done all day, then added, “Alive. Mostly.”
Jalen nodded slowly, accepting that. “Alive’s a start. Better than not, right.”
They talked of smaller things then, Jalen speaking of Civic Reclamation Corp, of bureaucratic hurdles, and the satisfaction of seeing medicine and supplies get where they were needed. He spoke of his children—Liana and Madrone, "absolute hellions"—and of his wife, Elara, with a quiet, steady warmth that was its own language. He spoke of the life he’d built, piece by deliberate piece, a structure with foundations and walls and a roof. Kieran listened, the familiar affection mixed with the hollow ache of observing a divergent future. This was the gravitational center. Kieran had chosen the perpetual motion. The drift was simply the cost of his freedom.
When they reached a junction where their paths inevitably diverged, Jalen stopped. “Well I’ve got a Chain-wide council hearing to attend. But I’m on station for a few months at least. It’d be great to grab a meal or something.”
“It would,” Kieran agreed, the nostalgia thick in his throat. “Hey, maybe we could even do a little flying for old time sake.”
“Now you’re talking. Yah know, I saw the Argyle up in deck 4 the other day. Still using her as cargo pull I guess.”
“Well that settles it. If for no other reason than to break her outta tug boat jail…”
“Always loved flying with you, Kier.” Jalen reached out and caught Kieran’s wrist to bring him in for a hug goodbye. Both their arms fell naturally into the old ways. Jalen’s thumb pressed gently against the comma-shaped scar on Kieran’s tendon. A simple touch. A silent question.
The warmth of the touch and the weight of the past locked them together. As both men turned to go, they somehow neglected to release each other’s arms, so that before either could complete a step, they found themselves snapped back together, face to face in a sort of amused mutual confusion. A moment passed. And another. Their eyes locked, and the years between them evaporated.
“My god,” Jalen whispered with a smile. “There you are.”
Jalen’s thumb brushed the side of Kieran’s cheek, a subtle pressure. He gave Jalen’s arm a final, solid squeeze, his thumb pressing briefly into the familiar spot on his wrist.
“Fly steady, old friend. Good Fortunes.”
“And to you, Jalen… Good Fortune.”
Jalen turned and walked away, his stride easy, the world parting for him the way it always had. Kieran stayed where he was, watching as the distance did its quiet work. The figure ahead grew smaller—thinned not just by space but by years, by everything unsaid. In that shrinking shape, Kieran saw the boy who’d once taught him how to love, and the man who’d shown him what stability looked like—and what he himself had refused. The air felt heavier now, or maybe it was just the hollow forming in his chest: the toll of always choosing motion over home.

=====




“Jalen?” Kieran’s face cracked into a smile of genuine, stunned delight. “What in the 13 cradles of starburst are you doing here!”
Their hands clasped, a firm, heartfelt grip that pulled them into a brief, back-thumping hug. For a fraction of a second, Kieran was fifteen again, hidden in the sweat-slicked, humming dark of a derelict freighter’s berth, sharing stolen beer and the terrifying, glorious discovery of like minded desire and first loves kiss.
“I could ask you the same. My stars it’s good to see your face, Kier. Look at you!” Jalen laughed, stepping back to take in the swarthy worn wonder that was Captain Vale. “Last I heard, you were hustling supplies on the Cygnus fringe. But that was years ago. Still captaining that old rust-bucket… what was she…The Star-Prancer?” Jalen teased.
“Stargazer,” Kieran corrected, a flicker of pride extinguished too quickly.
Jalen’s face fell, instantly remorseful. He forced a softer expression, the corners of his eyes crinkling. “Of course! The *Stargazer*. A classic. Always loved those galaxy-streamers.” His gaze held Kieran’s a moment too long, and in it, Kieran saw the same ghost—the memory of two boys who learned the geography of each other before they knew the map of the galaxy. “Tell me you at least upgraded the nav system.”
“Would you believe it’s mostly the same old girl? We’ve just… learned each other’s quirks over the years.” Kieran shook his head, the joy of the moment a sudden, warm sunbeam in the station’s chill. “But you my old friend. What are you doing here, anyway? I thought you had settled out on  Gharros Station.”
Jalen glanced around the bustling dock, then back at Kieran, his gaze fond and tinged with a familiar wistfulness. “I’m just doing my part. Turns out my piloting skills are still of value, at least in these desperate days. I’ve done some work on and off for the Civilian Corp., and from time to time pick up a long range run of food or supplies to the outer chain and back. Standard stuff. But you… you look like you’re still living the story we used to dream about.”
The words hung between them, charged with the entire unwritten volume of their past. *Our story.*
“Yeah… still dreaming. Someone’s got to,” Kieran said, his voice dropping a register. He offered a wink, a feeble shield against the sudden, sharp ache in his chest.

Jalen fell into step beside him as naturally as if they’d done it yesterday. They moved with the crowd, their rhythm syncing automatically, a muscle memory that transcended years of silence. 
“Relief runs,” Jalen continued. “Medical supplies, educational modules, the usual. Trying to bring a little light to the outer stations. So of course, everything sources from Erevos outward.” His eyes, warm and crinkled at the corners, scanned Kieran’s face, and the humor in them softened into something more concerned. “How have you been? Happy? Healthy? Truly, tell me everything.”
The question was so simple that it stole the air from Kieran’s lungs. Happy? The word felt foreign and clumsy in his mind, a concept for other people, people like Jalen who built things. He wanted to laugh, a sharp, bitter sound. He wanted to say GREAT!, to weave a beautiful lie about freedom and the thrill of the chase. He wanted to say Terrible!, to confess the hollow ache that had taken up residence in his chest. He settled for a shrug that felt like the most honest thing he’d done all day. “Alive. Mostly.”
Jalen nodded slowly, as if that were a real answer, and perhaps it was. “Alive’s a start. Better than not, right.” He had let him off the hook. Kieran was grateful, but he knew the question should be answered truly someday soon.
They talked of smaller things then, a delicate dance around the elephants in the room. Jalen spoke of his new position in leadership for the Civic Reclamation Corp. And of his current mission, the bureaucratic hurdles, the satisfaction of seeing crates of medicine and supplies actually get to the people and places where they were needed most. He talked about his children—Liana and Madrone, “absolute hellions” he called them with a father’s proud exasperation, his smile saying he adored every single bruise and broken vase they caused. He spoke of his wife, Elara, with a quiet, steady warmth that was its own language. He spoke of the life he’d built, piece by deliberate piece, a structure with foundations and walls and a roof.
Kieran listened, and it was like watching a holofilm of a life he’d almost had. A familiar envy curled in his gut, but it was tempered by a deep, genuine affection. This was what Jalen had chosen: stability, family, roots. A gravitational center. And Kieran… Kieran had chosen the drift. The perpetual motion. The exit always in sight. He’d told himself it was freedom. Now, standing next to the solid, real man Jalen had become, it felt less like freedom and more like a failure of courage.
When they reached a junction where their paths inevitably diverged, Jalen stopped. “Well I’ve got a Chain - wide council hearing to attend. But I’m on station for a few months at least. It’d be great to grab a meal or something.”
“It would,” Kieran agreed, his heart dropping into a familiar cadence of care and nostalgia. “Hey, maybe we could even do a little flying for old time sake.”
“Now you’re talking. Yah know, I saw the Argyle up in deck 4 the other day. Still using her as cargo pull I guess.”
“Well that settles it. If for no other reason than to break her outta tug boat jail…”
“Always loved flying with you again Kier.” Jalen reached out and caught Kieran’s wrist to bring him in for a hug goodbye. Both their arms falling naturally into the old ways. Jalen’s thumb pressed gently against the comma-shaped scar on Kieran’s tendon. A simple touch. A silent question. A physical language only they could speak. A whisper from a lifetime ago.
For Kieran, it all felt like the ghost of a vow never spoken, of a road not taken, of a life un-lived - a sweetness that was almost pain. It was time to walk away. As both men turned to go, they somehow had neglected to release each others arms, so that before either could complete a step, they found themselves snapped back together, face to face in a sort of amused mutual confusion. A moment passed.  And another. The two men stood there and for the first time in their brief encounter their eyes locked, one to the others.
“My god,” Jalen whispered with a smile, almost overtaken by the memory of Kieran’s 15 year old eyes that now pierced the span off time to gaze right back at him with their long lost innocence and unending love and devotion. “There you are…”
A single tear fell down Kieran’s cheek, but Jalen would not see it drop. He cupped the side of Kieran’s face as his thumb brushed it from his cheek and again whispered, “There you are.” 
He gave Jalen’s arm a final, solid squeeze, his thumb pressing briefly into the familiar spot on his wrist. 
“Fly steady, old friend. Good Fortunes.”
“And to you, Jalen… Good Fortune.” 
Jalen turned and walked effortlessly away, his figure slowly growing smaller as the distance grew between them. Kieran lingered, watching. As the crowd swallowed the distance, the man’s form blurred, yielding to a trick of perception, or perhaps the truth: the silhouette reducing not just in size, but in time. For a moment, Jalen was no longer the steady man with silver at his temples, but the shadow of the boy who once had taught Kieran how to love, and now had showed him the life he’d never have. Kieran didn’t understand quite why it hurt. He was not made for family life. Christ, he was not made for women…. but still… something ached as Jalen’s form receded into the crowded traffic of Erevos. He knew it, though he knew not why.




















4 Night Market
Stars in fields
The Night Market’s greatest trick wasn’t the massive fire cauldrons, or the ocean of kaleidoscopic organic sculptures that flowed through the ceiling arches like silken tendrils. It wasn’t even the impossible cuisine, sourced from all over the chain that highlighted every Night Market gala in memory. It was the floor.
When Erevos Station’s bottom core was renovated fifteen years ago, some mad genius with more credits than sense commissioned a transparent aluminum polymer floor three stories wide. Embedded in its crystalline layers was a holo-projection system so sophisticated it could render reality indistinguishable from illusion.
On Market nights, the floor became a living canvas. It morphed constantly, flowing through sequences that stole both breath and balance. One moment, a brilliant ocean—turquoise water so vivid you braced for the cold shock that never came. Fish darted beneath boots and heels, scattering at each footfall in patterns drawn from ancient Sehvanian archives. Then the ocean cleared, and suddenly you were walking on nothing.
The immense star field beyond the station’s hull stretched beneath you: an infinite drop into forever. Nearby stars burned too close. A nebula smeared purple and blue across the cosmos. The Dreary Star itself, that dim red dying thing that had murdered a solar system, stared like an unblinking eye.
First-timers stood paralyzed at the entrance, ancient survival instincts shrieking danger. Veterans had mastered their footing on the solid surface, yet still caught their breath when seafloor dissolved into star-scape—that moment when only faith in engineered transparency separated their soles from the endless dark.faith in engineered transparency separated their soles from the endless dark.
The Market owners knew exactly what they were doing. Vertigo and awe loosened purses. Gorgeous terror made people gamble. Negotiators lost their poker faces when their bodies insisted they were falling.
Kieran arrived on the upper circular foyer with time to spare. It had been a long, long time since he’d seen this famous Erevos Spectacle, and he found he needed a moment to collect his nerves.
“Sir?” ARIA stepped into his head.
“Not a good time, ARIA.”
“Yes, Sir, I know. I am unsure how much longer I will be able to hold off on this.”
“Still not able to trace the origin?”
“Nor the source even of the algorithm encrypting its carriers.”
“Well, that ought to keep you busy until I’m in the mood to dive into whatever that shitstorm is.”
“Certain memory modules containing data on how stubborn and infuriating you used to be are now being uncompressed from archive.”
“Some things never change, eh, ARIA?!”
“You took the words right out of my database, Sir. Enjoy the evening.”
As he circled the upper deck, children from all the Hangar districts of Erevos—those old enough to be out at this hour or clever enough to sneak—began to gather. Peering down into the grand ballroom through the slits in the railing, they whispered and pointed, giggled and dreamed of one day being old enough to participate in this storied Erevosian rite of passage. Kieran’s mind drifted back to the hundreds of evenings he and Jalen had spent as boys finding secret places to hide and watch this and the many other excitements that the cultures of such a megapolis as Erevos created under the sheer weight of its people living life. He wandered the upper decks, waiting for time to be right; waiting for Draven to appear, waiting for a dance over the star field expanse that had been a lifetime in the making.
The hum of the ballroom had hit a solid frequency. Kieran could feel it in his bones, and he moved to the top of the grand stairs to make his final calculations. He tugged on his jacket, ensuring all was in line and prepared.
Kieran’s tux was pure Old Erevos Glam—that rare, flamboyant bravado that lived somewhere between combat instinct and fashionista, combined with a healthy refusal to do anything halfway. He’d walked into Gharros Ring’s best atelier alone and told them: Make me impossible to ignore. Make me the poster child of Night Market Madness.
The shop’s seamstress and designers relished the challenge and the time crunch, and it was now clear as he approached the northern grand staircase and prepared to descend into the center of the evening’s energy, that all involved had succeeded wildly.
The jacket was military-cut but celebratory, midnight blue fabric that was at once dense and structured, and seemingly weightless all at once, and seemed to catch the light like deep water. At cuffs and collar, tiny salvaged mechanisms turned slowly, throwing sparks of motion across its surfaces. His shirt was white with a classic no-flap collar, fastened with a relic clasp shaped of folded, filigreed wings. No tie—never that obvious. Ship boots—to the knee, flared at the top and polished to such an extreme that they carried their own energy field—or perhaps that was just the manner with which he used his walk.
He stood proudly atop the stairs, ensuring the maximum exposure time to all in attendance on the floor below. He could feel the eyes turning heads, turning more eyes and heads. He could hear, even feel the lips move, the whispers all across the room. It was time. And as if perfectly cued, from atop the far opposing twin staircase emerged The Supreme Commander of Erevos.
Commander Draven was adorned in severe elegance: black tux, white tie, tails like wings. No embellishment. No concessions. Just pure, unadulterated class. Power without ornament.
Kieran’s heart stopped, along with half the room, but there were only two people alive in the universe as they stepped in tandem one stair to the next. The room held its collective breath as they seemed magically propelled from atop the balcony rails, down the elaborate balustrades and onto the dance floor.
They stepped onto the main floor just as the ocean projection gave way to stars, which had the effect of appearing to levitate the both of them into each other’s arms. Kieran looked miraculous—an absolute god of the starburst. Draven looked like experience, the kind of abject surety that everyone wanted to have, and nobody could ever achieve. Heads turned. Calculations shifted.
They made a devastating pair.
“You’re enjoying this too much,” Draven murmured.
“I’m enjoying it exactly the right amount,” Kieran said, hand possessive on his arm. “You look like a dream I had once.”
“I feel more like a nightmare.”
“Smile, darling. There’s not a single eye that isn’t on us right now. Besides, it’s all in the line of duty.”
Draven’s jaw tightened. “We are here for intel.”
“We’re here for the intel… eventually.” Kieran’s smile was all challenge. “First, we’re here because everyone needs to see what we’re worth. That’s my mission. Try looking like you’re having fun.”
“I don’t have fun.”
“I know. It’s tragic that you think people actually believe that.”
“Oh my God…” Draven stopped in his tracks as he saw her take the first step into the room from above.
“Oh, settle down. It’s not that…” and then Kieran saw her too.
Major Asha appeared at the top of the stairs. And once again, the world stood still for the entrance.
She didn’t do dresses. Her very essence screamed this fact to the heavens, so seeing her in one—and my god was she ever in one on this night—was like watching a star go supernova.
Emerald green, metallic sheen, sharp-cut like armor. One bare shoulder, the other capped with a sleeve that looked like a pauldron. Hair loose in waves, dark against her skin. A single cuff of salvaged metal engraved with coordinates.
Bare feet and anklets that trailed at heel but somehow never touched the ground. The seeming magic trick had the effect of lifting her over the ground like some sort of strange drone goddess. Each step across the projected stars was a dare, a declaration: she would be uncomfortably, impossibly ravishing to behold, but on her own terms. It was stunning to behold.
She was beautiful in the way wars began. And she looked utterly miserable about it.
“Don’t even think about leaving me standing here,” Kieran murmured.
But Draven was already moving. “If you laugh,” Asha said as he offered his hand, “I’ll break your fingers.”
“I’m not laughing, sis. You look—”
“Careful…”
“—terrifying.”
“Better.”
They moved onto the starfield floor, combat precision transformed into grace. Two weapons sheathed in elegance.
“You clean up well, brother,” Asha jabbed in honest admiration. “Any sign of our girl?”
“Not yet.”
“This dress has no pockets. If I need to fight—”
“You won’t. You could kill half this room with just a look so… I wouldn’t sweat it too much.”
“A battle tactic I learned from you.”
“Cute.”
The corner of her mouth twitched.
Then Kieran appeared, smiling like a blade of mischief to tap Asha on the shoulder. He went for the sharp side of her dress. “May I cut in?”
“Major, don’t you dare. Major… that’s an order,” Draven barked.
“You have no power here,” she delighted. “This is a dance, not a drill.” And with that, she winked at Kieran and handed the supreme commander over to him.
The Market watched. The Market stared. The Market whispered. Everyone. Draven took his hand, and as he did the floor swirled in a mass of clouds as if they had just flown up in the wonders of the long since forgotten skies of a stunning twin sunset on Sehrava 3.
Dancing with Kieran was a different danger. He moved with elegance laced in threat, closer than necessary, heat radiating through fabric.
“You’re creating a spectacle,” Draven murmured.
“That’s the idea. Everyone here is going to remember us, be talking about it in the morning. Look around… they already are.”
“God, I forgot. You’re an Erevos kid. This is probably the fulfillment of a lifelong dream of yours, isn’t it, Vale?”
“Don’t mock… and yes… so what if it is? It’s still work. This will earn me a sort of unspoken, undeclared power base all over Erevos by tomorrow afternoon. Being here is a tactical tour de force move.”
“Good story, Vale. Tactical or not, I can think of about a thousand places I’d rather be right now.”
“Are you sure about that? Because I can’t think of a single place in all the galaxy that I’d rather be.” Kieran looked directly into Draven’s silver-dusted impossible eyes as he said it.
Draven’s breath caught, then at exactly the wrong moment, ARIA broke the luster.
“Apologies, gentlemen, but…” ARIA interjected.
“Go ahead, ARIA,” Kieran said, annoyed, but ready to work.
“I have eyes on Mina. Upper gallery. Three o’clock.”
Focus snapped back. A woman in copper and black moved with both the care of age and the speed of stealth down the grand staircase, but instead of flowing into the crowd of luminaries, she quickly vanished into the service corridor, two guards in tow.
“Hegras Row,” Kieran continued smoothly.
“It would appear so, Sir. My records have her at booth number twelve,” ARIA confirmed.
“Same place she’s been since I was a kid. Some things never change.”
The song ended. Eye contact promised unfinished business.
“Well, let’s go,” Draven said.
“Not so fast, Commander. I am here for the agent. You are here as you are. There are about 100 politicians to glad-hand before you’re through. Asha and I have a mission to complete. See ya on the other side. Enjoy the Night Market, sexy!”
Kieran pulled away and turned with a flourish and released Draven, leaving him to be summarily eaten by bureaucratic vultures.
Asha, who was already on the move, pulled up right behind Kieran, tapped him on the shoulder and without a word, they vanished down the stairs and into the darkness, the vast rows and lanes and endless corners of Hegras Row to find the elusive, illustrious Mina.
Draven, unmoved by Kieran’s admonishment to mingle, was halfway across the main floor in the opposite direction when—approximately seven seconds from an escape—Commander Adrienne Veyr materialized from the crowd like a wild animal that had been waiting in ambush.
“Draven. How delightfully… unexpected this is. You must have lost a very large bet or something to force an appearance at our little political soirée.”
Veyr’s voice had that particular quality of oil pretending to be water—smooth on the surface, but you could feel the slick underneath rising to the top. He was tall, almost as tall as Draven, with symmetrical features that looked good in official portraits and meant absolutely nothing in combat. His dress uniform was immaculate. Too immaculate.
Draven smiled, even gave a chuckle. Anything less would give Veyr ammunition, and the man collected grievances like other people collected medals. “Commander Veyr. I wouldn’t miss this evening for all the world. You enjoying the Market?”
“Oh, immensely.” Veyr’s gaze swept the crowd, lingering on the elegant couples dancing on the star-projected floor. “Though I must express my… slight… concern, Commander. Don’t you have patrol schedules to oversee? Ships to command? Erevos doesn’t run itself, or didn’t you know?”
“I’m here on Station business,” Draven said flatly. “You needn’t worry your head over operational coherence, Veyr. It’s a bit above your pay grade.”
“Station business at the Market Gala?” Veyr’s smile was all teeth. “How… efficient.”
“Yes well, all in the line of duty, Veyr.”
“Indeed. Though I fear some of us have a more flexible interpretation of duty than others.”
The implication was clear: You’re out of line. Again.
“Is there something you needed, Veyr?”
“Just making conversation. Leadership isn’t just about tactical decisions, you know, Draven. It’s about presence. Politics. Knowing how to work a room.” Veyr gestured to the beautiful people, dripping wealth and status around them. “The Council is always evaluating command effectiveness. Not just in the field, but in all areas.”
There it was. The threat wrapped in bureaucratic poly-chatter.
“I’ll keep that in mind,” Draven said, his tone suggesting he’d rather eat Carom Droppings.
“See that you do, Commander.” Veyr’s expression hardened, the friendly mask slipping for just a moment. “Supreme Command is a heavy burden, and can be a slippery title for some men to hold on to. It would be a shame if you were to allow your personal… endeavors to cloud your professional judgment, Draven. I fear your reach has perhaps outpaced your grasp. Of course, I’m here, should you need me, for whatever support you may require.”
Draven’s jaw tightened. They both knew what this was about. Veyr wanted his position—wanted Supreme Command—and he’d been undermining Draven since the moment the Erevos Council overlooked him for the position two years ago, collecting every bent regulation, every prioritized mission over procedure, every decision that Draven ever made that could be twisted into insubordination or recklessness.
“I’m sure your call to Supreme Commander is just around the corner, Veyr… but it will be on some other outpost,” Draven said quietly. “Rising up is what happens when you spend more time polishing your record than doing the actual work.”
Veyr’s eyes went cold. “Careful, Commander. That attitude is exactly what the review board will be asking about. When things go wrong—and they will—someone will be held accountable. Make sure it’s not you.”
“Good advice, Commander. Let me offer you some of my own. This base is mine. And you are here under my protection and authority.”
“For now, perhaps.”
“Yes, well… for now, that opinion is that you are a mother fucking piece of outrun Vossular fodic shit, whose official protection by my officers is the most egregious misappropriation of station resource currently being forced on mine or any other administrative staff in all the chain.”
“How dare you…”
“Get the fuck out of my face, Veyr… and off my floor!”
He turned and melted back into the crowd, leaving a trail of indignation in his wake.
Draven stood there for a moment, watching him go, calculating the threat. Veyr was dangerous precisely because he didn’t need combat skills or tactical genius—just patience, cunning, and the willingness to destroy careers from behind a desk.
When all hell broke loose—and it would—Veyr would be there. Ready to point fingers, assign blame, lock him up. Ready to use chaos as a catapult to the Supreme Command he’d always wanted.
That was a mistake… one Draven knew immediately he’d pay for… but that was for later. His comms started to crackle. Kieran and the Major were on the hunt and on the clock.


Mina Hark
Hegras Row didn’t care about pomp or politics… hell, it didn’t even care about clocks. It lived in a loop of corridors that had stowed away its official designation, along with its direct light, decades ago. Steam rose from woks, choking eye lines, churning stomachs. Slabs of synthetic meat sweated on massive rotaries and fell to carving platters as large as speeders. People moved like air shot here, all sideways and shorthand. Booth twelve belonged to Mina Hark—a gloriously full-throttled, dark-eyed woman with necklaces stacked up so thick they seemed to support her head by way of chains and baubles alone. She possessed a sort of permanent squint that suggested you’d already disappointed her, long before you had a chance to please. Asha and Kieran were still working carefully through the dark of the boother lanes, barely able to see each other through the steam and din, when Mina herself reversed the work for them.
“Vale?” she exclaimed from across the darkened concourse. “Kieran Vale! My words and starlight.” She grabbed a small lantern beside her and shook it to signal her location across the lane. As the two approached, Asha naturally fell back a step. Mina reached up and outward to hug Kieran’s neck as he grew close to her table. Getting up was not amongst her frequent activities. But upon glancing over Kieran’s shoulder to his counterpart, she released Vale’s neck and slowly recoiled. “You bring a badge with you to a Night Market Gala? Bold.”
Asha placed a credit chit on the counter and didn’t push it. Mina ignored it in the manner of someone for whom theatrics were useful but never acknowledged.
Kieran snuggled into the booth and whispered to Mina in apology for the Asha parade incoming.
“Ms. Hark, what can you tell us about the various charity concerns associated with this evening’s event?” Asha pressed.
“Oh well, it’s a wonderful blessing to be able to help where I can. Any excess food supply and even prepared leftovers are packaged up and distributed twice a week to the various charities that deal in food distribution. The Forshaw Group, the Civic Health Society, and tonight’s primary benefactor, the newly minted Celeriac Collective.”
Kieran leaned in, surprised and concerned. “Your people do the packaging here? Ever dealt with Manifest splits?” Asha pressed. “Shell births. Runners too well organized to be self-starting.”
“Children learning to walk need helping hands,” Mina said, pouring tea like a ceremony and sliding cups they didn’t ask for. “We live in very charitable times, praise the gods.”
Kieran leaned forward, elbows finding the worn grooves in Mina’s counter like old friends. His smile slid into place—the same one he’d flashed years ago when wheedling intel about which security rotation had exploitable gaps. “Look, Mina, we don’t care about the black market keeping people fed. What we care about is that something rotten is playing at fresh goods—wearing charity’s face to mask darker purpose… and their numbers are building. Those runners with no traceable boss—you’ve seen them. I know you have. Once they leave your docks, where do they vanish to? Who are they answering to?”
Mina watched him for a count of three, then for four, and the corner of her mouth did something that was not softness. “You used to be a lot more fun, Kiery,” she said.
“Yeah, I know. I’m sorry. I’ve just run out of jokes,” Kieran said. “Promise—nothing personal, darling.”
“It’s alright, old friend. I see what the play is, here. I’m glad you’re home and in a better game, kid.” Mina smiled, her grin flush full of history and affection. She tapped the cred-chit once with a finger ringed in cheap gold. “There’s a place where schedules evaporate. Where the station seeps into the star field and forgets itself. Not the lower decks. Not the ports with cameras and parents. The seams between. If I were you—if you were smart and wanted to plant something without owning it—I’d find those seams and follow them. I’d make sure the janitor called in sick and the cameras blinked at the same time three days a week.”
Asha’s slate was already up, fingers moving. “Where? Show us.”
Mina’s eyes cut briefly to Asha, amused that she didn’t flinch. Then she looked back at Kieran. “Ask Dock master Alun whether Dock Nine is still haunted,” she said, very casual. “He’ll tell you no. His eyes will say yes.”
Dock Nine dropped into the space between Kieran’s ribs like a coin into glass. He kept his face obedient. Asha’s attention shifted toward him without physically touching him; it set his skin on alert.
“Mina, come on… D9?! Are you sure you’ve got this?” Kieran pushed.
“Dock Nine’s cordoned off. Hell, it’s only partially air-locked,” Asha said. “Been shut down since that freighter lost thruster control last year and took out four of the eight main structural columns.”
“Intake says a lot of pretty things,” Mina said. “You want the seam? You go where old doors open if you know how to knock. Alun knows how. And I’d wager he’s growing weary of knowing alone.”
“You didn’t hear anything from us,” Asha said. “And of course we didn’t hear this from you.”
Mina poured more tea. “I hear nothing, my dear. I sell tea.”
Kieran lifted his cup. Their fingers didn’t touch. “As always,” he said, “Good Fortunes, Mina. And thank you.”
Asha followed just behind Kieran as they sauntered down the Row toward the kitchens.
“I see that,” Asha called ahead, widening her stride.
“See what?”
“That walk. The swagger… You’re enjoying being on this side of the chase, aren’t you?”
“Maybe.” Kieran grinned. He skipped into sync so as to snag a kebab of unknown meat, tossed it into the air, and caught it directly between his teeth. A stealth pivot, and he snatched two glasses of Perlovian champagne from a passing tray. Then, thinking ahead, he somehow juggled the two into one hand and added two more.
They emerged from the Row onto the lower promenade spiral, both double-fisted with drink and bait, high on the chase.
“Another successful mission, partner. Who would’ve thought—you and I.”
Asha drained the first flute of champagne in one swig. “I’ll give you this much, Vale. You may be annoying… but you’re remarkably good at this work.”
“Careful, Asha. I might get the idea you actually like me.”
“Let’s not get crazy.”
“Good. Right, and hey, I don’t much like you either, Major Lady.” Kieran side-smirked the air.
“A little healthy animosity always keeps you honest, right Captain?”
“Whatever you say, Major.”
He tipped up his second glass and looked out over the blur of figures and the impossible ocean beneath their feet. Each face was a mask, a moment, a potential puzzle piece. He let the surface of the party wash over him, observing from the peculiar vantage of someone not quite of this world, not quite above it, but threading the seam that stitched every back alley and every grand staircase together into one tapestry. Loose ends everywhere. But the beautiful, terrifying thing about a gathering like this? If you pulled on the right thread, odds were damn good you’d unravel something someone thought was double-knotted.
He scanned the levels for signs of Draven, but the Supreme Commander was nowhere in sight. Asha watched from behind, fully tuned to his gaze as he caught the faint glimmer of Draven’s lapel. He was surrounded by a dozen mid-level brokers, all banking valuable seconds of face time with the Commander that mind prove useful at some magical moment in the future. Asha and Kieran both watched in awe as the master of all things softly spoke each subject’s problem into a seeming solution. Within mere moments, Draven was alone once more, surrounded by everyone, bested by no one—supremely in command of the room, and the night.
“He sure is something,” Asha admitted.
“God, you’re telling me. I can’t…” Kieran hushed in awe.
“Alright, alright. Control yourself, Vale.” Asha gave a short, irritated tug at the fabric of her dress, as if the attire itself were responsible for a temporary lapse in judgment. “That stunt you two pulled down there… Don’t know what I was thinking. I should have stopped it before your feet ever touched the floor.”
“We were dancing, Asha. It was just a dance. I think we’ll all survive.”
“You really don’t get it, do you? Nothing on Erevos is just anything, Vale. Especially not a son of the station, back from exile, arm-in-arm with the Commander. That little showstopper on Draven’s arm—that wasn’t just a dance. That was a broadcast. You took yourself off the board as a neutral player. You picked a side.”
A pair of teenage girls turned the corner, laughing and chatting like wildfire. The moment they caught a glimpse of Kieran, blush flooded both their faces, and silence filled their heads. They quickly stood up straight, turned forward, and calmly walked by.
“See that?”
“We just startled them. That’s all. That wasn’t…,” and as if on cue, the two girls started giggling again and turned back to steal a glance of Erevos’s favorite new son.
“Oh, really? Just wait, by morning… you’ll see—every network and gossip feed from Erevos to the goddamn Outer Chain.”
“I get it, okay… I just thought… I don’t really know what I thought.”
“Well, like it or not, you’re about to go from ghost to superstar in a single step. You’re not background anymore. You’re a symbol. Maybe even a liability.”
Kieran said nothing. He stared ahead.
“You’re trying to trick notoriety into making you powerful. But that’s all it will ever be—a trick. That kind of public power only makes you predictable. And predictable gets people killed.” She stopped herself. The silence did more damage than words could.
“You’d miss me if I went up in flames. Admit it.”
Asha stopped. Waited for him to notice. She could feel it coming anyway, unavoidable as ignorance. And with a few glasses of champaign coursing the back of her brain, it was an efficiency decision that forced the hand she’d play next.
“You’re right,” she finally blurted out. Dead as pan.
The words stopped Kieran in his formidable tracks and he turned, not once, but twice. A double pirouette that made Asha split a grin in spite of herself.
“Come again, Major.”
“You heard me. You’re right. I’d miss you. There.”
He lessened his walk into an almost skip as he closed the gap between them. Asha could see a public embrace in her future that was not about to happen if she could at all prevent the thing. She pressed one fully engaged arm hand into his oncoming chest. Stopped him dead once again.
“I’d bury you. Don’t think I wouldn’t. Probably in this exact… frock you’ve tortured my eyes with all evening.”
“ Asha, I really am…”
“A dead man if you tell anyone I said it, but yeah. I’d miss you, yah fuckin idiot.”
“Hmm,” Kieran mussed. “Surprises around every corner. Good ol’ Erevos.”
For a moment, they were both content to be content. The walking was enough. The silence that accompanied, a welcome byproduct not of tention, but of burgeoning trust.
When they reached the main access lifts, Asha broke back to business with her usual pitch and roll.
“Well, I’m in the completely wrong sector now, so id better…” she pulled back and motioned the general signal for about face.
“Thanks for the walk and talk, Major. Enjoyed it.”
And with that he watched Major Asha fade into he distant promenade haze. 

Kieran wandered. The station hummed under his feet, a breathing leviathan of old deck plates and new glamor. The concourse had changed—no longer the grit and ghosts of his youth, but a city of glittering neighborhoods, stacked like dreams just barely earned. He told himself it wouldn’t last. That the cameras weren’t really watching him. That the whispers had better targets. He told himself, lied to himself, that this would all fade by morning. But already he could see he was wrong. Kieren could smell a dud from a mile away. But this was dressed in something altogether closer to a ready made media explosion.
Every screen and holo-visual he passed was already dominated by footage of he and Draven. Viral footage. It had already been cut and packaged in that certain way that ensured it’s subjects will be forced to endure for many many cycles to come. Elegant slow pans, high angles, crowd reactions, synced audio. Every shot poised to turn a glance into a narrative, a whisper into a broadcast. His face was everywhere. The headline, the thumbnail, the trending tag, the burning center of a cultural neutron star. 
One person looked. Then ten. Then a hundred. Then thousands. Every holo-pad, every public screen, every damn emitter on the station was suddenly filled with the radiant tragedy of Vale and Draven. He tried to disappear into it. But Erevos wouldn’t let him. He felt it, the change.
Eyes that once dismissed him now studied. Shopkeepers paused mid-sale. Conversations faltered when he passed. The smugglers he once drank under tables now gave him the long, calculating look of predators re-sorting the food chain. He was no longer background noise. He was currency. And every glance was a transaction. He tried to lose himself in the crowd—stalls, noise, smell, spice, tech, grease. He tracked deals, dodged notice. But the stares lingered. The nods changed. Not contempt. Calculation. He was a symbol now. Of what, no one knew. And that made it worse. 
At he main lift corridor, a mass of gala goers huddled waiting thier turn to .. blah blah .. every 3rd head turned to clock his face in recognition and flip back to whisper, then every other, he heard the distant ding of another lift arriving far around the corner from eh main bank and made a darting move to escape the din . He lunged out of the crowd and hit fingers to door edge in time to rise the closing mechanics. Relieved, his melted into he tube.
The doors sealed. The hum of the ship folded around Kieran like a held breath.
Then—static.
A hiss bloomed in his ear, faint at first, then knifing sharp through the skull. The lights shuddered. The lift dropped half a floor and slammed to a stop.
“ARIA—ah—” He pressed a palm behind his ear. “Kill the channel—”
“Ahem… testing, testing. Is this thing on?”
The voice cut through distortion like silk over wire.
Kieran froze. He’d know that voice anywhere. “Soren.”
“Hello, darling. You’re a hard man to track down. I was starting to think you didn’t love me anymore.”
“You’d be right.”
“You wound me,” Soren said, in that too-human tone he always used when trying to sound sincere. “After everything we built together, you and I. The worlds we cracked open. I thought I’d earned a little loyalty.”
“What do you want.”
“Only a chat.”
The lift lurched again. Static burst in Kieran’s inner ear, then faded. When he looked up, the floor lights had dimmed to a pulse.
“There we go,” Soren said, satisfied. “Now we can talk properly.”
“You’re inside,” Kieran muttered.
“Of course I’m inside. Always easier to talk from the inside.”
Kieran’s throat tightened. “You shouldn’t be able to touch this system. You should be able to touch…”
“You?” Soren leered. “Oh, but I can. I am touching you—from the inside. Rather intimate I admit but, you were never one to shy away…”
“Jesus Christ!”
“Honestly, swearing to off-world gods… such pith!”
“What is this about.”
“Debt,” Soren said. “All of it. Every creditor, every lien. You know how I like to keep things tidy.”
Kieran laughed, sharp and humorless. “You bought my debt?”
“Not just yours. Tovan’s, the ship’s registry, the payout from that isotope haul you scavenged—those funds are mine too. Consider them collateral. You’ll get access again… once you’ve worked off what you owe.”
Kieran’s hands curled into fists. “We don’t owe you a damn thing.”
“Of course you do,” Soren said, voice low and coaxing. “You always have. Freedom’s expensive, Kieran. And you’ve been running a long tab.”
The lift vibrated as though with laughter.
“I could make things easy again,” Soren continued. “Money moving where it should. Doors opening instead of locking. You were always better when you were working with me, not against me. What are you doing now—security duty? Playing hero on that floating scrap heap?”
“Keep talking,” Kieran said. “I’m recording every word.”
“You think anyone’s going to believe a ghost in your head?” Soren’s tone slipped into something darker, the glitch in his laugh widening into static. “Face it, Kieran. I don’t need to own you. I just need to own everything you touch.”
The lights went black.
“Now,” Soren whispered. “Shall we start paying down that debt? Talk soon, darling.”
The connection fractured into static. Then silence.
The lift steadied, lights humming back to normal. The doors slid open onto the hangar bay. Everything looked normal—crew moving, the faint smell of coolant and ozone—nothing out of place.
Kieran pressed his hand to his temple, breathing hard. “Yeah,” he said softly. “Fine. Everything’s fine.”
But somewhere deep beneath skin and circuitry, something still listened.


5 The Cost of Yes
He wandered, becoming a ghost in in the vast Erevos machine, loitering with the specific, potent intent of a man trying to outrun the decisions he’d already made. The station’s artificial evening slid into dense, droning calm of the late, natural night. In the quiet spaces between the groans of machinery and the distant thrum of life support, Erevos seemed to breathe, a great leviathan of metal and life-teem. Kieran stood upon its ribs, feeling the vibration through the soles of his formal boots, trying to decide if he was meant to jump or be thrown into what was so inevitably before him.
He found Tovan in a forgotten sub-corridor, buried up to his elbows in a tangle of leaking ducts that smelled of spit and coolant and ozone. His sleeves were rolled past his elbows, revealing forearms mapped with fine, white scars, and he had a wrench clamped between his teeth like a pirate’s cutlass, muttering curses at a stubborn pipe joint that had refused to acknowledge the polite passage of time.
Tovan mock purred at the sight of Kieran in his only slightly disheveled finery. The metal in his mouth doing nothing to hide his stunned reverence. 
"My stars, Captain Vale. You look like you could seduce the entire universe into surrender. Like you walked through that Market and rewrote every definition of beautiful anyone ever knew.”
Kieran chuckled. “Stop it. I’ve had a shit night.”
“No, no. I’ve got one more.. hold on.. yes!” Tovan cleared his throat dramatically and gained a swooning. “Tell me, did anyone survive seeing you tonight, or did you leave nothing but swooning casualties in your star draped devastating wake?”
“What are you trying out lines for your next romance novel?” Kieran couldn’t resist the quip.
“Ha!” Tovan rippled. “I’ve got scores of dedicated journal for exactly that, yes! Don’t you get me started on the illustrations, they are graphic as fuck, even for me.”
“Oh… God, please no!” Kieran begged, only half joking.
“Seriously though,” Tovan leveled, “What are we doing here, man?”
“Come on, T. You know better than anyone what's two systems behind us. You were there. You’re not dumb.”
“And you really think Draven is the solution?” Tovan pressed him, working a shredded rag over a scarred-out conduit that deserved a scrap heap eight missions ago. “He sure wasn’t last time.”
“Look, I don’t know what to tell you. I said yes to some things, that, more likely than not, are gonna turn into a very long and complicated collection of no’s,” Kieran said, leaning into the candor of an old and battle-tested friendship they both knew could be leveraged.
Tovan sat back on his heels, wiping a smear of black carbon from his cheek with a rag that was arguably filthier. He squinted up at Kieran. “Right. So… and check me on this. We come flying in here. Hot. Hoping the old yard and the old flame might be able to hail this Mary. Miracle of miracles, Saint Draven actually asks for your help. No, NEEDS your help. How am I so far?” Tovan checked down.
“Annoyingly on target.”
“Yeah. So then, you can’t say no. Of course, you hate yourself for that. But the fact that he needs you means you never had a choice… or a chance. How am I doin’?”
Kieran bowed his head, a defeated smirk confirming volumes.
“There’s no way this ends any better than complete disaster, Vale. You know that, right?”
“I know that!” Kieran lashed back, defensive. “You don’t think I fuckin’ know that, man?!”
“Yet here we fuckin’ are. He must really have you by the balls!”
Kieran chuckled to himself. You have no idea how hard he’s got me! Fucking by… those balls!
“So what’s the part you want me to fix?” Tovan teased. “The leaking pipes, or the leaky life decisions? That’s a hell of a lot of holes, brother.”
“Everything needs fixing,” Kieran said, the words coming out more desperate than he’d intended.
“Good news,” Tovan said, his grin a flash of white in the grime. “I only fix parts that break loudly. The quiet, soul-crushing stuff is outside my purview.” He tapped a greasy knuckle against the groaning pipe. “You know, I like this station, Vale. I like the smell of hot solder and recycled oxygen. I like that the air doesn’t taste of smoke and desperation. If your particular brand of glorious, catastrophic foolishness can help us keep it that way, then by all means, be a fool. Just be a loud one. You know I’m gonna be right behind you no matter.”
“I know,” Kieran turned that over in his mind, examining it from all angles. It was a curiously solid piece of psychiatric advice to be coming from a man covered in lubricant.
“There’s… something else… you should probably know.”
“Let me guess you ran into Jalen Myrr today.” Tovan said nonchalantly, turning back to his work with a grunt of effort. J
“How the hell did you know that.”
“Well I was on your comms all day, You told me to be.. I may have…. lingered a bit too long…”
“Tovan!!” Kieran scolded.
“Hey, man I’m sorry. Not a lot of entertainment value in engineering, okay.”
“Jesus.”
“Ghost of the road not taken. Tough brother. But don’t go thinking you’re like some extra special, soul searching, tortured super gay or nothing. Running into an old ‘what if’ you left behind in some other port, this does not put you on the unrequited medal stand, okay?!” The wrench turned with a shriek of protesting metal. “Now either hand me the seismic calibrator or stop blocking the light. Your existential crisis is messing with my flow.”
Kieran smiled, deeply, his right hand up to his heart, as if to say a million words of thanks to one hell of an engineer/psychoanalyst die-hard friend. Tovan got it, appreciated it hard, and wished to God this man/child could find his way to actually listening some day. Kieran turned off and disappeared into the star-drifting jungle, leaving Tovan to conduct his symphony of repairs
“I’ll see yah later, T.” 
Kieran wandered through corridor after corridor; his feet carrying him on a tour of his ancient history. He walked the long, curving spine of the main concourse, his boots choosing the path by sheer muscle memory. He passed the rust-scabbed mezzanine where he and other dock rats had once slept in ragged nests of stolen coil blankets, dreaming of ships they’d never board, of lives they’d never get lucky enough to live. 
He passed a shuttered food stall, its metal grate plastered with layers of faded flyers—concert stubs for bands long since disbanded, promises of cheap thrills and easier loans, the ghost of a community that lived in the margins. He passed a massive observation window where the star-dusted blackness of space looked close enough to touch, a siren song of infinite escape that seemed at once to pull him near, and repel him back out… into… what? Whatever came next. Whatever worked. His head was a blaze.
He stopped. Somewhere in the middle of the middle of… somewhere. 
A moment…
Another…
Time is a malleable thing, when you don’t know where you’r going… Life will always zoom past you… unless you know how to make it wait… just wait… 
Kieran breathed deeply, the way Mina has showed him when he was barely 6. He breathed into his soul, searching for guidance.. for…
Her voice echoed back across the decades…
No Kiery.. darling. you cannot search for things. cannot force the stars to grant your wishes. You must state you claim and breath with an open mind, a trusting heart. A heart that loves without condition, a mind that calculates without devotion, Breathe in that which is all of us, and everything. and let the stars do the work of all thing.. Let you soul trust in the power of your desires. Life is Flow. not force. Breath.
The storm within him began to subside. He focused his, then opened his eyes, knowing this somewhere would be fine and right.
Breathe.
And then, because the station had always possessed a wicked, ironic sense of humor, and because he had indeed been taught well. The ghost appeared once again. Jalen.
Not the man himself, but his name. Directly before him, lit like a star shot on a public notice board, a digital slate glowing with relief schedules and cargo manifests was his name: Jalen Myrr. It platted there, neat and official: 
Celeriac Collective, The Council for Civic Continuity and The Charitable Civic Corporation are pleased to announce the Appointment of our New Lead Arbitrative Magistrate for Chain-wide Civic Distribution: Jalen Myrr. 
And then a memory, sharp and cold, rushed over his aching heart. A cramped cockpit, a busted comms unit, and Jalen’s face, lit by a flickering diagnostic screen. “One day you’ll have to choose,” Jalen’s voice swirled through his head. The whispers of decades-old truths, still tugging, still tender, painful to touch. “The universe waits for no one, Kieran. You have to choose.” Kieran winced at the irony and decades of running, powered by… no, cloaked in the guise of freedom—a freedom that had locked him down, betrayed his best interests, time and time again.
“If you don’t choose Kieran, someone else will do it for you. And then… they will own you… as sure as you take breath, even that breath will not be yours.” 
Kieran pushed Jalen’s voice out of his head. He understood. It was way too late, yes, but damn it he understood. Only now, in the empty, airlock corridors of a broken down star base, there was no more road to run on. Grow the fuck up you asshole!
He slowly lifted his hand and pressed two fingers to the cool glass of the notice board, right over Jalen’s name. He held them there until the heat from his skin fogged a small, temporary patch on the surface, obscuring the letters. When he pulled his hand away, the ghost of the name lingered in the condensation for a moment, then faded away.
He stood there, a suspended coil of regret and resignation. His frozen frame immutable for a moment so unmeasurable, it seemed wholly unbound, untimed. In the midst of this paused frame, a janitor, wielding a practiced grip over a screaming floor buffer rounded the corner. He stopped the whirling orbital right at Kieran’s feet. 
The old man had seen it all in his service to this place. Kieran’s frozen countenance should have registered exactly nothing special to him. But, for some reason, the man felt compelled to notice. He waited, staring, wondering what this troubled kid had been so swallowed by. 
The whirl of the buffer had worked its way into Kieran’s thoughts, slowly nudging, then pushing. Tugging, and finally bouncing him back onto the concourse of reality. Face to face with the glinted kind eyes of this master of the floor decks. 
“Welcome back, son. You okay?” 
Kieran took a breath.. and another.. he was. He was okay. The two nodded in understanding, a strange empathy in the old man's eyes that required no words, but said, “I see you there.. You're alright.. Promise.” 
Slowly they moved past each other. One step, another, Kieran turned to ask his name, the strange gesture warming his heart. 
“Hey, you mind if I ask your…” 
The old man was gone. Nowhere in sight. No janitor. He looked further on, no floor machine. He tracked back to his own feet, only to notice he was standing in the middle of a freshly buffed, perfectly mirror-shined swath of floor panel. 
Your losing it, Vale.. Jesus!
The quiet voice in his head, the one that had been his only compass for so long, had just run out of directions. There was nowhere left to go but forward. He turned and walked, without a single thought but with total certainty and purpose, toward the one place he least wanted to go, but most needed to be: the only place that might silence the ghost in his head. The fact that it made zero sense and was obviously the worst move he could make, made it the only possible step to take. 
He made the turn away from the hanger bays and the Aegis, maneuvering on pure muscle memory, until he was standing in front of the secured lift bay that served only the  suites of the stations upper command structure.  Immediately, the security panel clicked his reading into the system.
“Welcome Captain Vale. Identity confirmed. please proceed to the lift on your right.  He stepped in and the door closed and without asking announced “level 16 - Supreme Commander Raleth Draven. The doors opened , he stepped off the lift and found himself directly across the small hallway from the  door to  Draven’s quarters. 
Breathe you idiot. Breathe. He didn’t knock. He didn’t have to. The door slid open with a soft sigh, as if it had been expecting him all along. The room within was a study in controlled minimalism. Draven stood at the far side of the room at a small, sleek shelf that served as a bar, wearing only a well-tucked towel and a bare chest that could command legions. He was a glancing God when this close to naked. The sight took every bit of air from Kieran’s lungs.
Breathe you idiot. Breathe.
Draven, seemingly unaware of the mild panic attack by torso encounter taking place at his doorstep, continued pouring tea from a pot that was too elegant, too precise, to be casual. Sanctuary. No blood. No questions. No deals made in daylight.
Nerral saw the pull take Kieran. His grin sharpened. “You won’t touch me in there.”
He palmed the panel and slipped through.
