“Sorry, ma’am,” he rumbled, almost polite. “This place ain’t for you.”
He set her down and nudged her back a step that felt like a mile.
Kieran looked at Asha. His grin said, *just business, boss*. Then he dove through before the gap closed. The door sealed. The alley’s red went dark.
Heat punched him in the chest. Music chewed the air to pieces. Shadows moved like animals. Sweat lit up in neon. Men hung from rigs, cuffed and gasping. Others bent to rails, hands gripping polished steel. Mouths met mouths. No one pretended here. Hunger was a kind of prayer.
Hands brushed Kieran’s arms, his waist. Voices rose and burned away. He pushed through the heat, instincts split between fight and want.
And there—Draven. Jacket gone. Shirt open. A drink in his hand he wasn’t drinking. He looked carved out of control and set in a storm. His eyes found Kieran’s. Something dangerous passed between them. Relief. Fury. Need.
The crowd surged. Draven vanished. They were cast into a rough sea of bodies. Hands caught them, mouths tested them, sweat slicked their skin. They were swallowed whole, lost, found, pulled away, devoured again by the crush. Kieran reached for Draven, caught him, lost him, found him, lost him—each time the music hit harder.
Once, Kieran was caught in a chain of men, arms looped, breath shared, the heat a living thing. He let it take him for a count of ten, then tore free, the lack of Draven louder than the bass. Once, Draven was pressed to a rail, strangers on him like waves. He shoved off with a look that cut.
They fought toward each other and the Room kept changing the maze.
Nerral appeared like a trick of light. He slid past Kieran’s shoulder with a smile that meant trouble. “Enjoying yourself?” he breathed, and melted into the bodies.
Soren’s touch was here too, cold as wire under skin. Decoys moved with the same shoulders, the same jawline, the same scent notes designed to lie—Draven here, Draven there, Draven everywhere. Kieran grabbed one by the wrist and felt the false in the pulse. He let go.
“Kieran.” The real voice. Rough. Close.
He turned. Draven was pinned in a rig now, not bound but braced, fighting the current. Their eyes met across a river of men.
Kieran moved. The crowd tried to make a meal of him. He ducked, spun, took an elbow and gave a shoulder. For a breath a stranger’s mouth stole his, heat flaring; Kieran broke it and pushed on. Draven did the same on his side—steady, relentless, eyes never leaving Kieran’s.
Nerral ghosted between them again. “Run while you can,” he said to Kieran, and to Draven he grinned, “Call off your dog.” Then he vanished, a thread of smoke in steam.
The music broke and returned, harder. The sea rose again.
They slammed together at last. The impact knocked sense from both of them. Draven’s hand closed in Kieran’s collar. They hit a wall. The kiss landed like a strike. Hard. Years of no turning into now.
They parted a breath.
“We shouldn’t be here,” Draven said, voice rough.
“Then pull me out,” Kieran said, and pulled him closer instead.
A rig waited beside them. Cuffs open, lit like a dare. Draven’s gaze flicked to it and back. Kieran nodded once. Draven’s eyes went bright under the neon. He spun Kieran to the steel and set his wrists. The magnets kissed shut with a clean click. Not pain. A promise. Kieran’s body lit like wire.
“Say it,” Draven said at his throat.
“Say what?”
“That you want this.”
Kieran pulled against steel. “I always have.”
Draven’s mouth dragged down his neck. Kieran arched, heat roaring in his blood. Around them the Room howled. A man reached; Draven shoved him off with a look that meant *mine*. The bass hit harder. Somewhere in the dark, Nerral’s laugh slid like smoke.
“Kieran,” Asha’s voice called from a memory he didn’t have time for.
The sigil of the cuffs pulsed. Heat hit skin. The world narrowed to the space of two bodies and the drum of need. Around them the Room cheered and did not matter at all. They gave in—fully, finally, no more years between them.
The rest is breath and heat and the kind of mercy you only get in the dark.
The storm eased by inches. Sound came back as music. Color returned to neon. They leaned on each other and the rig till the floor felt steady.
They left the Room through a door that pretended to be a wall. On the other side: solvent, cheap food, the unimpressed look of a bouncer. The chalk mark was wiped from the inside. Rules waited in the hall like a man with folded arms.
6 Ghost Spark
1. Jump Day

On Erevos,  mornings were a sacred, savored thing. Consider, a civilization born under the cosmological firepower  and beauty of twin stars… the 
Who , if you stood in just the right hall with the bulkheads humming and the vents aligned, you could hear it: resonance cutting through steel, ricocheting down corridors, the singular sound of gravity changing its mind.
When that sound hit the Crossroads, you knew. It was a jump day. It was a market burst morning, and no one with blood in their veins wanted to be anywhere else.
Kieran slipped into the chaos the way a river accepts a stone—without ripple or resistance, simply absorbing it into the flow. Tovan slipped in after him, a splash of cheerful, unvarnished enthusiasm that made him stick out like a beacon, which ironically made him the most dangerous man present. Asha shadowed them from a few paces back, her posture a masterpiece of contained lethality that said she had infinitely better things to do than babysit wayward criminals, which was precisely why she had insisted on coming
“Can you feel it boss,” Tovan rejoiced.
“Are you kidding” ARIA,” he pinged his implant. “What do you think we have until the wave hits the crossroads?”
“I estimate that we are approximately 37 seconds away from the morning’s peak promenade harmonic burst. Happy Crossroading, gents.”
“Asha was less than impressed or amused. ”Crossroading!?” 
“Major, don’t take this the wrong way but, I promise, you are going to want to take my hand,” Kieran warned.

“Major,” Tovan piped in. “Take the Captains hand please.”

“I am more than capable of navigating the morning…”

“Asha!” Kieran was at full command yell. “Take my Goddamn hand.”

ARIA interrupted. “Crossroads burst in 4… 3.. 2.. 1…”

She grabbed Kierans hand as the market’s body suddenly surged through and beyond the seeming false forces of gravitation control. The decks let out a well tuned gasp, releasing every pair of feet and every body attached to them softly aloft. This marvel of a 3 second daily glitch in the station’s artificial gravity control matrix had started almost 30 years ago. the effect was such an instant and galvanizing communal course, that the failing lines of code were simply never refactored. The  ripple of movement, of millions of nerve endings briefly rewired, plus a well timed and oft practiced push off the deck sent Kieran - Asha in tow - clear across the main thouroghfair of the concourse in a chaotic wonder blitzed blink of an eye.  Tovan was midair, feet windmilling, mouth wide open in an ecstatic whoop only partly muffled by the crowd’s roar. 
Market regulars barely paused. An avalanche of bodies moved in new vectors, crowd surfing the momentary physics hiccup. The uninitiated—tourists, drifters, off-stationers—scrambled to catch their footing, some even slamming into bread stalls and spice wagons, setting off a comet trail of toppling crates and spilled glitter fruit.
Asha landed, essentially on Kierans back, her arms instinctively grabbing round his neck as Gravity returned, sucking her boots and those of the entire crowd unceremoniously back to the deck plates.
Kieran turned round, a grin from ear to ear, that must have mirrored exactly his  boyhood expression at the end of each morning burst. “I told you to hold on!” he screamed over the still raucous crowd.
“You certainly did Captain.”
Kieran laughed, breathless, the old adrenaline washing through him. “God, I’ve missed this.” He squeezed Asha’s hand before she could let go. 
Rest assured, I will be taking your bracing advice more seriously from now on.” She turned her head, adjusting her uniform and hair with precision, but failed completely in masking a slight grin of amusement.


2
 Tovan had traveled the farthest during the burst and found himself at the edge of exactly the wrong bridge corridor at exactly the wrong time and now found himself face to face with exactly the wrong guy. Broad-shouldered and boorish, he moved with a careful, menacing grace that reeked of power and control. His clothes seemed to serves as declarative armor, actively cloaking  the   lurking dangers beneath. 
Tovan thought for a moment that he had escaped his eye. Even the thought of a quick side stepped escape, however, brought the refocused laser eye contact he was so eager, yet powerless to avoid. 
The man smiled at Tovan, an expression that managed to be pleasant without showing a single tooth.
“I see you, Tovan. Can you see me?”
“Sael? Is that you? My goodness—what a surprise.” Tovan’s voice wavered, and even his knees seemed to knock loud enough to hear. “I was just coming to see you.”
“Were you now? How sweet. Shocking, but sweet.” Sael’s smile didn’t reach his eyes. “It’s funny, though—every time I come to see you, I’m either met with empty rooms and no Tovan, which is upsetting, or empty promises, which is worse.”
“I’m a busy man, Sael. Gotta earn so I can pay. Isn’t that what you always say?”
“Perhaps. I say many things to many people. But what I’m about to tell you now, Tovan—you’d do well to take to heart.”
“Sael, I know what you’re going to say, and you’re right. I’m way past my expiration date with you. I’m not proud of it, but I promise I’m working on it. I just need a little more time.”
“I grow weary of your obfuscations, sir.” Sael’s tone was silk over steel. “You need new lines, Tovan. I’ve heard all of this so many times that I fear I could lose interest in collecting interest from you. And  you very much want me to maintain my interest in you, isn’t that right Tovan?
“Yes. Of course I do, Sael. I’m good for the cash. You know I am.”
“We shall see. Today is Wednesday. You have forty-eight hours from…. Now. Don’t make me visit my florist on the weekend Tovan. The crowds are so very perturbing on weekends”
“Yes. Thank you, Sael. I’ll be here first thing Friday. Thank you.”
“Ta-ta, Tovan. Forty-eight.”
And then he was gone, melting back into the crowd the way men like him do—without the world ever acknowledging the subtraction.
Tovan blew out a long, shaky breath. “Well. That was… not the worst-case scenario.”
“What’s the worst-case scenario?” Asha asked, her voice neutral.
“He brings flowers,” Tovan said, his face utterly serious. “Sael gets sentimental when he’s decided it’s finally time to kill someone.”
Kieran clapped him on the shoulder. “How deep are you in there, T?”
“I’ve got it under control… barely. But I’ve got it Captain. Don‘t worry. I won’t let my red ledger  stain your deck plates. I swear.”
“Excellent news. Then you’re still alive enough to be useful. Come on. We have errands to run.”
The errands were illusions, each one a carefully placed thread in the web they were weaving. 
A vendor dispute Kieran solved by telling each man a different, flattering lie they were eager to believe. 
A back-room card game he entered with a handful of credits and exited with fewer, but with a name—Rilen Vost—that was worth a fortune. 
A nervous freight clerk with a bad memory, whom Kieran jolted into recollection by vividly reminding him of the time he’d heroically saved a kitten from a malfunctioning vent fan—an event that had never, in fact, occurred. 
At the edge of the main market, a narrow, unassuming alley yawned open, leading into a secondary, smaller world. The light here was cooler, bluer; the voices were fewer and lower; the station’s mechanical hum felt closer to the skin, less masked by artificial life. On a scarred metal door, half-hidden in shadow, a small symbol had been chalked in a sure, confident hand: a diamond, perfectly drawn, bisected by a single, clean crack.
Kieran stopped dead. The symbol was nothing to most—a piece of graffiti, a gang tag. To him, it was a whole language. An Obsidian sigil. Not the club itself, which was deeper, hidden, safer—this was just a whisper of it. A waypoint. A place where a man could go to be seen for what he was, to choose and be chosen, and to speak the truth of it out loud without a single word. Kieran felt his pulse kick up, a frantic drum against his ribs, for reasons that had absolutely nothing to do with smugglers or maps.
Asha watched him. Noted the minute freeze in his posture, the way his eyes locked on the symbol. She didn’t understand its full meaning, but she understood its effect on him. She filed it away. “Later,” she said, and her tone wasn’t unkind, just practical. A thing for another time.
“Later,” Kieran echoed, the word tasting like ash and promise. He hated the way his body responded to the possibility, the pull of it.
They took the long, circular route toward Dock Nine. The corridor here sloped downward, as if the station had been tired, half-asleep, when she’d grown this particular limb. The yellow safety line down the center was older, more faded; the grime seemed to respect it less. Kieran’s feet remembered every slight unevenness in the deck plating, every soft spot, and argued with none of them.
At the service gate, a worker in a stained jumpsuit was engrossed in a baked-in menu on a dusty screen, pretending he hadn’t seen them. Asha didn’t bother with credentials. She simply flashed a smile that contained the distilled essence of five different station regulations and two very specific threats. The worker’s eyes widened almost imperceptibly. He decided the regulations sounded infinitely more pleasant than the alternatives and quickly palmed the lock open.
Inside, the world changed. The sound of the market vanished, replaced by the deeper song of the station’s guts: the thrum of power converters, the hiss of steam, the groan of stressed metal. Kieran’s shoulders loosened without his permission. This was a language he spoke fluently. He crouched by a seam in the wall, running his fingers over it like a safecracker. He didn't look up. “There.”
Asha handed him a micro-tool from her belt without asking what he needed.
He worked quickly, his hands sure. The panel lifted away without a sound. Behind it, stacked with neat, military precision, were more black cases stenciled with that sanctimonious **CHARITY** logo. Far more than should have been there. Kieran didn’t touch them. He simply sat back on his heels and let the reality of it wash over him, his skin crawling with the implication. Whoever was using this route wasn’t just sneaking; they were moving in volume. They didn’t fear discovery; they feared being late.
“That proverb again,” Asha said, her voice low as she eyed the stenciling. “The one your friend Nerral was muttering when he ran last time. It’s their calling card.”
Kieran tasted the words in the air. “*A clean hand leaves a dirty print.*”
Asha gave a single, sharp nod. “Consortium evangelism. They think corruption is a purifying force.”
“I don’t like pious criminals,” Tovan muttered, shifting uncomfortably. “They’re so… judgmental.”
“You’re going to love these guys, then,” Kieran said, a grim smile on his face. “They tithe in knives and call it grace.”
They documented the find in Asha’s way—careful, deniable, precise. Images taken from angles that wouldn’t reveal their location, sensor readings logged to a encrypted partition. When Kieran resealed the panel, he replaced the nearly-invisible filament tripwire with the exact same tension, a hairsbreadth from triggering. It settled back into place like a sleeping serpent.
On the walk back toward the light and noise of the market, Asha fell into step beside Kieran
“You moved in there like you’d never left. Like it was home.”
“In a lotta way, I never did,” Kieran said, the truth slipping out before he could catch it. “I mean Really, what is it all - smoke and mirrors. I’m just good at stacking truths on top of lies on top of rumors, with a sprinkle of threat and a smearing of charm. It’s not talent, it’s discrete with a smile.”
"You want a moment of honesty?" Asha asked, eyes forward. "You scare me a little.”
“Scare you? Common, Major. I’m not sure anything scares you.”
“Well you said yourself, you a professional liar. Brilliant even. I've known plenty of liars—your brand isn't unique. But, what scares me is that you're also a good man; agood man who's been forced to become brilliantly, ruthlessly deceitful just to survive. It’s a special kind of selfishness. It twists you up, presses your soul into these smoldering corners of carelessness and scorn. The kind that scar. The kind that doesn't go away—that eats at everything you thought you were, everything you think you have left."
She paused, letting the words settle like ash.
"The scary thing is, there will come a day, a day when you have to reach deep down into the very depth of your soul in order to survive. When that day comes, you may find that there isn’t anything of you left but lies, and rot and ruin. Men like you tend to stand tall right up until the moment they have to prove it. Then they implode—and take a whole lot of good people with them."
Kieran kept his eyes on the corridor ahead. “You think I’m a good man.” It wasn’t a question.
“I think you’r a very good man, surrounded by a lifetime of bad. But that’s not was Draven sees,” Asha said, her tone pragmatic. “Draven only sees you. Everything else melts away when you walk into a room.”
“That’s not true, Asha. He’s not that naive. He knows what what.” 
“He is when it comes to you. All his bravado and boy banter is just a front. I’ve known Raleth long enough to know when he’s compromised. By you. A man with as much power as Draven… you must be careful Kieran.” Asha pleaded. And as if on cue…
“Captain, Major, we are ready to commence,” ARIA piped in. “I have one Fen Olik entering the market from the District 7 out cropping. 2 o’clock.” 
She flicked a glance back the way they’d come, toward the alley with the chalked symbol. “Whatever you think is waiting behind a door like that… it isn’t a solution. It’s just a different kind of echo.”
 “I know,” Kieran said, and for once, he meant it.
 “Good,” Asha said. “I’d hate to have to respect a man with even worse judgment than my own.”

Kieran almost chocked as her realized that they were both laughing at the same thing. Asha would wait till much later to practically flog herself to death over the incident. But the humor caught them and it was thier’s to share.
 The laughter seemed to lock them in tandem step, one to another, as if pulled taught by the same string. Without thought or acknowledgment, and surely for the first time, they turned to face each other. It happened in silence. It lasted a moment. It took no more effort than either was willing to give. Yet there, in the hush between heartbeats, it was settled: face to face, or back to back, neither would let the other fall.

The Breakpoint [alt with lure to Soren]

Nerrel Chase 1
The man they needed was less a person and more a rumor given a nervous system: Nerral, a corridor courier, a fixer of minor inconveniences, a professional coward when the winds of envy began to blow. He was a creature of pressure, and applying pressure was the only weather system Kieran knew how to command.
They set the board with the care of master tacticians. Asha seeded a false alarm three alleys over, a disruption that smelled authentically like a sudden, aggressive customs sweep. Tovan, with a few keystrokes from a hidden terminal, tripped a camera loop, making a deserted back stairwell flicker and distort as if haunted by a digital ghost. And Kieran, with the casual grace of a man sharing a secret, let it slip in the right ear that someone had seen Nerral near the secondary cargo lifts, looking jumpy, carrying a bag that felt like it was full of untraceable credits.
Nerral, bless his predictable heart, obliged the narrative by immediately fleeing it.
Kieran saw him first—a flash of a distinctive green jacket, the quick, rabbit-like look over the shoulder that screamed guilt to anyone who knew how to look. Kieran didn’t shout. He just moved, a predator entering the chase, a laugh caught in his throat like a held, joyous note.
“Left,” Asha’s voice was a whisper in his comm, calm and directive.
“Low,” Kieran confirmed, and took a narrow access drop two rusted stairs at a time, skidding the landing with a jolt that sang a familiar song of pain through his old ankle injury. Nerral shoved through a curtain of hanging fiber-optic cables; Kieran followed and found himself in a service corridor that had long forgotten its purpose, a forgotten artery in the station’s body.
A heavy door slammed shut ahead. Kieran threw his shoulder into it, once, twice—it groaned in protest, then yielded with a shriek of metal. The space beyond was a narrow maintenance bridge, a mere two-meter span of grated metal thrown over a deep, dark well that dropped away into the station’s unknowable depths. The roar of air processors and the shriek of unseen machinery echoed up from the void like an old, hungry god clearing its throat.
Nerral was halfway across. He didn’t look back. Cowards rarely do; they just run. Kieran didn’t run. He walked onto the bridge as if it were a boulevard, and he pitched his voice to carry, calm, conversational, exactly the way Nerral’s frantic fear wanted to hear it.
“If you stop right now,” he said, his voice cutting through the mechanical din, “I’ll tell you which one of your so-called friends is already counting the reward for selling you out.”

They moved faster, a unit now, a purpose solidified. The station’s ambient hum seemed to rise around them, like a great throat clearing before a pivotal song. Somewhere between the bustling stalls and the silent, secure hatches, Kieran’s body remembered a rhythm he had not learned from running or hiding. It was the rhythm of stepping beside someone, of matching a stride, of moving in concert. He did not think the word *partner*. He simply walked it.
And in the dark, forgotten places of Dock Nine, where a filament had been carefully lifted and laid back with exacting precision, a silent, listening thing woke from its dormant state. And in the deep, electronic silence, it took its first, data-rich breath.

Kieran kept pace, the thrill of pursuit pumping hot in his blood. This was familiar territory—the chase, the hunt, the moment when everything narrowed to predator and prey. Then a sound cut through the alley—low, insistent. The bass of a song that was more pulse than music, vibrating up through the metal flooring and into their bones. Kieran's head turned instinctively, drawn by a pull he couldn't resist.

The Obsidian Room.

Questions were unwelcome. What happened inside stayed inside, protected by codes older and stronger than any law Asha represented.

But it was too late. The door sealed behind him, cutting off the alley's red light and replacing it with something altogether more dangerous.




The calm held as they left the Room. The chalk mark had been wiped away from the inside; the corridor that received them smelled like solvent and cheap food and work. The ordinary world—the one with rules no one says out loud—waited in the posture of a bouncer: arms folded, unimpressed.

Of course it wasn’t. A clean hand leaves a dirty print. Kieran swallowed bile and bent over the holopool. Lanes painted themselves in light. He saw the canyon he’d named, the seam you could ride if you were stubborn or stupid.

Asha already had. The screen filled with his face—sweat-shiny, jaw set, behind him the patient chaos of a civilian bridge. Kieran’s chest hurt to look at him. This was a man who had built something. He was going to tear it down to save strangers.
“Don’t say be careful,” Jalen warned, reading Kieran like always. “Say go or say stop.”
“Stop,” Draven said, because he was the one who had to.
“Go,” Kieran said, because he was the one who would.
Jalen’s eyes flickered. Hurt and humor and history in that single blink. “That tracks.” He flashed a grin that had once made Kieran do stupid, holy things. “Tell him you were outvoted, Commander.”
“I never am,” Draven said, deadpan.
Jalen laughed. “Then you’ve never met my daughter.” The joke landed, stayed. “We’ll punch the seam. Buy you a minute. Use it.”
The minute he was offering felt like taking a lung out of his body to give to a drowning man. Kieran’s mouth went dry. “Jalen—”
“Later,” Jalen said gently. “If later exists.”
The link died. The room breathed in and held it.
“Positions,” Draven said.
Asha moved like a blade. Tovan swore at the console until it obeyed out of respect. Kieran took a chair he had no right to love and settled a hand on controls that sang different in his palm than they had yesterday. He felt the mark at his throat pulse with his heart and, instead of shame, he felt the jolt of a line thrown and caught.
“On my count,” Draven said quietly, so only Kieran heard. “Three. Two. One.”
Out in the black, a relief ship became a line of fire. It threaded the seam Kieran had drawn with his mouth a lifetime ago and left a wake like a wound. The grid hiccupped—one breath. Two. Now.
“Now,” Draven said aloud.
Kieran dove. The Aegis answered like a body taught a new rhythm and unwilling to fall back to the old one. Systems screamed, calmed, screamed again. Asha’s voice rode the noise like a rider who refuses to be thrown. Tovan begged, threatened, flattered the station’s temper until it let them pass.
They did what could be done with the minute Jalen gave them. It wasn’t enough. It never would be. It was everything. It always is.
After, when the worst was delayed and the day had been mortgaged for an hour more of breath, Kieran stood in the quiet by the viewport and watched the seam heal. He pressed his fingers to the glass, the way he had to Jalen’s name earlier, the way he had to Draven’s chest in a room where the rules made men honest.
Draven came and stood beside him. Asha stood on his other side. Family, ridiculous and real.
“This changes everything,” Kieran said, not asking.
“Yes,” Draven said.
“It doesn’t fix a thing,” Asha added, because she was incapable of being anyone but herself.
“No,” Kieran agreed.
He turned to them both. He didn’t say we. He didn’t have to. The station said it with the hum in her bones. The mark at his throat said it each time his heart threw its weight against his ribs. The key in his pocket said it with the soft weight of a promise he’d always been afraid to make.
“Then we ruin it well,” Kieran said, and, for the first time since he was a boy hiding under a stairwell with a stolen bottle and a borrowed future, he meant it as a vow.

Frost clung to every surface—girders, gantries, the skeletal remains of cargo drones frozen mid-task. The cold was so dense, so total that it acted like a pressure in the air that squeezed thought into whisper and breath into fog. Through the viewport, Dock 9 yawned open. The wreck of the freighter Solace hung there, impaled on its own docking spine—its hull pocked with coolant burns, cargo bay ruptured. The last isotope delivery. Quarantined six months, now their ticking time bomb. Asha pulled up the atmospheric analysis and swished the data to the holo display, its glowing blue-green flickering across the cockpit glass. “Gravimetric distortion detected around the wreck. Residual magnetic fields from the original containment burst.” Kieran strapped in at the auxiliary console, eyes scanning the telemetry. “We’re going to have to nudge her, not tow her. The clamps’ll shear if we pull too hard.” “Gentle as hell,” Draven said, echoing the old engineer’s mantra. “Lock the grapplers.” The sound was surgical: the deep thump of mag-tethers engaging, followed by a cascade of metallic pings reverberating through the Aegis’s frame. “Grapplers locked. Hull contact at four points,” Asha said. “We’ve got her.” Outside, flakes of frozen vapor drifted like falling ash. The Aegis’s maneuvering lights painted the wreckage in shifting halos of blue and red—an accidental funeral rite. Kieran’s hand lingered on the control surface, eyes fixed on the freighter. He patted the control board, a silent apology. Sorry I got us into this, girl! I won’t let you end up like that one. The ship vibrated softly as the engines flared to life. The wreck began to move, a slow, reluctant thing dragged out of its tomb. “ARIA,” Draven said. “Start timer.” “Countdown to critical temperature: seven minutes, forty-two seconds,” the AI replied. The timer pulsed in the corner of the viewport—blue, then yellow. Beyond the freighter, the void stretched black and endless. Erevos hung in the distance like a dying lantern. Kieran muttered, “Seven minutes to save a station. You ever get tired of living like this?” Draven glanced at him, the faintest trace of a smile ghosting across his face. “Every day.”
Scene: Preparing the Tow The Aegis hung motionless for a heartbeat, tethered to the corpse of the Solace like a surgeon about to make the first incision. “Mag-tethers holding,” Asha said. “No slippage. She’s heavier than she looks.” Kieran’s gloved hands hovered over the console. His breath fogged the inside of his visor as he flicked through the telemetry. “That’s because her cargo bay’s still full of gel packs. Frozen isotopes, sealed in place. She’s like a dead whale full of lightning.” “Keep poetry off the comms,” Draven muttered. Kieran gave a dry grin. “Aye, Captain. Removing metaphors. Engaging cables.” The Aegis’s hull shuddered as the secondary mag-lines fired. On the external cameras, they coiled across the void—silver snakes stitching ship to ship. The whole bay vibrated under the hum of electromagnetism. ARIA’s voice was steady, serene. “Magnetic tether integrity: stable. Estimated tow vector clear within six degrees of predicted arc.” Draven exhaled. “Alright. Let’s move her.” Kieran keyed in the tow sequence. The mag-tethers began to retract, pulling the Solace from her cradle with agonizing slowness. Through the viewport, the freighter’s hull scraped against the damaged docking spine, sending ripples of light through the frost like phosphorescent veins. The ship wailed—a groan of tortured metal echoing through the comms. “Contact at two points,” Asha said. “We’re moving.” “Steady,” Draven ordered. The freighter drifted out into open bay space, trailing vapor and dust. Kieran muttered, “Looks like she doesn’t want to leave.” Draven’s reply was low, half to himself. “No one ever does.” A faint shudder rippled through the Aegis’s deck. Not from the engines. A deeper vibration, more like a pulse traveling through the structure of the station itself. ARIA’s tone changed. “Commander, new data: unexplained EM activity outside the dock perimeter. Faint, distant… possibly interference.” Draven looked up, uneasy. “Interference from what?” ARIA paused. “Unknown source. Pattern irregular.” For a moment, no one spoke. Only the hum of the mag-tethers and the soft ticking of the hull as the temperature climbed. Kieran broke the silence. “Could be a sensor echo from the isotope disturbance thaw.” Asha replied almost immediately, as if she’d been waiting for the suggestion. “Negative. Signal origin is external.” Draven’s gaze drifted to the viewport—past the wreck, past the frost-glazed walls, into the deeper dark beyond Dock 9. “I’m starting a wideband scan,” Asha announced. “Low frequency.” “Acknowledged,” Draven replied. The lights dimmed as the scanners drew power. Static whispered through the comms—like a voice trying to form a word. From below in engineering, Tovan jumped on the comms. “Anyone else hear that up there?” Kieran’s hand froze over the console. “Yeah. Like someone knocking… from outside.” The static cut out. ARIA’s voice returned, perfectly calm. “No external vessels detected. All readings nominal.” Draven glanced at her console display—the data feed scrolling steady and clean. He didn’t believe a word of it.
Scene: Ghost Ship Arrival The Aegis drifted free of the dock mouth, the wreckage of the Solace in tow. Beyond the rim of Erevos, the void spread in every direction—black on black, punctured only by the station’s trembling beacon lights and the occasional shimmer of distant debris. “Tow line stable,” Asha said. “Trajectory aligned with vector nine-eight-seven. We’re in the clear zone.” Draven leaned back in his seat, exhaling through his nose. “Let’s get her clear, slow and clean. No—” Suddenly every readout panel, every sensor alarm on the bridge deck jumped to life. Asha screamed out the report. “Long-range contact detected.” Everyone froze. “Define contact,” Draven said, voice suddenly flat. “Unregistered vessel. Bearing zero-one-five, negative pitch five. No transponder. No flight path authorization.” Draven frowned. “Could be a stray hauler trying to get comm access. There’s been grid interference since—” “Negative,” Asha said. “Vessel trajectory consistent with an attack vector.” The word attack sucked the air from the room. Kieran straightened, checking his sensors. “That can’t be right. No one’s insane enough to fire on Erevos this close in.” Draven snapped, “Shields up. Red protocol.” The Aegis’s interior lights bled into crimson. The hull hum deepened as the defensive field charged, casting rippling shadows across their faces. “Target identification?” Draven asked. ARIA’s tone didn’t change, but something in it sounded hesitant. “No optical return. No transponder. EM profile inconsistent with known vessel classes. Possible cloaking interference.” “Meaning?” Asha asked. “Meaning,” Kieran said grimly, “we’ve got a ghost.” And then—light. A pulse of it, ultraviolet and sharp, cutting across the void in a horizontal slash. It hit the Aegis’s forward shield, scattering blue fire. The ship lurched sideways, the tether lines stretching taut as the Solace swung like a pendulum. “Shields holding,” Asha barked. “But they just took out half our lateral thrusters!” Through the viewport, the void flickered—and something shimmered into being. Not a ship, not exactly. More like a reflection of one. Its edges seemed to exist in disagreement with space itself, geometry folding inward, lights moving in impossible symmetry. The hull was jet-black, but the stars behind it bent and wavered like heat haze. ARIA whispered, “Vessel decloaking. Energy signature… anomalous. Hull composition unknown.” “Unknown?” Draven snapped. “It’s shooting at us—classify it as hostile!” The thing fired again. Three bolts of green-white plasma streaked toward them, but instead of impact, the Aegis’s sensors went wild—coordinates flipping, distance data glitching. The ship wasn’t firing at them. It was firing through them. Kieran’s jaw tightened. “It’s phasing.” “Meaning?” “Meaning it’s not entirely here.” The second salvo caught one of the drifting freighters in the approach lane. The blast lit the black like sunrise. The freighter folded in on itself, hull breaking apart silently, bits of burning alloy tumbling end over end. Draven clenched his fists. “They’re drawing us out. Aegis, full thrust, lateral spread—keep the Solace between us and that ship!” The engines flared, pulling the wreck sideways. The mag-tethers whined as the freighter swung across their line of sight, a massive shield of dead metal. “Dock command, this is Aegis,” Draven called into comms. “We have a hostile vessel engaging. Civilians in approach lanes—redirect immediately!” No response. Just static. ARIA’s voice returned, sharper now. “Comms interference increasing. Source: the hostile vessel. Signal encryption unknown. Attempts to decode producing recursive—” Her voice glitched, repeating itself in fragments: “—source—source—source—” before cutting off entirely. The ghost ship’s outline rippled again—gone one second, reappearing closer the next. Asha whispered, “How the hell is it that close? We’d have seen a burn—” “It doesn’t burn,” Kieran said. “It folds.” The next volley hit the Solace. Not a direct strike, just a glancing shot along her ruined hull. The frozen isotope gel within flickered blue—like something waking up. “Temperature spike!” ARIA’s voice snapped back online. “Isotope core destabilizing!” Draven slammed his palm against the console. “We’re done playing defense. Kieran—get me a way out before that thing decides to shoot the bomb we’re towing!” Kieran’s eyes flicked across the tactical screen, fingers moving faster than thought. “Working on it. But if that thing’s using phased targeting, we’re basically throwing rocks at a ghost.” Asha barked out a bitter laugh. “Then let’s make sure we throw fast.” The ghost ship circled, its silhouette phasing in and out of visibility like a nightmare half-remembered. Every few seconds, the sensors registered it in a slightly different position—as if space couldn’t quite decide where to keep it. Draven’s voice was steady now, the calm born of fury. “We’re still tethered. We can’t fight while we’re chained to that wreck. Get ready to cut lines.” Kieran hesitated. “We cut, and the Solace drifts straight into Erevos. The isotope blows, we take the station with us.” Draven met his gaze. “Then find me an option that doesn’t end with ten thousand dead.” Outside, the ghost ship’s weapons charged again—faint green fire building along its impossible hull. The countdown from ARIA began in a whisper. “Isotope critical threshold in three minutes.”
Scene: Tethered Combat The ghost ship’s plasma fire ripped past the Aegis, each bolt warping light like heat over a furnace. The tethers groaned under stress, the wreck of the Solace dragging them sideways. The mag-lines glowed cherry red, superheated against the invisible gravity flux the ghost ship’s presence stirred up. Asha cursed. “We’re tethered to a bomb and getting shot at by a ghost. Anyone else having a bad day?” Kieran’s hands moved in a blur over the console. “Bad? Try recursive. I’m watching our sensor feed rewrite its own history. The thing’s making spacetime forget we exist.” Draven’s voice cut through, steady and brutal. “Focus. Civilians are still in the approach lanes. If that ship turns its guns on them, Erevos burns. We hold this line.” “Hold?” Asha shot back. “We’re chained to a 60,000-ton fuel rod.” ARIA’s tone was flat, clinical. “Warning: Isotope gel core temperature now approaching 6 Kelvin. Breach threshold at ten. Current stability: fragile.” “Yeah, we noticed,” Kieran muttered. He stared at the readouts, brain turning the chaos into geometry. “Okay. Okay, what if we don’t hold? What if we throw?” Draven’s gaze snapped toward him. “Explain.” “The Solace has enough mass to make a slingshot maneuver possible. If we can coordinate tractor vectors, we could launch her on a ballistic arc—send her clear of Erevos, and maybe right through that ship’s cloaking field on the way out.” Asha frowned. “That’s not a tow, that’s a goddamn railgun shot.” Kieran grinned despite himself. “Yeah. And we’re the barrel.” Draven shook his head. “We don’t have the thrust.” “Not alone,” Kieran said, and reached for the comms. “But we’re not alone.” He flipped to an encrypted frequency. Static, then a familiar voice broke through—dry, cocky, tired. “…Kieran? Tell me you’re not doing what I think you’re doing.” Kieran’s smirk widened. “Hey, Jalen. Good to hear your charming paranoia. You still parked up in the Argyle?” A low whistle on the line. “Dock 11, upper Crescent. I can see your mess from here. Tell me you’re not tethered to that isotope freighter.” “Then I won’t tell you,” Kieran said. “You up for some synchronized idiocy?” Jalen laughed—short, disbelieving. “The Mahlstarohm? Are you serious? That move killed three test crews.” Draven cut in. “Make it four if you don’t move your ass. We’ve got civilians in the blast path.” There was a long pause. Then the sound of engines spooling up through the comm feed. “Argyle to Aegis,” Jalen said, voice firm now. “Two cruisers, one suicidal idea. Let’s dance.” Asha grinned despite the sweat on her brow. “You two rehearsed this move drunk, didn’t you?” “Multiple times,” Kieran said. “And once sober, which was the mistake.” “Focus!” Draven barked. The ghost ship fired again. The blast struck the freighter’s hull—glancing, but enough to send power feedback through the tethers. Sparks rained from the overhead conduits. ARIA’s voice cut through the chaos. “Warning: tractor systems at eighty percent load. Structural stress approaching catastrophic.” “Then we’d better do it fast,” Kieran said. “Jalen, align your tractor beam with my vector. You’re the upper pivot, I’m the pull. On my mark, full burn for three-point-two seconds. You’ll slingshot the freighter along the curve and we’ll release on my countdown.” “Copy that,” Jalen said. “Just like the old racing circuits.” Kieran smiled faintly. “Except this time, if we miss, we vaporize a city.” “Details.” The ghost ship’s silhouette flickered across the radar, shifting closer again. It began firing toward the civilian freighters, trying to bait the Aegis into breaking formation. Draven’s knuckles went white. “Do it.”
Scene: The Slingshot Burn ARIA’s voice threaded through the cabin, calm and absolute. “Combined tow mass: 64,300 metric tons. Required ejection velocity: station escape speed plus fifty-eight percent to clear gravitational interference.” Kieran rubbed the back of his neck. “Ten-point-four klicks a second. We’re dragging a frozen nuke.” “Correction,” ARIA said. “We are accelerating one.” He shot a grin toward Asha. “Comforting as always.” Then, into comms: “Argyle, align to my vector. We’ll build her up slow and spin off the fulcrum at twenty-seven seconds.” Draven stood behind his chair, hands clasped. “You’ve got the conn, Kieran. Make it count.” The Aegis groaned as he fed throttle into the engines. The hull’s vibration deepened, the tractor beams pulling the Solace forward in slow, reluctant increments. Above them, the Argyle flared to life, her twin drive cones carving a blue arc in the dark. “Velocity three thousand and climbing,” Asha called. “Containment temp eight-point-nine Kelvin.” ARIA’s tone was metronomic. “Fulcrum in twenty-two seconds.” The ghost ship had stopped firing, sliding somewhere off the right flank. Kieran could feel it out there—a ripple in the dark. “Fifteen seconds,” ARIA said. Jalen’s voice cracked through the comm. “Engines at eighty percent. Tractor lock’s steady. You sure about this math, Kieran?” “Math’s fine,” Kieran said. “Timing’s the killer.” “Good,” Jalen answered. “Wouldn’t want it too easy.” “Ten seconds. Prepare polarity inversion,” ARIA said. Kieran braced. “On my mark. Three… two… flip it.” Both cruisers inverted their tractor polarity. The shock hit like a punch. The Solace jolted forward, frost boiling off in silver ribbons. Every readout flashed red for a heartbeat, then stabilized. “Trajectory stable,” ARIA reported. “Velocity approaching target. We require additional thrust of point-five-eight percent.” “Argyle, burn now,” Kieran ordered. The engines screamed; the stars outside blurred. The Solace surged forward, a streak of light on the edge of control. ARIA counted: “Fulcrum passed. Tractor reversal required in three… two…” They flipped the beams again, from push to pull—helping sling the freighter out past the dock mouth. Momentum built, a perfect parabola. And then the ghost ship fired. Its plasma cut through the dark in a clean green line, striking the Solace along her ruptured cargo bay. “Direct hit!” Asha shouted. “Core breach warning!” “Reverse polarity, now!” Kieran yelled. “Catastrophic feedback detected,” ARIA intoned, almost mournful. The consoles exploded in light. Energy raced up both tractor beams, crawling back toward the cruisers faster than thought. For an instant the Solace was pure radiance—every seam venting fire. Then she was gone. The explosion flared without sound, a nova that erased shadow. The shockwave hit a civilian freighter dead-on, tearing it apart in a silent blossom of wreckage. The Argyle vanished behind the glare. ARIA’s voice cut through the roar. “Engine three offline. Shield grid collapsed. Structural fractures along dorsal seam.” Kieran slammed his hand on the thruster controls, but the ship responded sluggishly, wounded. “Argyle, talk to me!” A hiss of static, then Jalen’s voice: “Still here. Power’s gone, hull’s bleeding. But alive.” Asha’s hands shook over her console. “We lost half the lane. God, the civilians—” Kieran stared at the dying light. “We did everything right. The math was perfect.” Draven’s reply was quiet. “The math doesn’t care who dies.” ARIA broke in, detached as ever. “Radiation levels stabilizing. Probability of crew survival: seventy-two percent. Civilian losses: extensive.” The Aegis drifted, crippled. Across the void, the Argyletumbled slowly, one side glowing red from vented heat. Then ARIA whispered, “New contact. Hostile vessel reacquiring. It has… re-cohered.” Through the haze, the ghost ship shimmered back into partial form—scarred, but unmistakably alive. Kieran’s jaw tightened. “We hurt it. Didn’t kill it.” Draven’s hand landed on his shoulder. “Then stop breathing. Let it think we’re gone.” “ARIA,” Kieran said softly, “kill all systems. No lights, no noise.” Power faded. The Aegis went dark, sliding into the graveyard of drifting debris. Outside, fragments of metal and ice turned slowly, reflecting cold starlight. In the silence, the ghost ship prowled past, scanning, searching the dead. Kieran whispered, “We’re ghosts now.”
Reference Materials
[SUGGESTION: The following two sections appear to be planning documents. The “Beat Outline” is a structured plan for the scenes above. The “Narrative Summary” is a condensed, earlier draft of the entire sequence. They are included here for reference.]
Dock 9 Salvage / Ghost Ship Attack – Beat Outline (Refined) Set-Up: Strained Systems Erevos Station trembles under failing containment. The cooling fields are collapsing, the power grid hiccups like a dying heart. *  Cooling Failure: Power blackouts ripple through the lower bays as the Hyperphoton Isotope (HPI) gel pack is still only at 6,000 K—not yet safe for photonic mesh integration. *  ARIA’s Estimate: 72 hours until full readiness. But in 72 hours, Dock 9’s isolation grid will collapse. *  Veyr’s Proposal: Inject early with the cold isotopes—risky but maybe survivable. *  ARIA’s Odds: 65% destabilization risk versus 100% failure in waiting. They roll the dice. *  Immediate Catastrophe: The emergency injection overloads the already weakened grid. A cascade failure darkens Crescent Bays 4–9 and 14–22. Docking clamps fail. Freighters begin drifting. The whole underbelly of Erevos becomes a graveyard ballet of slow-spinning ships. *  Draven’s Decision: Authorize a retrieval—haul the quarantined freighter (last isotope shipment, six months ago) out before it melts down inside the dock. *  Crew Selection: Kieran volunteers—half guilt, half need for redemption. Draven joins as mission lead. Commander Veyr stays on Erevos for grid ops. Asha and Tovan fill out the Aegis crew. Beat 1 – Entering Dock 9 The Aegis detaches and drifts through the frost-lit ruin of Dock 9. Temperature rising. Anything above 4 K is critical. Frost crystals shimmer like trapped stars. *  ARIA: “Hull temperature 3.2 Kelvin. Isotope stable—for eight minutes.” * The Aegis eases under Dock 24’s collapsed framework, locking grapplers onto the freighter’s hull. Time is melting. Beat 2 – Preparing the Tow Mag-tethers bite into the wreck. Kieran spots odd relay boxes on the walls—installed but unwired, like decoys. *  ARIA: “Anomalous field generators detected. Function: unknown.” * A quiet red herring. For now. Beat 3 – (Omitted – Merges with Next)Beat 4 – The Ghost Ship Appears Long-range sensors ping something impossible. *  Draven: “Unidentified vessel on approach—no transponder.” * The “ghost” decloaks, firing warning bursts across their bow. Its weapons glow out of phase—as if they’re firing from a slightly different timeline. Beat 5 – Tethered Combat The Aegis is still tethered to the freighter. One wrong hit and the isotope cargo detonates. Civilian freighters are trapped in the approach lanes. The ghost ship begins firing near them to lure the Aegis away. *  Kieran’s Plan: Use the wreck as a projectile—slingshot it out of Dock 9 at escape velocity, aimed for open void (and maybe right through the ghost ship). But it needs two Star Cruiser–class vessels for the thrust pattern. Enter Jalen—old partner, flying the Argyle, two decks up. * Their comms crackle alive at the same moment—each having reached the same suicidal idea. Beat 6 – The Double Ship ManeuverThe Argyle positions above Dock 9. Aegis and Argyle synchronize tractor beams—one pulls, one pushes, a maneuver so delicate a single millisecond of lag could rupture both. Civilians clear out; Erevos’s traffic control screams chaos into the void. The ghost ship rains down plasma arcs as they execute the Mahlstarohm, the forbidden double-cruiser slingshot. Beat 7 – The Explosive Escape The launch sequence ignites. The wreck rockets free—then takes a direct hit before it clears the lane. Explosion blossoms like a newborn star. The tractors are still engaged—energy feedback slams both ships. *  Argyle: hull breaches, propulsion gone. *  Aegis: two engines dead, power flickering, but crew alive. * The ghost ship vanishes in the blinding flare. For a heartbeat, they think they’ve won. Then it reappears—decloaking amid the wreckage. Coming for survivors. They dive toward the debris field, cold and silent, cutting power. Erevos watches as the Aegis goes dark among the floating graves. Notes for Next Scene Transition This ending perfectly sets up a “ghost among ghosts” segment—quiet survival horror, playing with radar silence, drifting bodies, and sensor phantoms. It’s a tonal pivot opportunity: from explosive action to eerie aftermath.
Narrative Summary (Condensed Draft) When the obsidian room flickered back to normal, Draven’s pupils were still pinpricks from the blackout. Kieran had already opened the door, the corridor outside wavering with emergency strobes and the smell of melting polymers. “We need to go,” Kieran said, tugging Draven by the sleeve. They made it through the club’s service hatch just as the main lights came back up. Draven didn’t slow, already raising his sleeve comm. “Report.” Ash’s voice, compressed and staticky, piped in: “Same as before, Commander. Packs haven’t had injections in six months. The new isotope shipment is still in cold transit, ETA three days to thaw.” Aria, their systems analyst, broke in with a projection: “If we force the injection before the isotope’s fully stabilized, we’ve got a sixty-five percent chance of success. That’s if the core temperature stays under 10,000 K.” Kieran checked the nearest display. “Current temp?” “Ticking up from 5 K to 6 K,” Aria replied. Draven grimaced. “And the risk?” Aria hesitated. “Eighty-five percent chance the grid isolating Dock Nine fails in the next seventy-two hours.” Dock Nine. The old delivery, still in quarantine after the crash. If the core warmed even a fraction, every disaster model went red. Kieran’s jaw set. “We have to try the forced injection.” Draven nodded. “Do it.” He watched the wall display as Ash initiated the sequence. For a moment, the numbers stabilized. Then everything went black. Sirens screamed as power failed in a wave across the lower crescent—docks one through nine, then ten through twenty-two. The deck thrummed under their boots as containment shields dropped, and in the distance, the bulkhead groaned. Aria’s voice was thin and urgent. “Dock Nine’s heating up. The ship’s going to breach.” Draven inhaled. “We tow it out. Now.” Kieran was already moving, sprinting for the maglev. “Aegis is prepped. We’ll get there first.” By the time Draven and Kieran reached the Aegis, other pilots were already scrambling. “Get the ships out of seven, eight, and ten through fourteen. Clear a path,” Draven barked. The comms crackled with frantic affirmatives. “Commander,” Ash cut in, “four civilian convoys are inbound, already in the lanes.” Draven swore. “Prioritize evacuation. But keep them moving, or they’ll be sitting ducks.” Aegis dropped into the bay with a bone-rattling thud. The old wreck in Dock Nine was already sweating vapor, its hull warping under thermal stress. Draven eyed the magnetic clamps. “On my mark.” As the Aegis locked onto the derelict, the ghost ship materialized from the black, hull matte and silent. It opened fire, targeting the civilian transports and the retreating ships alike. “Veer, take the helm,” Draven shouted. “Kieran, with me.” They slid into their harnesses as the Aegis shuddered under direct hits. Kieran’s hands danced over the controls, and for a moment, Draven saw the boy he’d grown up with—reckless, brilliant, unbreakable. “We’re not going to outrun it,” Kieran said. “No. But we can outthink it.” Draven’s eyes flicked to the sensor readouts. “We need a second Star Cruiser. Argyle’s up on twenty-six.” The plan was unspoken but clear: slingshot the burning wreck, using both ships to hurl it at escape velocity—and, if possible, straight at the ghost ship. Aegis and Argyle locked in tandem, hulls groaning as they strained the tow cables. The ghost ship closed in, its cannons raking the civilian lanes. Jalen’s voice came over the comm, tight with adrenaline. “Ready on your mark.” Draven counted down. “Three. Two. One.” Kieran hit the burn. Both ships surged forward, the wreck between them, plasma jets screaming. The ghost ship loomed, firing point-blank. The derelict’s hull ruptured, and for a blinding second, the world went white. When Draven’s vision cleared, the comms were a chaos of voices. One of the convoys was gone, atomized in the blast. The Aegis had lost two engines; the Argyle drifted, venting atmosphere, but Jalen’s voice cut through the static: “We’re alive. Barely.” Kieran coughed, blood in his teeth. “Told you it would work.” Draven nodded, slumping in his harness. “Let’s get the survivors home.” The Aegis limped back toward the graveyard outpost, battered but intact. The ghost ship was gone. For now.


8 Ghostfire
The Aegis limped on one good engine and a prayer. Hull temperature climbed; coolant bled in glittering ribbons that crystallized and drifted like a comet’s tail. The tracer’s whisper jittered at Kieran’s wrist—then sharpened, a knife pulled clean.
“They’re back,” he said, voice raw from smoke.
Three signatures bloomed at once. The ghost ship in the lead—sleek, predatory, cloak shivering like heat over stone—escorted by two blades of light that burned hard and fast.
“Bow shields at twenty-eight,” Asha snapped. “Port emitters are—”
“Gone,” Tovan shouted from engineering, voice too bright with terror. “Port emitters are a memory I’m repressing!”
“Hard starboard,” Draven ordered. Calm. Unarguable.
Kieran threw the Aegis into the turn. The nearest escort’s fire chewed space where they’d just been. Concussive force slammed them sideways; a panel blew in the ceiling with a scream of rivets.
“Secondary fires on Deck Three!” Tovan coughed. “I need—ah, hell—containment foam is foaming where it shouldn’t!”
“Route emergency power,” Asha barked. “All nonessential—”
“Everything is essential when we’re dying,” Kieran hissed, and fed the last intact thruster a sliver more than wise. The ship protested, metal singing high and ugly, but obeyed.
The ghost ship didn’t harry. It herded. A subtle nudge of fire that pushed them toward the maintenance ring’s graveyard: bent trusses, rusted girders, mines that had slept and dreamed of teeth. The escorts fanned wide, sealing exits they hadn’t yet tried.
“This is deliberate,” Draven said, eyes like cut steel. “They want prisoners.”
Kieran laughed once—sharp, humorless. “They can want.”
They plunged into the graveyard. It swallowed them whole. Jagged hulls loomed like broken cathedral ribs. Mines blinked to life in cautious sequence, uncertain, then hungry.
“Collision warning,” the ship offered, politely doomed.
Kieran threaded a gap that shouldn’t have been a gap. The escort followed—and clipped the edge. The blast was blinding. Shockwave twisted the Aegis like a tin toy. Kieran’s forehead met the console. Warmth slid down his temple; the world doubled and tried to spin away.
“Stay with me, Vale,” Draven said—low, near, a hand clamped hard on Kieran’s shoulder.
“I’m here,” Kieran said, and made it true. Shake it off. Just a good slice.
“Micro-breach on Deck Two,” Asha called. “Seal that bulkhead. Tovan!”
“On it! Also on fire! Briefly! It’s out now! We are a functionally stylish inferno!”
The ghost ship slid into view between bones of ships, cloak peeled back for the kill. The remaining escort darted close on their six, biting, biting, biting—
“Give me something,” Draven said, almost a prayer.
Kieran’s mind threw cards: jettison, flare, feint, beg. He chose a card no sane man loved.
“Graveyard chain,” he said. “We make it sing.”
“Quarters are way too close,” Asha snapped. “Light that thing up, and we go up with it.”
“Not if we time it juuuust so,” Kieran said, pulse too fast. “We light one mine early, ride the shockwave through the collapsing truss work, sling around the battleship’s spine and let the wreckage eat our escort. The ghost ship’s smart; it’ll back off. Maybe.”
“Maybe?” Asha demanded.
Draven’s hand tightened. “Make me believe in maybe.”
“Trust me,” Kieran said, and shoved the Aegis into a dive so ugly it felt like falling. Mines woke and blinked, confused. Kieran lined their tail with a dense cluster and screamed into comms, “Tovan—overcharge the starboard reactor feed. Four seconds and dump it aft on my mark.”
“That is not science,” Tovan said, horrified.
“Nope. It’s art,” Kieran said. “On me…. Mark.”
Tovan screamed back—wordless, obedient—and the Aegis exhaled a gout of plasma into the minefield.
The first mine tripped. The second sang. The third howled. Fire leapt mine to mine, a chain of white suns. Shockwaves collided, interference patterns chewing wrecks into shrapnel. The escort behind them vanished in a flower of light.
“Brace,” Draven said, not loud, not panicked—command distilled.
The shockfront punched the Aegis. For one long, liquid moment, they were weightless, lifted, flung—the hull creaked like an old bone. Kieran yanked the nose a hair to port, found the gap he had no right to find, and the Aegis slid through collapsing trusswork as steel screamed and bent around them like a closing jaw.
Alarms turned to a single continuous wail. The lights flickered, died, returned as ghosts.
They burst free into a pocket of relative emptiness—if anything in this graveyard could be called that—and Kieran used the momentum to sling them around the spine of a dead battleship. The wreck’s thermal shadow wrapped them in cold.
“Kill everything,” he panted. “Hot, loud, bright—we’re a flare in the dark. Make us a stone.”
Asha’s fingers flew over the panels like a virtuoso at the keys; systems fell silent, one by one, until the Aegis was a hunk of cooling metal and the faintest whisper of life support. Tovan’s cursing cut off mid-oath as he obeyed.
Darkness took them. The only sound left was the soft wheeze of the ship catching her breath.
“Hold,” Draven murmured. “No one move.”
They held. Kieran tasted copper and burned insulation. Felt his own pulse in his wrists. Watched, through the cracked canopy, as the ghost ship drifted along the far side of the battleship spine—searching. Patient. A shark’s shadow in an ink-black ocean.
It paused. The cloak shivered. For five eternal seconds she leaned, stared, Kieran was sure they were made. Holding, stretching, suspended between next and no more.
Then in a blink that brought back breath, The ghost ship moved on.
They weren’t free yet. Draven gestured to hold further. Not a single bubble of air could move between them. 30 more seconds, sixty…
“Time.” Asha whispered.
“Now,” Draven said, and only then did anyone let a breath go.
The Aegis still didn’t move. They stayed colder than the dark, listening to the predator prowl until it slid away—bored or called elsewhere.
Kieran sagged back. The world fuzzed at the edges.
Draven’s hand was suddenly at the back of his neck again, grounding him. “Don’t pass out,” he said.
Kieran smiled, blood in his teeth. “Only if you ask nicely.”
Then the black came, soft as velvet, and took him.
—
He woke to pain and the taste of oxygen concentrate, an emergency mask pressed to his face. The cockpit was a ruin of blown panels and hanging wires, but it was there. Draven’s shadow loomed over him, silver eyes dim with exhaustion and something softer.
“We’re hidden,” Draven said. “For now.”
Kieran blinked, tried to sit. The universe objected. Draven’s hand settled, gentle iron. “Later.”
Kieran closed his eyes. “That was a miracle.”
Draven’s thumb brushed once—barely a touch. “That was you.”
“Same difference,” Kieran mumbled, and let the dark take him again—this time, by choice.




9 Ashes and Embers
Silence held for a long time after the predator passed. Not peace—never that—but the wary quiet of an animal with its belly to the ground, listening.
Life on the Aegis returned like dawn on a world with a broken rotation: slowly, unevenly, and with a few false starts. Lights came back in bands; air grew sweeter a breath at a time.
Tovan was the first voice to surface, ragged over the intercom. “Engineering to anyone with hands. If you can’t tell a spanner from a spoon, bring both.”
Asha’s reply floated up from the black like a buoy. “Copy. On my way.”
Kieran crawled toward waking. The mask fed him. His head throbbed a double beat that matched the pulse in his teeth. When he managed to stand, the cockpit swam and steadied. Draven didn’t say be careful; he offered an arm, and Kieran took it like a lifeline that neither of them would name.
They moved through the ship together, past compartments strewn with the detritus of survival—fire foam residue in drifting drifts, cracked lights, a trail of blood that had the decency to be Kieran’s.
In engineering, heat pressed like a palm. Tovan crouched among open panels, hair singed, eyebrows non-existent and not yet mourned. He looked up, face dirty and luminous with stubborn joy.
“We lived,” he announced, as if daring anyone to argue.
“Barely,” Asha said, sleeves shoved to elbows, hands steady as she reconnected a snake’s wedding of cables. Sweat ran in a fine line down her temple; she ignored it.
Kieran leaned against a rail and watched them breathe—the crew knitting itself back together fiber by fiber. The ache behind his ribs expanded, not pain so much as pressure. Gratitude, maybe. Or the terrifying urge to sit down and tell everyone he’d ever cared for that he was sorry he’d been late.
Tovan tossed a half-melted tool aside and popped open a storage locker with a flourish. “First aid, second aid, and third-degree coffee,” he declared. He held up a dented kettle like a trophy.
“That kettle died six months ago,” Asha said dryly.
“It retired,” Tovan said. “It will guest star in tonight’s production of ‘We Keep Breathing.’” He set it on a portable coil and waited for the sputtering thing to decide it still had meaning.
They drifted toward the galley in small increments, as much pulled by the idea of a table as by hunger. Kieran sat gingerly. Asha took the bench like a soldier who had counted her wounds and found them acceptable. Tovan collapsed opposite them with the theatrical sigh of a man who would live forever just to spite statistics.
Draven arrived last, after making a slow, quiet circuit of the hull the way a captain reads a ship by touch. He took a seat without ceremony, and the room accepted him.
For a while, they ate in the way people who have almost died must: whatever was hot, whatever was sweet, whatever quieted the shaking in their hands. The coffee was criminal. It was also perfect.
Conversation resurfaced in careful pieces.
Tovan went first, because Tovan always did. “When I was a kid,” he said, staring into steam, “my mother ran a salvage tug out past the Selene wrecks. Everything we owned smelled like burned insulation and sugar. She kept candy in the fuse box so I’d check the lines without her having to nag. When things went bad, she’d make me sit on the deck and sort jellybeans by color. ‘Order,’ she’d say, ‘is what we make when it feels like the universe has forgotten to visit for a while.’”
He laughed, scrubbed his sleeve over his eyes, pretended it was sweat. “Saved my life more than once. Today I sorted washers by size when the coolant lines blew. Felt like I was ten, sticky fingers and all. Though I can confidently report that washers taste nothing like jellybeans, no matter how hard you wish they would.”
“Saved ours too,” Asha said. The words landed like a medal.
Tovan pretended to accept it casually and failed.
Asha set down her mug with the care of a woman who treasured small rituals because the big ones were too often funerals. “First time I saw a ship die,” she said, tone even, “I was twenty. Boarded a patrol cruiser three minutes before it split along the spine like fruit. The noise it made…” She paused, as if letting the sound “ through her again. “Thought that was the worst of it. It wasn’t. It was the silence after. You could hear the heat leaving the metal.”
She breathed. “I learned that day how to count. Not kills. Survivors. It’s a different math. Better. Today’s number is four.” She looked at each of them, steady, unsentimental. “I’m keeping it.
Draven’s fingers traced the rim of his cup. He didn’t volunteer, at first. He was a man who let his stories choose him. Eventually, one did.
“My brother,” he said simply. Kieran’s head came up, surprised. Draven didn’t look away. “Three years older and had this thing about running headfirst into trouble. When our colony lost power one winter, and I mean dead of winter, he spent an entire day and night out there with the turbines—heat torch, no backup, half a plan at best. Came back with his hands black from frostbite, grinning like he’d won something.”
Draven paused, studying his scarred knuckles. “Next winter he started camping out … trying to anticipate the disaster, fix the world before the world had asked for help. Trouble was he only planned for power troubles and the cold. Whatever beast got him, he was no match for. We never heard from him again.”
His voice stayed level, matter-of-fact. “That’s why I joined fleet. You can’t outrun every disaster, but there’s always another way - if you’re smart enough to make sure that everyone else in the room is much much smarter… and braver than you. You find that crew, and you build systems that hold.” He looked up, something almost like pride in his tired eyes. “Today we actually did that. Messy as hell, but we built something that worked… together.”
Kieran, who told stories like coin tricks or confessions depending on the audience, looked at the three faces that had chosen to live with him and felt the shape of something he’d spent years misnaming. He cleared his throat.
“My dad was a maintenance tech on freight haulers,” he said. “Spent his whole career keeping broken ships limping between ports. Never owned anything that wasn’t secondhand, never made a decision that wasn’t practical.”
Kieran looked down at his hands. “One day he came home with this crazy grin and a bottle of real whiskey—not the synthetic stuff. Said he’d quit his job, bought a small transport, was going to run independent cargo. My mother thought he’d lost his mind.”
He paused, remembering. “Turned out he was tired of fixing other people’s mistakes. Wanted to make some his own for once.” A small smile. “He went broke in six months, but those were the happiest six months of his life.”
“Taught me that sometimes the smart play isn’t the right play. Sometimes you have to risk everything just to remember who you are.”
Silence followed. Not empty. Full. The ship hummed, tired and proud around them.
Tovan broke it with a sniff. “I hate your coffee, Asha.”
“It’s your coffee,” she said.
He brightened. “Then it’s perfect.”
They laughed. A small thing. A necessary one.
Gradually, the room softened. Shoulders lowered. Breathing deepened. Kieran watched Draven’s profile in the low light—the hard lines gentled, the eyes heavier, the mouth less guarded. He felt that familiar pull, yes, but under it something steadier: the ache of seeing the man, not the uniform or the heat.
He reached across the table, slow. Draven didn’t flinch. Their fingers touched. Not a claim. A contact. An allowance.
Asha pretended not to see. Tovan loudly banged another mug into usefulness.
For an hour, they talked in the low, wandering way of people who have earned rest: favorite ports and worst meals; the day Tovan stole a governor’s hat and sold it back at twice the price; the time Asha saved a child on a burning deck by bribing him with a chocolate bar; the way Draven once smuggled kittens off a condemned freighter and spent a week smelling like contraband joy.
Kieran told a story about sleeping on top of an engine for warmth and waking with little crescent burns along his spine that looked, in certain light, like constellations. Draven touched one of the scars with his gaze and something like reverence crossed his face.
The Aegis settled into the quiet with them. Systems purred. The hull ticked as it cooled. The dark outside held.
And then—because the universe is a comedian with bad timing—the comms chirped.
Not a shout this time. A chime. Soft. Insistent.
Asha’s slate glowed red at her elbow. She didn’t pick it up right away. For three heartbeats, she let the moment resist. Then she looked.
Her face changed. “Tracer,” she said. Her voice didn’t shake. “It’s moving. Deliberate vector. Convoy route.”
The warmth drained like a tide. Kieran felt the old electricity flood him, unwelcome and welcome, dread and purpose braided. He met Draven’s eyes. Whatever fragile thing had been building didn’t break. It set its teeth and held on.
“Playtime’s over,” Draven said, echoing himself, but softer—like a promise to bring them all back again.

Kieran stood. His body complained; he ignored it. “Let’s make the galaxy regret inviting us to the party.”

Tovan saluted with a greasy spoon. “Aye aye, Captain Bad Idea.”

Asha rose, already issuing orders that sounded like hope disguised as logistics. The Aegis groaned and shook herself and came awake, battered and ready.

They moved—out of the galley, back into the ribs of their wounded ship, back toward work and war and whatever waited. The stories didn’t vanish. They went with them, stitched into skin and steel.

Outside, the stars looked on, cold and bright and utterly impartial, as the Aegis slid free of her hiding place and turned her scarred nose toward the hunt.
Chapter Ten: Fault Lines
Chapter Ten
Fault Lines
The Aegis crawled from her hiding place, hull scorched, scars unpolished. She was a wounded animal following the scent of blood, and the tracer on Kieran’s wrist pulsed like a heartbeat.
“Vector confirmed,” Asha said, voice taut. “Celeriac ship broke cover three hours ago. Trajectory intersects with convoy lanes in Sector Twelve.”
“Civilian?” Draven asked.
“Mostly. One diplomatic transport, six freighters, two medical haulers.” She didn’t add the obvious: soft targets.
Kieran’s hands flexed on the yoke. His head still ached from where the console had kissed him, but the pain steadied him. “Then we don’t dawdle.”
The Aegis burned hard. The stars streaked, endless… merciless.
They dropped into Sector Twelve like a stone breaking the surface.
The convoy sprawled against the void: a line of patched freighters, engines glowing faint and weary, the gleam of the diplomatic carrier’s polished hull standing out like a jewel.
And already the sharks had gathered.
Three Celeriac strike craft darted in formation, small and vicious. Behind them, the ghost ship slid from cloak—serene, predatory, unhurried.
“Contact in weapons range,” Asha barked.
The comms filled with panic—civilian captains shouting, begging, promising credits they didn’t have. The diplomatic ship held silence, every second a prayer.
Kieran’s grin was sharp and humorless. “Time to ruin somebody’s day.”
The Aegis dove.
Plasma fire streaked past, close enough to sear paint. Kieran rolled them under, the ship groaning in protest. He threaded between two freighters, used their bulk as cover, and popped up to land a clean shot across a strike craft’s bow. The Celeriac pilot twisted away, wounded but not dead.
“Shields at forty percent,” Asha snapped. “One more hit and we’re meat.”
“Working on it!” Tovan shouted. Somewhere below, sparks flew. “I’m giving you power I don’t technically have, so don’t waste it!”
Kieran laughed once, wild. “Wouldn’t dream of it.”
The Aegis skimmed close to a medical hauler, so close Kieran could see wide eyes behind its bridge glass. He pulled hard, flinging the ship into a climb that rattled bones. Draven braced beside him, calm as stone but radiating heat.
“They’re not scattering,” Draven said. “They’re herding again.”
“Toward what?” Asha demanded.
The answer came in the form of a sudden flare: a minefield igniting in perfect sequence ahead of the convoy. Not random debris this time. Deliberate. Planted.
“They’re driving them into a kill box,” Kieran said, stomach cold.
“Not on our watch,” Draven said.
The next minutes blurred into survival. Plasma streaked. The Aegis shook. Kieran flung her into dives that scraped the edge of death, clipped shields, burned systems. A freighter took a hit it couldn’t shake and spun off, venting fire. The medical haulers screamed for help.
“Pick one,” Asha snapped. “We can’t save them all.”
Jalen sacrifice comes here.
Kieran’s hands clenched. His father’s voice rose in memory: Luck is courage that paid off. His own answered, bitter: Sometimes you lose the bet.
“Medical first,” Draven said. No hesitation. No compromise.
“Copy,” Kieran ground out, and threw the ship into the fire.
The Aegis cut between the haulers and the ghost ship, taking hits meant for softer hulls. Shields died with a final groan. The ship herself screamed like a wounded thing. Tovan roared curses that made the comms blush.
The haulers peeled away, limping but alive. The freighters weren’t so lucky. One detonated in silence, bright and brief. Another vanished into cloak-choked shadow.
When it was over, they had saved half. Maybe less. The rest were gone.
The Celeriac pack melted back into the dark. The ghost ship lingered just long enough to let them see it hadn’t been beaten, only amused, before vanishing into cloak again.
The convoy survivors whispered thanks over comms, voices ragged with grief. Then they limped away, too broken to linger.
Silence filled the Aegis. Not victory. Just survival.
—
Later, in the war room, the silence turned sharper.
“You disobeyed me,” Draven said.
Kieran blinked, incredulous. “I just saved two haulers full of medics.”
“You lost three freighters because you gambled on timing instead of formation,” Draven shot back, eyes blazing.
“They were already lost!” Kieran snapped. “I had to bet big or they all burned. You think I don’t feel it? You think I didn’t watch them die?”
Asha looked between them, face carved from stone. Tovan shifted awkwardly, grease-smeared and guilty.
“This isn’t about your feelings,” Draven said, steel in every word.
“No,” Kieran said, stepping close enough that the air between them hummed. “It’s about your control. You want me to be the good soldier at your side. I’m not. I never will be. I’m the mad bastard who wins when everyone else folds.”
Draven’s jaw clenched, breath sharp. Their eyes locked—anger, grief, lust tangled and sparking.
For a heartbeat, Kieran thought Draven might kiss him then, savage and furious. For a heartbeat, he wanted it more than air.
Instead, Draven turned away, voice low and final. “We’ll regroup. Then we hunt them.”
Kieran laughed once, bitter and raw. “Whatever you say, Commander.”
He left the war room before his hands could betray him.
Behind him, the Aegis groaned softly, a ship carrying too much pain and not nearly enough hope.
11  Fracture

Part I — Pressure Differential
The door to Command sealed behind them with a heavy, hydraulic sigh that sounded like the station itself letting go of a breath it had held for too long. Asha didn't just lock it; she engaged the manual deadbolt, a thick, chrome bar that slid into place with a definitive *thunk* that was entirely redundant. It was theater, a ritual performed for the benefit of the two men standing on opposite sides of the chilled, humming room.
“Comms off. Now.” She didn't ask. She unclipped her own unit, its tiny status light winking out, and placed it on the central steel table with a quiet, deliberate *click*. She waited.
Kieran’s mouth quirked. With a flick of his wrist, his own unit—a sleek, non-regulation model—spun across the polished surface, clattering to a stop beside hers with the disrespectful noise of a bottle hitting a bar after last call. Draven’s followed a heartbeat later, his movement economical, his hesitation just long enough to underscore that his compliance was a choice, not a reflex. The three devices lay there like silent, complicit witnesses.
Asha folded her arms. The climate control hummed, but the air felt still and heavy, like the moment before a storm breaks. “No ranks in this room. No orders. No charming evasion. Facts. Only facts.” Her voice was cold enough to freeze the condensation beading on the nearby coolant pipe.
The silence that answered her was a physical thing. It had mass and density, pressing in from the walls, from the ceiling, from the deck plates under their feet.
Tovan, hunched over a primary terminal like a nervous bird, was the one to finally shatter it. “Facts? Oh, I’ve got a whole bouquet of facts.” His fingers flew across the interface. The main wall display bloomed into a star-map of the Erevos sector, a luminous web of sanctioned relief lanes pulsing with serene, bureaucratic life. “See this?” He stabbed a finger at a minor tributary line, a faint capillary feeding a remote outpost. The data stream flickered, a barely perceptible stutter in the rhythm. “Ghost weight. Mass drift where there shouldn't be any. Something’s hitching a ride in the bloodstream and didn’t pay for the ticket.”
Kieran drifted closer, his prowler’s grace at odds with the sterile room. His eyes, usually alight with mockery or mischief, were now sharp, scanning the pattern. To anyone else, it was noise. To him, it was a melody played just half a beat out of time, a tell. “Dock Nine vector again,” he murmured, more to himself than to them. “They’re using the same seam in the fabric. That’s either arrogance on a galactic scale… or desperation.”
“Or confidence,” Asha countered, her gaze a pendulum swinging between him and Draven. “Confidence that their method is sound. Confidence that their information is better than ours. That means they know something we don’t.”
“Or someone they shouldn’t,” Draven said. His voice was low, even, a controlled detonation. His silver eyes lifted from the screen and landed on Kieran. And for the first time since the Obsidian, they didn’t feel like recognition. They felt like an audit.
Kieran’s laugh was a short, brittle sound, like glass cracking under pressure. “You think I’m the hole in your hull? That I’d sell my own neck to the Consortium for the privilege of being their useful idiot before they toss me out an airlock?”
Draven didn’t flinch. He didn’t need to. The accusation hung in the chilled air, a specter made of logic and fear, pressing down until even Tovan stopped his fidgeting and held very still.
