Asha stepped into the space between them, her voice becoming clipped, surgical, a tool to excise the emotion from the moment. “Soren uses intimacy as a fulcrum. It’s his signature. He’s done it before; he’ll do it again. Don’t make his job easier by letting your suspicion do half the work for him.”
Kieran swallowed, his throat suddenly dust-dry. In the silence, another voice echoed in his skull, warm and laced with a pity that felt like a brand: *Alive’s a start. But it’s not enough forever.* He shoved Jalen’s ghost down into the dark where it belonged.
“Fact,” he said, his voice hardening as he locked eyes with Draven, refusing to be the first to look away. “The drift is real. The crates are moving. Either we chase the thread, or we stand here in this frozen room and politely debate the length and sharpness of the knife going into our backs. Your call, Commander.”
The hum of the room seemed to deepen, the lights to dim a fraction. The station held its breath, waiting.


Part II — The Non-Interrogation
The hum stretched, taut and expectant, for one second. Two.
“Facts,” Draven said at last, and the word didn’t crack like an order; it settled into the room, heavy and final, like a stone dropped into deep water. “Did Soren make contact with you directly.”
It wasn’t a question. The question mark had been surgically removed, leaving only the necessary, sterile incision.
Kieran let the silence stretch to the very edge of rudeness. The oldest trick: make them think you’re comfortable in the quiet, that you’re not the one drowning in it. He stared at a point on the wall behind Draven’s head, where the star-map’s glow bled into a shapeless smear of light. Tovan’s nervous hiss of steam from a nearby console filled the background.
Asha snapped open a small felt pouch and upended it. Two stubby, matte-black cylinders—localized acoustic dampeners—rolled onto the table. She thumbed them on. The air in the immediate vicinity took on a thick, fuzzy quality, as if the room had grown a coat of static fur. “No recordings. No signal bleed. You speak. You breathe. That’s all.”
Kieran dragged his gaze back. “He… appeared,” he said, selecting the most passive, least revealing verb and immediately despising himself for the cowardice of it. “On the maintenance bridge over the spine well. He talked. I listened. Nerral nearly liquefied with fear.”
“Time stamp,” Asha prompted, her voice neutral. She’d seen through the dodge without making it a contest.
Kieran’s lip twitched. “Three days ago. Mid-shift. We could hear the drop-hammers from Level C through the grating. Felt them in our teeth.”
Draven didn’t move. The only sign of life was the subtle tension in the tendons of his hands. “What did he say.”
A shrug started in Kieran’s shoulders—habit masquerading as style. He forced it down. The dampeners hummed their sterile hum. The discarded comms on the table seemed to gleam with a guilty light.
“He said I’m him without the mask,” Kieran said, making the words dull, rote, trying to drain them of their power. “That you were building a pretty canyon for a river that’s only happy when it floods. That we always know when the other is gone because the air gets… simpler.” His mouth twisted. “His usual brand of poetic crap. He thinks he’s a mirror. He’s just a funhouse distortion.”
“Contact before that?” Draven’s voice was flat.
Kieran shook his head. It was nearly true. The pressure of another memory—a different doorway, a different whisper, years ago—tried to surface. He mentally stomped on it. “No.”
“And after?”
Kieran exhaled a laugh that contained no humor. “He doesn’t need to. He planted a seed in the station’s skin. When I cracked that CHARITY case, he rode the back-flow. You heard him. Maps and floods. He wants a timeshare in your head.”
“He already has one,” Asha said, not unkindly. Her eyes remained on Kieran. “Do you feel him pulling.”
“Always,” Kieran said, too quickly. The raw honesty slipped out before he could wrap it in something more palatable. He saw the flicker in Asha’s eyes and pushed on, angry at the exposure. “That doesn’t make me a leak. It makes me a receiver. I hear the frequency. I don’t broadcast back.”
Draven took a single step forward, then stopped as if the floor had issued a warning. “When he spoke on the bridge,” he said, each word precise, “you smiled.”
Kieran’s chin lifted. “You reviewing security footage of my facial expressions now, Commander?”
“I don’t need footage,” Draven said. The line should have sounded arrogant. It just sounded exhausted. “Did you smile.”
He could lie. He could shatter the dampened silence with something glib and defensive. He could say it was a predator’s reflex, a bluff, a tactic. All of it would be more elegant than the ugly, simple truth that emerged.
“Yes,” Kieran said.
A muscle in Draven’s jaw twitched, a tiny seismic event. “Why.”
“Because he expected me to flinch,” Kieran said, holding that silver gaze, forcing himself to put the uglier truth on the table between them, like a weapon surrendered. “And because a part of me… a deep, stupid part… wanted to. Because being insulted by a man like Soren feels perversely like being seen by a god you stopped believing in a long time ago.”
Tovan made a small, choked sound. Asha didn’t blink. Draven’s expression remained a mask of carved stone.
Asha broke the tension before it could shatter the room. “Define the contact. Mechanics, not metaphysics. Did Soren touch you? Were you within arm's reach? Did you accept or plant any physical device?”
“No. No. No,” Kieran said, grasping the lifeline of clinical detail. “He stayed in the shaft mouth like it was a stage. He let the air carry his voice. He never closed the distance.”
“Voiceprint?” Tovan ventured timidly.
“Not on the bridge,” Kieran said. “But the whisper from the case just now… it’s in the local mesh. If we pull the corridor’s buffer before the station’s scrubbers wipe it, you might get a nice, juicy, Tovan-shaped data packet to play with.”
Tovan brightened. “I can—”
“After,” Asha snapped. Tovan deflated.
Draven moved finally. Not toward Kieran, but to the table. He rested his fingertips on the cool steel beside the dead comms. It was a measured distance, carefully chosen. “You think I believe you’re the leak.”
Kieran sidestepped the trap in the phrasing. “I think you believe the leak would be delighted to wear my face.”
“And you?” Draven asked. “Do you believe that?”
Kieran let his posture loosen a fraction, a calculated show of ease. “I believe he thinks I’m softer ground than you. Easier to erode.” A mean, sharp smile touched his lips. “He’s wrong.”
Asha’s eyes cut between them, assessing, recalibrating. Then she spoke, her voice dropping into the tone she used for truths that were both necessary and dangerous. “Soren uses intimacy as a fulcrum. He tempts because force is messy and leaves evidence. So we break the fulcrum. New rules.” She held up a hand, ticking them off on her fingers. “One: No solo contact. You feel him in the walls, you call it. Out loud. Even if it tastes like blood. Two: No orders that compromise cover. Commander, I will physically eject you from a room before I let you make him look like your subordinate in front of the wrong eyes. Three: We treat every CHARITY crate as a live mouth. It whispers both ways. We do not feed it our division.”
“Agreed,” Draven said, the word coming out like a sigh of relief.
Kieran’s laugh was softer this time. “You two practice that synchronized agreement in the mirror, or is this just what command looks like on four hours of sleep?”
“Both,” Asha said, dry as dust. “Addendum: I am the point on the next contact. Soren talks to me first. Not to you, Vale. Not to you, Commander. We need to parse what he gains by choosing his audience.”
Kieran rolled the idea around, tasting the old, instinctive refusal. He was the man who walked into bad rooms alone. “If you’re taking point,” he said finally, the concession feeling strange in his mouth, “I’m at your shoulder.”
“Accepted,” Asha said. “And if you are at my shoulder, you keep your mouth shut unless what you’re about to say will save a life or buy us sixty seconds we can prove we needed.”
Kieran opened his hands in a gesture of surrender. “You wound me.”
“Not yet,” Asha said. Then she did the thing that changed the pressure in the room: she bled a little of her own truth. “Years ago,” she began, her gaze fixed on Kieran, letting him see the cost of the memory, “a transport pilot panicked during a skirmish near a settlement. Cut thrust, veered toward a schoolyard. Command said hold position. Draven broke formation. He took a demotion for it—they called it a ‘reassignment.’ I watched him burn every ounce of fuel he had, flying children out two dozen at a time until his engines were screaming. His record says infraction. My memory says it was the most merciful thing I’ve ever seen.” She paused, letting the image hang in the dampened air. “You asked me once what kind of man he is. He’s the kind who bleeds principle, Kieran. It’s not a leash. It’s an open wound. Don’t cut where he can’t clot.”
The room seemed to reshape itself around the story. Draven’s stillness wasn't rigidity anymore; it was the quiet of a man holding a line he’d paid for in pieces of himself.
Kieran’s throat worked. Words failed him. He nodded once, a sharp, jerky motion that was as close to a salute as he could get.
Draven’s focus never wavered. “Did he make you an offer,” he asked, the words clean and direct now. “Explicit.”
Kieran let the worst of it sit in the open. “He offered me the relief of being exactly as selfish as I’m terrified I might be.”
“And did you accept.”
“No.”
“Good,” Asha said, her voice thin and sharp as a wire. “Then let’s make sure it stays that way.”
She killed one dampener. The room’s ambient noise rushed back in, a wave of sound. She didn’t return their comms; she pocketed them like contraband. “We move. Service Spine C to Nine’s anomaly. We argue with our feet. Mouths shut unless it’s data.”
Kieran’s grin returned, cleaner now, scoured by the truth. “A walk. My favorite way to not have a conversation.”
Draven picked up the third comm on the table—the decoy unit Asha had confiscated. He weighed it in his hand, then set it down. “If Soren is routing through you,” he said, and there was no accusation left, only a grim resolve, “he will learn there are doors that do not open for him.”
Kieran tilted his head. “You volunteering yours?”
“I always have,” Draven said.
Asha hit the release. The hatch sighed open, spilling the cooler, unfiltered air of the corridor into the room. It smelled of metal and distance.
“Walking,” she said.
They fell in behind her—Draven a solid presence at her right, Kieran a half-step back on her left. The corridor lights flickered into a maintenance dim. Somewhere above, a fan stuttered, caught, and stuttered again, like a heart trying to find its rhythm.
They did not speak. The interrogation-that-wasn’t trailed them like a fourth member of their party, silent and grim.
Ahead, the spine sloped down toward the cold, industrial geometry of Dock Nine. The station’s vast breath seemed to draw in. And hold.
Of course. Here are the remaining parts of Chapter Nine, expanded and deepened to meet the requested scope and atmospheric intensity.

Part III — Corridor Transit, Argument in Motion
The hatch wheezed shut behind them, sealing them into the Service Spine’s narrow, ribbed throat. It was a world away from the clinical sterility of Command—here, the air was cooler, tasting of oxidized metal and the faint, sweet tang of lubricant. Conduits lined the walls like exposed nerves, bundled together in thick, snaking cables. Every third light fixture flickered with a lazy, arrhythmic delay, throwing their shadows ahead in jagged leaps before yanking them back into darkness.
Asha took point without discussion, her boots striking the grated floor with a clipped, metronomic cadence that dared either man to break rhythm. Draven followed, his posture ramrod straight, as if the cramped, utilitarian corridor were a parade ground under inspection. Kieran hung back a half-step, his hands shoved deep in his pockets, the earlier grin now a ghost on his lips.
The silence was a fragile thing, and Kieran, as always, was the one to shatter it.
“You know what makes ghosts bold?” he mused, his voice a low, conversational ripple in the metallic gloom. “The gleam of authority. You march a uniform into a place like this, and the truth doesn’t just hide—it mutates. If we walk in there like a sanctioned patrol, all we’ll find are clean floors and innocent faces. The real players will have already vanished into the walls.”
Draven didn’t turn. “Protocol exists for a reason. Chaos is not a strategy. Containment requires a visible, predictable presence.”
“*Containment*,” Kieran repeated, the word dripping with amused scorn. “That’s a polite word for making everyone so nervous they either shut up or screw up. We don’t want containment. We want *answers*. To get those, we go in soft. No shine, no epaulets. Let them mistake me for just another scoundrel until they get comfortable enough to slip.”
“You mean comfortable enough to believe your lies,” Draven said, his gaze fixed on the back of Asha’s head. “They see a reflection in you because you’ve made a life out of holding up a mirror to their own worst instincts.”
Kieran quickened his pace until he was walking almost abreast of Draven, the space between them crackling with unspoken challenge. “And you’ve made a life out of being the immovable object they break themselves against. Congratulations, Commander. We’re both perfectly predictable.”
Asha didn’t break stride. “Mouths shut unless it’s data,” she reminded them, her voice a whipcrack that echoed off the close walls. “The next word that isn’t a sensor reading or a tactical assessment will be your last. Am I understood?”
They lapsed into a brittle, seething silence. The only sounds were the thrum of the station, the scuff of their boots, and the maddening *click-hiss* of the failing lights, three distinct rhythms refusing to harmonize.
Halfway down the spine, they passed a junction where a primary coolant pipe wept a steady stream of condensation. Kieran reached out almost absently, flicking a bead of moisture with his thumb, watching it vanish into the darkness below. It was a nervous habit, a tactile anchor in a world that felt increasingly unmoored.
Draven saw the gesture—his eyes missed nothing—and his jaw tightened a fraction further.
“You mistake subterfuge for honesty,” Draven said, breaking the renewed quiet. His voice was low, meant only for Kieran. “It isn’t. It’s a surrender of control to the narrative of others.”
Kieran tilted his head, a predator considering its prey. “Funny. I thought that was the very definition of command. Letting others write your story for you.”
The barb landed, sharp and precise. For a heartbeat, it seemed Draven would stop, would turn and make the narrow corridor a crucible for their conflict. Instead, he exhaled, a slow, controlled release of breath, and kept walking.
Asha’s hand snapped up—a sudden, sharp gesture that had both men freezing in their tracks. Instinct overrode pride. Ahead, just past a grated floor panel, the air shimmered with a faint, almost imperceptible heat distortion.
“Anomaly,” she stated, her voice dropping to a professional, dispassionate murmur. “Service access pocket. We breach clean. No speeches, no heroics.”
She drew her sidearm, the motion fluid and utterly devoid of flourish. She checked the charge indicator with a glance, then gave a single, sharp nod.
Kieran leaned toward Draven, his whisper a blade of sound. “No speeches.”
This time, Draven didn’t rise to it. His silence was a weapon, and he wielded it perfectly.
They moved forward as one unit, the argument left behind them but humming in the space between their bodies, a second, more dangerous current beneath the hum of the spine.
---
Part IV — Clean Hand, Dirty Print
Asha signaled left with a sharp chop of her hand. The three of them melted into a shallow maintenance notch, a scar in the wall where a plaque hung crookedly, its screws loose. The air here was colder, smelling of ancient dust and the ghost of spent electricity. Condensation beaded along a leaking riser pipe, forming perfect, trembling spheres that clung to the metal grating, defying gravity.
Kieran dropped into a crouch, his fingers—sensitive as a safecracker’s—walking along a nearly invisible seam in the wall panel. “Here,” he breathed out, the word a cloud in the chill air. He didn’t need to look back; he could feel Asha’s presence covering the corridor, her weapon a silent promise of consequence. He could feel Draven’s watchful intensity, a pressure against his back.
The panel lifted with a soft, metallic groan, revealing a darkness within. A pocket, narrow and deep. And centered within it, sitting on a custom-cut piece of grey foam, was a single matte-black case. It was pristine. Unscuffed. Deliberate. The white stenciled letters—**CHARITY**—seemed to glow in the dim light.
*Too clean,* Kieran thought, every instinct honed by a lifetime of lies screaming in alarm. *A trap.*
“Type?” Asha asked, her voice low and steady.
“Not explosive,” Kieran murmured, eyes scanning the edges, the latch. “Bombs are messy. Loud. This is… elegant. It wants attention.” His gaze locked onto a tiny, almost microscopic bead of polymer nestled beside the primary latch. “Sensor. Trip it, and you wake up something you didn’t put to bed.”
Tovan would have squealed with delight at the engineering. Kieran felt a brief, sharp pang of his absence.
Draven’s voice was a low rumble. “Can you open it without triggering the alert?”
Kieran’s smile was a swift, grim flash. “We can ask it to talk to us first.” A microblade appeared in his hand as if by magic. He slid the tip under the latch, angling it until it caught the faint light. The tip kissed the polymer bead. A tiny, almost silent *tick* confirmed it was live, hungry.
“Shh,” Kieran whispered, a pointless, habitual soothing. He adjusted the pressure, a maestro conducting a silent orchestra of tension. The case’s seam released with a sigh of yielding metal.
Asha’s stance didn’t change, but the rhythm of her breathing hitched almost imperceptibly. “Color,” she said, the word automatic, a ritual borrowed from a different life.
“Green,” Kieran replied, and was unnerved by how the simple word steadied him.
He lifted the lid a fraction. That same sickly, beautiful blue light from Dock Nine seeped out, painting his lashes. The scent followed—ozone and something floral, like marigolds crushed in a cold, sterile hand.
“Mesh,” Kieran confirmed. “It’s not a weapon. It’s a—”
The word died in his throat.
The voice didn’t come through the air. It arrived through the metal. It traveled up through the knuckles of his hand resting on the case, through the deck plating beneath his knees, through the very bones of the station, vibrating into his marrow before it resolved into sound in his ears. When it did, it was clad in perfect, intimate clarity.
“You can polish a canyon wall until it reflects the stars,” Soren murmured, his voice a caress in the darkness. “It still floods when the river remembers what it is.”
Asha didn’t flinch. She pivoted minutely, her weapon still covering the corridor, her head tilting toward Kieran. “Audio only?” she asked, her tone clinical, dissecting.
Kieran forced his lungs to work. “Piggybacking the local mesh. The case is the transmitter. The bait is the broadcast.”
“Our broadcast,” Soren said pleasantly, as if joining a conversation he’d been invited to. “Hello, Commander. Hello, Major. Hello, Kieran.” The precision of the names made the cramped notch feel claustrophobic. “Tell me, did you enjoy your little confessional? I do so admire a room that teaches men the weight of the choices they pretend to make.”
The words were a scalpel, probing not at their actions, but at the fragile alignment they had just forged. Kieran’s grip tightened, then deliberately relaxed. Draven was a statue.
“Cut it,” Draven commanded, his voice flat.
“Cutting,” Asha acknowledged. She didn’t lunge for the case. In one fluid motion, she holstered her weapon and slammed the heel of her palm against the emergency override for the spine’s lighting grid.
The corridor convulsed. Standard illumination died, replaced by a throbbing, emergency crimson that bled up the walls, casting everything in a bloody monochrome. The constant, comforting under-hum of the station’s convenience network—the data stream that made the station feel alive—spooled down into silence.
The voice in their heads smiled. You could hear the curve of it. “Red becomes you,” Soren crooned. “It’s an honest color. It doesn’t pretend to be anything but what it costs.”
Kieran felt the familiar pull—the dark allure of the chaos Soren represented—warring with the new, unfamiliar tether to the people beside him. He gave ground to neither. “We’re going to bore you to tears,” he said, bending closer to the case, finding a second, nearly identical polymer bead. “No monologues. No flirtation. No deals. We just want your channel closed.”
“And yet,” Soren’s voice was laced with soft amusement, “you opened the box. We are so drawn to the same doors, you and I.”
Draven’s fingers curled against the cold wall. “Source,” he demanded of Asha, refusing to address the phantom.
“Localized,” she replied. “Bouncing on the convenience band, not core comms. But it’s reporting.” She tapped the conduit above her. “Pinging a listener. We’re already late to the party.”
“Fashionably so,” Soren purred. “I’ve been waiting an age to hear your particular brand of silence.”
Kieran found the fault line in the seam and twisted the blade with a surgeon’s finality. The case’s eerie light guttered and died. For one profound second, there was only the deep, red silence and the sound of their own breathing.
Then, thin and reedy, as if from a great distance, the voice filtered back through the corridor’s stripped-bare infrastructure.
“You can stifle a whisper,” Soren said. “But you cannot stop an echo.”
“Actually,” Asha said, and with the graceless, brutal efficiency of someone who valued results over aesthetics, she ripped the main power relay for the entire spine segment straight from the wall.
The world plunged into a deeper, more absolute crimson gloom. The vibration under their feet dropped to a subsonic growl. Somewhere down the spine, a large pump or fan shuddered into resentful inactivity.
The voice severed mid-syllable.
Silence. Thick, shocking, and complete.
Kieran realized he’d been braced for another taunt. The absence of it was a physical shock.
“Report,” Draven said into the void, his voice unnaturally loud, his attention still on Kieran’s hands.
“Mesh relay severed. Hard kill on this segment,” Asha reported, already moving. She pried up the edge of the wall panel to reveal a hair-thin filament glued along its inner edge. “Tripline. Non-explosive. For attention. Anything louder than a breath wakes a watcher.”
“Which watcher?” Draven asked, the words ice.
“The one that tells Soren when we’re getting close,” Kieran said. He went very still, feeling the shift in the air, the inevitable turn of the screw.
Draven didn’t disappoint. “Or the one that tells him when *you* are.”
The words landed, not shouted, but placed with devastating precision.
Kieran looked up from the dead case. In the hellish red light, Draven’s face was all sharp angles and deep shadows, a stranger’s face. The suspicion in it wasn’t clean anger; it was murky, polluted with a protectiveness that made it cut all the deeper.
“If I were his pipeline,” Kieran said, his voice soft, strained with the effort of control, “you wouldn’t be hearing philosophy. You’d be picking up pieces.”
Asha’s hand came up between them, a barrier. “Not. Here.”
Draven’s gaze shifted to her, but the intensity didn’t diminish. “He lifted the lid.”
“He gave warning first,” Asha countered, unwavering. “He killed the first signal. I killed the second. He doesn’t get to be your outlet because you’re furious that the flood is at your door.”
For a heartbeat, the air was pregnant with something irrevocable. Then Draven nodded, once, a gesture that cost him. “Logged.” It wasn’t an apology. It was a line item in a ledger of restraint.
Kieran exhaled, a long, slow breath. He turned the inert case and slid it into his duffel, the zipper sounding obscenely loud in the quiet. “We take this to Tovan. Let him flirt with it in a shielded room. And we stop treating every quiet corner like it’s a confessional booth.”
Asha reseated the panel with silent efficiency. She left the filament deliberately, almost imperceptibly slack—a trap for the next person to get careless. “We just announced we’re learning,” she said. “They’ll adapt.”
“Let them,” Draven said. He finally looked fully at Kieran, and his expression was complex—smeared with something that might have been apology at the edges. “We adapt faster.”
Kieran wanted to say something cutting about adaptation and sacrifice, about rivers and the walls built to contain them. He swallowed the vitriol and let a different truth stand.
“Your pretty canyon’s going to get a lot prettier before it shatters,” he said, the lightness in his voice a thin shield for the bruise forming beneath. “Let’s just make sure we’re on the right side of the break.”
Asha holstered her weapon, the sound a full stop. “We’re done.” She palmed the breaker. The spine groaned back to life, lights flickering, the hum rising like a beast rousing from sleep. The emergency red faded, replaced by the sterile white of standard illumination.
For two full seconds, they stood in the returning light, saying nothing. For two seconds, the station was just a station—metal, light, and function.
Then the moment broke.
“Move,” Asha said, and they did—out of the notch, back into the artery of the spine, the weight of the dead case in Kieran’s bag a tangible secret.
They didn’t see the tiny amber diode, hidden under a handrail half a corridor back, blink once wearily. It recorded the power cycle, noted the duration of the silence, and filed a new entry in a ledger light-years away. *Subject demonstrates adaptive countermeasures. Adjust doctrinal approach.*
The spine carried them onward, toward the next inevitable mistake.


Part V — Asha’s Cut
The spine’s lights stuttered back to full strength, but the silence between the three of them was a thicker, more potent darkness. They had taken three steps when Asha stopped dead, planting herself sideways across the corridor, blocking their path with the unassailable fact of her presence.
“Enough,” she said, and the word had no volume, only finality. It stripped the air bare. “We’ve bled enough doubt into this corridor to nourish him for a year. You will each give me the worst truth you’re carrying. Then we decide if this crew is still a crew.”
Draven’s eyes narrowed to slits. “This is not the procedure—”
“It is *exactly* my jurisdiction,” she cut him off, her voice a vibro-blade. “I am the only one in this triangle not confusing pride with a survival skill. You will answer. You will listen when I tell you it’s enough.”
The corridor seemed to shrink, the walls pressing in. Kieran leaned against the bulkhead, affecting a pose of casual ease, but the line of his body was taut as a wire.
Asha pointed a finger at Draven, a judge passing sentence. “You. Bottom line. If it comes down to him,” she jerked her head toward Kieran, “or the mission, where do you stand?”
Draven’s spine straightened. He didn’t look at Kieran. His voice, when it came, was stripped of all ornament, hollowed out by a honesty that was more terrifying than any threat. “If it is his life against the integrity of my command… I choose the mission.” He paused, the silence aching. “Until the moment I can’t.”
The raw admission was a physical blow. Kieran covered the flinch with a slow, sarcastic clap against his own arm. “Inspiring. They should put that on the recruitment posters.”
Asha’s gaze, cold and relentless, swung to him. “Your turn. No poetry. No deflection.”
Kieran’s smirk faded, replaced by something sharper, more real. “If he uses me as a public tool, a puppet to make his point, there will be no private ‘us’ to return to. That’s the line.” He shrugged, a gesture that failed to be casual. “He owns the station. He doesn’t own my compliance.”
“Logged,” Asha said. Then she did the unexpected: she sheathed her blade and offered her own vein. “Years ago, on a rock at the edge of nowhere, a civilian transport panicked under fire. Broke formation, veered toward a settlement—a schoolyard. Command’s order was ‘hold position.’ Draven broke rank. He took a full demotion for it. They scrubbed it as a ‘lateral reassignment to special duties.’” Her eyes never left Kieran’s. “I watched him burn every ounce of fuel in his tanks, flying children out two or three at a time until his engines were red-lining. His official record reads ‘disciplinary action.’ My memory calls it the single most merciful act I have ever witnessed.” She let the image hang in the air, a testament etched in fire and defiance. “You asked me what kind of man he is. He bleeds principle. It’s not a leash he wears; it’s a wound that never closes. So don’t you dare cut him where he can’t clot.”
Draven’s jaw was a hard line, the only sign the story had struck home. Kieran found he had no retort. The smirk had died completely.
Asha let the silence stretch, a taut wire, before laying down her law. “Next contact Soren initiates, he talks to me. Not to you, Kieran. Not to you, Commander. I will be the filter. I will be the wall. You will stand down.”
“That is not a viable—” Draven began, but Asha’s hand shot up, a guillotine falling.
“*Accepted*,” Kieran said, the word leaving his lips before he could stop it. He held Draven’s stormy gaze, let the challenge hang between them, then added, “On one condition. If she’s the wall, I’m the shadow at its base.”
“Done,” Asha said. “And if you’re the shadow, you are a silent one. You speak to save a life or to buy us sixty proven seconds. Nothing else.”
Kieran spread his hands in a gesture of surrender. “See? We’re building something beautiful here.”
Her eyes could have cut diamond. “Do not mistake this for a negotiation.” She looked from one to the other. “Are we clear?”
For a long moment, the corridor was just a space again, occupied by three people breathing the same air, bound by the same terrible, fragile truth. The fractures were there, visible and deep, but they were out in the open. Owned.
Asha stepped aside, clearing the path. “We move.”
They fell in behind her, the new rules written in the air between them, as fragile and necessary as a truce on a battlefield.


Part VI — Break Line
The spine’s ambient hum returned to its familiar, reassuring frequency, but the silence walking with them was a heavier, more complex thing. They moved in formation, the yellow guideline under their feet a taut string connecting three volatile points.
Asha’s eyes remained fixed ahead, her posture that of a general leading a fragile peace. Draven held the center, his spine rigid, his silence a fortress. Kieran drifted on the periphery, a live wire contained only by sheer will, the ghost of a smile nowhere to be found.
The lights flickered once, a brief, sympathetic stutter. A soft, system-wide alert tone echoed faintly through the corridor—a minor diagnostic ping, meaningless to most. But to the three of them, it was a trigger. They froze as one, instincts syncing in a way their words never could.
Draven turned his head, a minute gesture. His hand came up between them, not in command, but in offering. Palm open. A bridge across the silence.
For a heartbeat, Kieran hesitated. His fingers twitched at his sides. The pull was visceral, a need for the anchor Draven represented, for the solidity he so relentlessly mocked.
But the truths they had just bled onto the deck plates were still fresh. His own line in the sand was still drawn. To take that hand now would be to erase it.
He let the hand hang in the air, unclaimed. He summoned the ghost of his old armor, a sharp, empty smile. “Careful, Commander. Sentiment’s a dangerous commodity in a place like this. The floor may look clean, but it stains easy.”
Draven’s hand slowly closed. The offered bridge retracted, becoming a fist, then fell back to his side. He said nothing. The silence that followed was colder than before.
Asha was the one to break the spell. “We’ve got company,” she said, her voice cutting through the tension. She nodded toward a comms panel on the wall, its status light now pulsing with a green, civilian-priority signal. She tapped the screen to open the channel without awaiting permission.
A voice filtered into the corridor, crisp with the static of long-range comms and weary with the weight of responsibility. It was a routine request for final dock clearance and manifest verification, the dull poetry of logistics. Nothing out of the ordinary.
Until the sender ID scrolled across the display.
**MYRR, JALEN. CRC VESSEL “STEADFAST.”**
The name hit Kieran in the center of his chest with the force of a railgun slug. It wasn’t a memory knocking at the door anymore. The past had just kicked it off its hinges.
The soft alarm continued its patient, ignorant pulse.
Three people stood frozen in the harsh light of the corridor.
The fracture they had been desperately trying to map and manage had just been blasted wide open by a name from a different life.
Kieran froze. His breath hitched. His body folded, all the fight torn out in one cruel instant.
He swayed. His lips shaped a word—maybe fuck you, maybe please—and then the strain, the injuries, the blood loss from earlier crashes, the sheer weight of it all buckled him.
Kieran collapsed, hitting the deck hard enough that the sound silenced even Draven’s ragged breathing.
The comms were still shouting warnings. Asha’s voice was screaming for orders. Tovan cursed the ship into life or death below.
And Draven—Draven stood over Kieran’s body, chest heaving, hands shaking, the echo of his own words ringing in his ears like gunfire. Words he couldn’t take back.
Unforgivable.
The ship lurched as the Consortium closed in.
The Commander of Erevos Station’s proudest warship, and the man he’d just broken in every way, lay before him. And Draven realized too late: if Kieran didn’t wake, it wouldn’t matter what the Consortium did. He’d already lost everything.Draven’s voice was quiet, lethal. “You brought this to us.”
Kieran swallowed. “I didn’t know—”
“Didn’t know,” Draven repeated, each syllable sharp as a blade.
Asha’s slate pinged. She looked up, grim. “Multiple Consortium signatures inbound. They found us.”
Kieran met Draven’s eyes. In an instant he knew, with the clarity of a man staring down his past, that the next battle wasn’t just with the Consortium. It was with the man he’d just given himself to, in anger and in need, and who now had every reason to never forgive him.
Inbound ships be damned, Draven was at tops end, and nothing could stop what was boiling over in his soul.
“You’re a curse!” Draven roared. “Every ship you touch dies!”
“At least I touch something!” Kieran spat, swinging wild. His fist glanced Draven’s jaw. The commander slammed him back, harder.
Kieran staggered—straight into the corner edge of a console. His skull cracked against it with a sickening sound.
The world seemed to freeze.
Kieran’s eyes went wide, unfocused. His lips parted as if to retort, but the words slurred. His knees buckled. One arm jerked uselessly at his side.
“Kieran?” Draven’s fury vanished in an instant, replaced by terror so pure it hollowed his voice.
Kieran’s face twisted, wrong. His body sagged. The collapse wasn’t graceful—it was rag-doll sudden, boneless, a man undone. He hit the deck hard, half-pinned against the console.
Draven lunged, catching him too late. Blood ran from a split at the temple, but worse—his eyes. They didn’t track. His mouth tried to form words that came out broken, garbled.
“No,” Draven whispered, horror dawning. His hands shook as he lifted Kieran against him, as if holding tighter could glue him back together. “No, no, no… stay with me.”
Kieran’s breath rasped, shallow, uneven.
“Baby, no—please, stay with me!” Draven’s voice cracked, torn from someplace he’d never let anyone hear. His forehead pressed against Kieran’s, desperation spilling. “I’m sorry—I’m so sorry, I didn’t mean—stars, I didn’t mean it—don’t leave me—”
Kieran’s body convulsed once, then went slack, terrifyingly still.
Draven’s scream tore out of him raw and unguarded. “I love you! Do you hear me? I love you, damn you! Stay with me!”
Alarms blared. Asha’s voice shouted from the bridge: “Commander, signatures inbound—multiple! Consortium!”
But Draven heard none of it. The galaxy could have burned around him and he would not have let go. He rocked Kieran against him, one arm cradling the limp weight, the other pressing desperately against the wound, as if his hands could hold back death itself.
“Don’t you dare,” Draven whispered, over and over, lips against sweat-damp hair. “Don’t you dare leave me, Vale. Not now. Not ever. Stay.”
For the first time in a long, brutal career, Commander Raleth Draven was not a soldier, not a leader, not steel. He was a man who had just realized he might have destroyed the only person he’d ever let himself love.
And the Consortium was closing in.




12 Collapse
Draven’s hands were slick with Kieran’s blood.
He had commanded fleets through sieges, watched comrades die, sealed bulkheads on men who begged to be let out. He had done it all with a soldier’s iron. But now, with Kieran Vale limp in his arms, none of that discipline held.
“Stay with me,” he begged, voice cracked raw. “Kieran, damn it, stay.”
Kieran’s eyes fluttered but didn’t focus. His mouth worked, the sound that came out garbled, wrong, as if some cruel hand had cut the thread between thought and tongue.
Draven’s chest clenched until he thought his ribs might splinter. He pressed a palm against the gash at Kieran’s temple, the other cradling his head like something too fragile for the world. He would have given every medal, every year, every scar to trade places.
Alarms howled. Asha’s voice split the air over comms:
“Consortium ships inbound! Multiple vectors! Commander, orders?”
Orders.
Draven’s mouth opened, but no sound came. The universe was ending in his arms, and they wanted orders.
“Raleth!” Asha’s voice sharpened, dragging him back by the name almost no one used. “We need you.”
He sucked in air, forced his throat to obey. “Battle stations. Evasive pattern Delta. Keep the haulers between us and their guns.”
“Yes, sir.”
Her voice steadied him, but only barely.
Tovan’s shout followed from engineering: “I can give you engines or shields, not both! Choose fast!”
Draven’s gaze fell to Kieran’s slack face, pale under the smear of blood. Shields wouldn’t keep him alive. Engines might get them out. Neither was enough.
“Engines,” he rasped. “Get us moving.”
The Aegis shuddered, thrusters spooling up with a wounded howl.
Draven shifted Kieran in his arms, lowering him to the deck as gently as if it were silk, though every instinct screamed to never let go. He tore open the medkit one-handed, fumbling—fumbling, like a rookie. Compressors, sealant spray, neural patches. His hands shook too badly to aim them.
“Commander?” Asha again, voice hard with strain. Weapons fire crackled through the hull. The deck lurched under impact.
Draven sealed the wound at Kieran’s temple, pressed the neural patch to his neck, praying it would spark some signal back into the body that had always been too alive, too reckless, too much.
“Kieran,” he whispered, forehead pressing to sweat-damp hair. “I’m sorry. Stars, I’m sorry. I didn’t mean it, any of it. You’re not a curse. You’re—” His throat closed. He forced the words through anyway. “You’re mine. I love you. Stay. Please stay.”
Another impact rocked the ship. Asha cursed, Tovan screamed about overheating coils, alarms blared.
Draven lifted his head, rage cutting through terror. “Asha—full spread. Make them earn every meter. Tovan—if you can’t give me shields, give me fire. I don’t care if you burn the coils black.”
“Yes, Commander,” they both barked.
The Aegis fought back. Plasma spat into the dark, wild and furious. Consortium fire answered, bright and merciless.
Draven stayed kneeling, one hand steady on Kieran’s chest, feeling for each fragile rise and fall. With the other, he keyed commands, his voice low and lethal:
“Target their lead. Break their teeth.”
The ship obeyed, scarred and screaming. His crew obeyed, desperate and loyal. And Draven, for the first time in years, felt like he was breaking apart—because the man he had just called mine might be slipping away under his hands.
“Don’t,” he whispered again, as if the words themselves could anchor Kieran to the world. “Don’t leave me, Vale. Not now. Not ever.”
And all the while, the Consortium closed in.

The Drift
He was climbing.
The ladder stretched forever, knotted from pipes and ropes and rusted metal. His palms bled as he pulled himself upward, higher, higher, while the void below whispered his name in voices he half-remembered.
Every rung shifted when he touched it. A dice cup. A bottle neck. A deckplate slick with oil. A hand—his father’s hand, rough, heavy, closing too tight.
“You’re nothing but a curse,” the voice bellowed.
Kieran flinched. Looked down. His father stood below the ladder, face flushed with drink, eyes cold as winter metal. His mouth twisted around words Kieran had tried to forget.
“Useless boy. Everything you touch rots. You’ll kill us all, you hear me? You’ll—”
The voice warped. The face blurred, bones rearranging, and suddenly it was Draven’s mouth spitting the same venom. You’re not a captain—you’re a curse.
The two men merged, separated, merged again—father and commander, past and present, each word a blow.
Kieran’s grip faltered. His body wanted to let go, fall back into the dark, where at least the shouting would end.
But above him, the shape of a boy clambered, laughing—a younger Kieran, wild, barefoot, fearless. “Keep climbing,” the boy called. “We’re almost there.”
Almost where? Kieran didn’t know. His arms ached. His breath tore. His father’s roar shook the ladder. Draven’s words cut deep.
The boy slipped. Fell past him, eyes wide, mouth open in a silent scream. Kieran lunged, missed, and the void swallowed him whole.
“You couldn’t save him,” the voices jeered together, overlapping, louder, crueler. “You’ll never save anyone.”
Kieran’s eyes squeezed shut. His body loosened. He was ready to fall.
Then—
Another voice. Not venom, not rage. Breaking, raw, desperate.
Vale. Stay with me.
The faces below flickered. Father. Draven. Father again. Then—Draven, real this time. Not steel. Not ice. Silver eyes wrecked, voice trembling.
“I’m sorry,” it said. “Stars, I’m sorry. I didn’t mean it. You’re not a curse. You’re mine. I love you. Stay.”
The ladder swayed, cracked. His fingers slipped—until a hand seized his wrist, burning hot, anchoring him.
Not his father’s. Draven’s. Real. Strong. Trembling with fear.
Kieran gasped. The grip seared through him, pain so sharp it shocked him awake.
The void shattered. The ladder dissolved.
He was choking on air, lungs seizing, eyes flying open to light and alarms. Draven’s real face hovered above him—no blur, no venom—only terror, tears in his eyes, words spilling like a prayer:
“Baby, no—don’t leave me. Stay with me. I love you.”
Kieran coughed, blood on his lip, and rasped, “Still… here.”
Draven pressed his forehead to his, shaking. “Never do that again.”
Kieran’s grin was weak, crooked, defiant. “No promises.”
Then the Aegis shook under another barrage, alarms screaming, the universe clawing them both back into fire.

The Heart That Would Not Die
The Aegis was alive.
Oh, sure it wasn’t covered in flesh or bone exactly, but in old steel… metal that had seen too much. Every groan of her hull was a voice, every shudder of her ribs a heartbeat. She bled coolant like lifeblood, vented sparks like breath. She was scarred, battered, and furious.
And she refused to lie down.
Asha stood on her bridge, the center of a storm. Screens cracked and bled light. The deck pitched beneath her boots as if the ship herself staggered with each impact. Smoke curled like ghosts from the consoles.
Her hands moved fast, ruthless, sure. “Re-route fire. Pulse batteries, wide arc. Make them feel it.”
The crew obeyed. The Aegis obeyed. Plasma spat into the void, jagged, brilliant. One strike craft faltered. Another pressed harder, a vulture at the throat of a dying lion.
Asha didn’t look down at the dais, didn’t let herself see Commander Draven on his knees, didn’t let herself see Vale sprawled pale and still. She carried their absence in her chest like lead. She turned it into orders.
“Hold the line,” she whispered to the ship, as much prayer as command. “We are not finished.”
The ship groaned in answer.
In engineering, Tovan fought like a man inside a furnace. Steam hissed, fire snapped, alarms keened like wounded children. He was grease-slick, lungs raw, palms blistered. His tools were half-melted stubs. He screamed at the panels until his throat cracked, and the panels screamed back with sparks.
“You hold!” he shouted at the reactor core, at the trembling coils, at the trembling in his own hands. “You bloody hold! We’ve come too far to die now.”
His mind flicked to his mother’s tugboat, to candy tucked in fuse boxes, to the way she’d told him order could be made from chaos. He started sorting washers by size again—stupid, pointless—until his hands steadied. Then he jammed them into another impossible fix.
The ship bucked, slammed him against bulkhead. He coughed blood, laughed hoarsely, and cursed louder. “Come on, girl. Come on, Aegis. Don’t let them win.”
The engine throbbed under his palms. Hot, furious. Alive.
The Aegis fought.
Every shot she spat into the void was a howl of defiance. Every evasive roll was a desperate gasp. The Consortium pressed close, teeth flashing, trying to carve her open.
And in her bones, in her circuits, in the souls of the crew who bled and cursed inside her, she clung to one truth: she was not finished.
Her commander was bent to breaking, his heart torn raw. Her captain was down, his pulse a faltering whisper. But she carried their echoes. She carried all of them.
Asha’s voice was steel in the smoke: “Again. Fire again.”
Tovan’s laugh was madness in the dark: “You’ll break before I do, you bastards!”
The ship shuddered, burning, groaning, but still moving. Still alive.
Asha gripped the console, eyes blazing, and spoke to the ship as if to a lover bleeding out in her arms. “Stay with me. Just a little longer. They’re not allowed to take us. Not today.”
The Aegis answered in fire.
She was scarred. She was failing. She was magnificent.
And the hands inside her would not let her fall.
As long as there was breath in her corridors and pulse on her decks, she would carry them through.
Chapter Thirteen: The Gambler’s Hand
Chapter Thirteen
The Gambler’s Hand
The Aegis was running on fumes. Alarms screamed, decks buckled, the ship herself howled like a living thing in pain. Draven had retaken the command chair, voice steady but raw, as if every word cost him blood.
“Evasive pattern Delta-Seven. Keep them off the flanks.”
“Delta-Seven is shredded,” Asha barked, hands flying. “They’ve learned it.”
Draven’s jaw tightened. “Then improvise.”
Outside the viewport, Soren’s ghost ship danced through the stars. Sleek, black, cloaked in arrogance as much as tech. He didn’t go for the kill. He circled, darted in close for a strike, pulled back, circled again. Each pass cost the Aegis another piece of herself.
“He’s toying with us,” Asha spat.
“Always does,” Draven muttered, low.
Another blast. Panels blew. Sparks showered the bridge.
Draven gripped the armrest until his knuckles bled. His mind ran through every formation, every maneuver, every tactic burned into him by decades of war. None fit. None answered Soren’s dance.
“Shields collapsing,” Asha warned. “Hull at forty percent. We’re dead in five.”
Tovan’s voice roared through comms, half-choked by smoke: “Engines are a joke! I can keep them from exploding, but don’t ask for grace!”
Draven stared into the void, silver eyes blazing, but for the first time since he’d worn command, he had no move left.
Then—
“Don’t… fight him straight.”
The words were hoarse, broken. Kieran.
He staggered onto the bridge, pale as a ghost, one arm clutched tight to his ribs, blood drying at his temple. He should have been unconscious. He should have been dead. But his eyes—gods, his eyes still burned.
Draven was on his feet instantly. “You shouldn’t be—”
“Shut up.” Kieran dragged himself to the holo, swaying, fingers trembling as he traced Soren’s pattern across the map. “He’s showing off. Always has. Third pass, wide swing. He’ll herd his prey against the cover. Looks clean, but he’ll leave a seam. Watch for it.”
“You can’t know that,” Asha said, sharp with disbelief.
“I know him,” Kieran rasped. “I know the bastard better than anyone alive. I’ve lost to him, I’ve won with him. He wants the drama more than the kill. He’ll sweep left—wherever he thinks no one’s bold enough to strike.”
The ship shuddered again. Another pass. Another wound.
Draven’s hands closed around Kieran’s shoulders, steadying him. “Even if you’re right—we don’t have the firepower.”
“Not us,” Kieran said, lips curling into a dangerous grin. He pointed at the convoy feed—half-ruined freighters, limping haulers, the polished diplomatic envoy, all scattered and useless. “Them.”
Asha frowned. “They’re barely flying.”
“Exactly,” Kieran whispered. “Soren’s blind to weakness. He dismisses it. He won’t look twice at a ship he thinks is toothless. So we make the envoy the knife.”
Draven blinked, then caught it. “A decoy.”
Kieran’s grin widened. “No. A trap. We let him do exactly what he wants. Let him herd. He’ll swing left, confident, smug—and when he does, the envoy opens every hidden bay they’ve got and lights him up. Diplomatic ships aren’t unarmed, not really. They’re just quiet about it.”
Asha’s eyes flared. “Protocol guns. Point-defense arrays…” She concurred. “More than enough to sting.”
“Enough to blindside him,” Kieran said. His knees buckled; Draven held him upright. “But only if we bait him right. Only if we look broken enough to make him bite.”
The bridge went silent. Outside, the ghost ship’s lights glimmered, circling, patient, cruel.
Draven looked at Asha. Looked at the envoy, trembling on the screen. Looked at Kieran, bloodied and burning, alive because he refused not to be.
He nodded once. “Do it.”
The Aegis went limp, engines stuttering, weapons silent. The envoy drifted into place, a fat jewel waiting to be plucked.
Soren’s voice crackled over open comms, smug and honeyed. “Oh, Kieran. Always dramatic. Always dragging out the game. I’ll enjoy this one.”
The ghost ship swept wide, elegant, arrogant. Just as Kieran had promised. Left flank, yawning open.
Draven’s voice was a growl. “Now.”
The envoy came alive. Point-defense guns unfolded like fangs. Plasma spat point-blank into the ghost ship’s flank. Its shields shrieked, buckled, collapsed.
The Aegis surged up from her feigned death, every weapon burning, pouring fire into the breach.
For the first time, Soren’s voice wasn’t smug. It was furious. “You cheating—”
The rest was lost in the roar of fire.
The ghost ship reeled, cloak failing, hull scarred deep. It limped into the dark, retreating, bleeding. Not dead. Not yet. But beaten.
On the bridge, the crew exhaled in ragged disbelief. Asha sagged against her console. Tovan whooped brokenly over comms. The envoy’s captain sent a clipped salute, voice shaking with relief.
And Kieran?
He swayed in Draven’s grip, lips curled into a ruined smile. “Told you,” he rasped. “Better hand.”
Draven held him tighter, silver eyes blazing with something no one dared name.
Outside, the stars burned cold and bright, and the war was far from over. But for this breath, for this battered ship, for this broken crew—victory was theirs.
Chapter Fourteen: Ashes of Victory
Chapter Fourteen
Ashes of Victory
The battle was over.
The silence after was worse than the fire.
The Aegis drifted, scarred and bleeding, her engines whispering smoke into the void. The envoy limped at her side, hull scorched, comms alive with clipped gratitude. The freighters that survived huddled close, engines flickering like tired hearts. Too many were missing.
Asha stood on the bridge, shoulders straight but eyes hollow. Her voice when it came was steady, ritual. “Casualty report incoming.”
The list unspooled across her slate—names, codes, deck assignments. She read them in silence, lips tight, as if speaking them aloud would make them real.
Draven didn’t ask for numbers. He already knew they would be too many. He sat in the command chair, a scarred giant with hands that still shook where no one could see. His gaze stayed fixed on the tactical holo, now empty of enemies, as though staring long enough might summon the ghosts back to life.
Behind him, Kieran stirred. Alive. Barely. His chest rose and fell shallowly where medics had settled him on a stretcher, IV lines glowing faintly against bruised skin. Every rasp of his breath was a reminder: saved for now, but not safe.
Asha broke the silence with the sound of duty. “Two freighters lost in full. Three more damaged beyond repair. Civilian deaths—” She stopped. Swallowed. “Over four hundred.”
Tovan’s voice drifted in from engineering, hoarse and thin. “We’ve got leaks I can’t patch and coils that won’t cycle. She’ll fly, but not far. We need dock. Soon.”
The bridge hummed with the sound of exhaustion, of barely contained grief.
Draven rose at last, every inch of him carved from control that felt brittle, breakable. He moved to Kieran’s side, knelt, laid a hand on the smuggler’s wrist—light, reverent, as if even that might shatter him.
Kieran’s eyelids fluttered, a whisper escaping cracked lips. “Worth it?”
Draven’s throat tightened. He couldn’t answer. Not when the word no pressed harder than yes.
Asha saved him the lie. “We live. That’s what it cost.”
Her tone was steel, but her eyes burned.
The crew dispersed into silence, each carrying their own ghosts, their own private arithmetic of survival and loss.
Draven stayed kneeling by Kieran’s side long after the others were gone. His hand stayed on that wrist, feeling each fragile beat.
What did it mean? That survival was never clean. That victories bled as much as defeats. That every choice left scars on ships, on crews, on hearts.
And for Draven—for the first time in his career—it meant the line between command and love had shattered. He couldn’t draw it again. Not after nearly losing Kieran. Not after confessing what he hadn’t meant to say.
Not after realizing it had been true.
The Aegis drifted on, broken but alive. Her crew carried their grief like ballast. The war had not ended. The Consortium would not stop.
But for one scarred, battered ship, the question wasn’t whether they could fight again.
It was whether they had the strength to survive the meaning of what they’d already lost.








14 Ashes of Victory
The Aegis hung in dock, ribs braced open to the station’s scaffolds, lights doused to a workman’s glow. Sparks popped from welders’ torches, sharp bursts of daylight in the artificial dusk. On her hull, fresh steel gleamed too bright against burn-scorched plating. She looked older, heavier, but still alive.
From the viewport of Draven’s quarters, she was only a shadow among many.
Inside, the room carried the smell of polish and old leather, the faint hiss of the station’s circulators. The kettle on the shelf whistled low, ignored until Draven rose and poured two cups without asking.
Kieran was stretched across the couch, half-buried in the folds of a blanket gone crooked. A datapad rested on his chest, dimmed to sleep mode. His hair had grown in uneven, a lock forever falling into his eye; he didn’t bother to push it back.
Draven set a cup within reach, sat at the desk, and adjusted the lamp so its light fell softer.
Neither spoke.
The minutes stretched, measured by the tick of the kettle cooling. Outside the wall, welders cut and patched, the clang of hammers ringing faint through the hull.
Kieran stirred, one eye opening. “You’ll dent the cup if you keep staring at it that way.”
Draven looked down. His hand had tightened around the porcelain, knuckles white. He loosened his grip, set the cup down without answering.
The silence that followed wasn’t empty. It was filled—with the scrape of fabric as Kieran shifted, with the hiss of his uneven breath, with the knowledge of everything unsaid.
On the table lay a folded slate, its screen dark. Orders waiting. Routes. Reports. Neither man touched it.
Instead, Kieran dragged the blanket higher, closed his eyes again, and muttered, “Wake me if the station explodes.”
Draven watched him a long time, until the breathing evened, until the shadows softened. Only then did he reach across the desk and, very quietly, turn the slate facedown.
The stars outside burned, sharp and indifferent.

15 The Trap
The station never really slept, but there were hours—thin, stretched hours—when Erevos seemed to hold its breath. Kieran liked those hours best. The neon signs dimmed to maintenance glow, the corridors smelled more of coolant than of spice and sweat, and the people still awake moved quiet, half-hidden, as if they owed their lives to silence.
He leaned on the rail of a half-repaired observation deck, cigarette burning between his fingers, watching the black beyond the docking ring. The Aegis floated there, scaffolded, still scarred but whole enough to be proud again. Welders sparked around her flanks, tiny fireworks that painted her hull in flashes. She looked like a queen who had survived her own funeral pyre.
Kieran exhaled smoke, slow. His body still complained in aches and jolts—his ribs when he stretched wrong, his head when the lights flashed too bright—but he could walk, he could drink, he could feel the itch of restlessness gnawing at him again.
That was when the message came.
His slate vibrated once, discreet. A coded ping in an old format, one he hadn’t seen since before he’d ever crossed paths with Draven. A smuggler’s mark—four dots arranged like a gambler’s dice throw, tilted at an angle.
He thumbed it open. The text bloomed in simple, raw glyphs:
Vale. Black routes moving again. Consortium lanes you can’t chart on your own. Meet me. Old service decks, Dock 9. Midnight station time.
No signature. None needed.
Kieran felt the grin tug his mouth before he stopped it. The code was old, but the hand behind it was unmistakable.
Soren.
The last time he’d seen him, it had been across a holofeed—smirk sharp as ever, voice dripping venom. That smirk had haunted him since. Soren knew exactly how to get under his skin. And he knew the bait Kieran couldn’t resist: Consortium intel wrapped in smuggler’s shorthand, delivered in a way that felt like a dare.
He almost tossed the slate over the rail. Almost.
Instead, he killed the message, stubbed out his cigarette, and started walking.
The service decks of Dock 9 hadn’t been touched in years. Old scaffoldings rusted in corners, lights buzzed and flickered, the walls sweated with condensation that dripped into pools on the floor. The hum of the station was louder here, unbuffered, like the heartbeat of a tired giant.
Kieran moved with his usual swagger—shoulders loose, mouth set in its habitual smirk—but every step was measured. His hand brushed the inside of his jacket, where the grip of his pistol waited.
He didn’t like being lured. He liked less that he’d answered anyway.
“You’re getting sentimental in your old age,” he muttered to himself. “Could’ve stayed home, smoked, kissed the commander goodnight…”
The corridor bent into shadow. At the end was a hatch, paint stripped, handle stiff with disuse. A faint light leaked around the edges.
Kieran exhaled once, low, then pushed it open.
The space beyond was a cavern of forgotten steel—an old maintenance bay, long abandoned. Catwalks stretched in broken lines above a pit of machinery that hadn’t hummed in decades. Dust lay thick, except where recent boots had tracked through it.
He knew, then, he wasn’t walking into anything accidental.
“Vale.”
The voice rolled out from the shadows like smoke. Smooth, mocking.
Kieran turned his head. There he was.
Soren stepped into the light, and the years fell away. Tall, sharp-boned, hair longer than it used to be, tied back careless. That grin still intact—the one that promised sin and betrayal in equal measure.
“Well,” Kieran said, forcing nonchalance into his tone. “If it isn’t my least favorite ghost.”
Soren spread his hands as if to embrace the room. “And if it isn’t the one who got away. Twice, maybe three times, if we’re counting right.”
“I stopped counting when you started working for the bastards,” Kieran said flatly.
Soren tsked, stepping closer, boots echoing on the steel. “And yet, here you are. Answering my call like an old lover.”
Kieran smirked. “Don’t flatter yourself. I came for the intel.”
“Oh, you’ll get it,” Soren purred. “Just… not quite the way you imagined.”
Something in his tone set Kieran’s nerves on edge. He adjusted his stance, weight on the balls of his feet, ready.
But the shadows around them were already moving.
Figures peeled out of the dark—four, five, maybe more. Consortium muscle, masked, rifles gleaming in the flicker-light.
Kieran cursed under his breath.
Soren’s grin sharpened. “Did you really think I’d come alone?”
Kieran’s hand flashed to his pistol, cleared it, fired. One of the shadows staggered, fell. He dove for cover behind a rusted girder, shots sparking against steel. His heart pounded, his ribs screamed, but adrenaline burned through the ache.
“Still slippery,” Soren called, voice amused. “Still thinking you can shoot your way out.”
Kieran popped out, fired again, missed. The Consortium enforcers pressed in, herding him like wolves.
And Soren—Soren just stood there, watching. Smirking. Waiting.
——
He moved on instinct—low, fast, angles that had saved him more times than he could count. But the body keeps its own score, and Kieran’s had recent entries: stitches that pulled, a wrist that trembled when he needed it steady, a flash of white pain in his skull when he twisted too quickly.
The first enforcer came in sloppy, confident. Kieran met him with the butt of his pistol, a vicious crack across the mask. The second learned faster; the rifle butt caught Kieran’s ribs like a hammer, hot pain lighting his side. He grinned anyway—a ragged show of teeth—and rolled behind a collapsed scaffold.
“Don’t make them work for it,” Soren called lightly, strolling along the catwalk as if watching a street performance. “You’ll only embarrass yourself.”
Kieran fired once, blind. A spark, a curse, a shower of rust flakes. Two more shadows peeled off left, repositioning. Herding, not hunting. He’d taught Soren that trick, years ago.
He tasted iron. “You always did love an audience.”
Soren leaned on the rail, chin in palm, amused. “Only when the showrunner is worth the price of admission.”
An enforcer lunged. Kieran caught the rifle, twisted. The man went down; Kieran went with him, breath torn out of his chest. A boot stamped for his hand; he jerked away, losing the pistol but keeping his fingers. He kicked back, felt contact and heard a grunt, then a hiss—another rifle swinging down, too fast.
He ducked. Not fast enough. The stock cracked the side of his head. Stars burst behind his eyes. The world teetered.
“Easy,” Soren said, the word floating down like silk. “He bruises beautifully.”
“Come down here and admire it up close,” Kieran rasped, staggering to his knees.
Another blow. This one to the shoulder, aimed to numb. He rode it, rolled with it, came up on one knee and drove an elbow into a gut. The enforcer folded; a baton raked Kieran’s spine in answer, bright pain spiking down his legs. His breath went thin. The cold draft of the maintenance bay snuck under his jacket, found a sheen of sweat on his back, chilled him.
His vision swam. He blinked it clear and saw Soren descending, lazy, unhurried, boots ringing the metal stairs in a slow, mocking rhythm.
“Enough,” Soren said, and the enforcers obeyed. Rifles lifted, set to ready without firing. They held a circle open for their patron, a little stage just big enough for two.
Kieran laughed, raw at the edges. “You always loved a spotlight.”
“And you always pretended you didn’t,” Soren said, stepping into the circle. He looked Kieran over with proprietary interest. “How’s the head? Our mutual friend the commander leave any cracks I should be careful not to widen?”
Kieran spat blood to the side. “He hits harder than you.”
“Oh, darling,” Soren murmured, smile widening. “He loves you. Of course he does.” He tilted his head. “That’s why this will be so very useful.”
Soren crouched without warning, fingers sliding into Kieran’s hair, not rough enough to be pain but nowhere near gentle. Kieran held very still. The old habits arrived unbidden—measure the distance, map the exits, count the weapons, mark the rhythms of the enemy’s breath.
Soren’s thumb brushed the edge of the healing scar at Kieran’s temple. He clucked his tongue softly. “You’ve been careless.”
“You’ve been predictable,” Kieran said, forcing his mouth into an easy curve. “Same tricks, new mask.”
“Masks are to be enjoyed.” Soren’s hand tightened, just enough. “And tricks aren’t a flaw when they work.”
Kieran’s pulse jackhammered, but his voice stayed flat. “Say the part where you tell me I’m going to help you.”
Soren’s eyes lit, pleased. “You’re going to help me.” He leaned closer; Kieran smelled clean spice on his skin and something colder beneath. “You’re going to tell me where your commander keeps his eyes when he isn’t watching the horizon. You’re going to walk me through his blind spots—professional and otherwise. You’re going to leave little doors ajar. And then you’re going to watch me walk through them.”
“Or,” Kieran said, “I could kick you in the teeth right now and we’d both feel better.”
“You could try.” Soren’s fingers slid lower, to Kieran’s jaw, thumb pressing the hinge, forcing his mouth to open a fraction. A testing touch, invasive in its smallness. “But you won’t. Not yet.”
Kieran stared at him, unblinking. “Because of your friends with guns?”
Soren’s smile thinned. “Because you still think there’s a version of this where you get to choose.”
The circle of rifles tightened a hair. Breath ghosted inside Kieran’s chest, shallow. He wasn’t afraid of pain; he’d made a life that way. But humiliation—that was a different kind of blade. Soren knew it. He always had.
“Up,” Soren said, letting go. He rose without watching to see if Kieran obeyed, sure of the enforcers’ hands. They came down like a tide, seizing Kieran’s arms, hauling him upright. He bit down on the groan that wanted free.
Soren stepped close—too close. He reached without hurry, plucked the knife from Kieran’s sleeve that Kieran had half-forgotten was there. He admired it, weighed it in his palm, then leaned in and used the flat of the blade to lift Kieran’s chin.
“You taught me this one,” Soren said lightly. The cool metal traced the stubble along Kieran’s throat, slid to his collarbone. “How to take the edge off a man’s pride before you take the rest.”
Kieran’s hands flexed uselessly in their grips. “You learned the wrong lessons.”
“Or all the right ones.” The blade’s flat pressed lower, not cutting—never quite that honest—only reminding. The smallness of the touch made Kieran’s skin crawl. He forced his eyes away from Soren and let them roam the rafters, the catwalk, the patched section of wall behind Soren’s left shoulder where condensation pooled in a steady drip.
He marked it. Useless, probably. He marked it anyway.
Soren followed his gaze and laughed softly. “Always casing the room. Even now.” He lowered the knife, tucked it into his own belt—a theft more intimate than a blow. Then his hand found Kieran’s waist, fingers settling on the old leather of his belt as if reacquainting themselves. He drew Kieran closer by that grip, a parody of a lover’s touch, and murmured near his ear, “We could have had a different life.”
“You had your chance,” Kieran said. “You sold it for a shinier one.”
“Don’t be ungenerous,” Soren said. “I didn’t sell it. I invested.” His breath warmed the shell of Kieran’s ear. “And look at the returns.”
Kieran turned his head enough to catch Soren’s eyes. “You’re talking to the wrong man if you want envy.”
Soren’s smile returned, white and sharp. “No. I want a message delivered by the right mouth.” He gestured, and an enforcer stepped in with a compact module—dull metal, cables like veins.
Kieran recognized it. Not a weapon. A leash.
He went still.
“Don’t pout,” Soren said, sounding almost fond. “It’s a listener. A very small one. It won’t make you say anything you don’t want to. It will only hear when you do.”
Kieran’s laugh cut itself on his teeth. “You think I’ll walk back to Draven with that in my pocket?”
“Not your pocket,” Soren said, and his voice was all silk again. “Closer.”
The enforcer raised the module. Cold kissed the soft skin just under Kieran’s jaw as metal brushed there. Kieran jerked, an involuntary shiver. Hands on his arms tightened, holding him still.
“Easy,” Soren soothed. “It sits just under the skin. Barely a kiss. You won’t even know it’s there until I do.”
Kieran stared straight ahead at the condensation drip, counting the beats between drops. He’d survive this, and then he’d cut it out with a broken bottle in a station bathroom if he had to. He’d survived worse. He—
The sting was brief, precise. A hum shivered through his teeth, then faded. He exhaled slowly, lungs burning.
Soren stepped back to admire his work. “Perfect.” He tipped his head. “Say something pretty.”
“Go to hell,” Kieran said softly.
Soren’s eyes half-lidded, as if savoring the sound. “Closer than you think, darling.” He lifted a finger and traced a line down Kieran’s sternum through the open edge of his jacket, and the heat that touched Kieran’s face then wasn’t shame so much as a fury so bright he could have used it as a torch. But the rifles were still there. The circle still tight. The body still slow.
“Walk him,” Soren said without moving his gaze. “We’re done rehearsing.”
They dragged Kieran forward. He went, feet under him by habit, timing the pull of each grip, counting the steps to the stair. Soren kept pace, just ahead, walking backward with the unhurried grace of a man who believed the world owed him the courtesy of not falling apart under his feet.
At the stairs, Kieran stumbled deliberately. The enforcer on his left tightened his hold; the one on the right adjusted his footing. Kieran let his weight drop, dead-sudden, and wrenched his shoulders hard, trying to break free.
For a heartbeat, everything loosened.
He slammed his head back into someone’s face. Bone met cartilage. A howl. He twisted, brought his knee up wicked and uncharitable; someone cursed. He dove for the shadow under the catwalk, saw the hole he wanted—the gap in the rail—and threw himself toward it.
Something caught his ankle. Another something—no, Soren’s hand—closed in his hair and slammed him sideways into a strut. The world flashed white. The maintenance bay rang with echoes: his breath, their boots, Soren’s quiet chuckle threaded through.
“Still slippery,” Soren said, breath a fraction faster now. He used Kieran’s belt again, levering him upright. “I do miss that.”
Kieran breathed hard through his nose, tasted dust and copper. “You miss what you can’t own.”
Soren’s eyes warmed, as if Kieran had paid him a compliment. “Exactly so.”
They dragged him again. The corridor beyond the bay yawned with deeper dark. At its far end, a freight lift sat open, panel gutted, cables exposed and humming. A loose coil of chain lay on the floor beside it, oil-dark.
Kieran paced his breath to the footfalls of his captors. He catalogued the wrongness in his body—the drag in his left leg, the tremor in his fingers, the ice-spear pain in his ribs when he inhaled too deep. He looked for leverage and found only timing. Timing would have to do.
Soren walked backward into the lift, still watching him. “Draven will come,” he said, conversational. “He can’t help himself. He’s a hero. They always answer when you tug the right string.”
“Careful tugging my strings,” Kieran said, forcing the grin back onto his mouth like armor. “You might cut your hands.”
“I’ve bled before,” Soren said mildly. “I wear it well.”
The lift shuddered as it started down. The doors stayed open—no need for secrecy now. The shaft smelled like old oil and stale water. The enforcers’ gloves squeaked on their rifles; one had a torn mask, blood drying under his nose, courtesy of Kieran’s skull.
“Where?” Kieran asked, nodding toward the dark below. “A closet? A stage? Somewhere with better lighting than this?” He jerked his chin at the flicker above, bright-dark-bright, a strobe that made the air seem thicker.
Soren’s smile didn’t reach his eyes. “Somewhere quiet. Somewhere we can talk without the station listening. You and I have so much to catch up on.”
“Pass,” Kieran said.
“Not how this works.” Soren’s knuckles brushed Kieran’s cheek, a touch that made Kieran’s stomach clench for reasons that had nothing to do with fear. Memory is a traitor; it turns any hand into a familiar one if you let it.
Kieran did not let it.
“Tell me one thing,” he said, because speech buys seconds. “Why me? You could have taken any officer. Any dockhand with a badge. Why me?”
Soren’s lashes lowered; he looked almost bored. “Because he won’t lose you.” Then, softer, an intimacy edged in steel: “And because part of you wants to see if he won’t.”
The lift groaned to a halt. The doors opened to a tunnel that wasn’t quite part of the station anymore—brick older than the girders above, patched with modern conduits. A small cart idled to the side, silent, its canopy drawn. A smell of cold iron and something antiseptic hung in the air.
Kieran’s skin prickled. His heartbeat settled, paradoxically, into something slow and steady. The moment right before cards hit the table.
He looked at Soren and smiled like a man about to place a stupid bet. “Let me walk.”
Soren blinked. “Pardon?”
“Let me walk.” Kieran rolled his shoulders in the enforcers’ grips. “You want Draven to come? You want him frantic?” He tilted his head, let his grin turn feral. “Marks look better when they run. Tell your boys to ease up. I’ll make your show worth watching.”
Soren held his gaze, weighing it. He’d always liked gambles. He’d always liked Kieran’s nerve.
“Loosen him,” he said at last. The enforcers hesitated; he added, mild, “If he tries anything clever, break his knees.”
Kieran breathed out through a laugh. “Now we’re flirting.”
They eased their holds. Not much. Enough.
He took one step. Then another. Body screaming. Head buzzing. He measured the distance to the cart, the angle of Soren’s stance, the readiness in the rifles around him. There was no gap big enough to fit a man through. He moved anyway.
A hand brushed his back—Soren’s—possessive, guiding. Kieran fought the flinch. “Easy,” Soren murmured. “We’ll get there.”
Kieran turned his head a fraction, as if to speak, and let his voice go lazy. “You always did prefer the long way around.”
Soren’s smile was all teeth. “I like anticipation.”
Kieran nodded, took the last step into the cart’s shadow.
Then he moved.
He slammed his shoulder back into Soren’s chest, hard enough to make breath huff out. His heel hammered down on an enforcer’s instep. At the same time he ripped sideways, grabbing the canvas canopy with both hands and yanking it down like a curtain. The fabric fell over rifles, over faces, blinding them for a heartbeat.
He bolted.
Pain tore through his ribs. His vision tunneled. But his feet found the old rhythm, faster than his sense. He hit the tunnel at a sprint that was more prayer than strategy.
A shot cracked. Stone spat beside his head. Another. Another.
Soren didn’t shout. He laughed. “Run, then!”
Kieran took a corner too hard, boot skidding on damp. He caught himself on the wall, shoved off, kept going. The tunnel narrowed, widened, split. He chose left by instinct. A rusted grate hissed under his hand as he vaulted it. Air tasted like iron and old rain.
Behind him, boots thundered. Ahead, a door loomed—half-open, light seeping around it. He aimed for it like a lifeline, slammed it with both palms and ran into a wall of men.
Not enforcers. Station maintenance. Two, three, maybe four, eyes wide, hands full of tools. Startled, then alarmed, then—too late—stepping aside just enough for the wave behind Kieran to crash over them all.
He went down under bodies. Heat, weight, the scrape of knuckles, the snap of a cable bind. He fought until his muscles locked and his vision went white at the edges. He fought until hands pinned his wrists and knees and something cold kissed his neck—a warning shock that arced through him and stole the argument from his muscles.
He lay panting, cheek pressed to stone, breath fogging the floor.
Soren arrived last, of course. Not winded. Not even flushed. He crouched again, a mirror of the moment upstairs, and slid his fingers into Kieran’s hair to lift his head off the floor.
“You do make it interesting,” he said, eyes bright with delight. “I should thank you.”
Kieran’s mouth was a slash of red. He showed teeth. “You can thank me in person when this goes wrong for you.”
“It won’t,” Soren said gently. “Because here’s the other thing I learned from you: always make the choices that give you two wins.” He leaned closer. “If Draven comes, I get my prize. If he doesn’t, I get my message. Either way, I get you in the story’s center, where you belong.”
He let Kieran’s head down with careful hands, an intimacy crueler than a shove. Then he straightened, dusted his knees, and nodded.
“Bring him,” he said. “Play nice. He’s precious cargo.”
They hoisted Kieran. The bind on his wrists bit. His feet dragged. The maintenance men watched, faces shuttered; one made the sign people make when they don’t believe in gods but fear them anyway.
Kieran met the man’s eyes and mustered a grin that cost him. “Tell the commander,” he said, voice hoarse. “Dock Nine. Old service decks.”
Soren’s hand settled on the back of Kieran’s neck. “No need,” he murmured against his ear. “He’ll hear what I want him to hear.”
The little listener under Kieran’s skin hummed once, like a cat purring in a far room.
They took him into the dark.
*Chapter Sixteen: Draven Unleashed
Chapter Sixteen
Draven Unleashed
The briefing room reeked of hesitation.
Screens glowed pale with maps, sensor echoes, fragments of Consortium chatter. Officers bent over their slates, murmuring in tones meant to sound decisive. None of it mattered.
Because Kieran was gone.
Draven stood at the head of the table, fists braced on the steel. His silver eyes scanned every hesitant face. The pulse in his jaw ticked steady as a metronome.
“They took him at Dock Nine,” he said. His voice was steady, but beneath it ran something cold and furious. “We have his trail. We move now.”
Commander Veyr, all thin lips and polished medals, shook his head. “We don’t have confirmation of that. It could be a feint. We wait. Gather intelligence. The Collective wants us to rush in blind.”
Draven leaned forward. “And while we wait, they carve him open. Or worse.”
“It’s one smuggler,” Veyr snapped.
The word hit like a slap. The air went sharp.
