Asha’s eyes narrowed from her seat. Tovan shifted uncomfortably, grease still under his nails, called up for reasons that had nothing to do with tactics. The junior officers studied the floor.
Draven’s hands curled on the table. “He’s not expendable.”
“He’s not even military,” Veyr shot back. “He’s a liability who’s already cost us lives. You forget yourself, Commander.”
The silence after was absolute.
Draven straightened, iron unfolding from his spine. His voice came low. “You forget me.”
And then he walked out.
⸻
The corridor outside rang with his boots. Asha caught up to him, fast. “Draven.”
He didn’t stop.
“You can’t just—”
“I can.” His pace didn’t break.
She grabbed his arm. “Think. If you go alone, you don’t come back. If you take us without sanction, we all hang. What do you want, Commander? To save him, or to burn everything to embers?”
He halted. Turned. Their eyes locked.
“Both,” he said.
Something in his voice made her flinch—not weakness, but finality.
She studied him a long beat, then let him go. “Tovan’s in the bay. He’s been arming the shuttle since you walked into that room.”
Draven’s mouth almost curved. Almost. “Of course he has.”
⸻
The shuttle was stripped for speed. No markings, minimal lights, her belly packed with ordnance that could gut a corvette. Tovan leaned against her hull, sleeves rolled, hair sweat-slick, grin crooked.
“Knew you’d ignore the grown-ups,” he said cheerfully. “So I packed you a lunch.”
Draven checked the gear himself—rifle, sidearm, blades balanced for close work. Tovan offered a compact breach charge with a little flourish. “For the door you’re not supposed to get through.”
“Appreciated.”
Asha arrived with a small case. She didn’t hand it over right away. “You realize,” she said, voice flat, “if you die out there, we all go down with you.”
“Well then, don’t let me die,” Draven said simply.
She studied him. Then placed the case in his hand. “Med kit. For him. Not you.”
Draven’s jaw worked once. He nodded.
⸻
The shuttle cut loose from Erevos like a knife. Stars spread cold and endless. The Conllective’s trail gleamed faint on the scanner, a vector into the shadow of a derelict hauler orbiting too close to a dead moon.
Draven flew silent, every nerve taut. He thought of Kieran’s grin, bloody and defiant. He thought of his body dragged away in rough hands. He thought of the little listener under his skin, feeding every word back to Soren.
He thought of what he would do when he reached him.
The hauler loomed, rusted and skeletal, its hull grafted with Consortium tech—towers bristling, weapons spines jutting, a parasite welded onto old steel. Lights glimmered like eyes in the dark.
Draven’s breath came slow, steady. His hands moved with precision over the controls. The shuttle ghosted close, hugging shadow, sliding into the hauler’s blind side where old cargo locks yawned.
He strapped on his gear. Checked every weapon twice.
When the clamps bit and the shuttle kissed the hauler’s skin, Draven rose. He didn’t feel the shake in his hands until he stilled them.
He put his palm flat to the hatch. For a moment, his eyes closed.
“Hold on,” he whispered.
Then the hatch cycled, and the dark swallowed him.
The hauler’s guts were a museum of dead industry: conveyors seized with rust, cargo spiders frozen mid-climb, bulkheads pitted and patched with cheaper metal. Over it all, the Consortium had draped their new skin—sensor buds like barnacles, fiber lines pulsing faintly, gun pods grown where there had been none.
Draven moved like a shadow taught to hate. No wasted steps. No sound bigger than a breath.
The first guard didn’t know he existed until the blade kissed the seam under his ear. Draven rode the body down, caught the rifle before it clattered, and dragged the dead weight into the belly of a broken loader. He took the man’s comm bead and slid it into his own ear. Low chatter hissed, clipped and curt—sector calls, route confirmations, the boredom of men who thought their prey was already caged.
Two corridors on, a camera watched a hatch that had been welded shut long ago. Draven palmed a flexible lens onto the sensor, fed it a loop of itself, and slid under its blind stare. His hands knew the rhythm; his mind flicked between a map he didn’t have and a man he couldn’t lose.

Footsteps. Two. He flattened into a strip of shadow where pipe met rib. The men came laughing, talking about cards, about someone named Lya who poured drinks with both hands. Draven stepped between heartbeats, cut the first one’s laugh in half with a suppressed shot, seized the second by the harness and slammed him spine-first into steel. The man’s breath left him in a squeal; Draven’s knife took the sound away.
He pulled the bodies into a maintenance alcove and wiped the blade once, a motion as practiced as breathing. The rational part of his mind counted rounds, mapped angles, logged hazards. The rest pictured Kieran’s mouth bloody, Kieran’s wrists bound, Kieran’s voice—when it had come through that ruined whisper on the bridge—saying not yet.
The little listener under Kieran’s skin. If Soren had turned it to talk both ways…
Draven forced the thought down. Forward.
A hatch blocked the next intersection. Old, corroded, retrofitted with a cheap Consortium keypad. He wrapped the breach charge like a scarf around the locking collar, set the timer for one, braced himself, and turned his face away.
The pop was small, mean, efficient. The hatch sagged. He heaved it open and slid through into air cooler by a few degrees.
He smelled antiseptic. And fear.
On the far wall, a chain of status lights crawled. Holding cells. Five lit, one blinking. A bored guard sat on a stool with his feet on a crate, a magazine propped on his knee. Draven put a round through the magazine and into the skull beneath it. Before the man’s body finished falling, Draven was at the panel, reading numbers like a second language.
Cell 3: empty. 4: empty. 5: two signatures. 6: one. 7: biometrics spiking, cascade of stress markers.
He palmed the manual release for 3 and 4, then cut the panel’s throat with his knife to stop it screaming. Doors slid, and two exhausted men blinked at the world as if it had returned the favor. Dock workers, by the look. They stared at the gun in Draven’s hand, at the blood on his sleeve.
“Left corridor,” Draven said, voice low. “Up ladder, follow green pipes to exterior service. If you slow down, you die.” He didn’t wait to see if they obeyed. He triggered 7.
A shock prod greeted him sliding from the door gap, jabbing for his ribs. He caught it, twisted, and returned it in kind—blunt end to cheekbone, arc to sternum. The enforcer spasmed and sagged. Behind him, a woman in a torn shiftsuit scrambled back, eyes huge.
“Station security,” Draven said, truth sanded down to something usable. “Move.” He jerked his chin to the open corridor. “Run where they ran. Do not stop.”
She ran.
Cell 5 blinked at him like a heartbeat. Two lives. One crouched, one pacing.
He didn’t open it. Not yet. He needed the room quiet when that door slid.
“Soren,” he said into the dead air, very softly. He didn’t key a comm. He didn’t have to. If the spider was listening, the web would hum.
For a beat, nothing.
Then the smooth voice came from an overhead speaker he hadn’t seen, threaded through with laughter. Commander, Soren purred. You finally brought yourself.
“Open 5,” Draven said.
Ah ah. That one’s mine.
The locks on 5 clicked, slid, locked again. A game. Soren always played them when he thought there was time.
“Time’s the one thing you don’t have,” Draven said. He turned from the cells and moved deeper in, toward the sound of Soren’s voice.
He found the surveillance room by feel—the way any beast learns to find the den of the one who had been stalking it. Wall of screens, feeds from every corridor, every cell. A tech with head down, eyes glazed with trust in the cameras. Draven put him to sleep without breaking stride. The screens washed his face in ghost-light.
There. Kieran. The image sat in the third row, fifth column—color washed thin, light harsh. Bound to a chair with cable, wrists behind him, ankles lashed. Head down. Chest moving.
Draven’s world tunneled. He could see the pulse at the base of Kieran’s throat. He could see the bruise purpling along his jaw where the prod must have found him. He could see the carefulness in the way his shoulders were set—pain banked, stored for later.
In the adjoining channel, Soren’s face. Too close to a lens. Smile brittle as candy.
Commander, Soren cooed. I worry you’re about to be uncivil.
Draven put a bullet into the nearest camera dome. The screen fizzed and died. Soren’s smile widened in the surviving feeds.
There he is, he said. The man I’ve so wanted to meet without an audience between us.
“Open the door,” Draven said.
No. Come take it from me.
The speakers clicked off.
Draven breathed once, and it wasn’t steady. He touched two fingers to the screen where Kieran’s bowed head had been, a gesture no one saw, then turned and left the room with the kind of calm that makes planets nervous.
He moved faster, now that he knew the shape of the maze. Left past the dead loader. Up a rung-ladder that trembled. Through an access duct that stank of old coolant. He dropped silent into a wider artery where the Consortium had rooted most deeply—their lines thick along the walls, their sensors studding the ceiling like metal fruit.
Three guards at the T. One looked bored. One looked ambitious. One looked like he believed in the job. Draven shot the believer first.
He took a grazing hit along his shoulder for that choice, a bite of heat and thunder. It registered and was filed away with admirable neatness. He tossed a flash pellet into the corner as if littering, counted one-and-a-half, and moved through their blindness like a learned language.
Down the right. Another door. He palmed the handle and it didn’t move.
“Of course,” he said to the door, as if polite conversation might shame it into better manners. He set the last of his charges, stepped back, and used his forearm to shield his eyes. The charge snicked, coughed, and blew the lock core out in a gout of smoke. He kicked the door through its surprise.
Another corridor. Short. At the end, a window. Beyond it—hard white light, chair, cable. Kieran’s profile, stubborn even when empty of motion.
Between Draven and glass: two men. The first raised a rifle.
Draven didn’t fire. He dropped the rifle, took three steps, and was among them, a storm in a coat. He hammered the first man’s forearm with his own, the rifle skittering sideways; drove a palm into the second man’s throat with a casualness that made the body forget how to breathe; took the first man’s head and introduced it firmly to the wall. The second decided to remember his air and reached for a sidearm; Draven broke his wrist and let the gun fall to the floor because gravity was an accomplice he trusted.
Silence. Two bodies making the small terrible sounds they make when they begin to realize they will not be useful again.
Draven stood in front of the window. Kieran didn’t lift his head. Either he couldn’t, or he had decided not to give Soren anything so easy as hope.
Draven put his palm to the glass.
“Kieran,” he said.
The head came up, slow. One eye was swollen, the other bright and very, very awake. For a heartbeat too long, Kieran didn’t react. Then his mouth twitched, the ghost of a grin he’d wear to a funeral.
“About time,” he said. The glass wasn’t a comm. Draven read it in lips as well as in bones.
The lock on the door into the cell block clacked, and Soren’s voice came from the ceiling. Careful, Commander. The wrong step and the chair says hello.
Draven looked up. In the corner: a device that didn’t belong at a talk. Electrodes. Bad ones. Enough to stop a heart that was already limping.
He reached slowly for the doorplate, palmed it open, and stepped into the room with the stillness of a blade sliding into water.
Soren wasn’t here. Of course he wasn’t. He wanted the theater of separation, of glass, of choices.
Kieran’s mouth moved again. Don’t be noble, it said.
“Never am,” Draven said aloud.
The ceiling camera clicked as it adjusted. Soren’s voice softened, mock-sad. I always assumed you had more imagination than this, Commander. But perhaps it’s the uniform. So hard to think sideways in one of those.
Draven said nothing. He moved to Kieran’s side, put one hand against the cable at his wrist, one at the knot. He wasn’t gentle; he was efficient. The cable bit his palm; he didn’t mind the blood.
Ah-ah, Soren sang. There’s a tether on your boy. If his heart jumps too high, if yours does, if I get bored…
Kieran’s laugh was a rasp. “He doesn’t get bored.”
Draven’s gaze flicked to the base of Kieran’s jaw. A shadow under the skin. The little listener, humming its thin song. He wanted to cut it out with his teeth. He didn’t.
“Look at me,” he said. Kieran did. “Breath slow. Even. We stand, we walk. No heroics.”
Kieran’s mouth crooked. “You first.”
Draven’s hands freed the last knot. The cable fell. He went to the ankles, working fast.
The electrodes in the corner ticked. Soren’s voice turned bright. Time’s up.
The device sang.
Kieran jerked. Draven’s hand shot to his chest and found the pulse he’d memorized, pressed, counted. The jolt wasn’t a killing one. Not yet. Soren wanted a dance, not an ending.
Draven rose in a single motion, tore the stinging cables free with a hand that did not care for burns, and slung Kieran up with the other. Kieran’s body folded into him. Draven’s shoulder, already grazed, took the weight and welcomed it.
He looked at the camera and let it see his eyes.
“Door,” he said.
Come take it, Soren purred.
“Gladly.”
Draven put his shoulder into the exit and moved, Kieran’s breath warm against his throat, the room behind them buzzing its discontent. He entered the corridor faster than caution allowed, because caution was the province of men with time.
Rifles barked from the far end. Draven turned, presenting less of Kieran, more of himself, and the rounds thudded into the wall near his ear. He slid Kieran down behind a jut of metal, returned two quick shots, then was moving again, hauling the only thing the galaxy had left him that mattered.
He didn’t look back at the chair. He didn’t look up to see where Soren watched. He only moved forward, toward the part of the ship that looked most like night.
Behind them, the intercom crackled. Soren’s tone had changed—no longer amused, but interested. This is better, he said. Do run, Commander. I do love a chase.
Chapter Seventeen: The Revelation
Chapter Seventeen
The Revelation
The Aegis came back quiet.
Her hull still bore the scorch of the fight, and her engines carried a hitch in their rhythm, a hiccup of strain that no amount of fieldwork could disguise. She didn’t dock with fanfare. She slid into her berth like an animal wounded but alive, clamping down with a hiss of hydraulics.
The crew didn’t cheer. They just let out the kind of breath people forget they’re holding until it’s gone.
On the gangway, Asha stood ramrod straight, her jaw tight, her eyes betraying nothing except the faintest sheen of relief. Behind her, Tovan leaned against a bulkhead, grease already smearing the sleeve he’d wiped across his face.
When Draven descended, supporting Kieran’s weight with a hand braced at his back, neither officer spoke. They only moved, efficient—Asha waving medics forward, Tovan wordlessly pressing a new kit into Draven’s hand before turning away to hide the catch in his throat.
Kieran grinned at them both, crooked and bloodied. “You should see the other guy,” he rasped.
Asha’s mouth didn’t twitch. Tovan’s did, but he said nothing.
The medics fussed. Draven waved them off. “Later. He’s stable. I’ll handle him.”
Asha’s eyes narrowed, but she didn’t argue. She knew better.
⸻
Draven’s quarters were dim, the viewport shutters drawn. The silence here was different than the ship’s hum—closer, softer. The kind of silence that wrapped itself around bruises instead of echoing off them.
He set Kieran down on the couch, careful, but not so careful it felt like pity. Kieran sprawled like he always did, head tilted back, lips parted in a lopsided smile.
“You going to lecture me?” he asked.
Draven sat beside him. His uniform jacket lay abandoned across the desk, his sleeves rolled high. He didn’t answer. He only reached, slow, and touched the cut at Kieran’s temple with the edge of his knuckles.
Kieran caught his wrist, held it there. “Not so bad,” he murmured.
Draven’s hand trembled once. He stilled it by sheer will.
“You don’t get to almost die twice in one campaign,” he said. His voice was low, but the steel in it was meant for himself as much as Kieran.
Kieran huffed, a ghost of laughter. “Guess I like an encore.”
Draven looked at him then—not the grin, not the bravado, but the man beneath. He let his hand slide from temple to jaw, rough calluses brushing over stubble.
Kieran leaned into it. Just enough.
The kiss wasn’t like the last one. It wasn’t teeth and fury, the frantic burn of men trying to prove they were still alive. It was slow, deliberate, a press of lips that lingered until breath grew thin.
Kieran’s hand came up to the back of Draven’s neck, holding him there, as if afraid he’d pull away. Draven didn’t.
When they parted, their foreheads touched. Kieran whispered, “You came for me.”
“I always will,” Draven said.
Neither spoke again for a long while. They didn’t need to.
A chime at the door broke the quiet. Draven lifted his head, eyes narrowing out of habit. Kieran’s hand slid from his neck, reluctant, and he sank back into the couch with a muttered, “If that’s a fundraiser, tell them we gave at the office.”
“It’s Asha,” came the voice through the panel—flat, professional, threaded with something tighter. “And Tovan.”
Draven rose, palmed the door. Asha entered first, crisp as a blade; Tovan followed with a slate tucked under one arm and a coil of wire he’d forgotten he was holding.
Kieran raised two fingers in greeting. “We taking visitors now? Should’ve worn something scandalous.”
“You’re wearing blood,” Asha said. “It’s sufficient.”
Draven gestured them in. The room’s light adjusted, warming from amber to a clearer wash. Asha didn’t sit. She set her slate on the edge of the desk and cast the data to the wall—maps, lanes, ghostly vectors threading the trade spines like veins. Tovan flopped into a low chair with a sigh that ended in a wince.
“Talk,” Draven said.
Asha did. No preamble. “We pulled the signal nets after your… excursion. The listener Soren planted on Vale squealed hard when you breached their den. We traced the echo.”
Kieran’s jaw feathered. “You cut it out yet?”
Tovan held up the coil in his hand as if remembering why he’d brought it. “Got the kit on the bench. We’ll pop it the second we’re done playing show-and-tell.”
Asha zoomed the map. Lanes thickened, then spidered into a lattice that made Kieran’s eyes ache. “This is bigger than the smuggler routes you knew. The Consortium has turned the underbelly into a circulatory system. Not random runs—scheduled, disciplined, disguised as drift. They’re moving components in pieces—fuel here, casing there, guidance seeds tucked into cargo manifests as medical isotope packets.”
Tovan tapped the screen, zooming a route that kinked around the edge of a customs sweep. “And this? This is art. They’re hiding in the noise we all agreed to ignore. Resupply contracts, humanitarian corridors, even pilgrim visas.” He glanced at Kieran, a little sheepish. “Some of these labels are yours, Captain Bad Idea. Vintage Vale. The Consortium just… varnished them.”
Kieran didn’t look away from the map. “They’re using the old tricks because they work.”
“Not just use,” Asha said. “They refined. Every corridor has a redundancy three hops out. You close one, two others swallow the traffic. You force a checkpoint, they shift the manifest tags. They’ve designed their failure options.”
Draven’s voice came quiet. “To what end.”
Asha pinched to another layer, filtering profit out and purpose in. Blue threads faded; red ones glowed. They converged in clusters that pulsed like nodes.
“It isn’t theft,” she said. “It’s staging. They’re assembling something—in multiple locations, under cover of trade—the way you’d assemble a fleet if you didn’t want anyone to see a fleet until it burned you.” She looked at Draven, then Kieran. “They’re not pirates. They’re logisticians.”
Tovan scrubbed a hand over his face. “There’s more. We cracked a handful of their burst packets. Encrypted like a nun, but the headers we could read. The addressees aren’t freelancers. Some are shell companies in good standing with three nations. One is a charity that feeds miners. One’s a research institute. One’s a shipping guild union.”
“Fronts,” Draven said.
“Worse,” Asha said. “Partners. Some of them don’t know what they are. Some do.”
The room went very still.
Kieran pushed himself upright, slow. He studied a knot of routes that curved around Erevos’s orbit like a shy shape. “Show me the choke points.”
Asha blinked. “What.”
“The old black—” he caught himself, jaw tightening. Old reflex. Old pride. He gentled his tone. “The lanes we used when the nav nets were dirty. There are always places you have to funnel, no matter how clever you are. Narrowings. Where the gravity wells pinch or the patrol maps overlap like badly shuffled cards.”
Asha shifted the filter to gravitational overlays. The map bloomed with pale contours—the unseen hills and valleys that ships rode whether they admitted it or not.
Kieran pointed. “Here. The shoulder of the Kheron cluster. Looks like space, acts like a canyon. You can thread it if you know the slope - from there it’s a goddamn graveyard.  Every pilot from here to the expanse horizon has tried it but….” His finger traced the path. “I only know of 2 people that have even come close to getting through. Ugly place—signal echoes, mines that pretend to sleep—but if you ride the static, you’re invisible for twenty klicks. And—” he squinted “—there. Between Erevos and the diplomatic lanes. There’s a harmonics seam we used to joke about. You hit it right, you jump like a fish and no one sees the splash.”
Tovan whistled. “He’s not wrong. Our sensors read noise there. We assume it’s junk. Because it usually is.”
“It’s not junk now,” Asha said. Her jaw was a straight line. “It’s arteries.”
Draven stepped closer to the wall, the map washing his face in cold light. He saw it then—not dots and lines, but intention. Routes feeding hubs, hubs feeding… what? The nodes glowed like coals under ash. If he were planning a killing blow, he would hide the blade in plain sight. He would plant it in commerce and call it mercy.
“What are they building,” he said, not quite a question.
Asha glanced at Tovan.
The engineer made a face like a man who would rather be wrong. “We don’t have the recipe, but we’ve got a taste. Those ‘guidance seeds’ aren’t all guidance. Some are biophotonic mesh.” He raised both hands at Kieran’s look. “I know, I know, that sounds like something a graduate student says to impress a dean, but it’s an actual thing. Light-spun circuits that ‘learn’ signals. Used for fine steering. Or for… other things.”
“Other things,” Draven repeated.
Tovan swallowed. “If you put enough of them in the right places, you can teach a swarm to act like a single mind. Push it. Steer it. Drop it where you want, and it moves as one. We saw prototypes in the war. They were crude. These aren’t.”
Kieran’s smile was small and mean. “A net that pretends it isn’t there until it closes.”
“Over a convoy,” Asha said. “Over a capital. Over us.”
Silence pooled again, deep and cold.
Kieran spoke first. “Soren’s no mastermind. He’s a showman. A herald, like you said.” He nodded at the web. “He dances so we look at him, and under the music somebody counts crates.”
“And somebody above him keeps rhythm,” Asha said. “We’ve been chasing the drummer, not the conductor.”
Draven’s eyes slid to the slate he had left facedown on his desk, the one with orders he hadn’t opened. He thought of Veyr’s polished calm, of the officers with their tidy phrases. He thought of Soren’s smirk filling a feed, of Kieran’s head snapping back under a prod.
“We have to cut it,” he said.
Asha arched a brow. “Where. We cut one string, three more sing.”
“Not strings,” Kieran said, voice thoughtful. “Knots. Places where too much traffic ‘just happens’ to pass. Those are decisions. People made them. People can be… persuaded.”
Asha’s mouth twitched—the closest she came to approval. “We start with a list. Quietly.”
Tovan lifted the coil of wire. “And I start by making our boy less… chatty.” He glanced at Kieran’s jaw. “With your permission, Captain.”
“Not a captain,” Kieran muttered, but he tipped his head, baring the skin where the listener slept.
Draven moved before Tovan could. He cupped Kieran’s jaw in one hand, thumb pressing just below the pulse. The contact was practical—and not. “Steady,” he said. To Kieran. To himself.
Tovan unpacked a penlight, a micro-scanner, a tool that hummed an apologetic note. “This’ll sting.”
Kieran snorted. “Everything does.”
The tool clicked. Heat flared under the skin—bright, animal, gone. The scanner chirped. Tovan held up a sliver on the tip of his tweezers: a bead no bigger than a grain of rice, slick with a smear of blood. He dropped it into a leaded cup and snapped the lid with savage satisfaction.
“Tell your friend to eavesdrop on that,” he said.
Kieran exhaled, shoulders easing an inch. Draven’s hand didn’t leave his jaw. He felt, in the press of bone to palm, the way the tendons there flexed when Kieran swallowed.
Asha dimmed the map. The room felt smaller, realer without it. “This doesn’t end with Soren,” she said. “If we move on his routes, we tip our hand to the ones using him.”
“Then we don’t move on the routes,” Draven said. “We move on the people. Quietly. We cut hands, not strings.”
Kieran’s eyes slid to him, dark and bright together. “You’re thinking like a thief,” he said, not unpleased.
“I’m thinking like a man running out of honest options,” Draven returned.
Tovan blew out a breath. “Whatever we’re thinking, we need a place to think without ears. If the Consortium’s inside the trade guilds, they’re inside the station’s comm loops. Even this room…” He glanced at the ceiling. “I mean, we sweep it, but.”
“We take a walk,” Asha said. “Among the welders. In the noise.”
Draven nodded once. “Give us ten.” He looked to Kieran. “You can walk?”
Kieran flashed his teeth, the old bravado returning just enough to hide its seams. “I can dance.” He pushed to his feet and swayed only a little. Draven’s hand went out—reflex, habit—and Kieran took it without looking, fingers sliding into the grip as if they’d lived there for years.
They didn’t say what all of this meant. Not yet. But the space between them felt shaped for the words when they came.
Chapter Eighteen: Final Battle
Chapter Eighteen
Final Battle
The dying star filled the viewport like a wound in the universe.
Its corona flared wild, unstable, arcs of plasma whipping into the void. Around it drifted the bones of ships—hulks caught in the star’s gravity, slowly pulled into their burning grave.
And beyond that light, waiting like predators just outside the fire, came the Consortium fleet.
Five warships, sleek and black, with escorts in tight formation. Their hulls gleamed with new paint and cruel geometry. They didn’t look like smugglers’ ships. They looked like weapons built for one purpose: to break what stood against them.
On the bridge of the Aegis, the crew held their silence.
Asha’s hands gripped the tactical console, every line of her body strung tight. Tovan muttered over the comms from engineering, his voice already raw. Medics had begged Kieran to stay in the infirmary, but here he was in the pilot’s chair, shirt clinging damp to his back, grin stretched thin as wire.
And Draven, standing in the command dais, silver eyes fixed on the enemy.
“This is it,” he said, voice calm but carrying iron. “No patrols. No command to save us. If they break us here, Erevos is theirs.”
Asha didn’t look up. “Then we don’t break.”
The first Consortium salvo came without warning—a spear of light across the void. The Aegis lurched, shields shrieking. Kieran laughed through clenched teeth and drove them into a dive that skirted the star’s reach. Plasma arcs licked at their hull like hungry tongues.
“Close,” Tovan swore. “Too close!”
“Exactly close enough,” Kieran shot back. His fingers flew, coaxing the ship through turbulence that made the frame groan. “They won’t follow us in—not all the way. They’re too polished to risk getting dirty.”
Draven’s voice cut through. “Then we make the fire our ally.”
Asha’s eyes snapped to him. “Commander—”
“Load everything,” Draven ordered. “Pattern scatter. When they adjust, we fold and strike the flank.”
“They’ll see it,” Asha warned.
“They’ll see what they think is us,” Draven countered. He turned his gaze on Kieran. “Can you thread the star’s wake?”
Kieran’s grin was pure madness. “Thought you’d never ask.”
⸻
The Aegis dove deeper, weaving through the dying star’s fury. Arcs of plasma streaked across the viewport, each one enough to vaporize a cruiser whole. The Consortium ships held back, herding, cautious.
“Shields at thirty!” Tovan shouted.
“They’ll hold,” Draven said, though his knuckles whitened on the rail.
Asha fired in volleys—plasma bursts flaring wide, not to hit but to mislead. The Consortium tightened, shifting formation. The ghost ship at the lead hung back, watching, waiting.
Soren’s voice crackled over the comms, smooth as always: “Running again, Kieran? How many times must you make me chase you before you learn?”
Kieran barked a laugh, wild. “You still talk too much, sweetheart.” He cut the comm before Soren could reply.
The star’s corona roared. A ribbon of flame whipped across their bow. Kieran rolled the ship under it, plating shrieking, inertia hurling them sideways. Crew slammed into harnesses, teeth rattled.
And then—
“Now!” Draven shouted.
Asha fired the scatter volley, a storm of plasma bolts arcing into the void. The Consortium spread wide to intercept, shields flaring brilliant.
Kieran yanked them sideways into the star’s wake. The Aegis vanished into light and fire.
⸻
The star’s wake was a graveyard of fire.
Heat shimmered against the Aegis’ hull, hull plating keening under stress. Alarms wailed, then choked out as Tovan killed them one by one—better to fly deaf than panic. Every panel on the bridge glowed red, warning after warning screaming the same truth: too close, too fast.
“Frame integrity at sixty!” Tovan bellowed from engineering. “She’s holding, but you’re making her mad!”
“She likes me better that way,” Kieran muttered, teeth bared. Sweat streaked down his temple, his hands sure on the yoke as the ship bucked. He flew by instinct, not instruments—the sensors were blind here, blinded by stellar fury. Only a man who’d cheated every law of physics twice already could make sense of it.
Draven stood at his shoulder, voice steady as stone. “Three ships trailing. They think we’re crippled.”
“They’re half-right,” Asha said through gritted teeth. Her hands worked the tactical grid, painting vectors in ghost-light. “If we don’t punch out soon, they’ll pin us against the corona.”
Kieran glanced at Draven. “Say the word.”
Draven’s eyes narrowed, silver catching firelight. He pictured the enemy formation, pictured the rhythm of their chase. Soren’s dance. Always circling. Always arrogant.
“Wait,” he said.
The Aegis dove lower. Plasma plumes erupted above them, heat washing the hull in sheets of flame. The star’s roar filled every frequency, a constant scream of dying light.
Asha swore under her breath. “This is suicide.”
Kieran’s grin widened. “Best kind of foreplay.”
Draven ignored them both. His gaze tracked the formation tightening, tightening, until— “Now. Hard burn.”
Kieran slammed the thrusters. The Aegis surged upward, straight through the turbulence, shields flaring like a comet’s skin. For one impossible heartbeat, they were nothing but fire.
Then they broke free, behind the lead ship’s flank.
Asha didn’t wait. She fired everything. Torpedoes screamed, plasma lanced, scattershot chewed into shields already stretched thin by the star’s interference.
The lead ship reeled, hull lighting up with explosions. Its escorts scrambled, caught off guard.
Kieran whooped, wild, dragging them through the opening like a knife through silk. “Got you, bastard—”
The retaliation came fast. The ghost ship itself, Soren’s pride, swung wide and bared its teeth. A beam of raw energy tore across the void, slamming into the Aegis.
The bridge shook like a beast in chains. Panels burst. Asha went down hard, catching herself against the rail. Tovan’s voice roared through the comms: “We lost shield grid Beta! I can’t reroute, she’s fried!”
“Engines?” Draven barked.
“Alive—for now!”
Smoke filled the bridge. Kieran coughed, swore, and still kept his hands steady. His jaw was set, eyes blazing. “We’re not done. Not by half.”
Draven laid a hand on his shoulder—firm, grounding. “Then finish it.”
Outside, the battlefield writhed. The Consortium ships regrouped, their formation adjusting, circling like wolves. The dying star burned behind them all, a reminder of what awaited the losers.
The Aegis was bleeding. But she was still in the fight.
The bridge stank of ozone and burned plastic. Smoke curled from ruptured panels; sparks rained in brief, bitter flares. Every heartbeat felt borrowed.
On the viewport, the Consortium fleet shifted formation, the black shapes elegant and merciless. The ghost ship hovered at the center, its cloak flickering, hull gleaming with cruel geometry. It didn’t rush. It didn’t need to.
Kieran spat blood onto the floor, knuckles white on the yoke. “They’re circling up for the kill.”
Asha pulled herself upright, one sleeve torn, hair matted with soot. Her voice was steady, though it cost her. “Shields gone. Hull integrity falling. We’ve got one volley left.”
Tovan’s voice cracked through comms, ragged. “Engines are coughing their last, Commander. One more burn like that and she folds in half.”
Draven’s gaze stayed fixed on the ghost ship, his silver eyes unblinking. “Then one more is all we need.”
Kieran barked a laugh that was half hysteria, half thrill. “You’re speaking my language.”
Asha’s head snapped toward him. “Are you both insane? We don’t have the weight for a stand-up fight. We’re gnats against a furnace.”
Draven turned to her, voice quiet but sharp enough to cut. “Not if we choose where the furnace burns.”
She stared, then understood. Her throat worked. “The star.”
Draven nodded. “We drag them closer. Force them into the corona. Their shields won’t last.”
“They’ll see it coming,” Asha said.
“They’ll see us failing,” Draven corrected. “Because that’s what they expect.”
Kieran’s grin sharpened, blood on his teeth. “I can sell that. Sell it so hard they’ll be lining up for the feast.”
⸻
The Aegis staggered into the star’s embrace. Hull plates glowed red; the deck trembled with each flare of the dying sun. Kieran flew her like a drunk weaving through traffic, listing, stuttering, bleeding smoke into the void.
The Consortium moved in. Tightened their jaws.
Soren’s voice purred over comms, low and venomous. “Always the gambler, Kieran. Always bluffing with nothing in your hand.”
Kieran licked his lips, eyes wild. “Funny thing about bluffing, love—it only has to work once.”
Draven’s hand gripped the rail, steady. He felt the heat bleed through his boots, through the ship’s bones, into his own. He whispered, so soft only Kieran heard: “Now.”
Kieran killed the engines.
The Aegis dropped, limp, dead weight against the star’s pull. Alarms howled, the hull shrieked, the crew felt their stomachs lurch as the ship buckled toward the corona.
The Consortium ships followed, hunger overcoming caution.
And then, with a scream of tortured metal, Kieran reignited the drives—full burn, against gravity, against sense. The Aegis leapt sideways, skimming the corona so close fire danced across her plates.
The Consortium’s lead wasn’t so lucky. It followed into the wake—and the star claimed it. Plasma lashed, shields crumpled, hull burst. The explosion painted the void in white and red.
“Gotcha,” Kieran whispered, voice breaking.
The shockwave rippled through the field, scattering escorts. The Aegis burst from the firestorm, battered but alive, cutting across the enemy’s exposed flank.
Asha unleashed their last volley. Torpedoes screamed into fractured shields, ripping through weakened hulls. One escort went up in a blossom of fire; another spun out, crippled.
The ghost ship remained. Watching. Waiting.
Soren’s voice came again, sharper now. “You think you’ve won? This is only the beginning.”
Draven bared his teeth. “Then let’s end your beginning.”
The wreck of the escort drifted, burning. The star’s corona swallowed the debris with hungry light.
Only the ghost ship remained—sleek, arrogant, untouched except for a few scars glowing faint along its hull. It slid through the chaos like a predator unfazed by blood in the water.
On the bridge of the Aegis, the crew held their breath. Systems screamed warnings, the hull groaned like an old wound reopened, but no one spoke.
Draven’s eyes locked on that black shape. “One chance.”
Kieran’s grin was half-mad, half-exultant. “Then let’s make it a good one.”
Asha’s hands hovered above the tactical grid. “Weapons dry. All we’ve got left are point-defense and—”
“—and the ship herself,” Draven finished. His voice was iron, unyielding. “We ram them.”
The silence hit like a blow.
Tovan’s voice cracked through comms, ragged, furious. “Ram them? She’s barely holding together—”
“She’ll hold long enough,” Draven cut. His tone brokered no argument.
Kieran’s laughter startled them all—low, wild, sharp. “Finally. Something I can believe in.” He shoved the yoke forward, sweat dripping from his jaw. “Hang on, darlings.”
⸻
The Aegis surged, every system screaming, lights strobing red. She lunged across the void, battered engines clawing for speed. The ghost ship shifted to meet her, its sleek hull turning, weapon spines flaring.
“Point-defense online!” Asha barked. The bridge lit with fire as the Aegis’ last guns spat defiance, intercepting salvo after salvo.
Still, the ghost ship’s weapons tore into them—hull breaches flaring on decks four and six, atmosphere venting into the void. The Aegis shuddered, plates tearing, frame bowing under punishment.
“Close! Give me close!” Draven roared.
Kieran’s hands flew. He dove them under the ghost ship’s barrage, weaving through fire like a dancer through knives. Hull plating peeled away; alarms shrieked. But the Aegis came on. Closer. Closer.
Draven’s voice was steady. “Asha. On my mark—vent everything.”
She didn’t question. Her hands hovered, ready.
The ghost ship loomed, its guns tilting for the killing blow.
“Mark!”
Asha hit the command. The Aegis vented every scrap of fuel and plasma left in her scorched belly—an incandescent bloom that blinded the sensors of the ghost ship for one precious heartbeat.
Kieran drove them through it.
The Aegis scraped the enemy’s hull, claws of torn plating shrieking against steel. Sparks showered the void. Kieran twisted hard, and Draven’s hand slammed down on the manual release.
The last torpedo—wired dirty, jury-rigged, unstable—shot free point-blank into the ghost ship’s exposed flank.
For a moment, the void froze.
Then the ghost ship tore open in a blossom of white fire.
The blast hurled the Aegis spinning into the star’s glare, her systems failing one by one, but the enemy was gone—pieces scattering, burning, swallowed by the corona.
Silence filled the bridge. Shaking, raw, but alive.
Asha slumped against her console, breath ragged. “Tell me we… we just did that.”
Tovan’s laugh cracked, wild and broken. “We did it. By the gods, we did it.”
Kieran sagged back in his chair, grin split bloody. He turned his head toward Draven. “Told you I could thread it.”
Draven’s hand closed hard on his shoulder. He didn’t speak. He only let the pressure say everything words couldn’t.
⸻
They made it back to dock on fumes, half the ship sealed off, systems held together with prayer and wire. The med crews swarmed, the mechanics too, but Draven ignored them all. He guided Kieran off the bridge himself, one arm firm around him, and didn’t stop until the hatch to his quarters sealed behind them.
For a long moment, the silence pressed. Only the sound of their ragged breathing filled the room.
Then Kieran laughed—a hoarse, broken sound. “We lived.”
Draven’s hand was on him before he’d finished the words, crushing him back against the bulkhead, mouth hard and desperate. Kieran met it with equal ferocity, teeth and tongue, hands clawing at uniform and skin.
It wasn’t gentle. It wasn’t slow. It was release, catharsis, proof that they were still flesh, still blood, still theirs.
Kieran gasped as Draven tore his shirt, lips marking bruises down his throat. “Mine,” Draven growled, voice rough with something that wasn’t command but something darker, needier.
Kieran arched, grabbed his jaw, kissed him back hard enough to bruise. “Yours. But only because I choose it.”
Clothes scattered. Bodies collided. The room filled with heat, with the sound of skin against skin, with the rawness of men who had faced death and refused to give it the last word.
It was rough, it was urgent, it was a battle of its own—fought with mouths and hands and desperate hunger. And when it broke, when they collapsed together slick with sweat and shuddering breath, it wasn’t silence that filled the room.
It was the steady thrum of two hearts, still beating.
Chapter Nineteen: Choices
Chapter Nineteen
Choices
The Aegis slept in dock, her scars unhidden. Welders crawled her hull, patching plates that would never look the same, sealing wounds that would always creak in the dark. She had survived—but survival carried a price that gleamed in every uneven seam.
Inside, the crew staggered through a new rhythm. Asha drilled them like soldiers awaiting parade inspection, barking orders with clipped precision. Tovan rebuilt systems with manic laughter and blistered hands, swearing that “next time she’ll fly like sin itself.” They worked not because anyone ordered it, but because work was easier than stillness.
Draven found stillness anyway, because it was the one thing he couldn’t escape.
The promotion offer sat on his desk like a weapon dressed in velvet: Fleet Command, Rear Admiral Draven Raleth. A seat among brass and strategists, far from the bridge of a single battered ship. Far from the fire. Far from Kieran.
He hadn’t opened the file, but the weight of it pressed on him. He imagined Asha’s face if he accepted—pride sharp as a knife, though her silence would carry more. He imagined Tovan’s laugh, hollow at the edges. He imagined Kieran, leaning against the viewport, cigarette burning down to nothing, saying, So you finally traded the stars for a desk, Commander.
He wanted to crush the file under his fist. He wanted to pretend it didn’t exist.
The door slid open behind him. He didn’t turn. He didn’t have to.
“You look like a man planning his own funeral,” Kieran drawled.
Draven exhaled. “Do I.”
“You do.” Kieran strolled in, barefoot, hair still damp from a shower that smelled faintly of station soap. He wore loose trousers and nothing else, scars painted across his chest like medals he never asked for. He dropped onto the couch, sprawled as if the room was his. “So tell me, Commander. Do I bring flowers, or whiskey?”
Draven looked at him, and the tightness in his chest eased despite himself.
⸻
Kieran saw the slate on the desk. His grin tilted. “Ah. That explains the doom. They offering you a shinier chair?”
“Admiral,” Draven said flatly.
“Of course they are.” Kieran leaned back, arms spread across the cushions. “You’ve been saving their asses since before they learned to sit straight in uniform. So what’s the problem?”
Draven’s jaw tightened. “The problem is I don’t want it.”
Kieran’s grin faltered. Just a fraction. “Then don’t take it.”
“It’s not that simple.”
“It’s exactly that simple.”
Draven crossed the room, stood over him. “They’ll call it insubordination. Waste. They’ll strip the Aegis, scatter the crew.”
“And you’ll still be you.” Kieran’s gaze was steady, dark and unflinching. “Silver-eyed bastard who doesn’t know how to quit. You think Asha follows you because of your rank? You think Tovan bleeds into your engines because of your badge? You think I—” He broke off, shook his head. “You’re not your stripes, Draven. Never were.”
Draven’s throat worked. His hands curled at his sides, fists against the truth of it.
Kieran’s smile returned, slow and wicked. “But if you want the desk, take it. I won’t stop you. I’ll just steal your ship out from under you and see how long it takes before you come chasing me again.”
Draven barked a laugh, sharp and unexpected. “You would.”
“Of course I would.”
Their eyes held, the space between them charged with everything unspoken.
————-
Kieran stretched on the couch, catlike, lazy on the surface but coiled beneath. “You know what I keep thinking about?” he asked, eyes fixed on the ceiling.
Draven didn’t answer. He wasn’t sure he wanted to hear it.
Kieran’s voice dropped, quiet now. “How easy it’d be to walk away. Station’s full of ships that don’t ask questions. I could vanish before dawn-cycle. New name, new run, new scars. I’ve done it before.”
Draven’s breath caught in his chest. “And you would leave the Aegis. The crew.”
“Wouldn’t be the first time I left something burning,” Kieran snarled. He tilted his head, eyes gleaming with that dangerous grin. “But that’s not the question your really asking.”
Draven turned into the shadows to mask an obvious blush. Damnit, he’s got me pegged like a goddamn lapdog… “Sonofabitch…” the words slipped out loud… accidental but absolute.
“Excuse me!?” Kieran jumped back, half laughing, half ready to pounce.
“Nothing,” Draven ditched out. “Besides, I don’t know what the fuck your asking me.”
Kieran had him cornered now. What fun! “No darling, you seem to be getting a bit confused. Your the one asking question. Let me help you. The question is really whether I would leave you. That’s what you want to know. What you need to know.”
Draven stepped forward before he knew he was moving. He stood over him, shadow falling across Kieran’s sprawled form. “Say it! Say the words. Tell me what I already goddamn know! Say it, again and again… Make me hear it.”
Kieran smirked up at him. “What, that I’d run?”
Draven’s voice was low, rough. “Say it again.”
Kieran’s grin faltered, but only to sharpen. “Yeah…”
Draven pressed. “Yeah? Yeah what? Say it Vale!”
Kieran sat up, as if to spit the words in his face. “I’d run.”
The reality was untouchably stark. Not to be looked at for long, but neither of them could take their eyes off the other. Locked in shame and need; in lust and terror, the moment held, floated frozen in mid ache until…
The world snapped.
Draven’s hands were on him, dragging him up from the couch, slamming him back against the wall. The kiss was savage, bruising, a collision of teeth and breath. Kieran gasped, laughed into it, then bit back just as hard.
“Mine,” Draven growled against his mouth.
Kieran’s head tipped back, throat bared in challenge. “Prove it.”
Draven’s lips crashed down on his neck, biting, marking. Kieran arched, groan breaking free as teeth sank into skin just hard enough to bruise, to claim. His hands fisted in Draven’s shirt, yanking it open, buttons scattering across the floor.
“Thought admirals weren’t allowed to get dirty,” Kieran rasped, voice hoarse.
“I’m not an admiral,” Draven snarled. His mouth dragged lower, bruising trails across chest and shoulder, claiming every inch. “I’m yours.”
Kieran’s laugh turned ragged. “That’s cheating—” He broke off as Draven bit harder, groan turning into something guttural, wordless.
They moved together rough, frantic, tearing at clothes, at patience, at every wall left standing. The room filled with the sound of skin and breath, the sharp crack of bodies colliding with bulkhead and floor.
Kieran gasped, nails raking Draven’s back. “Mark me,” he demanded, voice breaking. “Leave it where they’ll see.”
Draven’s mouth crushed his again, teeth sinking into his lower lip, copper tang blooming between them. “Done.”
The room smelled of sweat and ozone, of burned circuits and bruised skin. Clothes lay in a trail from couch to wall to floor. The couch itself sagged sideways, one leg cracked from the violence of bodies that hadn’t cared about furniture.
Kieran lay on his back, chest heaving, skin flushed with heat and marked by teeth and hands. A constellation of bruises already darkened his throat, his shoulder, the sharp line of his collarbone. He traced one lazily with his fingertips, smirking through swollen lips.
“Admiral, hm?” he murmured, voice low and rasped. “Can’t exactly picture you giving speeches like this.”
Draven sat beside him, back against the wall, sweat running down his temple. His chest rose and fell in steady rhythm, every scar lit by the low amber glow of the cabin lights. He looked less like a commander and more like a man stripped down to the truth.
“I’m not taking it,” Draven said.
Kieran rolled his head to study him. “That simple?”
“Yes.”
“You’ll make enemies.”
Draven’s mouth curved in something too thin to be a smile. “Already have.”
Kieran huffed a laugh. Then, softer: “And if I’d run?”
Draven turned, eyes catching his in the half-light. “I would have hunted you down. Across stations, across sectors. You wouldn’t have liked how it ended.”
Kieran’s grin softened, turned dangerous in another way. “Maybe I would.”
The silence that followed was thick, but not uncomfortable. Draven reached, slow, and brushed hair from Kieran’s temple where blood had dried in a thin line. His hand lingered.
“You keep tempting fate,” he said quietly.
“That’s my charm,” Kieran murmured.
“No.” Draven’s thumb pressed the hollow of his throat, feeling the pulse there. “This is.”
Kieran swallowed, leaned into the touch despite himself. His smirk faded, replaced with something rawer. “So what are we, then?”
Draven didn’t answer right away. He let the question hang, let it settle between them. Then he said, simply, “Necessary.”
Kieran closed his eyes. For once, he didn’t argue.
————
The chime at the door came soft, almost hesitant. Draven didn’t answer. He was still on the floor beside Kieran, both of them half-dressed, skin gleaming in the low light.
The chime came again, sharper. Asha’s voice followed, clipped as ever. “Commander. Urgent.”
Draven exhaled through his nose. He rose, pulling his shirt on without buttoning it, and palmed the door.
Asha entered like a storm held tightly in its sheath. She didn’t look at his state of dress, didn’t acknowledge Kieran sprawled on the couch with a grin sharp enough to cut. She went straight to the desk, dropped a slate onto it hard enough that it rang.
“They’re inside,” she said.
Draven’s eyes narrowed. “Explain.”
She tapped the slate. Files opened: transmissions, routing orders, trade manifests. “Consortium has infiltration beyond smugglers. Inside the fleet. Inside our own supply lines. We’ve been carrying their crates for months, calling them aid shipments. High command either doesn’t see it—or doesn’t want to.”
Kieran sat up, the grin fading. “Which means if we push, they already know where and how.”
Asha’s jaw flexed. “Which means there’s rot. And we can’t cut it with orders alone.”
The weight of her words settled heavy.
Behind her, Tovan appeared in the doorway, hair a wreck, eyes bloodshot. He waved a datachip like it was burning his fingers. “Also, funny story. You remember the debt markers I told you I burned on Cindral Station? Turns out they’re not burned. Turns out the crime lord who held them is still breathing and would very much like me to settle up—preferably with Consortium tech.”
Kieran snorted. “You do pick your friends.”
“Look who’s talking,” Tovan shot back, then looked at Draven. “Point is, they’ll come knocking. Might already be.”
The air in the room thickened, heavy with new truths.
And then—because fate didn’t waste time—the comm on Draven’s desk crackled. A voice, unmistakable, smooth even through static.
Soren.
“You didn’t think I’d burn with my ship, did you, Kieran? No, darling. I walked away with something much prettier. And I’ll be seeing you very soon.”
The comm cut, leaving silence that hummed like a wound.
Kieran’s fists clenched, knuckles white. Draven’s face was carved from steel.
Asha looked between them both. “Then it begins.”
Draven nodded once. “Then it begins.”
Chapter Twenty: Seeds of Tomorrow
Chapter Twenty
Seeds of Tomorrow
Erevos Station turned at a slow, regal spin, bands of light wreathed in the black. From a distance she looked whole. Up close, the seams showed: new welds bright as scars, repair drones stinging her skin, a faint pulse of warning beacons that hadn’t been there last month.
Inside, the alarms were polite. That was how you knew something was wrong—they were trying not to frighten anyone.
Asha stood at the central holopool in Command, one palm braced on steel, the other coaxing maps into shape. She’d stripped the traffic grid down to bones. Lanes, permits, customs beacons—gone. What remained were movements: pulses of mass, whispers of heat, patterns that ships made when they thought no one watched.
“Again,” Draven said quietly.
She ran it again. The web drew itself ghost-blue, threads sliding, knots glowing. Kieran stood at his shoulder, chin lifted, the old gambler’s concentration tightening his mouth. On a lower tier, Tovan swore softly to himself, soldering an adapter that no one had approved.
“There,” Kieran said, fingertip touching a flicker between two nodes. “They’re ghosting a convoy through a maintenance vector. Not big ships. Too many small ones. Like… like a school of fish pretending to be a whale.”
Asha pinched the scale. The image resolved. Not ships. Pods. Dozens—no, hundreds—of thumb-sized signatures riding the station’s thermal vents.
Tovan’s soldering iron stilled. “Oh, that’s ugly.”
Asha’s voice flattened. “Biophotonic mesh. The swarm.”
Draven’s knuckles went white on the rail. The horror wasn’t in what it could destroy; it was in how quietly it could do it. A cloud of learning light, seeding itself in circuitry, teaching itself the station’s rhythms until an off-switch looked like a suggestion instead of a command.
“How long?” he asked.
Asha studied the drift, the rate of spread, the gentle curve of the swarm’s approach toward the inner rings and—her mouth tightened—Command power. “Hours. Maybe less.”
“Command knows?” Kieran asked.
Draven and Asha looked at each other. They didn’t answer.
The holopool flared amber with an incoming priority link. Commander Veyr’s face filled the air—polished, un-rumpled, eyes calm in the way of a man who had never spent a night next to a hissing reactor.
“Commander Draven,” he said, voice even. “You and your crew are relieved.”
Asha didn’t blink. “On what grounds.”
“On mine,” Veyr said. “You’ve compromised security by engaging in unauthorized actions against civilian partners.”
Kieran laughed, soft and mean. “Civilian partners. That’s a gorgeous costume for a wolf.”
Veyr didn’t look at him. “You will remain on-station while a board reviews your conduct. The Aegis will be remanded to Fleet.”
Behind Veyr, the ghost of a reflection in glass—someone standing still as a blade. Silver hair. A tilt of the head that reeked of contempt even blurred by distance.
Soren.
Kieran stepped closer to the holo. “You just can’t stay dead, can you.”
Veyr’s gaze cut, finally, to Kieran. It held something like distaste, and a trace of fear. “Security will escort you to holding. Command out.”
The link snapped to black.
Silence. For half a heartbeat.
Then Asha said, very gently, “Orders?”
Draven’s pulse pounded in his throat. His mind catalogued the facts with the cold precision of a soldier too long at war: the swarm riding the vents; Command compromised or cowardly; Soren standing in a reflection where only officers should stand; a crew that would follow him into hell if he pointed.
He didn’t point at hell. He pointed home.
“Get to the Aegis,” he said. “All of you. Anyone loyal, anyone with hands. Tovan, prep a purge—thermal and EM. I want that swarm off our skin before it learns the shape of our heart.”
Tovan was already running, tools clattering. “On it, on it, on it—”
“Asha—” Draven’s eyes flicked to her, to the holopool, to the swelling pattern in the station’s bones. “We can’t clean Erevos without burning her. But we can keep the fire off our decks.”
Asha’s jaw clenched. “And the station?”
“We warn every captain who’ll listen,” Kieran said, dark and bright together. “The rest… we give them teeth.”
“How,” Asha demanded, but she was already moving, stripping the map, caching it to a drive in her sleeve. Her gaze flicked to Draven, asking a different question with her eyes.
Are we running? Or are we staying to die tidy?
“We’re leaving,” Draven said. The words scraped his throat. “We come back when we can win.”
Asha nodded once. It cost her more than she would ever admit. “I’ll get as many off as I can.”
They moved.
The station seemed to feel it—some subtle shift in intent, a change in the flow of bodies. Doors decided to stick; lifts decided to pause. Polite alarms grew less polite. Somewhere deep, bulkheads closed where they shouldn’t. The swarm learned the pattern of their footsteps and drifted like dust toward warmth.
Kieran felt the hairs lift on his arms as they cut across a maintenance bay. The lights hummed. The air smelled sweet, faintly wrong.
“Here,” Tovan called over comms, breathless. “I’ve got a microburster keyed to the Aegis’ hull frequency. Ten-meter skirt. Anything with biophotonic signatures gets cooked if it tries to come aboard. Possibly also anyone standing too close without pants.”
Asha replied dryly, “We’ll keep our trousers on.”
“Speak for yourself,” Kieran said, wincing as the door at Deck C jammed halfway. He shoved a pry bar through the gap, muscles screaming. Draven stepped in beside him, shoulder against steel, their bodies a single lever. The door gave with a shriek.
They spilled into the docking gantry—and into an armed line of station security.
Guns lifted. Boots braced. Faces closed.
A captain Kieran half-remembered from a card table stared down the barrel at Draven. “Stand down.”
“No,” Draven said.
The captain swallowed. His rifle didn’t lower. Behind him, rank upon rank—too many for a clean fight. The gantry was a bottleneck by design; it made ship seizures practical and dramatic. Kieran could admire the architecture and still hate it.
Asha stepped forward, hands empty. “There’s a contagion in the vents. You’ve seen the glitch in the lights. That isn’t wiring. You stand here long enough and your guns will decide they don’t need you.”
“Propaganda,” the captain said, but the word wavered. A light above him stuttered in a rhythm that wasn’t random.
Kieran threw his voice low and intimate. “Look at your rifle’s indicator, mate.”
The man glanced. The charge glyph blinked… and then blinked again, off-beat. A learning circuit, misfiring as something curious crawled through it.
Fear cracked his discipline. It was enough.
Draven moved like a blade. He didn’t shoot. He spoke, voice cutting the air with command that remembered saving this man’s life twice over a decade of siege. “Fall back. That’s an order.”
Reflex took the rifle a fraction lower. Asha used the gap like a key, sliding through with liquid grace, knocking a barrel aside with two fingers and a promise of pain. Kieran smiled at another guard with all the teeth in the world and said, “We’re doing you a kindness,” even as he robbed him of his weapon with a flourish.
Tovan’s voice: “Ramp’s hot. Come kiss the girl.”
They ran.
They crossed onto the Aegis as the swarm reached the gantry proper. It looked like dust until it didn’t—until it glittered, a cloud of microscopic knives tasting new air. The Aegis’ hull shuddered as Tovan’s burster lit, a halo rippling, the cloud hitting it and flaring like frost on a window before it burned to nothing.
“Close!” Asha snapped.
The hatch sealed. Heat rolled through the plating and was gone.
The ship woke.
Even maimed and mended, she breathed like a living thing, systems syncing, coils humming, lights rising from red to ready white. Aegis-class ships were designed for sudden flight. None had been designed to flee their own home.
“Lines cut,” Asha reported from tactical. “We’re on internal power. External clamps—”
The clamps didn’t release.
“Dock still has us,” she said, voice flint.
Kieran slid into the pilot’s chair, body remembering every ache and ignoring it. “Tovan, I need those claws off my girl.”
“Working on it!” he sang, somewhere between terror and glee. “If I overcharge grid gamma, we can convince the clamps to forgot they like us.”
“Convince them faster,” Draven said. “Asha—broadcast narrow-band on worker loops. No encryption. No rank. Just truth.”
Her eyes flicked; she obeyed without comment.
Draven leaned toward the mic, voice steady, intimate. “This is Draven. There’s a contamination in the vents. If you can get to a ship, do. If you can’t, isolate power to your compartments, cut every nonessential circuit. Take your families and go to ground. This isn’t cowardice. This is survival.”
Kieran watched the station through the viewport as he listened to Draven make promises men needed to hear. Welders froze mid-spark. A child on a mezzanine pressed a hand to the glass. Across the gulf, a tug cut its lines and spun free, engine coughing, then catching. Lights went out, one ring at a time, not in fear but in strategy. Erevos dimmed like a giant holding her breath.
The clamps groaned. Tovan whooped. “They love me, they love me—oh gods they hate me—okay they love me again. Free!”
The Aegis lurched. Kieran took her weight, engines low, subtle, so as not to draw fire from guns that might still pick targets of their own accord. He eased her out, as gentle as disentangling himself from a lover who needed to sleep. The gantry loomed, then fell away. Erevos filled the world—beautiful and wounded, her lights blinking messages in the old dockhand code: GO / QUIET / COME / BACK.
“Course?” Asha asked.
Draven didn’t answer. Not yet. He stared at the station the way a man looks at the house he grew up in when it is on fire. He kept his face calm because everyone else had their eyes on him; his hands were shaking where no one could see.
Kieran reached, without looking, and hooked his pinky around Draven’s on the rail. A stupid, secret touch. A necessary one.
“Vector Kheron shoulder,” Draven said at last. “We vanish in the canyon Vale named. We find the knots. We cut hands.”
Asha’s mouth tilted at one corner. “A thief’s plan in a commander’s mouth.”
“Adaptation,” Draven said.
“Growth,” Kieran corrected, grin crooking despite the ache in his chest.
They were almost clear when the comm spat a tone that tasted like blood: Veyr again, voice calm, a little pleased.
“Commander Draven, you are fleeing under arms. This is treason. Return to dock or be designated hostile.”
Asha’s fingers hovered over the weapons grid. Tovan swore softly. Kieran’s shoulders tensed.
Draven exhaled. “Ignore.”
“Sir,” Asha warned, eyes flicking to the defense grid spooling up along the ring. “If Command has hands on the batteries—”
Another voice cut across Veyr’s. Smooth as ever. Soren.
“Let him go,” he purred, not bothering to hide himself now. “Let him take his little ship and his little crew and his very pretty smuggler. They’ll bring back stories. And presents.”
Kieran leaned toward the mic without permission and smiled like a knife. “Leave a light on, sweetheart. We’ll be back.”
Soren’s laughter filled the channel, and for a heartbeat Kieran could see him—lounging behind Veyr’s shoulder, fingers resting on the rail, eyes bright with triumph. “Take care on the Kheron shoulder, darling. The canyon has… new walls.”
The channel cut. The defense grid did not fire.
Erevos let them go.
For now.
They cleared the ring. The black took them, huge and cold and full of places to disappear. A breath—one, two, three—and the crew’s voices rose in the old ritual, not a cheer, not a prayer, but a promise to the ship and to each other.
Then the aft hatch alarm chimed.
Asha’s head snapped up. “We’ve got a breach.”
Tovan’s voice layered over hers, suddenly small. “Engineering hatch—port-side secondary. I locked it. I swear I locked—”
The lights dimmed for a fraction of a heartbeat, then steadied.
“Seal it,” Draven ordered, already moving.
“I can’t,” Tovan said, and there was something in his voice Draven had never heard. Not fear. Recognition. “Someone jammed it… from the inside.”
Kieran was on his feet before his seat knew he’d left. “Soren.”
Asha’s hands flew. Internal cams fizzed to life, one after another. Empty corridor. Empty storage. A shadow at the edge of a frame—too quick, too smooth. Another angle caught a boot, a smear of blood like a fingerprint dragged across a wall.
And then: a figure, stepping into full view at last in a maintenance crossway, head cocked, smile lazy, coat immaculate despite the crawlspace.
Soren lifted two fingers in salute to the nearest lens.
The band on his wrist glinted. In his other hand, something small and metallic dripped a thread of biolight—like a bead of liquid star.
Tovan swore, once, like a man being told the sea had claws. “He’s holding a seed.”
Asha’s voice was a wire pulled too tight. “That’s the ‘prettier thing’ he walked away with.”
The intercom by the crossway crackled. Soren didn’t lean in. He didn’t have to. The ship carried him.
“Miss me?”
Kieran moved—reckless, lethal, half-ready to tear open bulkheads with his bare hands.
Draven caught him by the wrist. Not to stop him. To tether him. “We do this together.”
Kieran’s stomach dropped and settled; his grin was all teeth. “Family style.”
Asha’s gaze flicked between the men, between the cams, between the map blooming fresh in her mind of her own ship turned into a field with a fox in it. “He got aboard in dock,” she said. “He knew our purge radius. He waited.”
Tovan looked sick. “If he seeds that thing into our coils, she’ll learn our heartbeat faster than I can scramble it.”
The deck hummed, a note too low to hear and impossible to ignore. The Aegis felt the threat in her bones.
Draven lifted his chin, met the nearest lens as if he could look through it and into Soren’s eyes. “You’re in my house,” he said. “You will not leave it.”
The reply was laughter that sounded too much like a promise.
The Aegis swung her nose to the dark lanes of Kheron as if she couldn’t help herself, as if escape were the only thing that made sense. Inside her skin, under her ribs, a duel began that no one on deck could see: light learning circuits, hands learning each other’s tells, a hunter and a hunted trading places with every blink.
“Seal compartments behind him,” Asha snapped. “Bleed atmosphere two rooms ahead. Tovan—build me a cage that sings his seed to sleep.”
“On it,” Tovan breathed, already tearing panels open.
Kieran checked the charge on his pistol, then tossed it to his other hand. Draven offered him a blade. Their fingers brushed, and in that touch were vows neither was foolish enough to speak out loud.
“Let’s go catch a ghost,” Kieran said.
They ran.
Behind them, the viewport filled with the canyon of stars that would swallow their ship if they misstepped by a hair. Ahead of them, in the narrow veins of the Aegis, Soren smiled and seeded the future.
Erevos turned slowly in the distance, lights dimmed, wounds shining. Somewhere in her rings, a child pressed a hand to a window and whispered a name the black would never say back.
The Aegis slid into the Kheron shoulder, guns cold, heart hot, a crew of stubborn souls carrying grief like fuel.
In the belly of the ship, a lock cycled. The lights flickered.
And the chapter—this one, at least—ended just before the doors blew.
THE END







————



In Command, the biophotonic seed was entombed in a lead-lined box with the reverent care afforded a holy relic that bites. Asha logged it under an encryption that would masquerade as a spare parts invoice if cracked. Tovan made two jokes that died in his throat when the seed emitted a low, resonant hum against its container, like a wasp trapped in a glass.
“Congratulations,” Asha said to Draven. “You’ve got yourself walls crawling with parasites, delivered with a saint’s signature on the invoice.”
“And a fox in my henhouse,” Draven said, his eyes on Kieran.
Kieran lifted two fingers in a lazy salute. “You’re welcome.”
Asha shook her head, but the ghost of a smile threatened the corner of her mouth. “He’ll burn the whole place down for the insurance.”
“Only if I ask him nicely,” Draven said.






“Next step,” Asha prompted.
Kieran tapped the slate. The names shifted again, a constellation realigning to his touch. “Fen Olik first. His vanity will do the talking. After him, the guild councilor’s disgraced brother. He nurses his pride at Two-Fifths, third stool from the door, his back to the wall in case the creditors he sold out come calling. Then the suddenly solvent fixer. If her lies are polished, it means she’s had practice, which means we’re already behind.”
Asha nodded. “I’ll mobilize a team.”
“No,” Kieran said, almost to himself. “No uniforms. No official presence. You send soldiers after ghosts, the ghosts learn to use guns.”
“Who goes, then?” Draven asked.
Kieran looked up, a slow, sharp smile spreading across his face. “Me.”
Asha didn’t argue. “Take Tovan. If something explodes, I want someone there who can make the explosion *interesting*.”
Tovan saluted with a sparking probe. “On it, boss.”
Draven said nothing. Kieran waited for the prohibition, the condition, the leash.
Instead, he received only a look that tasted like a dangerous alloy of trust and warning.
“Back in three hours,” Draven stated. “If you’re not, I come looking.”
“How gallant,” Kieran said, feeling a unfamiliar curve on his lips. “Keep the tea warm.”
He shouldered his duffel. Tovan clapped him on the back with enough force to rattle his bones. Asha’s eyes tracked them to the door, calculating risk against reward and finding the equation unsettling.
They left Command behind, stepping into the station’s artificial afternoon. The light was warmer, the crowds thinner, the air itself pregnant with the sense of something momentous beginning to stir.
Kieran set his course by deep-seated instinct: market, then back-alleys, then the places where the music died and the real work began. Draven’s key rode heavy in his pocket. It brought the kind of access he swore he’d never need, but already, he knew… he could taste it… comfort had snuck in. But it was not for now. Clear the mechanism. The4 old haunted corridors are just ahead.
The exit was still there. A left turn, a descent into the neon-drenched forgetfulness of the lower decks, a future of anonymous freighters and clean slates. He could still shed this fragile, newfound belonging like a stolen coat.
He didn’t.
“Let’s go make some new friends,” he said.
“Or remind the old ones why they fear us,” Tovan added cheerfully.
“Those we have in ample supply,” Kieran replied, and smiled at the thought.
The station turned. Somewhere deep in its core, a lock engaged with a definitive click. Somewhere high above, a door decided it would be less yielding next time. And in the spaces between, two men walked toward the gathering storm, and the storm prepared to welcome them home.








Nearly sixty years had passed since the twin stars of Aureth collapsed into ruin, and the descendants of Sehrava III and IV now drifted across the interstellar dark in their thirteen linked arks. In that time, Erevos had grown into the indispensable heart of this fragile diaspora—a hub of governance, commerce, and quiet secrets.
What had once been a weapon of war was now a metropolis of survivors. Its luminous rings gleamed against the void, a defiant testament to adaptation. Yet beneath the hum of trade convoys and power grids lay the same contradiction that had defined its birth: the machine built for conflict now sustained by forbidden peace; a civilization that lived only by breaking the rules it swore never to repeat.
Erevos had become more than a station. It was a mirror—polished in steel and guilt—reflecting everything the people of The Chain had lost, and everything they still dared to hope might endure.

High above expansive latticework of  cargo bays, dockhands in pressure-scarred exosuits rode mag-tracks like barnacles on the ceiling, shouting obscenities that clanged off the braces that  linked hangar to hangar in a semi-circle so immense that it’s vanishing point felt like and engulfing; a never ending spiral-clade  that defied the brains natural  comprehension of scale.

“Aegis, you are cleared for docking at Hangar Two-Four,” squeaked the familiar directives of Erevos Control Tower 2. “Welcome home, Captain Vale.”

“Copy that, Control. Aegis on final for lucky twenty-four.”
Kieran Vale eased the old girl through the approach lattice, her plating bustling like an aging prizefighter shouldering into the ring: scarred as hell, proud as ever, overconfident as always, and replete with enough surprises stashed away to make a clergyman blush on Sunday morning.

The Aegis was a heavily modified cargo cruiser, StarChaser Class. Conceived as highly adaptable support fighters and armored cargo runners during the war, her essential systems were designed with at least three—and sometimes up to five—redundancies, each strategically distributed to eliminate the chance that one shot could incapacitate an entire system. This made StarChasers the one true love of any captain endowed with a bit more guts than sense, and the ultimate demon temptress for any engineer cocky or stupid enough to believe the devil himself could ever be outsmarted by a goddamned grease monkey.

So was the stock from which the Aegis had come: a blunt-nosed, sleek-sided, deep-bellied, conundrum-laden bucket of organized chaos with a hyperdrive… well, three hyperdrives. In this and all other things, she was the perfect mirror and foil for her Captain Vale, and his second and Chief Engineer.

“Tovan,” Kieran squawked on the ship’s main engineering frequency. “All systems check. Eyes sharp.”

“Yes, sir, we are all systems check and ready to tuck in,” Tovan returned, as if the order had physically aroused him stem to stern. The son of a bitch had had a hard-on for the last weeks out there. Kieran could only take so much of this slough of a dirty old exhaustingly straight fool wandering about the corridors, cock in hand in the middle of nowhere. He found it far too revolting and yet historically far too tempting to come down on a side for. Except it was fucking Tovan! Disgusting.

Vale’s lanky hands worked the controls like they were extensions of himself, every nerve attuned to the subtlest twitch and purr. The skids kissed the strip. The bay’s field shimmered once, locking atmosphere. He could’ve done it blindfolded. In fact, once or twice, he had.

“You’re home, sweetheart,” he murmured, palm flat to the console as though he could feel her heartbeat. The adrenaline slid through him, warm as liquor.

The comm crackled. Tovan again, brash as ever: “Landing complete. Cargo safe. Now: a drink. Better, three. Preferably with tiny umbrellas that mock me for living this well for this long.”

“Make it back from intake alive,” Kieran said, flicking switches with the ease of ancient ritual, “and I’ll buy you the entire parasol factory. Until then, do me a favor—front and center with the shiny crates. Stickers polished. Let ‘em sparkle. Everything dirty gets buried deep. Sealed tight.”

“You heard him,” Tovan’s voice rang off metal. “Shiny saints to the altar, sinners to the tombs. Be gentle with the sinners, boys. They’re sensitive.”



Scene 2 – Cargo Ramp, Security, and Draven’s Entrance

Kieran slid through the corridor with just enough momentum to come to a halt perfectly in front of the main docking ramp of the ship. He slammed the side of his fist on the release panel, sending the bright, vehement shimmer of hydraulics through the air as the Aegis’s ramp slowly acquiesced; its mag-sealed mouth opening wide to the artificial world beyond. Heat rolled up from the deck. Kieran thumbed his collar open as he descended, a deliberate carelessness he told himself wasn’t for anyone’s benefit. He took the last step slow, savoring the way the station’s gravity settled into his bones.

This is good. He dared to think. Yeah, this can be done. Good to be home.

Only fools tempt fate like that this early, this blatantly, and as such, before the word home even fizzled through his head, a security detail fell into formation, ready and waiting on the far side of the intake paddock.

Kieran lit a cigarette, dragged it deep, let the ember flare a lone star in the dim, then ground it out on the corner of a crate. The crate beeped, prim as a nun. Unauthorized contact.

“Don’t get all precious cargo on me,” Kieran muttered, giving it a pat.
“Hands off the merchandise.”

The voice came flat and practiced from a uniform too new to be comfortable. Security, posture stiff enough to crack, boots that hadn’t learned grime. A man wearing authority like a rented suit.

Kieran lifted his hands in parodied surrender—left palm scarred with a pale comma, right stained with grease along its lifeline. “I assure you, officer, all my ill intent is purely conceptual.”

The officer’s gaze dropped to Kieran’s duffel and the faded registry stamp of an alias so old it had nostalgia baked in. His mouth thinned. His sidearm stayed holstered, but the air held the threat.

“If I was here to cause any real trouble,” Kieran winked and deflected with a studied mastery that bested most masters, “don’t you think we’d both already be having a much more interesting day about now?”

His charm elixir wasn’t exactly having its usual effects. Someone had trained this officer well. And as if on cue, that ‘someone’ was about to step into the story.

From just beyond the far main hangar doors, a shadow emerged; stretching across the deck plates, wrenching towards them like waves of ink over corrugated steel. A trick of the light at first, but then… the edges began to materialize. No trick. A man. Kieran’s gaze slowly traced up the shadow-cast like he was reading a map. He didn’t need to look beyond the silhouetted shoulder line to recognize such familiar terrain. Shit. A year of painful hell and glorious rapture flooded his head. Kieran’s mouth went dry as he whispered the name, like a prayer, a curse, a plea all in one.
Raleth Draven.
He wore black the way a verdict does, his shoulders a straight line of unassailable conclusion. His stride a controlled, unhurried event that parted the crowd through sheer gravitational pull. Kieran felt a familiar, unwelcome tightening in his chest. I’m not ready for this. The very first glimpse of his eyes—silver, unchanged, unflinching, impossible—confirmed the sentiment.
The security officer stiffened, his posture snapping into a near-salute. “Commander.”
“I’ll take it from here, Lieutenant. You are relieved.”
“Yes, sir!” The officer turned and—in what Vale could only imagine must be a maneuver trained specifically to avoid the ire of Commander Draven—the man quite literally vanished into the steam and smoke of the hangar without a trace.


Scene 3 – Reunion and Tovan’s Interruption
