“You’re a long way from the river of regrettable choices,” Draven said, his tone amused, his voice mildly mocking. “I was surprised as hell to see your name fly across my desk this morning. Finally got up the nerve to show your face back in polite society, then. Good.”
“Finally grown into that illustrious ego of yours, then. Great! It’s a smashing look, Draven, honestly. Authority drapes well on you, my friend.”
“Let’s not be so hasty jumping to conclusions on your honesty or our friendship just yet, okay Vale.”
“No, of course.” Kieran could fucking shoot himself for that one. Time to get back in this game with him. He smirked, remembering what the rules actually were, and how exceedingly… rewarding the rewards.
Draven took the moment to glance past Kieran to the assemblage zone for 24, where Tovan and his crew were finishing up the fresh coat of innocence on the Aegis’ crates of declared cargo.
“That yours?” Draven asked, motioning with his dimpled chin toward the sparkling display.
“What, Tovan?” Kieran jostled. “Well I think, to be fair, you’d have to ask him that. But he certainly does seem to have a knack for hanging about.”
Tovan, feeling the hair on his neck wilt, turned to wave a sheepish hello to Draven. “Commander.”
Draven hollered across the docking bay. “Still slummin’ around with this scoundrel, eh Tovan?”
“Well, yah know what they say about the scoundrel you know, Commander… as opposed to various other… um… scoundrels… or people… normal people… that you may…” Tovan had lost the thread and his nerve. He chuckled as if to ask forgiveness for the unsatisfying retort and quickly scurried back up the plank and into the belly of the Aegis.
“Again… that yours?” Draven bullied back.
“Let’s just say their previous owner and I are having a philosophical disagreement about the nature of possession.”
“As usual, you’re picking the wrong moment for a joke. Ship’s manifest?”
Kieran pulled the metal file board from his knapsack.
“Right here, and of course said crates feature prominently. All cargo documented and accounted for.”
“Great,” Draven said, taking the manifest board. He flicked two fingers, a gesture of such casual command and control it was more powerful than a shout. Another security officer appeared out of nowhere. Draven gestured to the crates, and the officer quickly moved to cart them away.
“Let’s walk.”



Scene 4 – Rumors, Orders, and Heat
The two men squared up side by side and began to stroll along the edge of the bay together, the hangar noise falling to a manageable thrum. A line of viewports spilled hard white starlight across the deck. Through the glass the starfield looked close enough to lick.

“How was the belt?” Draven asked, the words technically polite. His voice was low, gravel kissing velvet.
“Hungry,” Kieran said. “Traders are spooked. A lot of ships running dark. Rumors are thick enough to start standing on their own.”

“Rumors about what?”

“Depends who’s buying the round.” Kieran let the grin come again, fast and bright. “What’s clear is someone is pushing something somewhere nothing was meant to go. Some say it’s eastern sector pirates. Some say corruption in the civic core. Some say you.”
He felt Draven’s attention sharpen, a pressure like a hand between his shoulder blades. “Is that what you say?”
“Oh, shit, Draven. What I say doesn’t matter. It never has. I’m not important enough. At my level, it’s what I do that counts.”
“Whatever you say, Vale.”

“There—see? You’re not even listening now.”

“I get it, okay!” Draven recoiled. “What you do.”

“Yes. And I don’t make a habit of slow dancing with rumors. It’s bad for business. That’s all I’m saying. Okay?” Kieran quipped. “I leave that to men with fancier shoes; the kind who like to lead, give orders, booze hound and womanize.”
Draven stopped and turned. “Lost me there!” He chuckled, a glint in his eye that needed no explanation. Kieran’s momentum brought him a half-step closer than was strictly wise. The station lights etched the planes of Draven’s face in pale gold and gunmetal shadow. He smelled like clean soap and ion charge and something darker that Kieran remembered too well. He reached for the railing—he was gonna need it otherwise.
“I guess that all tracks.” He drawled. “You never were any good at taking orders.” He didn’t move. He didn’t have to. Authority radiated off him like a temperature.
Kieran closed in, almost lips to Draven’s ear by the time he started to speak, whisper really. “Oh, I think you know exactly how good at taking orders I can be, SIR! The trick is, I have to want it… really… fucking… want it… Really Fucking Bad.” Kieran purred, for the first time with true and unguarded cock on his breath as he said it.
“This is such a bad idea already,” Draven lamented, dripping pre-cut in his tightening trousers.
For a breath and then another, the hum of the docking bay seemed to dim. The last time had tasted like blood and victory—hands in hair, teeth on lip, the hard push of a wall against Kieran’s spine as they tried to climb into each other through mouths and breath and stubbornness. The memory slid between them, hot as a current.
The cacophony of the bay—the shriek of cranes, the thrum of engines—seemed to recede, the space around them becoming a silent, intimate theater.


Scene 5 – Authority, Asha, and the Manifest
“You better hope you’re clean, Vale. This is not the ragtag operation you left three years ago.”

Kieran took a breath and moved to defend himself, but Draven shut it down, stopping their collective forward momentum en masse.

“Do yourself a favor and learn fuckin’ fast how to determine whether, at any given moment, you are having words with the Supreme Commander of Erevos Station, or whether you are instead speaking to me, Kieran. You got that? Guess which time this is.” He continued their stroll. “Now, that manifest is already in Major Asha’s hands—you remember Asha, of course. Don’t worry, you’ll get to catch up in about three minutes. As a gesture of our history, and in good faith, if you need to tell me something that those line items do not… you better believe you’ll get a hundred times better outcome if I hear it from you instead of in four minutes from Asha. Go.”

“Draven, that’s not fair. What the hell am I… I’m here to do business like any other cruiser. What do you want me to say? You know what this is. Don’t play Mr. Clean with me.”

“Oh Christ, Vale, come on. You and I both know you have always had a talent for carrying the wrong thing into precisely the right room. Are you really gonna stand here and tell me that there’s nothing untoward that we need to worry about lurking in the Aegis’ hold right now?”

“Untoward—that’s a pretty complicated word for you, Draven. Are you feeling alright after the stress of its proper usage in a sentence?”

“That’s good,” Draven sloughed it off. “Very clever. Very cute. Once my people clear it through, you’ll have it all back at dock for distribution.”

“Most thoughtful of you!”

“Uh huh,” Draven was on the move toward the security cordon, and to the unwavering juggernaut of Asha and the Aegis’ very… questionable… manifest.

Kieran’s heart stopped… only half the reasons good. He needed something, anything solid enough to top out as leverage, or even countermeasure. “Can we just drop this for a second?” He chased after Draven now approaching the main doors. “Draven!? Ray? Stop. Please.” And then with enough purpose and volume that it seemed to grind the entire hangar to a silent halt: “Raleth!”

That had done it. Draven stopped cold in his tracks. His head hung over, his shoulders cringing upward. Nobody had called him Raleth since the academy. Well, no one except… Kieran.

“I can never tell if you’re playing the welcoming committee or a blockade squadron,” Kieran continued. The sincerity and desperation for a sliver of flat-out honesty were so baked into his words, it was hard for Draven to ignore or resist.

He turned slowly, his huge palms turning out and then upwards. His face followed as he broadly gestured to the massive hangar doors that now towered above him, framing him like a colossus. “Maybe I just like standing in doorways.”

“Maybe I’m just trying to figure what’s on the other side.”

Draven smirked, dead serious. “Curiosity is not gonna be your best play here, Vale.”

“Come on. Just a hint. What kind of world am I walking into here?”

“The kind a smarter man would walk away from.”

“Flattery is such a mixed bag with you,” Kieran admitted. “I find I’m both insulted and tempted at the same time.” He was deeply unnerved to find he meant both.

Overhead, a crane screamed on rusted joints. A transport coughed a death rattle. Somewhere, a child’s laugh was sharply shushed. The station breathed on, a vast, indifferent beast.

Draven’s gaze swept over Kieran’s duffel, then back to his face. “You’re dirty.” 
“If I had known you’d be here to greet me, I would have bathed.” 
“Not like that,” Draven said, and Kieran felt a sudden, unwelcome heat beneath his skin. “I don’t need a manifest to inventory your sins, Vale. I can smell the skimmer grease from here. The sweet, chemical tang of Kern sealant. Let me guess, you took a job that wasn’t yours to take. And this is the only place you can offload the goods fast enough to stay ahead of the bad.” 
“Ownership is a fluid concept.” 
“So is freedom. You’re about to experience a drastic shift in your perception of both.” Draven turned his head, his attention focusing on a cordoned-off section of the bay where station customs had swathed a palette of crates in caution-yellow tape. A tech in a sterile smock stood staring at a soft, eerie blue luminescence seeping from a cracked lid. A wide berth was both implied and required. 
“Ah,” Kieran said. “Those seem… cheerful.” 
“Celeriac routing marks under an amateur-grade scrub,” Draven stated. “A for effort. F for execution.” 
Kieran’s playful demeanor crumbled, the smile fading from his face. His mouth formed the silent shape of the word Celerian Collective. The rats who’d run him ragged across three star systems, the bastards who’d tried to put a bounty on his head. They’d never let go. Behind him, his past, which had been loitering just out of sight, shuffled its feet. 
“Walk with me,” Draven said. 
“An invitation or an imperative?” 
“It’s a polite alternative to being carried.” Draven’s eyes softened by a degree so minute it was almost cruel. 
Every instinct, honed by a lifetime of slippery exits and narrow margins, screamed for him to refuse. To melt into the lower decks where the neon bled into the shadows, to find a game where his name wasn’t a liability, to book passage on any ship headed toward oblivion. 
“Please.” 
Kieran didn’t move. 
“This looks bad,” he said. “Me, standing here with the local constabulary.”

“Do I look like constabulary to you?” Draven asked, his voice dangerously soft. It wasn’t a question of appearance, but of essence.  
Kieran hitched the strap of his duffel higher on his shoulder and fell into step. 
They moved into a maintenance corridor that smelled of chilled coolant and astringent lemon solvent. Kieran fixed his eyes on the scuffed yellow line running its center, a path to nowhere, and tried to ignore the magnetic pull of Draven’s presence beside him.  “So look…” Kieran dipped his toes into the waters of full disclosure. “Asha’s probably gonna find a few unsavory … line items during her… yah know, an under stamped old clearance ID bullshit buried in the coding on that goddamned manifest,” Kieran said, the words aimed at the floor. “But it’s not my work. It’s third-party hold over bullshit. You know what goin on out there. This  is pre-req to any silver or lesser contract for conveyance in the zone. don’t play dumb and paint me bad with that kinda crap.. I’m not your problem. You know that Draven.”  “You’re standing on my station, breathing my air, with another man’s catastrophe in your wake. I’d say that makes you very much my problem.”  “Your station. Christ! You always did love a reclamation project.”  Draven chuckled. This was too easy. “You’r right, come to think of it. It’s exactly what I always used to call you, darling. You ought to feel right at home here.”

“I don’t take another personally sanctioned step until you give me the play I deserve Commander.!”

“Fine. We’ll do this in a way you’ll be able to process and accept,” Draven said finally. “I’m requesting your cooperation, Captain Vale. There’s a situation on-station and you are uniquely qualified to help me in this probe.”  “A situation. Christ, when isn’t there a situation?” Kieran said, but the lightness cost him something. He could feel the shift, the way Draven’s posture meant business. “All right. Make it interesting.”  Draven inclined his head toward the cargo lifts. “Not here.”  They crossed the bay. Dockhands glanced up and glanced away, the universal expression of people who knew better than to make eye contact with trouble dressed in a uniform and a smuggler’s grin. As they stepped into the lift, a pair of tourists in glittering holidaywear started to follow; Asha appeared out of nowhere and blocked them with a smile made of stainless steel. The doors whispered shut.  The lift’s hum tucked around them. For a long beat neither man spoke. The tiny cabin had the charged intimacy of a confession booth, or a fuse box on the edge of overheat. Kieran let his head lean back against the cool metal and studied Draven from under his lashes.  “You look tired,” he said.  “You look pleased about it,” Draven said.  Kieran’s mouth curved. “Only when it means you're finally coming to bed.”  “Remember our little talk about learning when to when .. Starts NOW Captain.” A muscle jumped in Draven’s jaw. The lift stopped. The doors slid open to reveal a corridor designed by someone who loved right angles and glossy black surfaces. At its far end, a pair of guards stood in front of a sealed door marked with a discreet military sigil.

Scene 6 – The War Room and Major Asha
Draven keyed them through. The room beyond wasn’t a commander’s office so much as a war room: holoprojector in the center, map lines crawling like constellations caught in a net; a long table scattered with reports and the kind of mugs that said sleep was a negotiable luxury. Asha had beaten them there and was already calling up data.

“Gentlemen,” Asha said, with a nod that included Kieran in the category by the slimmest margin possible. “Captain.”

“Major,” Kieran said pleasantly. “A pleasure, as always! How long has it been? Three years…?” He resisted the temptation to wink.

“Three years, five cycles, and thirteen sessions. Give or take an hour or two.” Asha found her precision powerful. Kieran found it foolish to declare.

“My, my. Asha, I’m touched. I am! Deeply, deeply… deeply, deeply touched. And I don’t care what other people say, counting the hours and minutes we two have been parted is in no way creepy. No sir. Nor does it suggest, as others I’m sure have surmised, that you may harbor a special yearning or in fact, their words not mine, in fact a secret love for me…. No! No, you pay them no heed Major. For they don’t have what we have. They can’t possibly understand the true depths of…”

Draven finally cut him off. “Alright, that’s enough. Smirk all you like for about the next five seconds—where it might still be even remotely apropos. Major, what do you have to show us that might be of interest to the good captain… on this day?”

Asha stepped forward. No preamble. She drew a holopad from her coat and tossed it onto the table so it spun once before locking upright, light spearing up into a rotating lattice of data.

“What am I looking at.” Kieran asked. Though with each line of data, it was becoming painfully clear.

“Why, don’t you recognize it Kieran? That there is your life. The last eight months of it, at least. Name a day,” she said.

“What? You’re bluffing.” Kieran hoped.

“Any day in the last eight months,” Draven ensured. “Pick one.”

He laughed, but it came out thin. “Two weeks ago. Yesterday.”

Asha’s fingers flicked.

“Docked at Krithos Outpost. Two hours in bay seven. Met with Tolan Vrek in the maintenance sub-levels. Contraband: millicite cores marked as industrial scrap. Payment routed through four shells and a religious cooperative. You left by hatch nine to avoid the cameras.”

Kieran’s grin flattened. Shit. Shit shit shit.

“Another?.”

“Three months ago. Say March 12.” If they had even a breath of this one, he was toast.

“Yeltrin’s Moon. Private meet with Helka Dros. Nine crates of unlicensed ore slid onto a refugee freighter under falsified manifests. You played cards afterwards with Orin Sahr in a back room. Paid him with stim vials disguised as coolant. He still owes you.”

Shitty shitty fucktard bastard-camp.

The holopad flickered and spilled more—names, dates, faces, routes—like a personal diary written in a stranger’s hand.

Kieran stared at it, silent for a long five seconds before he tossed it aside.  “Well, looks like… looks like you’ve really taken this whole obsession thing to the next level.”
Draven punched in to level the thing. “Kieran, come on.”
“ What do you want from me? A confession. Fine!  Yeah, okay… you’ve got me. I’m a smuggler! OOOOh. Mystery solved. Congrats Major!” 

Asha let the data collapse back into the pad. “Look, we don’t care about what goes on off-station, Captain Vale. We’re not here to haul you in. We’re here to show you that we already know, and we don’t care. In fact, we thought its important for you to be made aware the fact that somebody has it out for you.”



Scene 7 – Surveillance and the Bargain
“What makes you say that?” Kieran pried.
“We didn’t set that surveillance on you. We didn’t ask for it. We didn’t even have to pay for it.”
“Somebody has been feeding us your every move… every day,” Draven warned. “We knew you’d be docking today before you even registered on the inter-chain long-range surveillance system.”
“So what’s the point here, kids? What can I do for you. You must have an ask. What do you want?”
Asha’s voice was cool steel. “Your cooperation, your connections and your expertise.”
“For what?”
“Temporary clearance. Credits. And anything we find in your hold that might otherwise knock you in the brig—we ignore it.”
Kieran barked a laugh, but there was no humor in it. “Look I already told Draven I was holding under deck. I’ve got nothing to hide.”
“As far as you know.”
“You saying I don’t know whats on my own ship?”
Asha slid another digital intake pad in front of him. “Here’s today’s mysterious communiqué. Your arrival. Your manifest. Your crates. Down to the serial numbers.”
“So… I gave you all that info back at the hanger.”
“Kieran,” Draven leaned in. Too close to be accidental. “We’ve had this exact document, complete with your signature and clearance codes, crate IDs, even discrete individual palate and destination schemas… since last week. 
“That’s impossible. I didn’t take half of this on until two days ago.”
Kieran leaned back, eyes flicking between Draven and Asha. “Alright, you’ve got my attention. Take me to the  next level.”
Asha flipped another pad down. “The crates you took on two days ago… same paperwork, numbers, content…” she scrolled the manifest.
“What the fuck am I looking at.”
“They were delivered 2 weeks ago to Drosshaven.”
“By a freighter that hasn’t had a hyperdrive, let alone left dock 46 in over a year.” Draven was driving the point… to the point. “By a captain who’s been dead for 6 months.”
“And look who signed the initiation documents for the ghost ship to Drosshaven…”
Kierans head began to pound, spin even, as he stared at the authorization document with a signature and digital identification hologram so authentically familiar to him that the sight quite literally stole his breath. - CAPTAIN KIELAN VALE.
“Okay…okay… but this is easily identified as impossible… inaccurate… falsified. Obviously.”
“True. It took 6 seconds for ARIA to cross reference and spit this back out, but that’s after you supplied your codes. If you had gone somewhere else to offload this shit, somewhere without an ARIA to cross-reference. Somewhere that was not already on high alert… This paper trail says you are in 3 places with 6 times the cargo running 9 types of illegal conveyances across the system.”
“You’re good Vale but…” Draven stopped.
“This is not meant to fool anyone. It’s meant to scream for your attention.”
Kieran took a deep. breathing knew… he was not going anywhere anytime soon.
“I take it back, major. I do not know what’s in my own hull.”
“Let’s talk about what just docked,” Asha began, stepping into the projector’s heart. She flicked her fingers and the room filled with a slow-rotating schematic of Erevos Station’s outer rings. Red points bled into existence, one after another, until they marked a lattice Kieran didn’t like the look of at all.
“Pretty tiny there,” he said. “Still, though… too regular for debris. Too organized to be an accident.”
“According to ARIA, they’re receivers. Tethered to the hull in places where we shouldn’t be able to detect them. Only did because someone got sloppy—installed the last two off-axis so they caught the light wrong and triggered the  plate-clearing arms.”
“But who? Why?”
“That’s what we’re trying to find out,” Draven said. The rotation paused, red lights burning cold. “We believe they’re part of a mesh designed to piggyback the station’s comms. Deliver data to an external point. Or bring something else in.”



Scene 8 – ARIA and the Aegis
Kieran was eager to get his hands on the holocontrol matrix. Asha was qualified to manage the grid, but there was no one in the galaxy who could make this old holo-engine sing like Vale.
“Asha, may I?”
Asha gladly handed him the reins. “I was hoping you would want a spin. She’s a beast, and word is, you’re the ringmaster.”
Kieran slid on the old-glove style control apparatus and single flip oculus. Instantly—although the system had been security-swept just this morning to guard against this very moment happening sans authorization—the system recognized his signature.
Welcome, Kieran Vale. Priority authentication and full access matrix engaged.
Kieran smiled, truly touched by the gesture. “Missed you too, ARIA.”
“Jesus Christ, Asha!” Draven barked. “He was locked out two hours ago, you said.”
“Sir, I am telling you, the guy is practically the system’s grandfather!”
“Watch it, lady.”
ARIA – Autonomous Regulation & Integration Authority.
She was the first starbase full-system install of the new advanced NOHCM interface—Neuro-Ocular Holo Control Matrix. The system combined holo-gloves to manipulate the HUD virtual management engine with an advanced (at the time, barely working) ocular overlay that doubled as an inner-ear neuro-link to motor centers. It was intended to blossom into a wholly mind-controlled interface. It never did. Although legend had it this man—Vale—had…
“Ugh,” Kieran removed the sticky old gloves. “Never liked these much.” He tossed them into Draven’s face and called for ARIA to, “Fire up the old scan arms.”
Suddenly a circulating arm came down, circled Vale’s head, scanned it in a nanosecond, and was gone.
“Got everything you need there, Ari?”
“I do, sir. What a beautiful brain.”
“Christ. Vale, this is not joyride time,” Draven nudged. “Can we move on from you and your mother’s reunion here?”
“Oh, sorry. Of course.”
“Where would you like to go today, Kiery?” ARIA inquired.
“Kiery, huh? Aww.” Asha mocked.
“Hey, I was seven the first time me and ARIA met, isn’t that right, ol’ girl?”
The computer corrected him: “Actually, Kier—sorry… Captain Vale,” the computer actually chuckled. “Forgive me. It is somewhat foreign to my circuitry to address you formally, but I will make the adjustment. As I was saying, you were actually six years, ten months, and fourteen days to be exact, sir.” ARIA literally pulled up security footage of the first time the former brass had let him onto the mainframe to train with her.
“Very cute. But can we please refocus,” Draven snapped. “You can spend all morning flirting if you help us out.”
“I’m sorry, I’m just impressed with the advancement in conversational acuity here. I know I’ve been away, but ARIA, who been working with you on new tech integration?”
“No one, sir. My phot-neuronic Optimizing circuitry allows me to both monitor and adapt to the needs of the overall station and it growing infrastructure. In short, every piece of language I am exposed to makes me more adept at language. Every page of station bureaucracy I process and track, improves my ability to track and process official station paperwork. Redtape… am I right!?”. Optimization and adaptation algorithms have allowedGreat. Okay, so—mesh, you were saying?”
“Designed to piggyback the station’s comms,” Asha confirmed.
“We don’t know who or why yet,” Draven said. “But there’s no way these markers are innocent.”
Asha continued the layout. “We’ve locked down the outer rings. Quietly. The fewer people who know, the better.”
“And you want me because…?”
“Nobody can even do what you’re barely thinking about doing with ARIA right now, and we need that depth to fight whatever this is. That, and you brought in a crate from the Carth Belt,” Draven said evenly. “Manifest says industrial salvage. The ship that offloaded to you had three of these receivers on its hull.”
Kieran rocked back on his heels. “You checked my hull.”
“I check everyone’s hull,” Draven said. “Don’t look wounded.”
“Just impressed,” Kieran said. “If I were smuggling something catastrophic, I’d have put it somewhere prettier than a salvage crate.”
“That’s why we’re still talking,” Draven said. He held Kieran’s gaze a heartbeat longer than strictly necessary. “I don’t think you knew. I don’t think you’d be standing here if you had.”
ARIA’s voice cut through, steady and clinical: “According to my analysis, they’re looking for… the Aegis.”
The realization—the multitude of implications, all bad—began sinking in across their faces.
“Or rather,” Asha whispered, “what’s on board.”



Scene 9 – The Crate and the Collective
They moved instantly, as a unit, back toward the lifts: Draven at Kieran’s shoulder, Asha with her slate already firing orders down a dozen lines. The corridor smelled faintly of citrus cleanser, the kind used to hide the sharper scents of stress and sleeplessness. The lift hummed, closed, opened again on the lower decks where work was honest and everything smelled like oil.
On the approach to the Aegis, a pair of tourists were taking illegal holos with the ship at their backs, practicing menacing poses. Tovan shooed them with wild arms, hair sticking up like he’d lost a fight with a static field.
“Captain,” he said, spotting Kieran. “We have guests! Important ones. I dusted.”
“You did not,” Kieran said affectionately, clapping him on the shoulder as they climbed the ramp. “Just thought I’d give Commander and the Major a little tour.  Showcase your impeccable housekeeping.”
“I’m honored,” Tovan said gravely. “Should I hide the good plates?”
“We don’t have plates,” Kieran said.
“Then we’re ahead of schedule,” Tovan concluded, cheerfully.
“Cargo hold, quickly,” Draven snapped.
Tovan hesitated. “The hold, you say, well… uh—”
“It’s okay, T,” Kieran cut in. “We’re gettin’ deputized, ol’ buddy.”
Tovan let out a classic yee-haw. “Somebody call my mother, goddamnit! Finally gonna prove that crazy ol’ bitch wrong about ONE thing!”
The smugglers deck clamps released, and they sank slowly into the belly of the Aegis beast.
The cargo hold waited, cool as a breath. The crate sat in its clamps, unlovely as a cinder block. Its surface was pitted from a dozen hard years in the belt. Kieran felt a prickle under his skin, like the air in a room right before thunder.
Asha set up the field. Blue light crawled over the crate, a veil that would harden to glass if anything inside decided to behave like a problem. Draven stood at Kieran’s elbow, close enough that the heat of him melted into Kieran’s awareness, an undeniable second gravity.
“Ready?” Asha asked.
Kieran flicked his eyes toward Draven’s hand. The commander’s knuckles were relaxed, fingers loose at his side. The scars on them were pale against his skin, old stories from ugly days. Kieran’s mouth went dry. He did not reach for that hand.
“Ready,” Draven said.
Kieran popped the locks.
For a moment, nothing happened. Then the lid sighed open, the air inside rushing out with a smell of cold metal and old dust. Asha’s field hardened, a whisper of power. Kieran leaned in.
“Industrial salvage,” he said, keeping his tone deliberately unimpressed. “Scrap coils. Capacitor guts. Some pretty stones if you squint.”
“Pretty,” Asha said skeptically.
Kieran reached in with the crate’s manipulator, lifting a tangle of fiber and copper. Beneath, something caught the light—sleek, delicate, ugly in the way a parasite is ugly. Draven’s hand came up, not touching Kieran, but close enough that the warning brushed the hairs at his wrist.
Kieran used the manipulator to nudge the thing into full view.
It was the size of a palm. Filigreed with microantennae. Marked with a sigil that made Asha inhale between her teeth.
“Celerian Collective,” she said.
The word felt like a drop in pressure. Even the ship seemed to listen harder.
Kieran let out a breath he hadn’t realized he’d been holding. “Well. You were right about interesting.”
Draven’s gaze cut to him. There was heat in it, yes, always, a steady burn that had nothing to do with the room. But there was something else now—something like resolve, like the moment a gambler pushed all his chips to the center of the table because he’d decided losing was a story for someone else.
“What the hell are they doing… here?”

Chapter Twelve and a Half: The Drift
Chapter Twelve and a Half
The Drift
He was climbing.
The ladder stretched forever, knotted from pipes and ropes and rusted metal. His palms bled as he pulled himself upward, higher, higher, while the void below whispered his name in voices he half-remembered.
Every rung shifted when he touched it. A dice cup. A bottle neck. A deckplate slick with oil. A hand—his father’s hand, rough, heavy, closing too tight.
“You’re nothing but a curse,” the voice bellowed.
Kieran flinched. Looked down. His father stood below the ladder, face flushed with drink, eyes cold as winter metal. His mouth twisted around words Kieran had tried to forget.
“Useless boy. Everything you touch rots. You’ll kill us all, you hear me? You’ll—”
The voice warped. The face blurred, bones rearranging, and suddenly it was Draven’s mouth spitting the same venom. You’re not a captain—you’re a curse.
The two men merged, separated, merged again—father and commander, past and present, each word a blow.
Kieran’s grip faltered. His body wanted to let go, fall back into the dark, where at least the shouting would end.
But above him, the shape of a boy clambered, laughing—a younger Kieran, wild, barefoot, fearless. “Keep climbing,” the boy called. “We’re almost there.”
Almost where? Kieran didn’t know. His arms ached. His breath tore. His father’s roar shook the ladder. Draven’s words cut deep.
The boy slipped. Fell past him, eyes wide, mouth open in a silent scream. Kieran lunged, missed, and the void swallowed him whole.
“You couldn’t save him,” the voices jeered together, overlapping, louder, crueler. “You’ll never save anyone.”
Kieran’s eyes squeezed shut. His body loosened. He was ready to fall.
Then—
Another voice. Not venom, not rage. Breaking, raw, desperate.
Vale. Stay with me.
The faces below flickered. Father. Draven. Father again. Then—Draven, real this time. Not steel. Not ice. Silver eyes wrecked, voice trembling.
“I’m sorry,” it said. “Stars, I’m sorry. I didn’t mean it. You’re not a curse. You’re mine. I love you. Stay.”
The ladder swayed, cracked. His fingers slipped—until a hand seized his wrist, burning hot, anchoring him.
Not his father’s. Draven’s. Real. Strong. Trembling with fear.
Kieran gasped. The grip seared through him, pain so sharp it shocked him awake.
The void shattered. The ladder dissolved.
He was choking on air, lungs seizing, eyes flying open to light and alarms. Draven’s real face hovered above him—no blur, no venom—only terror, tears in his eyes, words spilling like a prayer:
“Baby, no—don’t leave me. Stay with me. I love you.”
Kieran coughed, blood on his lip, and rasped, “Still… here.”
Draven pressed his forehead to his, shaking. “Never do that again.”
Kieran’s grin was weak, crooked, defiant. “No promises.”
Then the Aegis shook under another barrage, alarms screaming, the universe clawing them both back into fire.

Chapter Seven
Aftermath and Ashes

The station’s artificial night cycle had begun, the lights along the main thoroughfares dimming to a soft, blue-tinged glow. Kieran found himself in his assigned quarters—a small, sterile room that still smelled of disinfectant and new paint, a far cry from the familiar, grimy nooks he usually claimed. It was Draven’s doing, of course. A room with a lock that recognized his key. A place that was his.
It felt like a cage.
He lay on the bunk, still dressed, boots on, hands behind his head, staring at the ceiling. The constant, deep thrum of the station was a vibration through the frame of the bed, a heartbeat that was usually a comfort. Tonight, it felt like a countdown.
He closed his eyes, seeking the blankness of sleep, but it was a futile hope.
The dreams came too fast, too vivid, a chaotic montage of the day, of his life, all twisted together.
Draven’s voice, a low command in the dark of the Obsidian: “Breathe.”
The feel of silk on his wrists, not as restraint, but as an anchor.
Jalen’s hand on his arm, a touch from the past that felt like both a blessing and a condemnation. “Alive’s not enough.”
Asha’s flinty eyes, seeing straight through him. “Predictable gets people killed.”
Soren’s whisper, a venomous kiss in his ear from a different time, a different life: “You’re me without the mask.”

And underpinning it all, the old, familiar roar of his father, drunk and furious, shouting the same words Draven had thrown at him across the war room table what felt like a lifetime ago: “You’ll never be anything but a liability!”
He woke with a gasp, jackknifing into a sitting position, his heart hammering against his ribs like a trapped bird. The collar of his shirt was damp with sweat. The room was dark, silent except for the ever-present hum. He stared into the shadows, half-expecting one of them to detach itself and speak.
Alive. Mostly.
The words echoed in the silent room. But as he sat there in the dark, the aftershocks of the dream still trembling through him, he wasn’t sure how much longer that was going to be true. He wasn’t sure which would break first—the station under the weight of the conspiracy they were uncovering, the fragile new thing between him and Draven, or himself, caught between the man he had been and the man he might have to become.
The ashes of the day settled around him, and in the quiet dark, Kieran Vale, for the first time in a very long time, felt completely and utterly alone.

Chapter Twelve and Three Quarters: The Heart That Would Not Die
Chapter Twelve and Three Quarters
The Heart That Would Not Die
The Aegis was alive.
Oh, sure it wasn’t covered in flesh or bone exactly, but in old steel… metal that had seen too much. Every groan of her hull was a voice, every shudder of her ribs a heartbeat. She bled coolant like lifeblood, vented sparks like breath. She was scarred, battered, and furious.
And she refused to lie down.
Asha stood on her bridge, the center of a storm. Screens cracked and bled light. The deck pitched beneath her boots as if the ship herself staggered with each impact. Smoke curled like ghosts from the consoles.
Her hands moved fast, ruthless, sure. “Re-route fire. Pulse batteries, wide arc. Make them feel it.”
The crew obeyed. The Aegis obeyed. Plasma spat into the void, jagged, brilliant. One strike craft faltered. Another pressed harder, a vulture at the throat of a dying lion.
Asha didn’t look down at the dais, didn’t let herself see Commander Draven on his knees, didn’t let herself see Vale sprawled pale and still. She carried their absence in her chest like lead. She turned it into orders.
“Hold the line,” she whispered to the ship, as much prayer as command. “We are not finished.”
The ship groaned in answer.
In engineering, Tovan fought like a man inside a furnace. Steam hissed, fire snapped, alarms keened like wounded children. He was grease-slick, lungs raw, palms blistered. His tools were half-melted stubs. He screamed at the panels until his throat cracked, and the panels screamed back with sparks.
“You hold!” he shouted at the reactor core, at the trembling coils, at the trembling in his own hands. “You bloody hold! We’ve come too far to die now.”
His mind flicked to his mother’s tugboat, to candy tucked in fuse boxes, to the way she’d told him order could be made from chaos. He started sorting washers by size again—stupid, pointless—until his hands steadied. Then he jammed them into another impossible fix.
The ship bucked, slammed him against bulkhead. He coughed blood, laughed hoarsely, and cursed louder. “Come on, girl. Come on, Aegis. Don’t let them win.”
The engine throbbed under his palms. Hot, furious. Alive.
The Aegis fought.
Every shot she spat into the void was a howl of defiance. Every evasive roll was a desperate gasp. The Consortium pressed close, teeth flashing, trying to carve her open.
And in her bones, in her circuits, in the souls of the crew who bled and cursed inside her, she clung to one truth: she was not finished.
Her commander was bent to breaking, his heart torn raw. Her captain was down, his pulse a faltering whisper. But she carried their echoes. She carried all of them.
Asha’s voice was steel in the smoke: “Again. Fire again.”
Tovan’s laugh was madness in the dark: “You’ll break before I do, you bastards!”
The ship shuddered, burning, groaning, but still moving. Still alive.
Asha gripped the console, eyes blazing, and spoke to the ship as if to a lover bleeding out in her arms. “Stay with me. Just a little longer. They’re not allowed to take us. Not today.”
The Aegis answered in fire.
She was scarred. She was failing. She was magnificent.
And the hands inside her would not let her fall.
As long as there was breath in her corridors and pulse on her decks, she would carry them through.
Alt - Chapter Two: Business… Before… Pleasure (draft 1)

Chapter Two — Business Before Pleasure  Morning on Erevos Station wasn’t sunrise so much as a systems choice: lights brightened by precise degrees, music from the concourses softened into a polite murmur, and the scent diffusers traded night’s smoky indulgence for something like citrus and steel. Kieran woke to the ship’s quiet—Tovan’s snore muted by a bulkhead and the Aegis’s heartbeat steady as a purr.  He let himself lie there a moment, watching the ceiling panel cycle through diagnostics, and willed the heat in his blood to cool. It didn’t. It never did after Draven. The man had a way of taking up space inside a body he wasn’t even touching.  By the time Kieran rolled out of his bunk and scrubbed off the worst of the station grit, Asha had pinged him twice with the kind of tidy, clipped messages that made him want to spill coffee near her slate by accident. He didn’t. He liked living.  In the war room, the holograph was waiting—Erevos Station skinned open like a diagram in a textbook, arteries of utility shafts and comm lines tracing blue light through black. Asha had stacked windows of data like cards: ship registries, purchase orders, a list of maintenance crews that read like a prayer or a threat depending on where you stood.  Draven was already there. He’d shed the formal jacket for a black undershirt and a holster at the small of his back that his uniform regs probably frowned at. The fabric pulled clean across his shoulders when he moved. Kieran pretended not to notice and failed.  “Captain,” Asha said, with a nod sharp enough to cut. “We’re isolating the receivers’ signature. Whoever installed them cloned a repair crew’s authorization codes. Sloppy in execution, but bold.”  “Sloppy and bold,” Kieran said, hooking a hip against the table. “Either amateurs or geniuses who don’t care if they get caught.”  “Or professionals who want us to think they don’t care,” Draven said. He swiped the holo; the red points from yesterday flared brighter. “We traced three units to this quadrant. They’re dead now, but they were active for at least seventy-two hours.”  “To what end?” Kieran asked.  “That,” Asha said, “is your part.”  “Our part,” Draven corrected mildly. “You know the belt runners who’d take this contract. Your salvage crate says your network reaches places we don’t.”  Kieran let the compliment land without making a joke of it. “You want names. Routes. Back doors.”  “And leverage,” Asha said. “A way to turn one of them into a mouth.”  Kieran looked at the map, followed the line of the outer ring with his eyes until it dovetailed into the docking spines—the places where the station breathed, where ships kissed and peeled away and left traces like fingerprints. “You’ll want Lower Three,” he said. “Vendors set up early. Nobody asks questions if you’re carrying a coil or three. There’s a man there named Fen Olik. Sells antique star charts and fresh lies.”  “Friends?” Asha asked.  “Occasional business,” Kieran said. “He likes pretty men and ugly money. Lucky for you, I’m both.”  Draven’s mouth did that not-smile. “Take Asha.”  “Excuse me?” Asha and Kieran said at the same time, from opposite ends of skepticism.  “You’re a visible corrective,” Draven told Asha. “If this goes sideways, you’re the only one I trust to shoot around him and still hit the right targets.”  Asha considered this and then, damn her competent heart, nodded. “Fine. But he follows my lead.”  Kieran slid his palm in a lazy salute that was not regulation anywhere. “After you, Major.”  They went. The station corridors were awake now: dockhands on shift two, travelers in expensive fabrics engineered not to wrinkle, kids with pockets full of contraband sweets from worlds they’d never see. Lower Three yawned open beneath them like a market built in the ribs of a sleeping god—stalls welded to girders, lights hung from cables, wares arranged in the kind of chaos that only the sellers fully understood. It smelled like oil, fried batter, and a hundred different colognes all pretending to be subtle.  Asha didn’t blend; she edited the space around her. Kieran did. He slipped into the flow like he was made for it, nodding to vendors, touching here, smiling there, letting old acquaintances think the Aegis had brought him back for their benefit. He watched faces tilt when Asha passed—respect, curiosity, the quick calculation of risk.  Fen Olik had the kind of stall that would have looked like trash to anyone who didn’t know what to look for. Star charts curled in cylinders like sleeping snakes, a box of obsolete navcores, a tray of compasses from the era when people still pretended magnetism was romance. Fen himself was compact, silver hair braided close, rings on rings on rings until his hands looked armored.  “Captain Vale,” he said, as if tasting an import he’d been promised and nearly gave up on. “I thought maybe the belt ate you.”  “Spit me back out,” Kieran said. “Fen, this is—”  “A friend who doesn’t smile,” Fen said, considering Asha like a puzzle with teeth. “Major. My respects.”  Asha inclined her head just enough to keep her sidearm from catching the light. “We have questions, not time.”  “I sell answers by the ounce,” Fen said. “But for you, Major, I could be persuaded to throw in a taste.”  Kieran let his laugh be something Fen could keep. “We’re curious about new receivers, hull-tethered, power-sipping. Clean installs, mostly. Someone putting jewelry on my station.”  “Your station,” Fen said, amused. He leaned back. “I have seen some pretty things with ugly purposes. I have also seen ugly things with pretty prices.”  “Names, Fen,” Kieran said, soft enough that the stall’s noise swallowed it whole. “People moving this gear.”  Fen’s eyes flicked to Asha, to the way she didn’t bother pretending she wasn’t listening, to the way her fingers rested on the lip of the stall as if she could feel the pulse of the station through the metal. His gaze came back to Kieran with something like pity. “You know better than to bring a law badge to me.”  “She’s not the badge you think,” Kieran said. “And this isn’t that conversation.”  “It’s always that conversation,” Fen said, but he reached under the table and came up with a small, battered slate. He placed it down like an offering and spun it halfway to face Kieran. “For old times.”  On the screen, a list of ship tags scrolled, not names but the kind of aliases smugglers assigned when they wanted plausible deniability: Blue Orchard, Touchstone, Mercy’s Echo. Kieran’s breath hitch was microscopic. He knew two of them. He knew who captained them. He knew what they’d trade to keep those tags from becoming charges.  Asha saw it, because Asha saw everything. Her gaze skimmed the list, and Kieran could feel the choice want to form in her mouth—the one where she said we take this, we flip them, we roll the line.  “Payment,” Fen said cheerfully.  Kieran leaned closer, braced his forearm on the table so that the edge pressed into muscle. He lowered his voice until it could have been a purr or a warning. “I’ll bring you a navcore from the Acheron graveyard, Fen. The kind that sings when you hold it.”  Fen’s rings clicked as his fingers tapped the slate. “You do love me.”  “Don’t get jealous,” Kieran said. “He’s my type, too.” He tilted his chin toward the security pickup down the row—sleek and black and currently donated to two officers pretending to browse a spice rack. Asha didn’t turn. She didn’t have to.  “Done,” Fen said. “And Vale? Be careful where you put your hands. Some things on this station bite.”  “Some things like that,” Kieran murmured, and let his smile cut.  They were two corridors away before Asha spoke. “You know two of those tags.”  “Do I?” Kieran said.  “Don’t be cute,” Asha said, but there wasn’t heat in it. There was calculation, and under that, a respect she hadn’t yet decided was wise. “We can use that.”  “I can,” Kieran corrected gently. “You can supervise.”  Her mouth threatened something like a smile and didn’t pay out. “Is this where you ask me to give you just enough leash to hang us both?”  “Only if you promise to pull,” Kieran said.  Back in the war room, Draven didn’t look surprised when Asha handed him the tags. He looked like a man whose ugly theory had chosen to be right. He and Asha spoke in shorthand for a while—deployment arcs, low-visibility sweeps, a plan to spook the ships into blinking without making a scene.  Kieran listened and then, because it was expected of him, interrupted. “You’re going to scare them into docking restrictions. They’ll dump cargo, burn tags, vanish into the back alleys of the belt. You’ll spend a year chasing ghosts.”  “And your suggestion?” Draven asked, polite, inviting, dangerous.  “Let me talk to one,” Kieran said. “Quietly. You don’t change an ecosystem by throwing rocks into it. You change the current.”  Asha folded her arms. “And why would they talk to you?”  “Because I’ve been where they are,” Kieran said simply. “Because they know I won’t give them to you unless I can make it worth it. Because every captain lies different, and I speak that language.”  Silence spread like a cool cloth. Draven watched him as if cataloguing how the words sat in Kieran’s mouth, whether they were true because Kieran believed them or because he wanted them to be.  Finally: “One conversation,” Draven said. “You choose the captain. Asha chooses the location. I choose the distance from which I’m listening.”  “Very collaborative,” Kieran said, bright. “We’re practically married.”  “Don’t,” Asha said, with feeling.  Kieran angled closer to the table, just enough that his shoulder brushed Draven’s when he reached to swipe the holo. The contact sparked like static—small, mean, delicious. Draven didn’t move away. He didn’t look down. He did, however, go still in that way that meant he had decided not to react and the deciding had cost him.  Kieran felt something unpleasantly like satisfaction and smoothed it down into charm. “This one,” he said, tagging Mercy’s Echo. “Captain’s called Jace Nerral. He likes favors. He likes to owe them even more.”  “We’ll set it,” Draven said.  The rest of the day wore the shape of work. Messages coded and sent. A meeting fabricated in a maintenance corridor where the cameras were old, the light bad, the air thin. Tovan, drafted for his talent at making breakdowns look real, “accidentally” stalled a cargo tram right where Asha wanted pressure. The station hummed around them, oblivious or pretending to be.  Evening fell by decree again, lights easing down to intimacies, music warming back into the bloodstream of the lower decks. Kieran stood in the Aegis’s narrow galley and drank water he didn’t want, watched condensation bead and slide. His reflection in the steel looked like a man trying not to think about certain rooms on certain levels where air tasted like want and the walls remembered.  A quiet rap sounded on the open hatch. Draven stood there without his holster, hands empty, shoulders set in that particular way that meant he was holding a line and didn’t need anyone to see how much effort it took.  “We have Nerral in two hours,” he said. “You’re sure about him.”  “As sure as anyone can be in this business,” Kieran said. “He likes shiny. He likes danger. He likes men who tell him no.”  Draven’s eyes flicked down and up, not missing the hook Kieran left bare. “And you like to test limits.”  “Only when they deserve it,” Kieran said, softer than was smart. The ship hummed. The small space between them filled with the old gravity.  For a breath, they did nothing about it. Then Draven exhaled and stepped back, as if the ship’s air had cooled enough to trust. “Don’t be late,” he said.  “Do I ever—”  Draven’s look answered the question. Kieran grinned. “Right. Two hours.”  When Draven left, Kieran leaned his hip against the counter and closed his eyes. He let himself think about the Carth belt, about a kiss that had tasted like a dare and a promise in equal measure. He let himself remember the way Draven’s hand had fit around the back of his neck, the way his voice had dropped when he said Kieran’s name, the way the world had narrowed to breath and pressure and the need to win and surrender at once.  His body remembered faster than his brain. He let the swell of it crest and didn’t chase it, savoring the ache the way a gambler savors the itch in his fingers before the cards hit the table.  “Business,” he told the empty galley. “Then pleasure.”  Outside, the station turned toward its chosen night. Somewhere below, a door marked with a sigil of interlocked cuffs waited patient as a trap. Kieran washed his glass, set it upside down to dry, and went to meet a man who might change the game, while trying very hard not to think about the other man who already had.

——————NOTEs ON CH 2———————-

This chapter draft has a compelling sense of tension and atmosphere, effectively continuing from the previous chapter. Here are some thoughts on the highlighted text, along with suggestions for a smoother transition from Chapter 1:
### Transition Opening Suggestions

1. Recap Key Elements: Consider starting with a brief reflection from Kieran about the events of Chapter 1, perhaps a quick thought about his uneasy partnership with Draven or the stakes of the mission. This can help ground readers who may need a reminder of where they left off.
2. Internal Conflict: Open with Kieran’s internal conflict as they move through the station. A line like, “He felt the weight of their last words hanging heavy in the air, a reminder that every step deeper into the station pulled him away from his past” could set the tone right away.
3. Immediate Action: Start with a sense of urgency, such as the sounds of alarms or a rush of people around them. This can create an immediate hook that pulls readers back into the action.
### Overall Thoughts
1. Character Dynamics: The banter between Kieran, Draven, and Asha adds depth to their relationships. You might want to emphasize Kieran’s conflicting feelings about Draven further, showing moments of hesitation or nostalgia as they move through the station.
2. Descriptive Language: Your sensory details continue to shine, especially with the description of the station. Consider adding even more sensory elements—like the taste of recycled air or the buzzing of machinery—to create a more immersive experience.
3. Pacing and Flow: The pacing flows well, though some transitions could be smoother. For example, when moving from the conversation about the humanitarian lanes to Asha’s observations about the discrepancy, you might insert a line or two that shows Kieran’s thoughts in between to maintain connection.
4. Tension Building: The tension around the biophotonic mesh is fantastic. Expanding on Kieran’s emotional reaction to discovering it could heighten the stakes. Does it remind him of past encounters with the Consortium? Does he fear what it represents for their mission?
5. Dialogue Clarity: The dialogue is engaging and character-driven. You might tighten some exchanges for impact. For example, Asha’s warnings about Kieran’s charm can be made more succinct to emphasize her concern more sharply.
6. Symbolism of the Key: The key that Draven gives Kieran can have more significance. Maybe Kieran can reflect on what it symbolizes—access, control, or even a false sense of security—before he makes the decision to keep moving forward.
7. Expanded Ending: The ending of this chapter wonderfully sets up a sense of impending conflict. You could expand on Kieran’s inner thoughts as he walks toward the unknown, perhaps hinting at his fears or hopes about the encounters that lie ahead.
Overall, this chapter draft has a strong narrative thrust and vivid character interactions. By refining the transition from Chapter 1 and expanding on emotional reflections, you can enhance the stakes even further and create a more compelling read. Keep up the great work!

Bringing Jalen into the story at this point is an intriguing choice and can add significant emotional weight and complexity to the narrative. Here are some thoughts on the timing and potential implications, along with an analysis of the highlighted text:
### Thoughts on Introducing Jalen Early
1. Emotional Impact: Introducing Jalen early allows readers to feel the history and connection between him and Kieran right from the start. Their shared past can serve as a poignant backdrop for future conflicts, especially when Jalen becomes entangled with the Consortium.
2. Conflict and Betrayal: The idea of Jalen being unwittingly caught up in the Consortium's schemes adds a layer of tragedy. It heightens the stakes for Kieran when he must grapple with the implications of betrayal from someone he once deeply cared for. This internal conflict can drive Kieran’s character development.
3. Foreshadowing: Jalen’s eventual sacrifice can be foreshadowed through subtle hints in their interactions. You might drop breadcrumbs about his current situation and hint at his motivations for being involved in relief work that might intersect with the Consortium. This builds tension and anticipation for readers.
4. Themes of Redemption: Jalen’s determination to make things right can resonate with the overarching themes of sacrifice and redemption. His character can embody the struggle to overcome past mistakes, mirroring Kieran's own journey of self-discovery and loyalty.
### Analysis of the Highlighted Text
1. Atmospheric Setup: The opening lines effectively establish the station's atmosphere and Kieran's mixed feelings about Draven's authority. The metaphor of "station's veins" is strong and could be expanded to explore Kieran's inner turmoil about authority and control.
2. Character Dynamics: The banter between Kieran and Asha reveals their rapport well. Consider adding more internal thoughts from Kieran after Asha's remark about him being a "liability." This introspection can enhance the complexity of Kieran's character and his self-perception.
3. Tension and Stakes: The tension builds nicely when they confront the biophotonic mesh discovery. Kieran’s reaction here can showcase his fears or past experiences with the Consortium. You might explore what this discovery means for him personally—how it connects to his past or his relationship with authority.
4. Dialogue and Humor: The humor in the dialogue is well-placed and provides a nice contrast to the tension. However, ensure that the humor doesn’t dilute the weight of the situation. Perhaps emphasize Kieran’s nervousness or apprehension through his jokes to keep the tone balanced.
5. Imagery and Description: The descriptive language is vivid, especially in portraying the station’s atmosphere and the tension in the command room. Expanding on the sensory details during key moments, like the discovery of the case with "CHARITY," can immerse the reader further.
6. Character Motivations: Kieran’s desire to resist authority is clear, which is great for his character arc. You might delve deeper into his motivations during key exchanges, showing how his past experiences shape his current decisions, especially regarding Draven and Jalen.
7. Ending Hook: The conclusion of this section sets up an exciting shift toward action. Consider leaving the reader with a strong emotional pull—perhaps a moment of doubt or realization from Kieran as he steps into the unknown, reinforcing the theme of belonging versus drifting.
### Overall Thoughts
Bringing Jalen into the narrative at this stage can enrich the plot and deepen character relationships. The highlighted text already does a fantastic job of establishing mood and tension. By layering in more emotional depth and internal conflict for Kieran, particularly in relation to Jalen, you'll be able to set the stage for impactful future events. Keep up the great work!Bringing Jalen into the story at this point is an intriguing choice and can add significant emotional weight and complexity to the narrative. Alt - Chapter One: Docking Bay Reunion

Chapter One — Docking Bay Reunion  - 1st draft  The docking bay of Erevos Station was a cathedral of steel and light, a vast ribcage of girders that thrummed with the pulse of ships coming home bloody and triumphant. Dockhands in pressure-scarred exosuits rode mag-tracks along the ceiling, shouting to one another over the baritone roar of the fusion vents.   Kieran Vale brought the Aegis in for docking with a true masters touch; her final meters by muscle memory alone. His lanky hands easy on the controls, every nerve attuned to the subtle twitch and purr that meant the Aegis was a happy old girl. The landing skids kissed the strip. The bay’s field shimmered once, locking the atmosphere. He could’ve done it all blindfolded, like so many countless times before.   He exhaled. The adrenaline slid through him warm as liquor.  “You’re home, sweetheart,” he murmured to the ship, palm splayed against the console as if he could feel her purr. Tovan’s voice crackled over the internal: “We stuck the landing. Buy me a drink, boss. Or three. Preferably with small umbrellas.”  “Make it back in one piece and you can have the whole paper parasol factory,” Kieran said, but his attention had already snagged on the procession forming beyond the glass: station security in charcoal uniforms, sleek as knives. 

“Let’s do this,”Tovan   The bottom fell out of Kieran’s breath for a beat, then came back sharper.  They hadn’t seen each other since the Carth belt—since an argument that tasted like fuel and ended in a kiss that burned hotter. They’d left it there, unfinished, neither man willing to give ground.  The Aegis’s ramp fell with a hiss. Heat rolled up from the qdeck. Kieran thumbed his collar open as he descended, a deliberate carelessness he told himself wasn’t for anyone’s benefit. He took the last step slow, savoring the way the station’s gravity settled into his bones.  Draven waited ten paces away. Up close he was worse: uniform jacket open to the throat, dark hair shorn close, those eyes the particular silver of a blade that had seen use. He’d skipped the standard gauntlets; his hands were bare, the knuckles scarred. The little defiance tugged at Kieran like a hook.  “Kieran Vale,” Draven said, and Kieran felt his own name slide between them like a secret. “Your paperwork says you were due three days ago.”  “My luck says you’re welcome I made it at all,” Kieran answered, letting his grin show a shade too much teeth. “Got you something shiny for your trouble.”  He jerked a thumb over his shoulder at the sealed cargo crate currently secured in the Aegis’s belly. Draven didn’t look. His gaze stayed on Kieran, tracing the open collar, the smear of engine soot along his jaw, the easy way he kept his weight on the balls of his feet like he might bolt or pounce.  One of Draven’s officers—Asha, if Kieran remembered right, with eyes like a laser level—stepped forward. “We’ll log the manifest and—”  “After,” Draven said, without taking his eyes off Kieran. Asha’s mouth compressed, but she folded back into place.  Kieran tipped his head. “Commander wants a private word. Should I be flattered or worried?”  “Both,” Draven said. The corner of his mouth moved—almost a smile, except it didn’t soften anything. If anything, it made the distance between them feel more electric.  They walked the edge of the bay together, the noise falling to a manageable thrum. A line of viewports spilled hard white starlight across the deck. Through the glass the starfield looked close enough to lick.  “How was the belt?” Draven asked, the words technically polite. His voice was low, gravel kissing velvet.  “Hungry,” Kieran said. “Traders are spooked. A lot of ships running dark. Rumors are thick enough to drink.”  “Rumors about what?”  “Depends who’s buying the round.” Kieran let the grin come again, fast and bright. “Some say pirates. Some say a new corporate war. Some say you.”  He felt Draven’s attention sharpen, a pressure like a hand between his shoulder blades. “Do you?”  “I say I don’t dance to rumors,” Kieran said. “I leave that to the men who like to give orders.”  Draven stopped. Kieran didn’t, not quite; momentum brought him a half-step closer than was strictly wise. The station lights etched the planes of Draven’s face in pale gold and gunmetal shadow. He smelled like clean soap and ion charge and something darker that Kieran remembered too well.  “You never did take orders well,” Draven said. He didn’t move. He didn’t have to. Authority radiated off him like a temperature.  “Only when they’re interesting,” Kieran said softly.  For a breath and then another, the hum of the docking bay seemed to dim. The last time had tasted like blood and victory—hands in hair, teeth on lip, the hard push of a wall against Kieran’s spine as they tried to climb into each other through mouths and breath and stubbornness. The memory slid between them, hot as a current.  “Let’s try this in a way you’ll tolerate,” Draven said finally. “I’m requesting your cooperation, Captain Vale. There’s a situation on-station.”  “When isn’t there?” Kieran said, but the lightness cost him something. He could feel the shift, the way Draven’s posture meant business. “All right. Make it interesting.”  Draven inclined his head toward the cargo lifts. “Not here.”  They crossed the bay. Dockhands glanced up and glanced away, the universal expression of people who knew better than to make eye contact with trouble dressed in a uniform and a smuggler’s grin. As they stepped into the lift, a pair of tourists in glittering holidaywear started to follow; Asha appeared out of nowhere and blocked them with a smile made of stainless steel. The doors whispered shut.  The lift’s hum tucked around them. For a long beat neither man spoke. The tiny cabin had the charged intimacy of a confession booth, or a fuse box on the edge of overheat. How long has it been? 3 year…?”He resisted the temptation to wink.

Asha was qualified to manage the grid, but there was no one in the galaxy that could make this old holo engine sing like Vale. “Asha, may I?” 

Asha gladly handed him the reins. I was hoping you would want a spin. She’s a beast and word is your the ringmaster.”

Instantly, although the system had been security swept just this morning to guard against this very moment happening sans authoriZation, the system recognized his signature. Welcome Kieran, Vale. A>R>I>A> - Autonomous Regulation & Integration Authority

She was the first star base full system install of the new advanced NOHCM interface - Neuro-Ocularcular Holo Control Matrix. The system  combined holo gloves to manipulate the HUD virtual system management engine, as well as an advanced (at the time barely working) ocular overlay that intended to double as an advanced inner ear neuro-link to your motor centers. Archive chapter 2
3. Lower Three

The station corridors were awake now: dockhands on shift two, travelers in expensive fabrics engineered not to wrinkle, kids with pockets full of contraband sweets from worlds they'd never see. Lower Three yawned open beneath them like a market built in the ribs of a sleeping god—stalls welded to girders, lights hung from cables, wares arranged in the kind of chaos that only the sellers fully understood. It smelled like oil, fried batter, and a hundred different colognes all pretending to be subtle.

Asha didn't blend; she edited the space around her. Kieran did. He slipped into the flow like he was made for it, nodding to vendors, touching here, smiling there, letting old acquaintances think the Aegis had brought him back for their benefit. He watched faces tilt when Asha passed—respect, curiosity, the quick calculation of risk.

Fen Olik had the kind of stall that would have looked like trash to anyone who didn't know what to look for. Star charts curled in cylinders like sleeping snakes, a box of obsolete navcores, a tray of compasses from the era when people still pretended magnetism was romance. Fen himself was compact, silver hair braided close, rings on rings on rings until his hands looked armored.

"Captain Vale," he said, as if tasting an import he'd been promised and nearly gave up on. "I thought maybe the belt ate you."
"Spit me back out," Kieran said. "Fen, this is—"
"A friend who doesn't smile," Fen said, considering Asha like a puzzle with teeth. "Major. My respects."
Asha inclined her head just enough to keep her sidearm from catching the light. "We have questions, not time."
"I sell answers by the ounce," Fen said. "But for you, Major, I could be persuaded to perhaps extend last week's two-for-one on intrigue sale. Oh, Kieran darling, you should have seen it. Such deals! Such steals in double focus to make all four of your envious beautiful eyes spin like tops!"
Kieran let his laugh be something Fen could keep. "Next time, Fen! I'll be there. But today... today, we're in the market for new receivers, hull-tethered, power-sipping. Clean installs, mostly. Someone putting jewelry on my station."
"Your station," Fen said, amused. He leaned back. "I have seen some pretty things with ugly purposes. I have also seen ugly things with pretty purposes. What I remember usually has to do with the price, pretty or ugly."
"Names, Fen. That's all I'm after. And as for price, you and I go back a long time. If you can think of a single instance when I've not done right by you, then whatever the price comes to, I'll double it." Kieran said it soft enough that the stall's noise swallowed it whole.
"This is a good deal, if I can backdoor a mark against you. I've got to think about this...” faking deep memory scroll - as Fen knew he was right. “You bastard, if you aren't about the fairest son of a... wonderful father and mother as we know... Yes, I trust you to take care of me, Vale. What kind of names can I bring to your attention?"
"The people moving this gear and the people moving those people."
Fen's eyes flicked to Asha, to the way she didn't bother pretending she wasn't listening, to the way her fingers rested on the lip of the stall as if she could feel the pulse of the station through the metal. His gaze came back to Kieran with something like pity. "You know better than to bring a law badge to me."
"She's not the badge you think," Kieran said. "And this isn't that conversation."
"It's always that conversation," Fen said, but he reached under the table and came up with a small, battered slate. He placed it down like an offering and spun it halfway to face Kieran. "For old times."
On the screen, a list of ship tags scrolled, not names but the kind of aliases smugglers assigned when they wanted plausible deniability: Blue Orchard, Touchstone, Mercy's Echo. Kieran's breath hitch was microscopic. He knew two of them. He knew who captained them. He knew what they'd trade to keep those tags from becoming charges.
Asha saw it, because Asha saw everything. Her gaze skimmed the list, and Kieran could feel the choice want to form in her mouth—the one where she said we take this, we flip them, we roll the line.
"Payment," Fen said cheerfully.
Kieran leaned closer, braced his forearm on the table so that the edge pressed into muscle. He lowered his voice until it could have been a purr or a warning. "I'll bring you a navcore from the Acheron graveyard, Fen. The kind that sings when you hold it."
Fen's rings clicked as his fingers tapped the slate. "You do love me, don’t your Captain."
"Don't get jealous," Kieran said. "He's my type, too." He tilted his chin toward the security pickup down the row—sleek and black and currently donated to two officers pretending to browse a spice rack. Asha didn't turn. She didn't have to.
"Done," Fen said. "And Vale? Be careful where you put your hands. Some things on this station bite."
"Some things like that," Kieran murmured, and let his smile cut.
They were two corridors away before Asha spoke. "You know two of those tags."
"Do I?" Kieran said.
"Don't be cute," Asha said, but there wasn't heat in it. There was calculation, and under that, a respect she hadn't yet decided was wise. "We can use that."
"I can," Kieran corrected gently. "You can supervise."
“Where we off to next, Captain?”
“Oh, a little oasis I like to to frequent when I’m feeling low. The Office of the Registrars of Civic Commerce.” He grinned back at her with as much silliness as he had in him.

Her mouth threatened something like a smile and didn't pay out. "Roles right off the tongue, that one. Is this where you ask me to give you just enough leash to hang us both?"
"Only if you promise to pull," Kieran said.

They moved through corridors that Kieran knew in his bones from childhood—the scuffed yellow line down the center, the wall panels that hummed half a tone higher than the ones next to them. He didn’t need to look to take the fastest route to Civil Registries; he could have done it blindfolded, counting the vents by sound. That bothered him more than the caffeine.  Registrar Phael occupied a narrow office with tired plants and a desk that tried too hard to be respectable. He was fleshy in the face and delicate in the hands, his tie a little too tidy for a man who claimed to be overwhelmed. He lit up at the sight of Draven’s insignia, then doused himself.  “What a pleasure,” Phael said, voice damp. “What can Civic do for—ah. Manifest reconciliation. Of course.” He turned to his terminal with the theatrical air of a saint presenting relics.  Asha slid a slate onto his desk as if she were knifing it into the wood. “We’re looking at ten incorporations in the last quarter. Shell companies with devotional names and lifespans measured in hours. They all routed through your sign-offs.”  Phael swallowed visibly. “Charitable fronts arise when there is need,” he said, hands fluttering. “We err on the side of mercy. The system processes them—if the paperwork is in order, what would you have me do?”  “Notice when all the signatures smell the same,” Kieran said, mild as tea. He let his gaze drift around the office—framed commendation, chipped mug, data plaque with a tiny crack. Phael’s thumb kept rubbing at the edge of a drawer, over and over. The man was leaking tells.  Phael blinked fast. “I hardly think—”  “You don’t need to think,” Asha said. “You need to show us the back end. Date stamps. Origin IPs. Anyone who held your password longer than ten minutes.”  Phael started to bluster. Draven didn’t raise his voice. He said Phael’s name once, quietly, as though reminding him how to spell it. Phael’s mouth closed like a file being saved. He keyed access. The terminal vomited columns.  Kieran looked and didn’t touch. That was Asha’s ground here and she worked it with clean, quick hands. But ARIA was a different story. Within 15 seconds of the logs being opened, she had grabbed every hidden pattern and abject discrepancy and whispered the next movie into Kieran’s head. He couldn’t stop seeing the shapes in the numbers anyway—the way two incorporations shared an IP block from a decommissioned habitat, the way three approvals came from the same chair at different hours with the same jitter in the timestamp. He made a small, amused sound.  Phael rallied. “In times of crisis,” he said, “people form associations. They do their best.. Aren’t we all just trying to do our best here?”  “‘Your best shouldn’t include forgery,” Asha said. “"We appreciate your assistance, Registrar. I’m afraid I’m going to have to ask you to remain on station until further notice.  And if you find yourself in the market for updated  security credentials, from now on, that request routes directly through my office. Clear?."

“Oh absolutely clear, Major.” Phael was all head down and docile toned. He was a pro at being a nobodies somebody. “I’m honored to be afforded such a personal touch in these matters.” 
Back in the corridor, Kieran expelled a breath like he'd been drowning in there, lungs burning with the effort of restraint. "He's not the brains," he hissed, knuckles white against the wall. "But fuck—what a perfect specimen of a greased approval drone. Smiles that would lick boots clean, and a stamp ready to authorize his own execution if someone with the right clearance asked nicely."
 “He’s tip of the iceberg. Guarantee theres fifty more rubber stamp facilitator drones just like him planted in every corner of the supply chain,” Asha sighed. “That’s not even under our noses, It’s cheek to cheek and screaming at us.”

“Well, now we know.” Kielan looked to Asha to gauge her resilience factor.

“Now we fuckin know,” she repeated. And then in totally unison of voice and purpose, they both proclaimed, “NEXT!”



Fen Olik ran a yard piled with the sins of ships—racks of stripped panels, spindles of wiring, half-gutted engines. He had the hunched look of a man who slept under his own counters and the grin of someone who’d stolen from you and would sell your wallet back with a coupon.  “Commander,” Fen said, oily delight spreading. “Always a pleasure to host the honorable. Who’s your friend? Looks like trouble with good cheekbones.”  “I’m nobody,” Kieran said. He smiled in a way that showed Fen both that he was not fooled and that fooling him would be sport.  Asha didn't bother with niceties. "Your yard's been playing hot potato with inventory that magically multiplies between manifests. Three crates go in, ten come out—it's quite the miracle. And those loader IDs you've been stamping? They're showing up in places so far above your pay grade you'd need a deep space telescope just to see them."
 Fen laughed. “You give me far too much credit, Major. Such lofty accusations; and before breakfast? Truly, I’m flattered. But we both know, that I am a humble recycler of goods. A forger of one person’s dream from the discarded junk of another.”  “You’re more of a human conduit,” Kieran said, gentle, coaxing the rat out from under the crate. “Isn’t that right Fen? And, being conduit, it’s an absolute imperative that you know which way each and every current runs. You know, or your dead.  We’re not asking for sainthood. Fen’s grin twitched. 

“Commander! My goodness, I didn’t even see you there. What a welcome addition to our little investigatory chit chat.
He scratched the back of his neck where a holo-tag would sit if he wore one.  “Of course, as you know, I am always ready to help the greater good , the order of things whenever called upon. Night market in the morning felt like a trick, but Hegra Row didn’t care about clocks. It lived in a loop of corridor that had had its official designation peeled off a decade ago. Steam rose from woks. Slabs of synthetic meat sweated on hot plates. People moved like water, all sideways and short laughs. Bold.”  Draven didn’t show his teeth. Runners too well organized to be self-starting.”  “Children learning to walk need helping hand,” Mina said. “We live in very charitable times, praise the gods.”  Kieran leaned forward, elbows finding the worn grooves in Mina's counter like old friends. Not the ports with cameras and parents. The seam between. If you were me—if you were smart and wanted to plant something without owning it—you’d find the seam and feed it. You’d make sure the janitor called in sick and the cameras blinked at the same time three days a week.”  Asha didn’t blink. “Where.”  Mina’s eyes cut briefly to Draven, amused that he didn’t flinch. His eyes will say yes.”  Dock Nine dropped into the space between Kieran’s ribs like a coin into glass. He kept his face obedient.
