Intake said—”  “Intake says a lot of pretty things,” Mina said. “You want the seam? You go where old doors open if you know how to knock. Alun knows how. And i’d wager he may be growing weary of knowing alone.”
 “You didn’t hear this from us,” Asha said. “and of course we didn't hear this from you.  Mina poured more tea. “I hear nothing. I sell tea.”  Kieran lifted his cup. Their fingers didn’t touch. “As always,” he said, “be well my dear… and thank you.”  They moved back into the artery of the station, past steam and laughter and the sharp smell of frying garlic. Draven didn’t speak until they were in a quieter corridor with the hum turned down and a panel buzz that Kieran recognized as a faulty transformer.  “Dock Nine,” Draven said.  “Dock Nine,” Asha confirmed, words clipped short to keep them from growing teeth. “We don’t take a squad. We don’t light it up. We go quiet.”  “Just us,” Draven said.  Kieran’s instincts threw up a hand and then sat down. He could feel the station in his back teeth. He could also feel Draven beside him the way a man feels weather. The last time they’d done something stupid together, it had ended with blood and a laugh and a kiss that hadn’t known how to stop. He didn’t intend to rerun that tape. His body hadn’t gotten the memo.  “Alun first,” Kieran said, because work saved lives. “He’ll say Dock Nine’s fine. He’ll tell us the cameras work. He’ll be lying. He doesn’t like lying. He’ll tell the truth with his neck.”  “You think he’ll crack for you,” Asha said.  “I think people who hate their jobs like to imagine they’re confessing to a friend,” Kieran said. “I can be that, for thirty seconds at a time.”  Draven’s mouth did the almost-smile that wasn’t soft. “You always were a masterpiece of ruin,” he said. It should have been cruel. It felt like a compliment.  Kieran refused to let his grin answer the part of him that was still twenty and stupid and loved being seen by that voice. “You know it’s not yours,” he said, half automatic, part defense.  “Never said it was,” Draven replied. 


 They found Dockmaster Alun in a glass box overlooking three empty berths and a row of loaders sleeping like cattle. He was a tall, tired man with hands that had learned how to be gentle because everything he touched was heavier than him. He straightened when he saw Draven’s insignia, but his eyes did not brighten. Kieran liked him immediately.  “Commander,” Alun said. “Major.”  “Inspection,” Asha said. “Maintenance reports for Nine.”  “Routine,” Alun said, a lie that wore yesterday’s shirt. He tapped at a console. The first three pages scrolled easy. The fourth hung like a bad tooth.  “Camera feed,” Draven said.  Alun swallowed. “Offline intermittently. After the fallout—parts scarcity—priority triage—”  “Say it without nouns,” Kieran said softly. “Say the way it sits in your gut.”  Alun’s jaw worked. He looked down at his hands. “Dock Nine’s been ghosted three times in six weeks,” he said. “No manifests. No alarms. You don’t see it unless you know how to read the loader wear and the way the dust moves.”  Asha’s slate lifted, hungry. “How.”  Alun’s fingers trembled as he traced the pattern—where grease smudged that shouldn’t, where a loader’s battery cycle ran off by forty-two minutes, where a panel’s screws were turned back in by a left hand. Kieran watched the man’s shame bloom and hated whoever had made him choose between duty and living with himself.  “Who else knows?” Draven asked.  “No one,” Alun said. “Because if I put it in the system, I’d be writing my own incompetence. And it isn’t that.” The last two words came like a swallow of something sharp.  Draven nodded once in a way that promised the man he was seen. “We’ll take a look.”  Alun hesitated. “I didn’t say yes.”  “You didn’t have to,” Asha said. “You called us.”  He took them to the service hatch because that was the fastest way to the seam Mina had promised. The corridor beyond was too clean in stretches and too filthy in others, as if maintenance had been done by a chart and not by a person. The air had that stillness of places that had been forced to hold their breath.

Ghost Paper Morning
Chapter Two — Ghost Paper 
Morning on Erevos Station wasn’t sunrise so much as a systems choice: lights brightened by precise degrees, music from the concourses softened into a polite murmur, and the scent diffusers traded night’s smoky indulgence for something like morning purity. Kieran woke to the ship’s quiet—Tovan’s snore muted by a bulkhead and the Aegis’s heartbeat steady as a purr. 
He let himself lie there a moment, watching the ceiling panel cycle through diagnostics, and willed the heat in his blood to cool. It didn’t. It never did after Draven. The man had a way of taking up space inside a body he wasn’t even touching. 
By the time Kieran rolled out of his bunk and scrubbed off the worst of the station grit, Asha had pinged him twice with the kind of tidy, clipped messages that made him want to spill coffee near her slate by accident. He didn’t. He liked living. And there were 3 other softly easy softly eager pings waiting his attending to as well. It had been awhile since he and ARIA were symbiotically linked. He had forgotten what her neuro-nudge felt like - a slight tug at the temple crest that would feel almost sweet if it didn’t happen to read as almost stroke at first.

ARIA could read his vitals now, so the mainframe goddess of Bravos took the morning liberty. “Goodmorning, Sir. Hope this id not registering at too hot in your head.”

“"Naw, you nice a subtle little whisper in there. If you were man’s voice, I’d be all hot and bothered over it. Morning ARIA.”

“I have those files you requested last night, and a few other tidbits of welcome back briefs I took the liberty of assembling.”

“Good, let’s make that permanent. Morning report sort of thing, okay.”

“Affirmative Captain.”

“Ari, you just in my ear right now. Captains a. bit..”

“Yes of course. Apologies Kier.”

“Anything Else?”

“Yes, in fact. A rather cryptic contact request routed through secondary station comms to my backup emergency lines…”
“What? With me name ion it?  That’s a bit creepy.”

“Shoot the signal tracking to my personal station on the bridge I’ll take a look at it when I’m back after the morning.”

“Of course, sir. Oh that’s Major’s 4th ping. Should I…”
“Christ! Shoot it thru, I’m off. Tovan, I want you on this whole thing yeah. cover me close all day. I’m gone.” and with that the Aegis had rag tag marching orders and her Cap cap was off to hell hell.

He tipped the comms on to answer Asha’s ping about 3.5 seconds before arrival at main ops.

“Captain,” she was not happy, “I thought we said 6:30! Do have any idea what…”

Kieran waltzed through the doorway, “Major! Right on time, yeah? 07:30 just like we said.” 

“Good. Fine. Welcome Captain. I’ve taken the liberty of preparing an initial hologrid and ARIA workstation for you. If you’ll just remove the jacket and step into the Initiative Control scanner for full access integration.”

Kieran obeyed and as he took off his jacket, tossed it to Asha mockingly as he scanned the room.

Draven was already there. He’d shed the formal jacket for a black undershirt and a holster at the small of his back that his uniform regs probably frowned at. The fabric pulled clean across his shoulders when he moved. Kieran pretended not to notice and failed.

“Fancy new tech.” Kieran mocked. “Need me to strip any further or bend over or anything? I do a mean ‘turn your head and cough’ bit.” 

“Morning Captain Vale,” Draven chopped in. “Welcome to a very public and highly logged, recorded and transparent room, Captain.”

“Commander. Morning. Got it. Yes.” Kieran stepped into a small cylindrical chamber for full body DNA integrity and identification imprinting. 

The scanner whirred and hummed as the door sealed behind him, a sterile chill sweeping over his skin. Kieran stood still, hands resting at his sides, feeling foolishly exposed. The small chamber was too quiet, the hard metal walls reflecting the thin buzz of machinery—an unsettling contrast to the chaos that often engulfed the station’s operations center.

God I hate being probed!  The thought made him smile and curl his toes into the deck, the way a grating voice or a casual threat of knife to the throat might. Breath dumbass. It’s just photons.

A wave of discomfort washed over him, lingering in the pit of his stomach. It wasn’t just photons of course. being pressed into this kind of structure again. It was the scrutiny, the eyes everywhere; the way Draven's presence loomed, even now, as if his shadow was part of the scanning protocol. Kieran focused on a flickering light above him, willing away memories of their last encounter—heated words, hurtful distances, and a passion that felt like it was always just out of reach. Just out of reach…. why am I chasing this again. 

He knew, deep under the bullshit, if everything that went down yesterday was to just  melt away - never happened, If the Aegis came in clean, did it’s business and he never even saw Draven  - like so many quick stops in at Erevos before - Kieran knew he would have found a reason to linger.  It had been a long time. He was curious. Would the two of them even … fit anymore.

"Initiating secondary scanning protocol.," ARIA’s voice broke through the silence, and the chamber pulsed with a warm light, wrapping around him like a blanket. "This will only take a moment."

He focused on the sensation of the light grazing his skin, trying to ground himself in the present. Beyond the closed door, he could hear Asha’s clipped tones addressing someone, her disciplined cadence like a metronome—steady, precise, reliable. 

His eyes drifted back to Draven, whose silhouette was framed by the entryway, the sharp lines of his uniform cutting pathways though his tortured head. 

Always the cool operator. Always in control. That’s what Kieran Vale wanted to be, to present to everyone. Very little ever got in the way of that projection. He was a pro. But Draven. Draven was too much of a pull, too much of question unanswered, a thirst  - impossible to source enough liquid for. And in that cone of silence, on morning one of this idiotically bad idea now in motion, Kieran knew all the answers to his Draven conundrum already. He was so fuckin thirsty, so goddamn ready to fuckng eat him alive; over and over and over again. 

An alarm broke his drifting thoughts. “Captain,” ARIA injected. “Your heart rate is spiking. Please try to remain neutral as I complete the scan.”

“Almost done Vale. Sorry,” Asha said with surprising empathy. She was feeling just as much anxiety as she imagined Vale was. she could understand the heightened levels. Though she was unaware just how misplaced this bout with caring actually was to mark .

The scanning apparatus recoiled and there cylinder hissed as the isolation locks released and allowed Vale to rejoin the outside world.

“Scan complete. Congratulations Captain Vale,” ARIA reported. “You DNA and nexro-identitfyers are imbedded and you have full command access to my systems.”

“Don’t sound too excited there ARIA,” Draven pipped in as he floated down from the upper command perch to join them.

“ I’m sorry Commander,” ARIA defended computorially. “It was in no way my intention to imply…”

Kieran jumped in to save the machine. “He’s messing with you ARIA. Maybe later you and I will work on your humor and interaction subroutines. Seem a little rusty.”

“Thank you Captain. I would appreciate the upgrade.”

Asha pressed in. “Shall we?”

The holograph flared to life as Vale walked onto the main control paddoc. His euro-link implant pinched at his temple as it integrated the pushed firmware upgrade. Kieran picked up the ocular piece and slide it into place over his right ear. instantly the inner tech and ocular device clicked into synchronization. The flood of control and information now rushed over him. 

“Jesus Christ,” Vale started to misstep. “ I can’t… wow that’s a fuck-ton of …” and he summarily passed out from information overload. Draven jumped into the stream and caught Kierans body on the way down. 

Seconds later and Kieran’s consciousness returned. eyes open to find Draven looming above him, too near, his silver eyes ablaze with an intensity that sent a shiver racing down Kieran's spine. Where he discovered Draven arms wrapped around him, holding on for dear life… or … 

Droplets of water traced a path from Draven’s jawline, splashing onto Kieran’s skin like tiny sparks of fire. Instinct drove Kieran's hand toward Draven's face. The Commander’s  jaw clenched; he released a breath that was more a growl than a sigh, yet remained anchored to the spot.

"Stay still," Draven commanded, voice roughened to a husky whisper. "I got you. You're okay. Was just too high on the in flow from the ocular feed."

Asha slowly approached, both amused, and deeply annoyed. She hadn’t really grasped what her job was going to turn out to be here, if this gay shit escalated much more. She calked her as she stood over them, “What is this? Best two out of three?”

Kieran wrestled to gane footing and stand. Draven helped until it was clear he was about to get punched and then backed off and retreated to his tea and perch.
 “Apologies, Kier, I’ve reduced the flow speed by 35%. This should be more tolerable as you acclimate to the control device.”

“Thanks ARIA,” Kieran uttered as he regained his composer and moved focus to the task awaiting. “Much better." Let’s… Let’s begin.”

Vale barely had to think about what he thought he wanted to see and explore before it was all around him and coursing through him. Erevos Station skinned open in holo form like a diagram in a textbook that had just exploded by grenade from the inside. Arteries of utility shafts and comm lines tracing blue light through black. Asha had stacked windows of data like cards: ship registries, purchase orders, a list of maintenance crews that read like a prayer or a threat depending on where you stood. 
“Captain,” Asha said, with a nod sharp enough to cut. “We’re isolating the receivers’ signature. Whoever installed them cloned a repair crew’s authorization codes. Sloppy in execution, but bold.” 
“Sloppy and bold,” Kieran said, hooking a hip against the railing. “Either amateurs or geniuses who don’t care if they get caught.”  “Or professionals who want us to think they don’t care,” Draven said. He swiped the holo; the red points from yesterday flared brighter. “We traced three units to this quadrant. They’re dead now, but they were active for at least seventy-two hours.”  “To what end?” Kieran asked.  “That,” Asha said, “is your part.”  “Our part,” Draven corrected mildly. “You know the belt runners who’d take this contract. Your salvage crate says your network reaches places we don’t.”  Kieran let the compliment land without making a joke of it. “You want names. Routes. Back doors.”  “And leverage,” Asha said. “A way to turn one of them into a mouth.” 
Kieran stared into the hologrid, tracking the arterial blue lines that webbed through the station’s anatomy. The outer ring wasn’t just a circle—it was a living border, a battered membrane throbbing with the condensed chaos of Erevos. He zoomed in, fingers splaying, watching the data cascade as every dock, conduit, and safety bypass unfurled in microcosm. The outer ring was the station’s skin, but the docking spines—those long, serrated projections—were its nerves: hypersensitive, always firing. At the seam where ring met spine, the base metals fused in crude welds and nanosealant, but in the overlay, the meshwork of comm pings and heat signatures told a different story. Every ship that berthed or shuttled, every tug, skiff, and xeno-barge: all left an aftertaste in the digital aether, a pollution of signatures and electromagnetic residue that only a native or a predator would really know how to read.

He let the data run—speeding through cycles, overlaying past and present manifests. Something was clearly off. It didn’t matter how long he’d been away, the invisible rhythms of Bravos were eternal in Kierans bones.  There! And instantly he could feel ARIA notice it as well.  The station’s slow dance with physics and cargo traffic were revealing patterns in the noise. The nav record showed the same cargo tugs, the same maintenance pod, looping every day at 1400. Dull. But cross-check with the ARIA-predictive: some of those ships weren’t logged as present for the last seventy-two. On the map, the absences looked like scars—blank notches where signatures should have been. In the real world, that meant someone had docked, done their work, and ghosted. Either a masterful job or a deliberate omission.

He remembered, with uncomfortable clarity, how those docking spines pulsed with hazard during the war runs—how raiders would cling to the outer surface, magboots and hacksaws, breathing the frost of vacuum and waiting for a window. The new kids on the Authority force thought the inside of the station was danger, but Kieran knew the outside was worse. The spines were where the predators lingered, and where the smart money set up their receivers, relays, and traps.

Kieran called up a different overlay, subdermal network: the access tunnels and utility corridors snaking through the inner layers, linking the main ring to service hubs and to the black-market chambers that the Consortium pretended didn’t exist. It was a wonder Erevos stayed in orbit at all—too many off-the-books splice jobs and not enough oversight. But that was the point. If his guess was right, whoever set the mesh receivers had done it fast and dirty, camouflaged by the endless maintenance cycles and the relentless churn of bodies and cargo.

Suddenly, ARIA stalled on something for a nano second and pinged it to Kierans near implant. He could stop - it would be too obvious, but something in the data package and flared imagery made his mouth souplike a penny on the tongue. Who the fuck is..

He tried to process all 587 stream at once, but… gone… he’s have to circle back later to chase whatever ghost had just photobombed his head.

Vale zoomed further, tracing the pale blue of the station’s own nervous system alongside the angry red of the flagged receivers. Each point pulsed, then faded. The first two units had been set in the old comm repeater behind Dock 14—obsolete, but still functional if you knew how to tweak the sequence. The third was a wildcard: it had been piggybacked to a food-vac recycler, buried in a maintenance closet on the low end of the ring. The kind of closet that doubled as a break room for freelance repair crews, which meant the device would have been surrounded by two things: boredom and alcohol. The perfect cover.

He leaned back from the display, considering. Draven watched from the upper deck, sipping his bitter Authority tea and doing the thing where he made himself impossible to ignore, even when he was silent. Asha never looked away from the grid; she always tracked the data, as if the violence inside her would spill out if she let it rest. They were both waiting for him to solve the puzzle.

Kieran was beginning to put the puzzle together. If you wanted to seed a mesh, the main spines were too obvious—everyone scanned them. But the inner maintenance paths, the ones connecting the ring’s spine rooms and the break points, were never on any visitor’s tour. They were staffed by lifers, people who’d sold their last shreds of dignity for a pension and free station housing. The only ones who’d notice a new receiver would be the ones too bored or too drunk to care.

He dropped a marker on the overlay, highlighting a stretch of the dockside where the break rooms clustered. Then he played back the last twenty-four hours of activity, filtering for any crew or visitor with no logged destination. The program coughed up a handful of faces, most blurry and tired, but one stood out: a lean, pale guy in gray civvies, moving too quickly, hands in gloves even though the station air ran hot. He carried a toolbox, but it wasn’t one of the standard issue—Kieran knew the difference. He flagged the timestamp, ran a rapid cross, and smiled as a second camera caught the same guy stepping out into the market arcade ten minutes later, toolbox gone.

He watched the video twice. The man’s face was unremarkable, but he had the gait of someone who’d spent time in gravity—hips rolled a little, feet planted. Not a spacer, then. A contractor, or a runner. Kieran was betting on runner.

He turned to the others, still holding onto his train of logic. “That’s our guy. He seeded the mesh, then dumped the kit. Probably got paid at one of the market stalls. If we move now, we might still find the drop.”

Draven arched an eyebrow. “You want to canvas the whole market arcade with just a sketch and a hunch?”

Kieran shrugged. “It’s better than sitting here waiting for the Consortium to finish what they started. Besides, I know half those vendors—they owe me more than a few favors.” He paused, then added, “And if we get hands on the drop, we might get a print or a signature, something worth flipping.”

Asha was already prepping her sidearm with methodical focus. “You’ll want Lower Three,” he said. “Vendors set up early. Nobody asks questions if you’re carrying a coil or three. There’s a man there named Fen Olik. Sells antique star charts and fresh lies.”
“Friends?” Asha asked.
“Occasional business,” Kieran said. “He likes pretty men and ugly money. Lucky for you, I’m both.”
Draven’s mouth did that not-smile. “Take Asha.”
“Excuse me?” Asha and Kieran said at the same time, from opposite ends of skepticism.
“You’re a visible corrective,” Draven told Asha. “If this goes sideways, you’re the only one I trust to shoot around him and still hit the right targets.”
Asha considered this and then, damn her competent heart, nodded. 
“Fine. But he follows my lead.”
Kieran slid his palm in a lazy salute that was not regulation anywhere. “After you, Major.”

“Don’t wait up honey,” Kieran tossed back to the commander with a flash of the eye and a flint grin.

“Call me honey again Captain. Just try.”

The pulling mass of red photonic scatter and information collapsed folding into nothing as Kieran stepped out of the command paddock and grabbed his jacket and skipped forward to catch up with Asha - already a solid 50 yards down the concourse already.

Draven watched him go. The room emptied of support staff. He lingered, his eyes scanning the vast waking scuffle of humanity on display out the windows. His mind trying to grip how much he enjoyed watching Kieran work.. and how long he had just enjoyed watching Kieran go. As this inner examination continued, Draven began boiling under the heat of just how excited he was to have Vale around again… and then his boiling boiled over, as he realized what was bound to go down between them… had to happen. Needed to.. Fuck he needed them to go down… 

Draven broke the thought freeze with a massive fist tot he command centers steel door as he stormed out into the bravos false mid morning craze.



The station corridors were awake now: dockhands on shift two, travelers in expensive fabrics engineered not to wrinkle, kids with pockets full of contraband sweets from worlds they’d never see. Lower Three yawned open beneath them like a market built in the ribs of a sleeping god—stalls welded to girders, lights hung from cables, wares arranged in the kind of chaos that only the sellers fully understood. It smelled like oil,
 “You’ll want Lower Three,” he said. “Vendors set up early. Nobody asks questions if you’re carrying a coil or three. There’s a man there named Fen Olik. Sells antique star charts and fresh lies.”  “Friends?” Asha asked.  “Occasional business,” Kieran said. “He likes pretty men and ugly money. Lucky for you, I’m both.”  Draven’s mouth did that not-smile. “Take Asha.”  “Excuse me?” Asha and Kieran said at the same time, from opposite ends of skepticism.  “You’re a visible corrective,” Draven told Asha. “If this goes sideways, you’re the only one I trust to shoot around him and still hit the right targets.”  Asha considered this and then, damn her competent heart, nodded. “Fine. But he follows my lead.”  Kieran slid his palm in a lazy salute that was not regulation anywhere. “After you, Major.”  They went. The station corridors were awake now: dockhands on shift two, travelers in expensive fabrics engineered not to wrinkle, kids with pockets full of contraband sweets from worlds they’d never see. Lower Three yawned open beneath them like a market built in the ribs of a sleeping god—stalls welded to girders, lights hung from cables, wares arranged in the kind of chaos that only the sellers fully understood. It smelled like oil, fried batter, and a hundred different colognes all pretending to be subtle.  Asha didn’t blend; she edited the space around her. Kieran did. He slipped into the flow like he was made for it, nodding to vendors, touching here, smiling there, letting old acquaintances think the Aegis had brought him back for their benefit. He watched faces tilt when Asha passed—respect, curiosity, the quick calculation of risk.  Fen Olik had the kind of stall that would have looked like trash to anyone who didn’t know what to look for. Star charts curled in cylinders like sleeping snakes, a box of obsolete navcores, a tray of compasses from the era when people still pretended magnetism was romance. Fen himself was compact, silver hair braided close, rings on rings on rings until his hands looked armored.  “Captain Vale,” he said, as if tasting an import he’d been promised and nearly gave up on. “I thought maybe the belt ate you.”  “Spit me back out,” Kieran said. “Fen, this is—”  “A friend who doesn’t smile,” Fen said, considering Asha like a puzzle with teeth. “Major. My respects.”  Asha inclined her head just enough to keep her sidearm from catching the light. “We have questions, not time.”  “I sell answers by the ounce,” Fen said. “But for you, Major, I could be persuaded to throw in a taste.”  Kieran let his laugh be something Fen could keep. “We’re curious about new receivers, hull-tethered, power-sipping. Clean installs, mostly. Someone putting jewelry on my station.”  “Your station,” Fen said, amused. He leaned back. “I have seen some pretty things with ugly purposes. I have also seen ugly things with pretty prices.”  “Names, Fen,” Kieran said, soft enough that the stall’s noise swallowed it whole. “People moving this gear.”  Fen’s eyes flicked to Asha, to the way she didn’t bother pretending she wasn’t listening, to the way her fingers rested on the lip of the stall as if she could feel the pulse of the station through the metal. His gaze came back to Kieran with something like pity. “You know better than to bring a law badge to me.”  “She’s not the badge you think,” Kieran said. “And this isn’t that conversation.”  “It’s always that conversation,” Fen said, but he reached under the table and came up with a small, battered slate. He placed it down like an offering and spun it halfway to face Kieran. “For old times.”  On the screen, a list of ship tags scrolled, not names but the kind of aliases smugglers assigned when they wanted plausible deniability: Blue Orchard, Touchstone, Mercy’s Echo. Kieran’s breath hitch was microscopic. He knew two of them. He knew who captained them. He knew what they’d trade to keep those tags from becoming charges.  Asha saw it, because Asha saw everything. Her gaze skimmed the list, and Kieran could feel the choice want to form in her mouth—the one where she said we take this, we flip them, we roll the line.  “Payment,” Fen said cheerfully.  Kieran leaned closer, braced his forearm on the table so that the edge pressed into muscle. He lowered his voice until it could have been a purr or a warning. “I’ll bring you a navcore from the Acheron graveyard, Fen. The kind that sings when you hold it.”  Fen’s rings clicked as his fingers tapped the slate. “You do love me.”  “Don’t get jealous,” Kieran said. “He’s my type, too.” He tilted his chin toward the security pickup down the row—sleek and black and currently donated to two officers pretending to browse a spice rack. Asha didn’t turn. She didn’t have to.  “Done,” Fen said. “And Vale? Be careful where you put your hands. Some things on this station bite.”  “Some things like that,” Kieran murmured, and let his smile cut.  They were two corridors away before Asha spoke. “You know two of those tags.”  “Do I?” Kieran said.  “Don’t be cute,” Asha said, but there wasn’t heat in it. There was calculation, and under that, a respect she hadn’t yet decided was wise. “We can use that.”  “I can,” Kieran corrected gently. “You can supervise.”  Her mouth threatened something like a smile and didn’t pay out. “Is this where you ask me to give you just enough leash to hang us both?”  “Only if you promise to pull,” Kieran said.


  










 “Registrar first,” Asha said, business wrapping her syllables. “Then the broker. Then the fixer. If any of them breathe wrong, we follow it.”  Draven’s glance hit Kieran and held. It wasn’t a question; it wasn’t an order. It was a current you either swam with or drowned in. “Walk,” he said.  They moved through corridors that Kieran knew in his bones from childhood—the scuffed yellow line down the center, the wall panels that hummed half a tone higher than the ones next to them. He didn’t need to look to take the fastest route to Civil Registries; he could have done it blindfolded, counting the vents by sound. That bothered him more than the caffeine.  Registrar Phael occupied a narrow office with tired plants and a desk that tried too hard to be respectable. He was fleshy in the face and delicate in the hands, his tie a little too tidy for a man who claimed to be overwhelmed. He lit up at the sight of Draven’s insignia, then doused himself.  “What a pleasure,” Phael said, voice damp. “What can Civil do for—ah. Manifest reconciliation. Of course.” He turned to his terminal with the theatrical air of a saint presenting relics.  Asha slid a slate onto his desk as if she were knifing it into the wood. “We’re looking at ten incorporations in the last quarter. Shell companies with devotional names and lifespans measured in hours. They all routed through your sign-offs.”  Phael swallowed visibly. “Charitable fronts arise when there is need,” he said, hands fluttering. “We err on the side of mercy. The system processes them—if the paperwork is in order, what would you have me do?”  “Notice when all the signatures smell the same,” Kieran said, mild as tea. He let his gaze drift around the office—framed commendation, chipped mug, data plaque with a tiny crack. Phael’s thumb kept rubbing at the edge of a drawer, over and over. The man was leaking tells.  Phael blinked fast. “I hardly think—”  “You don’t need to think,” Asha said. “You need to show us the back end. Date stamps. Origin IPs. Anyone who held your password longer than ten minutes.”  Phael started to bluster. Draven didn’t raise his voice. He said Phael’s name once, quietly, as though reminding him how to spell it. Phael’s mouth closed like a file being saved. He keyed access. The terminal vomited columns.  Kieran looked and didn’t touch. That was Asha’s ground here and she worked it with clean, quick hands. He couldn’t stop seeing the shapes in the numbers anyway—the way two incorporations shared an IP block from a decommissioned habitat, the way three approvals came from the same chair at different hours with the same jitter in the timestamp. He made a small, amused sound.  Phael rallied. “In times of crisis,” he said, “people form associations. They do their best.. Aren’t we all just trying to do our best here?”  “‘Your best’ shouldn’t include forgery,” Asha said. “"We appreciate your assistance, Registrar. I’m afraid I’m going to have to ask you to remain on station until further notice.  And if you find yourself in the market for updated  security credentials, from now on, that request routes directly through my office. Clear?."

“Oh absolutely clear, Major.” Phael was all head down and docile toned. Smiles that would lick boots clean, and a stamp ready to authorize his own execution if someone with the right clearance asked nicely."

“Well, now we know.” Kielan looked to Asha to gauge her resilience factor.

“Now we fuckin know,” she repeated. And then in totally unison of voice and purpose, they both proclaimed, “NEXT!”

Asha glanced over at Kielan who was full out waiting for a high five, bonding moment. She would not be so easily tamed, but relief washed over her turned away face - her vagabond captain cohort was actually useful - they might just understand eachother.    Fen Olik ran a yard piled with the sins of ships—racks of stripped panels, spindles of wiring, half-gutted engines. Who’s your friend? Looks like trouble with good cheekbones.”  “I’m nobody,” Kieran said. He smiled in a way that showed Fen both that he was not fooled and that fooling him would be sport.  Asha didn't bother with niceties. "Your yard's been playing hot potato with inventory that magically multiplies between manifests. Three crates go in, ten come out—it's quite the miracle. And those loader IDs you've been stamping? They're showing up in places so far above your pay grade you'd need a deep space telescope just to see them."

A forger of one person’s dream from the discarded junk of another.”  “You’re more of a human conduit,” Kieran said, gentle, coaxing the rat out from under the crate. “Isn’t that right Fen? And, being conduit, it’s an absolute imperative that you know which way each and every current runs. You know, or your dead.  We’re not asking for sainthood. We’re asking for the name you use when you call to say the package has legs.”  Fen studied Kieran, eyes creasing in honest appreciation. “I see you know the song, Captain..”  “I wrote some verses, here and there,” Kieran said.  Draven cleared his throat from the silent far corner of the booth. it was a gesture that demanded forward progress now, or this gets a lot more serious. Fen’s grin twitched. 

“Commander! My goodness, I didn’t even see you there. What a welcome addition to our little investigatory chit chat.
He scratched the back of his neck where a holo-tag would sit if he wore one.  “Of course, as you know, I am always ready to help the greater good , the order of things whenever called upon. This I have always done, Draven. Yes? You know this. The person you seek… does not show their face… may not even have a face. They will send runners. Runners who may be partial to the wondrous cuisine of Hegra Row, booth twelve - I shouldn’t wonder. but I’d hurry… as  they very much dislike being kept waiting.”  “Useful,” Asha said dryly. “Appreciated, and dully noted Fen.”  “I have my moments,” Fen said. “And as always when they can be of use to you marvelous keepers of our beautiful Erivos, this is doubly blessed for us all.”  On the way out, Fen called, “Your friend’s right, you know. About the current. It runs under the holy lanes now. Makes a man feel blasphemous just getting paid.”  Kieran didn’t look back. “Blasphemy’s just honesty with better lighting.”  Night market in the morning felt like a trick, but Hegra Row didn’t care about clocks. It lived in a loop of corridor that had had its official designation peeled off a decade ago. Steam rose from woks. Slabs of synthetic meat sweated on hot plates. People moved like water, all sideways and short laughs. Booth twelve belonged to Mina Hark—a narrow, dark-eyed woman with rings stacked up her fingers and a permanent squint that suggested she’d been disappointed early in life and had never fully recovered.  “Mina,” Kieran said, letting familiarity be both greeting and warning.  She looked him over with the patience of a cat weighing whether a hand deserved skin. “Vale,” she said, and the way she said it made his name into an inventory. “You bring the badge to breakfast? Bold.”  Draven didn’t show his teeth. He didn’t need to. “We’re looking for the shadow under the clean paper,” he said.  “Ah, yes… the Ink blotter, smudge layer… shadow,” Mina murmured, pouring tea like a ceremony and sliding cups they didn’t ask for. “People are so spiritual these days.”  Asha placed a cred chit on the counter and didn’t push it. Mina ignored it in the manner of someone for whom theatrics were useful.  “Manifest splits,” Asha said. “Shell births. Runners too well organized to be self-starting.”  “Children learning to walk need helping hand,” Mina said. “We live in very charitable times, praise the gods.”  Kieran leaned forward, elbows finding the worn grooves in Mina's counter like old friends. His smile slid into place—the same one he'd flashed years ago when wheedling intel about which security rotation had exploitable gaps. "Look, we don't care about the black market keeping people fed. What we care about is that something rotten is playing at fresh goods - wearing charity's face and their numbers are building fast. Those runners with no traceable boss—you've seen them. I know you have.  Once they leave your sight, where do they vanish to?"  Mina watched him for a count of three, then for four, and the corner of her mouth did something that was not softness. “You used to be fun,” she said.  “Yeah I know. Sorry. I got tired,” Kieran said. “Promise it ain’t personal darling.”  “It’s alright old friend. I get the play, I’m glad your home and in a better game, kid” Mina smiled, her grin flush full of history and affection.. She tapped the cred chit once with a finger ringed in cheap gold. “There’s a place where schedules evaporate. Where the station forgets itself. Not the lower decks. Not the ports with cameras and parents. The seam between. If you were me—if you were smart and wanted to plant something without owning it—you’d find the seam and feed it. You’d make sure the janitor called in sick and the cameras blinked at the same time three days a week.”  Asha didn’t blink. “Where.”  Mina’s eyes cut briefly to Draven, amused that he didn’t flinch. Then she looked back at Kieran. “Ask Dockmaster Alun whether Dock Nine is still haunted,” she said, very casual. “He’ll tell you no. His eyes will say yes.”  Dock Nine dropped into the space between Kieran’s ribs like a coin into glass. He kept his face obedient. He felt Draven’s attention shift toward him without physically touching him; it set his skin on alert.  “Dock Nine’s maintenance,” Asha said, as if the word could be forced back into paperwork and bolts. “We shut it partially after the Veyra-9 fallout. Intake said—”  “Intake says a lot of pretty things,” Mina said. “You want the seam? You go where old doors open if you know how to knock. Alun knows how. And i’d wager he may be growing weary of knowing alone.”
 “You didn’t hear this from us,” Asha said. “and of course we didn't hear this from you.  Mina poured more tea. “I hear nothing. I sell tea.”  Kieran lifted his cup. Their fingers didn’t touch. “As always,” he said, “be well my dear… and thank you.”  They moved back into the artery of the station, past steam and laughter and the sharp smell of frying garlic. Draven didn’t speak until they were in a quieter corridor with the hum turned down and a panel buzz that Kieran recognized as a faulty transformer.  “Dock Nine,” Draven said.  “Dock Nine,” Asha confirmed, words clipped short to keep them from growing teeth. “We don’t take a squad. We don’t light it up. We go quiet.”  “Just us,” Draven said.  Kieran’s instincts threw up a hand and then sat down. He could feel the station in his back teeth. He could also feel Draven beside him the way a man feels weather. The last time they’d done something stupid together, it had ended with blood and a laugh and a kiss that hadn’t known how to stop. He didn’t intend to rerun that tape. His body hadn’t gotten the memo.  “Alun first,” Kieran said, because work saved lives. “He’ll say Dock Nine’s fine. He’ll tell us the cameras work. He’ll be lying. He doesn’t like lying. He’ll tell the truth with his neck.”  “You think he’ll crack for you,” Asha said.  “I think people who hate their jobs like to imagine they’re confessing to a friend,” Kieran said. “I can be that, for thirty seconds at a time.”  Draven’s mouth did the almost-smile that wasn’t soft. “You always were a masterpiece of ruin,” he said. It should have been cruel. It felt like a compliment.  Kieran refused to let his grin answer the part of him that was still twenty and stupid and loved being seen by that voice. “You know it’s not yours,” he said, half automatic, part defense.  “Never said it was,” Draven replied.  They found Dockmaster Alun in a glass box overlooking three empty berths and a row of loaders sleeping like cattle. He was a tall, tired man with hands that had learned how to be gentle because everything he touched was heavier than him. He straightened when he saw Draven’s insignia, but his eyes did not brighten. Kieran liked him immediately.  “Commander,” Alun said. “Major.”  “Inspection,” Asha said. “Maintenance reports for Nine.”  “Routine,” Alun said, a lie that wore yesterday’s shirt. He tapped at a console. The first three pages scrolled easy. The fourth hung like a bad tooth.  “Camera feed,” Draven said.  Alun swallowed. “Offline intermittently. After the fallout—parts scarcity—priority triage—”  “Say it without nouns,” Kieran said softly. “Say the way it sits in your gut.”  Alun’s jaw worked. He looked down at his hands. “Dock Nine’s been ghosted three times in six weeks,” he said. “No manifests. No alarms. You don’t see it unless you know how to read the loader wear and the way the dust moves.”  Asha’s slate lifted, hungry. “How.”  Alun’s fingers trembled as he traced the pattern—where grease smudged that shouldn’t, where a loader’s battery cycle ran off by forty-two minutes, where a panel’s screws were turned back in by a left hand. Kieran watched the man’s shame bloom and hated whoever had made him choose between duty and living with himself.  “Who else knows?” Draven asked.  “No one,” Alun said. “Because if I put it in the system, I’d be writing my own incompetence. And it isn’t that.” The last two words came like a swallow of something sharp.  Draven nodded once in a way that promised the man he was seen. “We’ll take a look.”  Alun hesitated. “I didn’t say yes.”  “You didn’t have to,” Asha said. “You called us.”  He took them to the service hatch because that was the fastest way to the seam Mina had promised. The corridor beyond was too clean in stretches and too filthy in others, as if maintenance had been done by a chart and not by a person. The air had that stillness of places that had been forced to hold their breath.  Kieran felt the first prickle on his forearms before he heard anything. It was the sensation of standing in a room that hadn’t decided whether to be empty. He looked at Draven and found the man already looking at him. No words. Just a shared tilt of heads toward the same wrongness.  “Here,” Asha said softly, half a step ahead. She pointed with two fingers at a seam in the wall paneling where a cable hugged too snugly. “That’s new.”  Draven’s hand went to Kieran’s shoulder before he seemed to decide against it. He let it fall. Kieran’s skin burned where the touch didn’t happen. Unhelpful body.  They followed the cable to a junction box that had been certified three months ago and opened two nights earlier by someone with a shaky left hand and the right security privileges. Inside, the guts didn’t look wrong—no obvious bombs, no blinking treachery—just an additional spine laid alongside the one the station owned. A brace. A leech. Elegant as sin.  Asha swore under her breath in a language Kieran didn’t speak. She slid a sensor along the line. It hummed a small, pleased tone.  “Mesh,” she said. The word had no drama in her mouth. “A seed. Two more at least down this corridor, if this is the spacing. No signatures. No maker prints. Someone has been careful.”  “Careful enough to make ghosts walk,” Kieran said. He crouched and traced where the cable kissed the junction. “And to make schedules forget to exist in certain places at certain times.” He looked up, found Draven’s gaze. The man’s eyes were steady and too bright in the poor light. “It’s bigger than rerouted credits.”  Draven nodded, one precise dip. A decision moved through him like a current finding a path. “We don’t touch it here,” he said. “We map it. We find the other seeds. We don’t let anyone spook. We bring in a lab we trust.”  Asha’s mouth tightened. “Trust is expensive on this station.”  “Then we pay,” Draven said. The way he said it made Kieran’s pulse do a wild thing. He remembered that look from other rooms and other fights. It meant the man had decided to hold a line so hard that the line itself would strengthen.  Kieran stood too fast and had to pretend it was on purpose. “Mina gets a gift basket,” he said, because jokes were a brace. “Fen gets a warning. Phael gets a new password and a minder.”  “And you,” Draven said, stepping closer than a commander needed to be, “get dinner and a longer conversation about what useful means when it stops being short-term.”  The words were quiet enough not to echo. They still felt like a match struck in low oxygen.  Kieran’s mouth did something between a smile and a defense. He let the heat of Draven’s nearness roll over him once, like standing too close to an engine to see if it would burn. It would. “Find me a lab first,” he said. “Then we can talk about appetites.”  Asha cleared her throat like a gavel. “We have work,” she said, which was true and also a mercy. “Dock Nine is a seam. We follow it until it widens or we stitch it shut.”  She started marking the map on her slate, dropping silent tags at intervals, a breadcrumb trail only three people would be able to read later. Kieran watched her hands move, professional and relentless. He felt Draven’s attention like a weight on the side of his face. He didn’t turn to meet it. Not yet.  The corridor exhaled the faintest breath, as if something somewhere approved of being seen.  Kieran thought of the gas giant tearing itself apart, of millions pushed into lanes no one was watching closely enough, of holy paperwork and secret spines. He thought of Soren’s voice, silky with necessity. He thought of Draven’s hand nearly touching his shoulder and the way his own body had leaned toward that absence. He thought of the Aegis, patient and old and loyal.  “Let’s get a lab,” he said. “And let’s be very, very quiet until we have one.”  They turned back toward the hatch. The hum of the station sounded different now that they knew where to listen. Somewhere behind them, inside the wall, something sleek and patient continued to do exactly what it had been built to do.  Kieran didn’t look back. He would look tomorrow. Tonight, he’d keep breathing.  End of Chapter Two.























































































































































































































Homecoming Secrets
On the walk back toward the light and noise of the market, Asha fell into step beside Kieran. “You moved in there like you’d never left. Like it was home.” “In a lotta way, I never did,” Kieran said, the truth slipping out before he could catch it. “I mean Really, what is it all - smoke and mirrors. I’m just good at stacking truths onto of lies on top of rumors, with a sprinkle of  a threat and a smearing of charm. It’s not talent, it’s discrete with a smile.”
 "You want a moment of honesty?" Asha asked, eyes forward. "You scare me a little.”
“Scare you? Common, Major. I’m not sure anything scares you.”
“Well you said yourself, your a professional liar. Brilliant even. I've known plenty of liars—your brand isn't unique. But, what scares me is that you're also a good man; a good man who's been forced to become brilliantly, ruthlessly deceitful just to survive. It’s a special kind of selfishness. It twists you up, presses your soul into these smoldering corners of carelessness and scorn. The kind that scar. The kind that don't go away—they eat at everything you thought you were, everything you think you have left."
She paused, letting the words settle like ash.

"The scary thing is, there will come a day, a day when you have to reach deep down into the very depth of your soul in order to survive. When that day comes, you may find that there isn’t anything of you left but lies, and rot and ruin. Men like you tend to stand tall right up until the moment they have to prove it. Then they implode—and take a whole lot of good people with them."
 Kieran kept his eyes on the corridor ahead. “You think I’m a good man.” It wasn’t a question.
 “I think you’r a very good man, surrounded by a lifetime of bad. But that’s not what Draven sees,” Asha said, her tone pragmatic. “Draven only sees you. Everything else melts away when you walk into a room.”
“That’s not true, Asha. He’s not that naive. He knows what’s what.” 
“He is when it comes to you. All the bravado, the boy banter is just a front. I’ve known Raleth long enough to know when he’s compromised. By you. A man with as much power as Draven… you must be careful Kieran.” Asha pleaded. And as if on cue…
“Captain, Major, we are ready to commence,” ARIA piped in. “I have one Fen Olik entering the market from the District 7 out cropping. 2 o’clock.” 
She flicked a glance back the way they’d come, toward the alley with the chalked symbol. “Whatever you think is waiting behind a door like that… it isn’t a solution. It’s just a different kind of echo.”
 “I know,” Kieran said, and for once, he meant it.
 “Good,” Asha said. “I’d hate to have to respect a man with even worse judgment than my own.”

Kieran almost chocked as her realized that they were both laughing at the same thing. Asha would wait till much later to practically flog herself to death over the incident. But the humor caught them and it was thier’s to share.
 The laughter seemed to lock them in tandem step, one to another, as if pulled taught by the same string. Without thought or acknowledgment, and surely for the first time, they turned to face eachother. It happened in silence. It lasted a moment. It took no more effort than either was willing to give. Yet there, in the hush between heartbeats, it was settled: face to face, or back to back, neither would let the other fall.
ArchiveCh 1: Erevos Station - HOME


The docking bay of Erevos Station was a cathedral of steel and light, its massive crescent array of hangers  forged from equal parts star grit and existential reality. First built to cradle an armada of 350 battle cruisers, it had been repurposed into something stranger: an emissary of peace turned megapolis. Its vast ribcage of girders still thrummed with ancient ambition, tempered now by commerce and coercion—a pairing potent enough to bring even the hardest war-born pilots to their now war torn knees.  High above, dockhands in pressure-scarred exosuits rode mag-tracks like barnacles on the ceiling, shouting obscenities that clanged off the braces linking hangar to hangar in a semi-circle so immense it seemed to vanish into the starfield.  “Aegis, you are cleared for docking at Hangar Two-Four,” squeaked the familiar directives of Erevos Control Tower 2. “Welcome home, Captain Vale.”  “Copy that, control. Aegis on final for lucky twenty-four.”  Kieran Vale eased the old girl through the approach lattice, her plating bustling like an aging prizefighter shouldering into the ring; scarred as hell, proud as ever, over confident as always and replete with enough surprises stashed away to make a clergyman blush on Sunday morning.
The Aegis was a heavily modified cargo cruiser, StarChaser Class. Conceived in a time of immense conflict, her essential systems were designed with at least 3 and sometimes up to 5 redundancies - each one strategically distributed so as to eliminate the chance that one shot could incapacitate an entire system. This made StarChasers the one true love of any captain endowed with a bit more guts than sense, and the ultimate demon temptress for any engineer cocky or stupid enough to believe the devil himself could ever be outsmarted by an goddamned grease monkey. 
So was the stock from which the Aegis had come. A blunt-nosed, sleek-sided, deep-bellied, conundrum-laden bucket of organized chaos with a hyperdrive.. well 3 hyperdrives. In this and all other things, she was the perfect mirror and foil for her Captain Vale, and his second and Chief Engineer.  “Tovan,” Kieran squacked on the ships main engineering frequency. “All Systems check. Eyes sharp.”  “Yes, sir, we are all system check and ready to tuck in” Tovan returned, as if the order had physically aroused him stem to stern. The son of a bitch had had a hardon for the last weeks out there. Kieran could only take so much of this slough of a dirty old exhaustingly straight fool wandering about the corridors cock in hand in the middle of no where. He found it far too revolting and yet historically far too tempting to come down on a side for. Except it was fucking Tovan! Disgusting!  Vale’s lanky hands worked the controls like they were extensions of himself, every nerve attuned to the subtlest twitch and purr. The skids kissed the strip. The bay’s field shimmered once, locking atmosphere. He could’ve done it blindfolded. In fact, once or twice, he had.  “You’re home, sweetheart,” he murmured, palm flat to the console as though he could feel her heartbeat. The adrenaline slid through him, warm as liquor.  The comm crackled. Tovan again, brash as ever. “Landing complete. Cargo safe. Now: a drink. Better, three. Preferably with tiny umbrellas that mock me for living this well for thislong.”  “Make it back from intake alive,” Kieran said, flicking switches with the ease of ancient ritual, “and I’ll buy you the entire parasol factory. Until then, do me a favor—front and center with the shiny crates. Stickers polished. Let ‘em sparkle. Everything dirty gets buried deep. Sealed tight.”  “You heard him,” Tovan’s voice rang off metal.. “Shiny saints to the altar, sinners to the tombs. Be gentle with the sinners, boys.  They’re sensitive.”

Kieran slid through the corridor with just enough momentum to come to a halt perfectly a-front the main cargo ramp of the ship. He slammed the side of his fist on the release panel, sending the bright, vehement shimmer of hydrolics through the air as the Aegis’s ramp slowly acquiesced; it’s mag sealed mouth opening wide to the artificial world beyond. Heat rolled up from the deck. Kieran thumbed his collar open as he descended, a deliberate carelessness he told himself wasn’t for anyone’s benefit. He took the last step slow, savoring the way the station’s gravity settled into his bones. 
This is good. He dared to think. Yeah, this can be done. Good to be home.
Only fools temp fate like that this early, this blatantly, and as such, before the word home even fizzled through his head, a security detail fell into formation, ready and waiting on the far side of the intake paddock.
Kieran  lit a cigarette,  dragged it deep, let the ember flare a lone star in the dim, then ground it out on the corner of a crate. The crate beeped, prim as a nun. Unauthorized contact.  “Don’t get all precious cargo on me,” Kieran muttered, giving it a pat.  “Hands off the merchandise.”  The voice came flat and practiced from a uniform too new to be comfortable. Security, posture stiff enough to crack, boots that hadn’t learned grime. A man wearing authority like a rented suit.  Kieran lifted his hands in parodied surrender—left palm scarred with a pale comma, right stained with grease along its lifeline. “I assure you officer, All my ill intent is purely conceptual.”  The officer’s gaze dropped to Kieran’s duffel and the faded registry stamp of an alias so old it had nostalgia baked in. His mouth thinned. His sidearm stayed holstered, but the air held the threat.  “If I was here to cause any real trouble,” Kieran winked and deflected with a studied mastery that bested most masters, “don’t you think we’d both already be having a much more interesting day about now?”
His charm elixir wasn’t given the proper time to cure and work it’s way into the officers head quickly 
From just beyond the far main hanger doors, a shadow emerged; stretching across the deck plates, wrenching towards them like waves of ink over the corrugated steel. A trick of the light at first, but then… the edges began to materialize. No trick. A man. Kieran's gaze slowly traced up the shadow-cast like he was reading a map. He didn’t need to look beyond the silhouetted shoulder line to recognize such familiar terrain. Shit! A year of painful hell and glorious rapture… flooded his head. Kieran’s mouth went dry as he whispered the name, like a prayer, a curse, a plea all in one.  Raleth Draven.  He wore black the way a verdict does, his shoulders a straight line of unassailable conclusion. His stride a controlled, unhurried event that parted the crowd through sheer gravitational pull. Kieran felt a familiar, unwelcome tightening in his chest. I’m not ready for this. The very first glimps of his eyes, Silver. Unchanged. Unflinching. Impossible,  confirmed the sentiment.

The security officer stiffened, his posture snapping into a near-salute. “Commander.”  “I’ll take it from here, lieutenant. You are relieved.”   “Yes sir!” The officer turned and in what Vale could only imagine must be a learned, trained for maneuver designed officially to avoid the ire of Commander Draven, the man quite literally vanished into the steam and smoke of the hanger - without a trace. 
“You’re a long way from the river of regrettable choices,” he said, his tone amused, his voice mildly mocking. “I was surprised as hell to see your name fly across my desk this morning. Finally got up the nerve to show your face back in polite society then. Good.”  “Finally grown into that illustrious ego of yours, then. Great!  It’s a smashing look Draven, honestly. Authority drapes well on you, my friend.”

“Let’s not be so hasty  jumping to conclusions on your honesty or our friendship just yet , okay Vale.”

“No, of Course.” Kieren could fucking shoot himself for that one. Time to get back in this game with him. He smirked, as he remembered what the rules actually were, and how exceedingly… rewarding the rewards.

Draven took the moment to glance past Kieran to the assemblage zone for 24, Tovan and his crew were finishing up the fresh coat of innocence on the Aegis’ crates of declared cargo. 

“That yours,” Draven ask motioning with his dimpled chin toward the sparkling display.

“What, Tovan?” Kieran jostled. “Well I think, to be fair,  you’d have to ask him that. But he certainly does seem to have a knack for hanging about.”

Tovan, feeling the hair on his neck wilt, turned to wave a sheepish hello to Draven.   “Commander.”

Draven hollered across the docking bay. “Still slummin around with this scoundrel, eh Tovan?”

“Well yah know what they say about the scoundrel you know, commander, .. as … apposed to  various other.. um… scoundrels.. or people .. normal people…that you may… ” Tovan had lost the thread and his nerve. He chuckled as if to ask forgiveness for the unsatisfying retort and  quickly scurried back up the plank and into the belly of the Aegis.

“Again… that yours?” Draven bullied back.

“Let’s just say their  previous owner and I are having a philosophical disagreement about the nature of possession.” 

“As usually, you’re picking the wrong moment for a joke. Ships Manifest?”

Kieran pulled the metal file Board from his knapsack. 
 “Right here, and of course said crates are feature prominently, all cargo documented and accounted for.”
 “Great,” Draven said, taking the manifest board. He flicked two fingers, a gesture of such casual command and control that  it was more powerful than a shout. Another security officer appeared out of nowhere. Draven gestured to the crates and the officer quickly moved to cart them away. 

“Let’s walk”

The two men squared up side by side and began to stroll along the  edge of the bay together, the hangar noise falling to a manageable thrum. A line of viewports spilled hard white starlight across the deck. Through the glass the starfield looked close enough to lick.  “How was the belt?” Draven asked, the words technically polite. His voice was low, gravel kissing velvet.  “Hungry,” Kieran said. “Traders are spooked. A lot of ships running dark.
