He had never thought one would arrive in his crate. Soren’s face ghosted in the back of his mind like a brand he was not permitted to name. He swallowed it down.

Draven’s eyes hardened in a way Kieran had only ever seen when the man had to choose between orders and instinct. “We’re not going to call this anything grand on record,” he said. “We’ll log it as unauthorized tech. We’ll lock it down and run it through a lab, quietly. But this”—he put the smallest of nods at Kieran—“this is why I want your hands on the matrix. You make this system sing!”












































































ARCHIVE CH1 : Docking Bay Reunion

The docking bay of Erevos Station was a cathedral of commerce and decay, its vaulted ceilings lost in a haze of frozen breath and exhaust. Arc lamps burned like fallen angels, their cold halos glazing wet steel. Gantries sketched a skeletal architecture against the gloom, and service drones hissed along their rails, their clicks and whirs a language of sterile gossip. A ship slid into its berth with a sound like a knife finding its sheath—a sigh of finality that was neither gentle nor kind. Kieran watched the landing flares bleach the world in pulses of stark white and absolute black, a nervous tic of light and shadow, as if the station itself was testing its own pulse.  He took a final drag from his cigarette, the ember a lone, defiant star in the dimness, then ground it out on the surface of a sealed crate. The crate emitted a soft, offended chime. Unauthorized contact. He smiled and gave it a consoling pat.  “Hands off the merchandise.” The voice was flat, practiced. A security officer, his boots too new, his jaw too set, a man who wore his authority like a costume that hadn't yet molded to his shape.  Kieran tilted his head, a pantomime of appraisal. “Is that your first warning? Or your last?”  “It’s the only one you’re getting.”  “Generous,” Kieran said. He raised his hands, palms open. The left was mapped with a permanent grease stain along its lifeline; the right was marked by a pale, comma-shaped scar. “See? All my ill intent is purely conceptual.” His body felt loose, a deliberate mockery of the officer’s stiff posture.  The officer’s eyes dropped to the duffel at Kieran’s feet, to the faded registry stamp of an alias so old it had acquired a kind of sentimental patina. The man’s mouth tightened. His sidearm remained holstered, but the air between them grew heavy with its potential.  Kieran met the unspoken threat with a smile that had disarmed better men. “If I was here to cause real trouble, officer, we’d both be having a much more interesting day.”  From just beyond the far main hanger doors, a shadow emerged; stretching across the deck plates, wrenching towards them like waves of ink over the corrugated steel. A trick of the light at first, but then… the edges began to materialize. No trick. A man. Kieran's gaze slowly traced up the shadow-cast like he was reading a map. He didn’t need to look beyond the silhouetted shoulder line to recognize such familiar terrain. Shit! No, no, no. A year of painful hell and glorious rapture… flooded his head. Kieran’s mouth went dry as he whispered the name, like a prayer, a curse, a plea all in one.  Raleth Draven.  He wore black the way a verdict does, his shoulders a straight line of unassailable conclusion. His stride a controlled, unhurried event that parted the crowd through sheer gravitational pull. Kieran felt a familiar, unwelcome tightening in his chest. I’m not ready for this.  And those eyes. Silver. Unchanged. Unflinching. Impossible.  The security officer stiffened, his posture snapping into a near-salute. “Commander.”  “I’ll take it from here, officer. You are relieved.”   “Yes sir!”  

“You’re a long way from the river of regrettable choices,” he said, his tone amused, his voice mildly mocking. " I was surprised as hell to see your name fly across my desk this morning. Finally got up the nerve to show your face back in polite society then. Good.“  “Finally grown into that illustrious ego of yours, I see.  It’s smashing on you Draven. Authority drapes well over you. 









  “Let’s just say their  previous owner and I are having a philosophical disagreement about the nature of possession.” 
“As usually, you’re picking the wrong moment for a joke. Ships Manifest?”
Kieran pulled the metal file Board from his napsack.  “Right here, and of course said crates are feature prominently, all cargo documented and accounted for.”
 “Good,” Draven said, taking the manifest board. He flicked two fingers, a gesture of such casual command and control that  it was more powerful than a shout. Another security officer appeared out of nowhere. Draven gestured to the crates and the officer quickly moved to cart them away. 
“Let’s walk”
The cacophony of the bay—the shriek of cranes, the thrum of engines—seemed to recede, the space around them becoming a silent, intimate theater. “You always had a talent for carrying the wrong thing into precisely the right room. He’s just going log your things in through security intake. I’m sure there’s nothing untoward we need to worry about in there, right?”

“Untoward, that’s a pretty complicated word for you Draven. Are you feeling alright after the stress of it’s proper usage in a sentence?”

“That’s good,” Draven sloughed it off. “Very clever. Very cute. Once my people clear it through, you’ll have it all back at dock for distribution.   “Most thoughtful of you,” Ki  “Uh huh,” Draven was on the move. 

Kieran’s heart stopped… only half of the reasons, good. He needed something, anything solid enough to launch a decision from. “Can we just drop this for a second” He chased after Draven now approaching the main doors. “Draven!? Ray? Stop. Please.” And then with enough purpose and volume that it seems to grind to a silent halt the entire hanger. “Raleth!”

That had done it. Draven stopped cold in his tracks. His head hung over, his shoulders cringing upward. Nobody had called him Raleth since the academy. Well no-one except… Kieran.  
 
“I can never tell if you’re playing the welcoming committee or a blockade squadron.” The sincerity and desperation for a sliver of flat out honesty so baked into Kieran’s words, it was hard for Draven to resist.

He turned slowly, his huge palms turning out and then upwards. His face following as he broadly gestured to the massive hanger doors that now towered above him, framing him like a colossus. “Maybe I just like standing in doorways.”

“Maybe I’m just trying to figure what's on the other side.”  Draven smirked, dead serious. “Curiosity is not gonna be your best play here, Vale.”  “Come on. Just a hint. What kind of world am I walking into here? ”  “The kind a smarter man would walk away from.”  “Flattery is such a mixed bag with you,” Kieran admitted. “I find I’m both insulted and tempted at the same time.” He  was deeply unnerved to find he meant both.  Overhead, a crane screamed on rusted joints. A transport coughed a death rattle. Somewhere, a child’s laugh was sharply shushed. The station breathed on, a vast, indifferent beast.  Draven’s gaze swept over Kieran’s duffel, then back to his face. “You’re dirty.”  “If I had known you’d be here to greet me, I would have bathed.”  “Not like that,” Draven said, and Kieran felt a sudden, unwelcome heat beneath his skin. “I don’t need a manifest to inventory your sins, Vale. I can smell the skimmer grease from here. The sweet, chemical tang of Kern sealant. Let me guess, you took a job that wasn’t yours to take. And this is the only place you can offload the goods fast enough to stay ahead of the bad.”  “Ownership is a fluid concept.”  “So is freedom. You’re about to experience a drastic shift in your perception of both.” Draven turned his head, his attention focusing on a cordoned-off section of the bay where station customs had swathed a palette of crates in caution-yellow tape. A tech in a sterile smock stood staring at a soft, eerie blue luminescence seeping from a cracked lid. A wide berth was both implied and required.  “Ah,” Kieran said. “Those seem… cheerful.”  “Consortium routing marks under an amateur-grade scrub,” Draven stated. “A for effort. F for execution.”  Kieran’s playful demeanor crumbled, the smile fading from his face. His mouth formed the silent shape of the word Consortium. The rats who’d run him ragged across three star systems, the bastards who’d tried to put a bounty on his head. They’d never let go. Behind him, his past, which had been loitering just out of sight, shuffled its feet.  “Walk with me,” Draven said.  “Is that an invitation or an imperative?”  “It’s the polite alternative to being carried.” Draven’s eyes softened by a degree so minute it was almost cruel.  Every instinct, honed by a lifetime of slippery exits and narrow margins, screamed for him to refuse. To melt into the lower decks where the neon bled into the shadows, to find a game where his name wasn’t a liability, to book passage on any ship headed toward oblivion.  “Please.”  Kieran didn’t move.  “This looks bad,” he said. “Me, standing here with the local constabulary.”  “Do I look like constabulary to you?” Draven asked, his voice dangerously soft. It wasn’t a question of appearance, but of essence. This wasn’t about station law. It was a quieter, more permanent kind of war.  Kieran hitched the strap of his duffel higher on his shoulder and fell into step.  They moved into a maintenance corridor that smelled of chilled coolant and astringent lemon solvent. Kieran fixed his eyes on the scuffed yellow line running its center, a path to nowhere, and tried to ignore the magnetic pull of Draven’s presence beside him.  “The crate wasn’t mine, you know,” Kieran said, the words aimed at the floor. “I’m not your problem.”  “You’re standing on my station, breathing my air, with another man’s catastrophe in your wake. I’d say that makes you very much my problem.”  “You always did love a reclamation project.”  “I like to think of it as a masterpiece of ruin.”  “Clever phrase. But… not yours, is it? I’ve heard that somewhere before.”  Draven chuckled. This was too easy. “You’r right, come to think of it. It’s exactly what I used to call you, darling. You ought to feel right at home here.”  A hatch labeled OPERATIONS—AUTH ACCESS slid open under Draven’s palm. The room within was a study in utilitarian authority: a steel table, a wall of dormant displays, two chairs. The air hummed with the station’s latent power.  Neither man sat. They stood on opposite sides of the table, a gulf of history and intention between them.  Kieran's impatience reared up, if only to mask every other emotion he was drowning in. “What the hell am I doing here, Raleth?”  “I require eyes… expert eyes in places where a badge tends to become more of a blinding beacon,” Draven began. “That crate is a symptom. The disease, I’m afraid, is lodged much deeper.”  Kieran swallowed hard…  “Someone’s using our humanitarian lanes as their very own personal arterial expressway. You know the underbelly of this sector better than it knows itself.  The names that operate in the negative spaces. You could drum up more actionable intel in an afternoon than an entire security squadron could in two months.”  “I’m guessing you don’t have that kind of time.”  “Yeah, and that includes now, so… what’ll it be? Yes… or no?”  “Maybe,” Kieran hedged.  “Wasn’t one of the options, Vale. Yes or no. Decide.”  Kieran flanked for a moment. “I see your negotiating skills are still for shit.”  “Respectfully,” Draven said. “This isn’t up for negotiation. I’m not gonna insult you with a detention cell, you’d just escape. I will not pretend I can best you at your own game,  in your own element. I can’t. I am simply stating a need… for you. For your unique skillset. If your answer is no, you walk out that door and I find another tool for this task. We will, in that case, both find our sleep troubled for different reasons, but  rest assured, I will still sleep.”  Kieran’s laugh was a sharp, brittle thing. “Oh please. You won’t sleep in the grip of a threat like this. You’ll throw everything you’ve got at this problem, even if  your everything doesn’t stand a chance of solving anything.”  Draven pushed. “Then help me. Say yes!”  “You know it’s a good thing you’ve got a badge. You’d never survive in the real world, Draven.”  Kieran’s quips now failing to hide the obvious. He was desperate. Draven was desperate too. Though their reasons were wildly  different, the feeling was universal, the same. Kieran was the only way to pierce through the plotted haze of this threat. Draven knew it. And he knew that Kieran knew he did. “Alright, fuck negotiation. Let’s jump straight to the bribery.”  “Took you long enough, tough stuff,” Kieran grinned, jumping into a seat. The game was on again. “Lay it on me.”  “Amnesty for your last three port violations,” Draven listed, his tone devoid of inflection. 

Kieran wondered if Draven really knew how deep those violations reached, and toward whom. Later… It would have to wait for later.  “A legal transponder code,” Draven continued,“Should you develop a preference for oxygen untainted by deceit. And…”  Kieran’s eyes closed.  “And…?”

Draven dropped the bomb. “A key.”

A key? A way out, or just another lock? The question, unspoken, was in his gaze. “A key to what?”  “To me,” Draven said without a blink of hesitation, and the two-letter word hung in the air, deliberate and immense. “To my private comms. My quarters. My ship in dock. To the parts of this station that haven't forgotten the weight of promises.”  The atmosphere in the room compressed, charged. A cooling pipe ticked like a waiting clock.  Kieran swallowed and slowly opened his eyes to find Draven had been staring at him all along. Kieran was caught. Eyes locked. Huge mistake. “You’re confusing professional and personal—”  “Yes,” Draven stamped.  “Why?”  “Because I know it’s the only way I can get you. Because separation was a lie we told each other, and it cost us. So I’m asking you to choose. I need you to choose. Refuse me, and I’ll have to find another asset. Accept me, and I swear I will move entire star systems to protect you.”  “Why?” Kieran begged again, his guard obliterated. His pain, bare.  Draven turned away, the admittance on his lips about to reach for the brightest of starlights. “Because I can’t find another you.”  Kieran looked at Draven's back, he could see him breathing, bracing. A sudden shared truth seemed to replace the oxygen between them. His gaze drifted out the window, into the immense seductive blackness of space. Freedom was a tangible thing out there—a lift, a card game, a ship, the comfortable anonymity of being a ghost in the machine. A story told in whispers.  He looked back at Draven. A different kind of story. One he already knew the ending of, and yearned for in spite of it all.  “This is a profoundly bad idea,” Kieran said, and meant that it’s the only idea that has  ever made sense. The ghost of a life lived on the fringes, of a life he was desperate to escape, seemed to tug at his soul.  “Undoubtedly,” Draven agreed. “But it’s the only one that makes any sense.”  Kieran was shaken by the echo in his head. “Give me the night to consider it.”  “You’ve already considered it,” Draven said, and the certainty in his eyes felt like a stone shifting in Kieran’s gut.  Kieran’s mouth twitched. He hated him for his perception. He loved him for it. “Fine. I’m in—but it will be under loud, vocal, sometimes infuriating, and certainly continual protest.”  “I’d expect nothing less.” Draven’s mouth tilted in a brief, satisfied grin. “Anyway, I’ve missed that voice—especially when it’s under protest,” he said, dry as dust. 

A real grin finally broke through on Kieran’s face.  Draven slid a data-slate across the table. “It’s settled then. Here’s where we are. Three unregistered shipments passed through Dock Nine under charitable cover. The manifests are digital ghosts. I want the names behind the names.”  Kieran left the slate untouched. “Finish the bribery. My compensation.”  Draven held his gaze. Kieran had him by the balls. He was… fine with it. “As I said, amnesty. Keys. And when this business is concluded, a clean start. Not a forever, Vale. Not a guarantee, but… A sort of genesis chance. A rebirth.”  Kieran’s hand came down on the slate. The glass was cool, the future colder. “Don’t make promises your position won’t let you keep.”  “My position is the promise, and if that fails, you have my word. You have me,” Draven said.  Kieran drew the slate toward him. He let that last bit slide over him. He had to, or he’d fucking melt away. The hum of the station seemed to intensify, the walls leaning in to listen. “Then we’re in business.”  “Good,” Draven said, and finally took a seat. “Now. Tell me who I’m hunting.”  Kieran activated the hologrid. It bloomed to life in his hands, names and routes rearranging like a flock of starlings startled into flight. Old, deeply ingrained instincts took hold. He fed the system false leads, then taught it how to unravel them.  “We’ll start with the charity convoys,” he instructed. “They’re antiseptic. Too antiseptic. No one is that clean unless they’re scrubbing away sin. From there—” His finger tapped a connection that bled into three tributaries. “—you’ve got a merchant guild councilor whose brother had his shipping license revoked, a night market fixer who suddenly developed a punctual relationship with her landlord, and—ah. There he is.” His lips curved. “Fen Olik. That walking scrapyard would auction off his own mother if he thought she could bring a good price per pound. We should be able to get that bird singing with very little feed.”  Draven watched the map contort and clarify under Kieran’s direction, not bothering to mask his approval. “Admit it. You missed this, didn’t you?”  “I didn’t say that.”  “You didn’t have to,” Draven said. He gestured to a small counter in the corner. “There’s tea.”  Kieran snorted. “Of course there is.”  He moved to pour it, the mundane action a welcome anchor. The kettle hissed steam. The cups were plain, ceramic, chipped at the rim. A thin veneer of domesticity over the heart of a war room.  He handed one to Draven. Their fingers brushed. A simple, physical fact that contained multitudes.  “You’re a bureaucratic disaster, you know that.” Kieran said lightly.  “And you’re a force of nature without a warning label,” Draven returned. “Try not to obliterate my station in your wake.”  Kieran lifted his cup in a mock toast. “In this, and all things, I make no promises, Commander.”  “Well, at least you’ve learned that much,” Draven said, and drank.  Kieran did the same. The tea was scalding. He burned his tongue and welcomed the clean, sharp pain.  Outside, the immense wheel of Erevos turned on its axis.  Inside, two men made the conscious choice to unravel, together.
The Aegis Cycle : Burnthrough
BURNTHROUGH: Beat-by-Beat Scene Outline 
CHAPTER 1: Docking Bay Reunion
Romance Beat: Opening Image + Theme Stated + Catalyst
Scene 1: Kieran's Arrival (Opening Image)
• Setting: Erevos Station docking bay - "cathedral of commerce and decay"
• Action: Kieran disembarks, immediately confronted by security officer
• Character: Establishes Kieran as charming rogue with dangerous past
• Tension: Unauthorized contact with crate, hint of contraband
• Hook: Draven's shadow appears before the man himself
Scene 2: The Reunion (Theme Stated)
• Setting: Same docking bay
• Action: Draven materializes, dismisses security, confronts Kieran
• Dialogue: "You never did take orders well" (THEME STATED)
• Character: Power dynamic established - attraction vs. authority
• Tension: Years of history in every glance and verbal parry
• Plot: Draven confiscates Kieran's bag "for his quarters"
Scene 3: The Proposition (Catalyst)
• Setting: Operations room - sterile authority vs. intimate history
• Action: Draven reveals Consortium crate, makes his offer
• Character: Kieran's resistance vs. temptation, Draven's desperation masked as command
• Dialogue: Terms negotiated - amnesty, key, "lifetime warranty"
• Plot: CATALYST - Kieran agrees to help hunt Consortium
• Romance: "The key to me" - access to Draven's private spaces/heart
CHAPTER 2: Business Before Pleasure

• Emotion: Sweetness that's almost pain, goodbye to what might have been

• Action: Attempted sleep, but dreams invade

• Psychology: Dreams mixing past and present, fear and desire
• Stakes: Identity crisis - who is he becoming?

• Setting: Dream landscape mixing memory and fear
• Action: Faces morphing - Draven, Jalen, Asha, Soren, his father
• Character: All voices of judgment and expectation colliding
• Psychology: Fear of being "just a liability" echoing through time
• Wake: Gasping return to reality, sweat and terror
Scene 7: The Choice Crystallized
• Setting: Dark quarters, station humming around him
• Action: Kieran sits in darkness, processing everything
• Character: Moment of absolute honesty with himself
• Resolution: Despite loneliness, despite fear, choice is made
• Growth: No longer planning exits, committed to staying
• Romance: Key in pocket and mark on throat as proof of belonging,
• Transition: Ready for whatever comes next, no longer running

OVERALL ARC ANALYSIS (Complete Novel):
Romance Progression:
• Act 1: Unresolved tension → Professional partnership → Emotional commitment
• Act 2a: Sexual tension building → Physical surrender → Point of no return
• Act 2b: Reality testing love → Crisis fracturing bond → Dark night of soul
• Act 3: Choosing each other → Love as strength → Public commitment
Plot Progression:
• Act 1: Consortium threat discovered → Investigation begins → Team forms
• Act 2a: Evidence gathering → Soren introduced → Stakes escalating
• Act 2b: Personal crisis → All is lost → Darkest hour
• Act 3: Final gamble → False victory → True climax → New beginning
Character Arcs:
• Kieran: Drift → Commitment → Choice → Belonging → Partnership
• Draven: Duty → Need → Love → Sacrifice → Integration
• Asha: Suspicion → Truth-telling → Loyalty → Strategy → Victory
• Jalen: Past → Stability → Sacrifice → Honor → Memory
• Soren: Temptation → Manipulation → Mirror → Rejection → Defeat
Thematic Resolution:
• Choice over need - Characters choose love despite easier options
• Belonging over freedom - True freedom found in chosen connection
• Trust as strength - Vulnerability becomes tactical advantage
• Love as foundation - Relationship enables greater heroism, not hindrance
Word Count Distribution:
• Chapters 1-7: ~35k (current)
• Chapters 8-11: ~20k (Act 2b expansion)
• Chapters 12-19: ~30k (Act 3 climax & resolution)
• Total Target: ~95k words
This structure delivers a complete romance arc while maintaining thriller pacing and ensuring every beat serves both plot and character development.
The Aegis Cycle : Burnthrough Alt outline 2
  ### BURNTHROUGH: Beat-by-Beat Scene Outline  **CHAPTER 1: Docking Bay Reunion**   **Romance Beat: Opening Image + Theme Stated + Catalyst**   - **Scene 1: Kieran's Arrival (Opening Image)**     - **Setting**: Erevos Station docking bay - "cathedral of commerce and decay"     - **Action**: Kieran instructs Tovan, his engineer to unlock the “clean” crates right away, give the appearance of total transparency, and then tells him against his desire to disembark that he must stay behind and ensure none of Dravens people do any snooping around beyond what tovan unloads right away. Kieran then disembarks, immediately confronted by a security officer.     - **Character**: Establishes Kieran as a charming rogue with a dangerous past. briefly introduced his long time engineer Tovan, who he leaves on the ship to look after everything    - **Tension**: Unauthorized crate - ancient clearance codes, hint of contraband.     - **Hook**: Draven's shadow appears before the man himself.  - **Scene 2: The Reunion (Theme Stated)**     - **Setting**: Same docking bay.     - **Action**: Draven materializes, dismisses security, confronts Kieran.     - **Dialogue**: "You never did take orders well" (THEME STATED).     - **Character**: Power dynamic established - attraction vs. authority.     - **Tension**: Years of history in every glance and verbal parry.     - **Plot**: Draven confiscates Kieran's crates to run through security   - **Scene 3: The Proposition (Catalyst)**     - **Setting**: Operations room - sterile authority vs. intimate history.     - **Action**: Draven reveals that the markers on the crate contraband Kieran brought in, have links to as ship that came though a week earlier and had some if this  biophootonic mesh module attached to its hull..   - **Character**: Kieran's resistance vs. temptation, Draven's desperation masked as command.     - **Dialogue**: Terms negotiated - amnesty, key, "lifetime warranty."     - **Plot**: CATALYST - Kieran agrees to help get tot he bottom of who’s running rogue right under the stations nose.     - **Romance**: "The key to me" - access to Draven's private spaces/heart.  ---  **CHAPTER 2: Business Before Pleasure**   **Romance Beat: Set-Up + B Story Introduction**  
Scene 1: Aegis Morning Rituals
Action : ARIA and Kieran establish connection and working schema
Plot: Both ARIA and Tovan are monitoring Kieran and the entire days action.
Subplot/mystery: mA strange comms ping re routed through Aria subsystems aimed at Kieran has surfaced
 - **Scene 2: Command Introduction (Set-Up)**     - **Setting**: Command center - Kieran enters Draven's professional world.     - **Action**: Team briefing, Asha.     - **Character**: Asha immediately suspicious,    - **Plot**: Mission parameters established,     - **World-building**: Station politics, humanitarian cover operations.  - **Scene 2: Dock Nine Discovery**     - **Setting**: Service corridors, hidden maintenance areas.     - **Action**: Team investigates, finds more Consortium crates.     - **Plot**: Scale of infiltration revealed - "moving in volume."     - **Tension**: Tripwire nearly triggered, careful documentation.     - **Foreshadowing**: "A clean hand leaves a dirty print" proverb.  - **Scene 3: Jalen Encounter (B Story)**     - **Setting**: Main concourse.     - **Action**: Chance meeting with Kieran's past love.     - **Character**: Jalen represents the stable life Kieran never chose.     - **Romance**: Contrast to Draven - light vs. dark, safety vs. danger.     - **Emotion**: Nostalgia, regret, path not taken.     - **Plot**: Target identified - need to find Nerral for information.     - **Romance**: Subtle tension between Kieran and Draven post-reunion.  ---  **CHAPTER 3: The Cost of Yes**   **Romance Beat: Debate (Extended)**   - **Scene 1: Kieran's Wandering/Doubt**     - 1st battle inserts - original draft archive
Chapter FOUR
The Breakpoint
The alley twisted like a vein, lit only by the red flicker of failing neon. Jace Nerral moved fast, boots splashing through a shallow run-off, coat flaring behind him. The sound echoed off the narrow walls—splash, splash, splash—a rhythm that matched Kieran's heartbeat as adrenaline flooded his system.
Kieran kept pace, the thrill of pursuit pumping hot in his blood. This was familiar territory—the chase, the hunt, the moment when everything narrowed to predator and prey. His body knew this dance, had learned it in alleys much like this one across a dozen different stations. The difference was that this time, he was the hunter.
The weight of Asha's presence at his shoulder grounded him, reminded him that this wasn't the old days when he'd been running from station security instead of running with it. The irony wasn't lost on him—Kieran Vale, smuggler turned temporary law enforcement, chasing down one of his own kind. The thought should have bothered him more than it did.
"Stop!" Asha barked, drawing her weapon. The command rang off the alley walls with the kind of authority that made civilians freeze in their tracks.
Nerral didn't. He skidded around a corner—and slammed into a dead end.
Kieran slowed, catching his breath as they closed in. The alley terminated in a blank wall of corrugated metal, stained with rust and decorated with graffiti in three different scripts. No escape routes, no convenient maintenance hatches, no dramatic fire escapes. Just a trapped smuggler and the slowly closing jaws of consequence.
Nerral's eyes darted, sharp and quick, calculating odds with the desperate precision of a man who'd built his life on knowing when to fold. He had the lean, wiry look of someone who lived on credit he hadn't earned and charm he couldn't quite cash. His coat was expensive but worn, his boots polished but scuffed—the uniform of a man always one job away from either prosperity or prison.
I know that look, Kieran thought, recognition settling in his chest like a familiar weight. I've worn that look.
"Vale," Nerral said, breathless, but trying for charm anyway. His smile was the kind that had probably gotten him out of trouble more often than it had gotten him into it. "Didn't expect to see you playing law dog.”
The words hit harder than they should have. Law dog. As if Kieran had betrayed some fundamental truth about who he was supposed to be. As if working with Draven somehow made him a different species of predator.
"Even mutts bite when they're hungry," Kieran said, surprised by the edge in his own voice. The retort came out sharper than he'd intended, carrying more truth than he was comfortable with.
Am I hungry? he wondered. Or just tired of running?
"We need names, Jace. Who's buying the receivers?”
Nerral smirked, though sweat glistened at his temple like scattered diamonds. "You think I'd sell out the Consortium? I like breathing.”
The Consortium. The name hung in the air like smoke, carrying all the weight of reputation and threat that kept the black market spinning. Kieran had worked for them once—not directly, never directly, but in the way that water eventually flows toward the ocean. Everyone in their line of work touched the Consortium eventually, whether they admitted it or not.
Asha stepped forward, gun steady in a two-handed grip that spoke of countless hours on the range. "Then start liking prison.”
For a heartbeat, no one moved. The standoff crystallized—three people in an alley, each holding cards they weren't ready to show. Kieran could feel the weight of choice pressing down on all of them, the moment when words would either solve everything or make it infinitely worse.


Chapter Six
After   glow / math


The Obsidian Room’s doors sealed the way a secret does—softly, completely. The corridor outside felt too bright, too clean, as if the station were pretending it couldn’t smell what clung to their skin.
Kieran’s wrists ached where the cuffs had kissed bone. His throat thrummed where Draven’s hand had steadied him on the edge of coming apart. He should have felt wrung out. Instead, his body hummed like a ship fresh from overhaul—every panel tight, every system singing.
They didn’t speak in the lift. The hum of its magline filled the space where words might have tried to live. Draven stood beside him, breathing measured, shirt half-buttoned badly enough to make Kieran’s mouth curve despite everything.
The doors slid open on the Aegis’s deck. Tovan, in a heroic act of timing, took one look from Kieran’s wrecked hair to Draven’s open throat and pivoted on his heel.
“I will be literally anywhere else,” he announced to the corridor, and vanished.
Kieran snorted. The laugh cracked something brittle. He caught Draven’s sleeve as the commander started to step away.
“Don’t,” Kieran said, the word rawer than he meant it to be. “Not yet.”
For a heartbeat Draven resisted—habit, pride, the thousand small disciplines he wore like armor. Then he exhaled and let himself be towed inside the ship.
Kieran closed the hatch. The galley’s low lights softened steel into warmth. He turned, leaned his spine against the door, and took Draven in: the bruised mouth, the jaw shadowed in stubble, the eyes still hot around the edges even as the commander tried to file himself back into order.
“You okay?” Kieran asked, voice quieter than the Aegis’s heartbeat.
Draven’s answer took a moment, as if he were inventorying parts. “Yes.” A beat. “Are you?”
Kieran huffed. “I’m dangerously close to smug. Does that count?”
That nearly-smile threatened Draven’s mouth and didn’t quite land. He stepped closer, not touching, but near enough that Kieran could feel the heat of him. “We should debrief.”
“Gods,” Kieran said, letting his grin turn wicked, “say that slower.”
A tiny sound—almost a laugh—escaped Draven. He shook his head. “Nerral.”
Right. Jace Nerral, who had slipped into the Obsidian Room like a needle sliding under skin and disappeared in the chaos while Kieran and Draven had—well.
“Lost him,” Kieran admitted. “For a very good reason.”
“An excellent reason,” Draven said dryly. His gaze drifted to Kieran’s wrists. Silence folded again, warmer this time. “Come here.”
It wasn’t an order, not technically, but Kieran obeyed like it was. Draven took his hands, turned them over, thumb smoothing over the faint reddened marks. The touch was gentle enough to steal the breath from Kieran’s chest.
“I should be angry,” Draven said.
“You can be two things,” Kieran murmured. “Angry and…” He let the and hang, bright and taunting. “Pleased.”
Draven’s eyes lifted, heat banked but not out. “We can’t let it happen again in the middle of an operation.”
“Then we plan better,” Kieran said. “Or worse.”
This time Draven did laugh, a short, helpless sound that made Kieran feel reckless and tender in the same breath. The commander sobered a beat later, sliding into the work with practiced ease.
“While you were… indisposed,” Draven said delicately, “Asha tailed Nerral’s contact. Short man, silver implant at the jaw, right ear. She lost him at a service junction near Waste Dorsal Two.”
“Romantic,” Kieran said. “Anything else?”
Draven let go of Kieran’s hands. Kieran wanted the contact back so badly he had to lock his knees to keep from asking. “Asha’s running the junction in the system. I want you to skim the Obsidian Room’s adjacent corridors. Not the club itself.”
“You’re adorable when you lie,” Kieran said.
Draven’s look said don’t without speaking it. “We need a thread. You know the lower decks better than anyone on my roster.”
“What do I get if I’m good?” Kieran asked, half-tease. The other half didn’t feel like a joke.
Draven’s gaze dropped to Kieran’s mouth. Rose. “I’ll think of something.”
Kieran grinned, bright as a match-head, and pushed off the door. “Then let’s be excellent.”
———
Lower Three at station-night was feral and beautiful. The markets thinned to bones; the bones showed teeth. Music crawled through the metal like a living thing. Kieran kept to the edges, to the places where cameras were lazy and the air smelled like heat.
He didn’t go back into the Obsidian Room. He didn’t need to. The corridors around it carried its weather—men leaning against walls with the dazed, satisfied look of the recently ruined; bouncers with shoulders like bulkheads; a thin man with a jaw implant that caught the neon like a mirror.
Jaw implant. Right ear.
Kieran’s pulse kicked. He let the crowd carry him until he drifted opposite the man, then peeled into a side passage like a fish slipping into a current. He waited two beats, then looked bored while his eyes watched.
The man—contact, runner, something—checked his wrist slate, scanned the corridor, and moved. Not back toward the club. Toward Waste Dorsal Two, just as Asha had said. He keyed a hatch with an innocuous maintenance tag, slipped through, and let it seal.
Kieran approached, listening. Behind the bulkhead, a faint whine—too smooth for pumps. Comms, maybe. Or power sipping through something it shouldn’t. He crouched, ran his fingers along the seam, felt the slight warmth of a panel that had seen more hands than a cleaning schedule justified.
He didn’t have Asha’s authorization tricks, but he had Kieran tricks. Two minutes later the hatch popped with a sigh small enough to be ashamed of itself.
Inside: a service crawl, narrow as a confession. Cables bundled along one wall. Footprints where no one should have left any. The air tasted stale, a little sweet with solvent. Kieran moved on hands and knees until the tunnel opened into a node chamber big enough to crouch in. The whine grew louder, modulated—like a choir under water.
He found the source fast: a receiver unit tucked into the ribbing, disguised with a maintenance plate. Delicate. Mean. The kind of thing that didn’t just listen—it leaned the signal, nudging it into a path like a shepherd dog.
Kieran’s mouth went dry. He touched his comm. “Vale to Draven.”
Static. Then Draven’s voice, filtered low. “Go.”
“I’m in Waste Dorsal Two. I’ve got a unit like your red dots. Active. Puts a hand on our comms and steers them someplace they don’t belong.”
“Don’t touch it,” Draven said immediately.
Kieran’s grin surfaced even without an audience. “You have so little faith.”
Asha’s voice cut in: “He’s right. If it’s meshed, you trip one and five others sneeze. Sit tight.”
“About that,” Kieran said, peering closer. The unit wore a sigil, etched microscopic along its edge—three overlapping diamonds. “This one’s Consortium-branded. And our jaw-implant friend probably installed it twenty minutes ago.”
“Do you have eyes on him?” Draven asked.
“No,” Kieran said. “But I have a better idea. I can piggyback it.”
Silence. Then Asha, wary. “Define piggyback without using the words ‘blow us all to hell.’”
“I leave it live,” Kieran said. “But I stitch a whisper on top. A tracer—something that rides their signal home and tells us where ‘home’ is. We learn who’s listening.”
“That’s a risk,” Draven said.
“That’s interesting,” Kieran countered.
A beat. Then: “Do it.”
Kieran smiled, small and sharp. He pulled a cable from his wrist rig, jacked in, and started humming under his breath the way he always did when he talked to systems. The receiver fought him—slick, smart, refusing his handshake until he changed the rhythm and offered a different name. It let him in like a grudging host.
“Come on,” he murmured. “Be good to me. Let me ride you.”
Data shimmered across his lens. A web unfolded—thin threads running through Erevos’ belly to a point that wasn’t a point so much as an orbit, something moving, ghosting, just beyond the station’s outer ring.
“Got you,” Kieran whispered. He wove his tracer in—soft as a flirt, careful as a thief. It nestled. It rode.
“Vale?” Draven, a thread of strain in the voice now.
“Done,” Kieran said. “If they tug this line, I’ll feel it.”
“Get out,” Asha said.
Kieran backed the way he’d come, skin prickling. At the hatch he paused, listening. The corridor beyond murmured with distant market noise. No footsteps. He slid out, sealed the panel, and leaned his head against the cool metal.
“Draven?”
“Here.”
Kieran swallowed against a sudden rush of relief that had nothing to do with the job and everything to do with the voice on the line. “Coming home.”
—
Home, in this moment, was the Aegis. Asha was already there, sleeves shoved up, eyes bright with the particular joy of a good problem. Draven stood with his hands braced on the table, looking like a man who had wagered more than anyone realized and was prepared to keep going.
Kieran relayed, showed them the tracer’s path, the ghosting orbit. Asha whistled low. “That’s not a fixed relay. That’s a ship.”
“Close,” Kieran said. “Fast. And shy.”
“Consortium scout?” Draven asked.
“Or courier,” Asha said. “We spook it and it runs. We shadow it and it leads us home.”
“Home sounds like trouble,” Kieran said, cheerful.
“It is,” Draven said. His gaze flicked to Kieran’s wrists again, quick as a pulse. “You did good.”
Heat curled under Kieran’s skin, warm and smug and a little stupid. He leaned in, elbows on the table, and smiled up at the commander in a way that should have been illegal. “Say it slower.”
Asha made a face of pure suffering. “I’m begging you both to develop professionalism.”
“Tomorrow,” Kieran said. “Tonight I plan to bask.”
“You plan to sleep,” Draven corrected, but his voice had softened at the edges. “We’ll set a watch on the tracer. If it twitches, we move.”
Kieran’s mouth opened on come stay, and he shut it before the words could escape. Instead he nodded, businesslike for once. “Aye aye, Commander.”
They broke, the room gentled by fatigue. Asha vanished into the station’s veins with a squad and a promise. Draven lingered, inventorying the mess because he needed to touch something he could fix.
Kieran hovered in the doorway, stupid with wanting and smart enough to keep most of it off his face. “Hey,” he said, casual as a coin toss. “About earlier.”
Draven looked up. “Which part?”
Kieran’s grin was sharp. “The part where you were a saint about the fact that I’m a terrible influence.”
“You’re an accelerant,” Draven said. Thoughtful. “Useful in the right conditions. Catastrophic in others.”
“And where do we fall?”
Draven considered him like a puzzle he’d already decided to enjoy. “Ask me in the morning.”
Kieran’s chest did something reckless. He nodded once, turned away before he could turn back, and let the Aegis swallow him.
In his bunk, the ache in his wrists hummed like a memory. He lay awake too long, listening to the ship breathe, waiting for the tracer to twitch. When it did—when the faintest tickle ran along his rig like a finger dragging over skin—he smiled into the dark.
“Got you,” he whispered to the unseen ship beyond the ring, to the Consortium that thought it could touch his station and go unseen, to the man down the corridor who had put a leash on his worst instincts and called it patience.
He tapped his comm. “Vale to Draven.”
A beat, then: “I’m here.”
“It moved,” Kieran said. “Our ghost. It’s running.”
“Then so are we,” Draven said, and the warmth in his voice had nothing to do with the hunt and everything to do with the fact that, for the first time in too long, they were about to run together.
Chapter a
The Meet … no meet
The Aegis tracked the tracer like a hound with blood in its teeth. Out beyond Erevos Station’s glimmering rings, the ghost ship danced—never still, never visible for more than a heartbeat, its silhouette rippling like heat over stone.
“It’s taunting us,” Asha muttered, hands white-knuckled on the tactical display.
“Or testing us,” Draven said. His jaw was carved hard, his eyes silver with focus. “They want to see who’s chasing.”
Kieran leaned back in the pilot’s chair, letting his grin tilt reckless. “Then let’s give them a show.”
The Aegis lunged, thrusters flaring. The ghost ship darted, then vanished into a stretch of the station’s maintenance ring that no sane captain threaded at speed.
“Suicide,” Asha hissed.
“Tuesday,” Kieran said, and dove after it.
The maintenance ring was a cathedral of steel ribs and scaffolding, pipes thick as trees, shadows long enough to hide armies. The Aegis weaved between girders, sparks singing across her hull.
The ghost ship reappeared just long enough to flash a grin of its own—then dropped a beacon.
Too late, Kieran realized it wasn’t a beacon. It was a mine.
The blast tore the air. The Aegis screamed, alarms vomiting red across the dash.
“Shields down!” Tovan shouted from engineering. “She’s bleeding!”
“Keep her alive!” Kieran snapped back, knuckles white as he rolled the ship hard, skimming a support strut close enough to shear paint.
The ghost ship didn’t flee. It circled. Hunting.
Consortium tactics.
“Ambush,” Draven growled. “They never ran. They led us here.”
“Then we don’t die pretty,” Kieran said, adrenaline turning his grin feral.
Another flash. Weapons fire sliced the dark, chewing sparks from the girders. The Aegis bucked, hull plating shrieking.
Asha slammed the console. “Return fire!”
“I’d love to,” Kieran said, “but they’re—”
The ghost ship flickered again, as if phasing in and out of existence. Cloak tech. Advanced. Dangerous.
Tovan’s voice cut sharp: “I can bleed her engines into the comms array—make us scream louder than they can hide.”
“Do it,” Draven said, no hesitation.
Kieran laughed, wild and breathless, as the Aegis howled into the void. The scream shredded static, tore the cloak wide. For a breath, the ghost ship was naked—sleek, black, sigils crawling its hull like veins of light. Consortium.
Kieran fired. The Aegis kicked, plasma tearing through the black. The ghost ship shuddered, peeled away, limped into the dark. Not destroyed. Not gone. But retreating.
The Aegis coughed smoke, hull bruised, alarms still weeping. Alive.
Silence fell in the cockpit, broken only by the hiss of venting systems. Kieran sagged back in his chair, heart thundering. “That,” he panted, “was foreplay.”
Draven’s glare was supposed to cut. It didn’t. Not when his own chest was rising sharp with the same rush. Their eyes locked, heat sparking like flint.
Asha groaned. “If either of you makes this into innuendo, I swear I’ll eject myself.”
Kieran grinned at her, reckless, ruined. “Promise?”
⸻
Later, when the Aegis was breathing steady again, Kieran and Draven found themselves alone in the narrow passage outside engineering. The ship smelled of smoke and victory, sweat and ozone.
Kieran shoved Draven against the bulkhead, kissed him like a man still alive by inches. It wasn’t careful. It wasn’t tender. It was teeth and heat and the raw high of survival.
Draven let it happen for a breath, then seized control, flipping them so hard Kieran’s spine hit the wall. His mouth was hot, his grip bruising, his hunger unmasked.
“This,” Draven growled against his lips, “is insanity.”
“This,” Kieran gasped back, laughing into the kiss, “is us.”
When they broke apart, both shaking, both hard, both undone, it was Asha’s voice crackling over comms that saved them from finishing what they’d started.
“Command wants a report,” she said, tone razor-dry. “Unless you’re busy writing poetry in the maintenance shaft.”
Kieran pressed his forehead to Draven’s, panting, smiling crooked. “She knows us too well.”
Draven shut his eyes, exhaled, and stepped back. “We’re not done.”
Kieran’s grin sharpened. “We never are.”
Chapter b
Ghostfire
The Aegis limped on one good engine and a prayer. Hull temperature climbed; coolant bled in glittering ribbons that crystallized and drifted like a comet’s tail. The tracer’s whisper jittered at Kieran’s wrist—then sharpened, a knife pulled clean.
“They’re back,” he said, voice raw from smoke.
Three signatures bloomed at once. The ghost ship in the lead—sleek, predatory, cloak shivering like heat over stone—escorted by two blades of light that burned hard and fast.
“Bow shields at twenty-eight,” Asha snapped. “Port emitters are—”
“Gone,” Tovan shouted from engineering, voice too bright with terror. “Port emitters are a memory I’m repressing!”
“Hard starboard,” Draven ordered. Calm. Unarguable.
Kieran threw the Aegis into the turn. The nearest escort’s fire chewed space where they’d just been. Concussive force slammed them sideways; a panel blew in the ceiling with a scream of rivets.
“Secondary fires on Deck Three!” Tovan coughed. “I need—ah, hell—containment foam is foaming where it shouldn’t!”
“Route emergency power,” Asha barked. “All nonessential—”
“Everything is essential when we’re dying,” Kieran hissed, and fed the last intact thruster a sliver more than wise. The ship protested, metal singing high and ugly, but obeyed.
The ghost ship didn’t harry. It herded. A subtle nudge of fire that pushed them toward the maintenance ring’s graveyard: bent trusses, rusted girders, mines that had slept and dreamed of teeth. The escorts fanned wide, sealing exits they hadn’t yet tried.
“This is deliberate,” Draven said, eyes like cut steel. “They want prisoners.”
Kieran laughed once—sharp, humorless. “They can want.”
They plunged into the graveyard. It swallowed them whole. Jagged hulls loomed like broken cathedral ribs. Mines blinked to life in cautious sequence, uncertain, then hungry.
“Collision warning,” the ship offered, politely doomed.
Kieran threaded a gap that shouldn’t have been a gap. The escort followed—and clipped the edge. The blast was blinding. Shockwave twisted the Aegis like a tin toy. Kieran’s forehead met the console. Warmth slid down his temple; the world doubled and tried to spin away.
“Stay with me, Vale,” Draven said—low, near, a hand clamped hard on Kieran’s shoulder.
“I’m here,” Kieran said, and made it true.
“Micro-breach on Deck Two,” Asha called. “Sealing bulkhead. Tovan?”
“On it! Also on fire! Briefly! It’s out now! We are a functionally stylish inferno!”
The ghost ship slid into view between bones of ships, cloak peeled back for the kill. The remaining escort darted close on their six, biting, biting, biting—
“Give me something,” Draven said, almost a prayer.
Kieran’s mind threw cards: jettison, flare, feint, beg. He chose a card no sane man loved.
“Graveyard chain,” he said. “We make it sing.”
“Too close,” Asha snapped. “We go with it.”
“Not if we time it,” Kieran said, pulse too fast. “We light one mine early, ride the shockwave through the collapsing truss, sling around the battleship spine and let the wreckage eat our escort. The ghost ship’s smart; it’ll back off. Maybe.”
“Maybe?” Asha demanded.
Draven’s hand tightened. “Make me believe we live.”
“Trust me,” Kieran said, and shoved the Aegis into a dive so ugly it felt like falling. Mines woke and blinked, confused. Kieran lined their tail with a dense cluster and screamed into comms, “Tovan—overcharge starboard reactor feed for four seconds and dump it aft on my mark.”
“That is not science,” Tovan said, horrified.
“It’s art,” Kieran said. “Mark.”
Tovan screamed back—wordless, obedient—and the Aegis exhaled a gout of plasma into the minefield.
The first mine tripped. The second sang. The third howled. Fire leapt mine to mine, a chain of white suns. Shockwaves collided, interference patterns chewing wrecks into shrapnel. The escort behind them vanished in a flower of light.
“Brace,” Draven said, not loud, not panicked—command distilled.
The shockfront punched the Aegis. For one long, liquid moment, they were weightless, lifted, flung—the hull creaked like an old bone. Kieran yanked the nose a hair to port, found the gap he had no right to find, and the Aegis slid through collapsing trusswork as steel screamed and bent around them like a closing jaw.
Alarms turned to a single continuous wail. The lights flickered, died, returned as ghosts.
They burst free into a pocket of relative emptiness—if anything in this graveyard could be called that—and Kieran used the momentum to sling them around the spine of a dead battleship. The wreck’s thermal shadow wrapped them in cold.
“Kill everything,” he panted. “Hot, loud, bright—we’re a flare in the dark. Make us a stone.”
Asha’s fingers flew; systems fell silent, one by one, until the Aegis was a hunk of cooling metal and the faintest whisper of life support. Tovan’s cursing cut off mid-oath as he obeyed.
Darkness took them. The only sound left was the soft wheeze of the ship catching her breath.
“Hold,” Draven murmured. “No one move.”
They held. Kieran tasted copper and burned insulation. Felt his own pulse in his wrists. Watched, through the cracked canopy, as the ghost ship drifted along the far side of the battleship spine—searching. Patient. A shark’s shadow in a night ocean.
It paused. The cloak shivered. For five eternal seconds, Kieran was sure it felt them. He could feel it feeling them.
The ghost ship moved on.
They didn’t breathe for a count of thirty. Sixty. Ninety.
“Time?” Asha whispered.
“Now,” Draven said, and only then did anyone let a breath go.
The Aegis still didn’t move. They stayed colder than the dark, listening to the predator prowl until it slid away—bored or called elsewhere.
Kieran sagged back. The world fuzzed at the edges.
Draven’s hand was suddenly at the back of his neck again, grounding him. “Don’t pass out,” he said.
Kieran smiled, blood in his teeth. “Only if you ask nicely.”
Then the black came, soft as velvet, and took him.
—
He woke to pain and the taste of oxygen concentrate, an emergency mask pressed to his face. The cockpit was a ruin of blown panels and hanging wires, but it was there. Draven’s shadow loomed over him, silver eyes dim with exhaustion and something softer.
“We’re hidden,” Draven said. “For now.”
Kieran blinked, tried to sit. The universe objected. Draven’s hand settled, gentle iron. “Later.”
Kieran closed his eyes. “That was a miracle.”
Draven’s thumb brushed once—barely a touch. “That was you.”
“Same difference,” Kieran mumbled, and let the dark take him again—this time, by choice.



Chapter c
Ashes and Embers



Silence held for a long time after the predator passed. Not peace—never that—but the wary quiet of an animal with its belly to the ground, listening.
Life on the Aegis returned like dawn on a world with a broken rotation: slowly, unevenly, and with a few false starts. Lights came back in bands; air grew sweeter a breath at a time.
Tovan was the first voice to surface, ragged over the intercom. “Engineering to anyone with hands. If you can’t tell a spanner from a spoon, bring both.”
Asha’s reply floated up from the black like a buoy. “Copy. On my way.”
Kieran crawled toward waking. The mask fed him. His head throbbed a double beat that matched the pulse in his teeth. When he managed to stand, the cockpit swam and steadied. Draven didn’t say be careful; he offered an arm, and Kieran took it like a lifeline that neither of them would name.
They moved through the ship together, past compartments strewn with the detritus of survival—firefoam residue in drifting drifts, cracked lights, a trail of blood that had the decency to be Kieran’s.
In engineering, heat pressed like a palm. Tovan crouched among open panels, hair singed, eyebrows non-existent and not yet mourned. He looked up, face dirty and luminous with stubborn joy.
“We lived,” he announced, as if daring anyone to argue.
“Barely,” Asha said, sleeves shoved to elbows, hands steady as she reconnected a snake’s wedding of cables. Sweat ran in a fine line down her temple; she ignored it.
Kieran leaned against a rail and watched them breathe—the crew knitting itself back together fiber by fiber. The ache behind his ribs expanded, not pain so much as pressure. Gratitude, maybe. Or the terrifying urge to sit down and tell everyone he’d ever cared for that he was sorry he’d been late.
Tovan tossed a half-melted tool aside and popped open a storage locker with a flourish. “First aid, second aid, and third-degree coffee,” he declared. He held up a dented kettle like a trophy.
“That kettle died six months ago,” Asha said dryly.
“It retired,” Tovan said. “It will guest star in tonight’s production of ‘We Keep Breathing.’” He set it on a portable coil and waited for the sputtering thing to decide it still had meaning.
They drifted toward the galley in small increments, as much pulled by the idea of a table as by hunger. Kieran sat gingerly. Asha took the bench like a soldier who had counted her wounds and found them acceptable. Tovan collapsed opposite them with the theatrical sigh of a man who would live forever just to spite statistics.
Draven arrived last, after making a slow, quiet circuit of the hull the way a captain reads a ship by touch. He took a seat without ceremony, and the room accepted him.
For a while, they ate in the way people who have almost died must: whatever was hot, whatever was sweet, whatever quieted the shaking in their hands. The coffee was criminal. It was also perfect.
Conversation resurfaced in careful pieces.
Tovan went first, because Tovan always did. “When I was a kid,” he said, staring into steam, “my mother ran a salvage tug out past the Selene wrecks. Everything we owned smelled like burned insulation and sugar. She kept candy in the fuse box so I’d check the lines without her having to nag. When things went bad, she’d make me sit on the deck and sort jellybeans by color. ‘Order,’ she’d say, ‘is what we make when the universe won’t give us any.’”
He laughed, scrubbed his sleeve over his eyes, pretended it was sweat. “Saved my life more than once. Today I sorted washers by size when the coolant lines blew. Felt like I was ten, sticky fingers and all.”
“Saved ours too,” Asha said. The words landed like a medal.
Tovan pretended to accept it casually and failed.
Asha set down her mug with the care of a woman who treasured small rituals because the big ones were too often funerals. “First time I saw a ship die,” she said, tone even, “I was twenty. Boarded a patrol cruiser three minutes before it split along the spine like fruit. The noise it made…” She paused, as if letting the sound pass through her again. “Thought that was the worst of it. It wasn’t. It was the silence after. You could hear the heat leaving the metal.”
She breathed. “I learned that day how to count. Not kills. Survivors. It’s a different math. Better. Today’s number is four.” She looked at each of them, steady, unsentimental. “I’m keeping it.”
Draven’s fingers traced the rim of his cup. He didn’t volunteer, at first. He was a man who let his stories choose him. Eventually, one did.
“My brother,” he said simply. Kieran’s head came up, surprised. Draven didn’t look away. “He was older by three years and had a talent for running toward fire. When our colony’s power grid failed in winter, he spent a night out in the turbines thawing lines with a heat torch and a bad plan. He came back with frostbite and the grin of a man who thinks he outwitted death. He didn’t. He just postponed the argument.”
He folded his hands, knuckles scarred, voice low. “I joined fleet because rules are scaffolding. Because some fires you don’t outrun—you build around them. Today…” A small, tired smile. “Today we ran toward the fire and built scaffolding on the fall.”
Kieran, who told stories like coin tricks or confessions depending on the audience, looked at the three faces that had chosen to live with him and felt the shape of something he’d spent years misnaming. He cleared his throat.
“My father kept dice in his pocket,” he said. “Not fancy ones. Pitted bone, edges worn. He’d roll them when the ship shuddered, when the captain yelled, when the universe felt like it had teeth in our soft parts. ‘Luck,’ he’d say, ‘is just courage that paid off.’ He was wrong, and also right. He died in a docking accident because he thought he could out-charm physics.”
He smiled without showing teeth. “I learned two things. One: never bet what you can’t afford to lose. Two: sometimes bet anyway, because not betting is a slower death.”
Silence followed. Not empty. Full. The ship hummed, tired and proud around them.
Tovan broke it with a sniff. “I hate your coffee, Asha.”
“It’s your coffee,” she said.
He brightened. “Then it’s perfect.”
They laughed. A small thing. A necessary one.
Gradually, the room softened. Shoulders lowered. Breathing deepened. Kieran watched Draven’s profile in the low light—the hard lines gentled, the eyes heavier, the mouth less guarded. He felt that familiar pull, yes, but under it something steadier: the ache of seeing the man, not the uniform or the heat.
He reached across the table, slow. Draven didn’t flinch. Their fingers touched. Not a claim. A contact. An allowance.
Asha pretended not to see. Tovan loudly banged another mug into usefulness.
For an hour, they talked in the low, wandering way of people who have earned rest: favorite ports and worst meals; the day Tovan stole a governor’s hat and sold it back at twice the price; the time Asha saved a child on a burning deck by bribing him with a chocolate bar; the way Draven once smuggled kittens off a condemned freighter and spent a week smelling like contraband joy.
Kieran told a story about sleeping on top of an engine for warmth and waking with little crescent burns along his spine that looked, in certain light, like constellations. Draven touched one of the scars with his gaze and something like reverence crossed his face.
The Aegis settled into the quiet with them. Systems purred. The hull ticked as it cooled. The dark outside held.
And then—because the universe is a comedian with bad timing—the comms chirped.
Not a shout this time. A chime. Soft. Insistent.
Asha’s slate glowed red at her elbow. She didn’t pick it up right away. For three heartbeats, she let the moment resist. Then she looked.
Her face changed. “Tracer,” she said. Her voice didn’t shake. “It’s moving. Deliberate vector. Convoy route.”
The warmth drained like a tide. Kieran felt the old electricity flood him, unwelcome and welcome, dread and purpose braided. He met Draven’s eyes. Whatever fragile thing had been building didn’t break. It set its teeth and held on.
“Playtime’s over,” Draven said, echoing himself, but softer—like a promise to bring them all back again.
Kieran stood. His body complained; he ignored it. “Let’s make the galaxy regret inviting us to the party.”
Tovan saluted with a greasy spoon. “Aye aye, Captain Bad Idea.”
Asha rose, already issuing orders that sounded like hope disguised as logistics. The Aegis groaned and shook herself and came awake, battered and ready.
They moved—out of the galley, back into the ribs of their wounded ship, back toward work and war and whatever waited. The stories didn’t vanish. They went with them, stitched into skin and steel.
Outside, the stars looked on, cold and bright and utterly impartial, as the Aegis slid free of her hiding place and turned her scarred nose toward the hunt.
Chapter Ten
Fault Lines
The Aegis crawled from her hiding place, hull scorched, scars unpolished. She was a wounded animal following the scent of blood, and the tracer on Kieran’s wrist pulsed like a heartbeat.
“Vector confirmed,” Asha said, voice taut. “Consortium ship broke cover three hours ago. Trajectory intersects with convoy lanes in Sector Twelve.”
“Civilian?” Draven asked.
“Mostly. One diplomatic transport, six freighters, two medical haulers.” She didn’t add the obvious: soft targets.
Kieran’s hands flexed on the yoke. His head still ached from where the console had kissed him, but the pain steadied him. “Then we don’t dawdle.”
The Aegis burned hard. The stars streaked, endless and merciless.
They dropped into Sector Twelve like a stone breaking the surface.
The convoy sprawled against the void: a line of patched freighters, engines glowing faint and weary, the gleam of the diplomatic carrier’s polished hull standing out like a jewel.
And already the sharks had gathered.
Three Consortium strike craft darted in formation, small and vicious. Behind them, the ghost ship slid from cloak—serene, predatory, unhurried.
“Contact in weapons range,” Asha barked.
The comms filled with panic—civilian captains shouting, begging, promising credits they didn’t have. The diplomatic ship held silence, every second a prayer.
Kieran’s grin was sharp and humorless. “Time to ruin somebody’s day.”
The Aegis dove.
Plasma fire streaked past, close enough to sear paint. Kieran rolled them under, the ship groaning in protest. He threaded between two freighters, used their bulk as cover, and popped up to land a clean shot across a strike craft’s bow. The Consortium pilot twisted away, wounded but not dead.
“Shields at forty percent,” Asha snapped. “One more hit and we’re meat.”
“Working on it!” Tovan shouted. Somewhere below, sparks flew. “I’m giving you power I don’t technically have, so don’t waste it!”
Kieran laughed once, wild. “Wouldn’t dream of it.”
The Aegis skimmed close to a medical hauler, so close Kieran could see wide eyes behind its bridge glass. He pulled hard, flinging the ship into a climb that rattled bones. Draven braced beside him, calm as stone but radiating heat.
“They’re not scattering,” Draven said. “They’re herding again.”
“Toward what?” Asha demanded.
The answer came in the form of a sudden flare: a minefield igniting in perfect sequence ahead of the convoy. Not random debris this time. Deliberate. Planted.
“They’re driving them into a kill box,” Kieran said, stomach cold.
“Not on our watch,” Draven said.
The next minutes blurred into survival. Plasma streaked. The Aegis shook. Kieran flung her into dives that scraped the edge of death, clipped shields, burned systems. A freighter took a hit it couldn’t shake and spun off, venting fire. The medical haulers screamed for help.
“Pick one,” Asha snapped. “We can’t save them all.”
Kieran’s hands clenched. His father’s voice rose in memory: Luck is courage that paid off. His own answered, bitter: Sometimes you lose the bet.
“Medical first,” Draven said. No hesitation. No compromise.
“Copy,” Kieran ground out, and threw the ship into the fire.
The Aegis cut between the haulers and the ghost ship, taking hits meant for softer hulls. Shields died with a final groan. The ship herself screamed like a wounded thing. Tovan roared curses that made the comms blush.
The haulers peeled away, limping but alive. The freighters weren’t so lucky. One detonated in silence, bright and brief. Another vanished into cloak-choked shadow.
When it was over, they had saved half. Maybe less. The rest were gone.
The Consortium pack melted back into the dark. The ghost ship lingered just long enough to let them see it hadn’t been beaten, only amused, before vanishing into cloak again.
The convoy survivors whispered thanks over comms, voices ragged with grief. Then they limped away, too broken to linger.
Silence filled the Aegis. Not victory. Just survival.
—
Later, in the war room, the silence turned sharper.
“You disobeyed me,” Draven said.
Kieran blinked, incredulous. “I just saved two haulers full of medics.”
“You lost three freighters because you gambled on timing instead of formation,” Draven shot back, eyes blazing.
“They were already lost!” Kieran snapped. “I had to bet big or they all burned. You think I don’t feel it? You think I didn’t watch them die?”
Asha looked between them, face carved from stone. Tovan shifted awkwardly, grease-smeared and guilty.
“This isn’t about your feelings,” Draven said, steel in every word.
“No,” Kieran said, stepping close enough that the air between them hummed. “It’s about your control. You want me to be the good soldier at your side. I’m not. I never will be. I’m the mad bastard who wins when everyone else folds.”
Draven’s jaw clenched, breath sharp. Their eyes locked—anger, grief, lust tangled and sparking.
For a heartbeat, Kieran thought Draven might kiss him then, savage and furious. For a heartbeat, he wanted it more than air.
Instead, Draven turned away, voice low and final. “We’ll regroup. Then we hunt them.”
Kieran laughed once, bitter and raw. “Whatever you say, Commander.”
He left the war room before his hands could betray him.
Behind him, the Aegis groaned softly, a ship carrying too much pain and not nearly enough hope.

The Starfire Gambit
When the obsidian room flickered back to normal, Draven's pupils were still pinpricks from the blackout. Kieran had already opened the door, the corridor outside wavering with emergency strobes and the smell of melting polymers. 'We need to go,' Kieran said, tugging Draven by the sleeve.
They made it through the club’s service hatch just as the main lights came back up. Draven didn’t slow, already raising his sleeve comm. 'Report.'
Ash’s voice, compressed and staticky, piped in: 'Same as before, Commander. Packs haven't had injections in six months. The new isotope shipment is still in cold transit, ETA three days to thaw.'
Aria, their systems analyst, broke in with a projection: 'If we force the injection before the isotope’s fully stabilized, we've got a sixty-five percent chance of success. That’s if the core temperature stays under ten thousand kelvins.'
Kieran checked the nearest display. 'Current temp?' 
'Ticking up from five to six K,' Aria replied.
Draven grimaced. 'And the risk?'
Aria hesitated. 'Eighty-five percent chance the grid isolating Dock Nine fails in the next seventy-two hours.'
Dock Nine. The old delivery, still in quarantine after the crash. If the core warmed even a fraction, every disaster model went red.
Kieran’s jaw set. 'We have to try the forced injection.'
Draven nodded. 'Do it.'
He watched the wall display as Ash initiated the sequence. For a moment, the numbers stabilized. Then everything went black. Sirens screamed as power failed in a wave across the lower crescent—docks one through nine, then ten through twenty-two. The deck thrummed under their boots as containment shields dropped, and in the distance, the bulkhead groaned.
Aria’s voice was thin and urgent. 'Dock Nine’s heating up. The ship’s going to breach.'
Draven inhaled. 'We tow it out. Now.'
Kieran was already moving, sprinting for the maglev. 'Aegis is prepped. We’ll get there first.'
By the time Draven and Kieran reached the Aegis, other pilots were already scrambling. 'Get the ships out of seven, eight, and ten through fourteen. Clear a path,' Draven barked. The comms crackled with frantic affirmatives.
'Commander,' Ash cut in, 'four civilian convoys are inbound, already in the lanes.'
Draven swore. 'Prioritize evacuation. But keep them moving, or they’ll be sitting ducks.'
Aegis dropped into the bay with a bone-rattling thud. The old wreck in Dock Nine was already sweating vapor, its hull warping under thermal stress. Draven eyed the magnetic clamps. 'On my mark.'
As the Aegis locked onto the derelict, the ghost ship materialized from the black, hull matte and silent. It opened fire, targeting the civilian transports and the retreating ships alike.
'Veer, take the helm,' Draven shouted. 'Kieran, with me.'
They slid into their harnesses as the Aegis shuddered under direct hits. Kieran’s hands danced over the controls, and for a moment, Draven saw the boy he’d grown up with—reckless, brilliant, unbreakable.
'We’re not going to outrun it,' Kieran said.
'No. But we can outthink it.'
Draven’s eyes flicked to the sensor readouts. 'We need a second Star Cruiser. Argyle’s up on twenty-six.'
The plan was unspoken but clear: slingshot the burning wreck, using both ships to hurl it at escape velocity—and, if possible, straight at the ghost ship.
Aegis and Argyle locked in tandem, hulls groaning as they strained the tow cables. The ghost ship closed in, its cannons raking the civilian lanes. Jalen’s voice came over the comm, tight with adrenaline. 'Ready on your mark.'
Draven counted down. 'Three. Two. One.'
Kieran hit the burn. Both ships surged forward, the wreck between them, plasma jets screaming. The ghost ship loomed, firing point-blank. The derelict’s hull ruptured, and for a blinding second, the world went white.
When Draven’s vision cleared, the comms were a chaos of voices. One of the convoys was gone, atomized in the blast. The Aegis had lost two engines; the Argyle drifted, venting atmosphere, but Jalen’s voice cut through the static: 'We’re alive. Barely.'
Kieran coughed, blood in his teeth. 'Told you it would work.'
Draven nodded, slumping in his harness. 'Let’s get the survivors home.'
The Aegis limped back toward the graveyard outpost, battered but intact. The ghost ship was gone. For nowe.
